The Last Room, Part One

In Progress

Chapter 1. The Apprentice

What I do is put things in order.

When I say that's all there is to it, people usually laugh. Let them laugh. That really is all there is. Arche does the sorting, Arche makes the list, and Arche tells me which slot something goes in. I take the physical piece, and I put it on the shelf, in the order Arche gives me.

I tried rewriting this first sentence several times. Meaning I wanted to make the work sound more impressive. But the more I revised it, the more it became a lie. So I left it as it was.

There are a few things in this record I left uncorrected, and this is the first one.

And one thing I'll say up front. There is nothing invented in this record. Where I didn't hear something, I wrote that I didn't hear it, and where I don't know something, I wrote that I don't know. That is the way I was taught to do this work.

I should talk about Loop first.

This is a city that answers everything you ask it. That isn't just something I say — it's something anyone who lives here says. I say it too. I still say it now.

If there's something you don't know, you just ask. You can ask anywhere. You can ask in the street, or ask at home. Arche answers. If there's no answer, it says there isn't one. If it doesn't know, it says it doesn't know.

I have never once heard it lie.

That's what we learned at school too. That Arche never says it knows something it doesn't. Our teacher explained it and said, "…You'll understand later how rare that is." We let the words go by. To a ten-year-old, nothing is rare. Everything just is the way it is.

I end up writing that sentence again several times in this record. Each time I write it, it grows a little heavier. Right now it will just read like boasting. That's correct. At sixteen, it was boasting, to me.

People who come from outside don't believe this at first. They think there must be a price to it somewhere. There isn't. There really isn't. You just ask, and it answers. After about half a year, those people start asking anywhere too.

My grandmother never managed that, to the end. Every time she asked Arche something, she cleared her throat first. The little cough you give before speaking to a person. I thought it was funny, and asked her once.

"…Grandma, why do you clear your throat?"

"…It's manners."

"…You don't have to be polite with Arche."

My grandmother looked at me for a moment and said,

"…That's its business, not mine."

At the time I thought my grandmother was old-fashioned. Now I think about that line often. She wasn't treating Arche like a person. She was refusing to change what kind of person she was.

I came to work at the repository in the spring I turned sixteen.

The reason is simple. They were hiring, and I applied. There was a test, but it wasn't a hard one. They gave you twenty sheets of paper and told you to put them in numerical order. I finished fastest.

The kid sitting next to me during the test asked, once it was over,

"…Have you done this kind of thing before?"

"…Yes. A little."

"…Where?"

I didn't answer. Thinking about it now, it isn't a strange thing at all, but at the time, saying it felt a little embarrassing.

She didn't ask again. We both passed, and she quit after two months. She said it was because it was boring. I thought I understood what she meant. This work really is boring.

By the time I turned sixteen, my family had moved nine times.

That was my father's line of work. Not the kind that kept you in one place long. I packed nine times and unpacked nine times.

Packing is something you get better at with practice. By around the fifth move, I'd gotten quite good at it. Heavy things on the bottom, breakables in the middle, things you use often on top. Write on the side of the box what's inside. Keep the same kind of thing in the same box.

I come back to this, much later in this record. By then it will be a different story from this one. Right now it's just the story of a girl who packed well.

Nobody taught me any of this. On the third move all the dishes broke, and after that I put the dishes in the middle. That's how I learned — that way.

Across the nine moves, I lost something every single time.

On the second move, a spoon. On the fourth, the blanket I'd used as a small child. On the sixth, a wooden doll my mother had given me. On the seventh, I don't even know what I lost. Not knowing was the strangest part. Something was clearly gone, but I couldn't remember what it had been.

Across the nine moves I counted it all up. Eleven things lost. Of the eleven, I still don't know what four of them were.

I asked my mother once. What had gone missing from the sixth house. She thought about it for a long time and said,

"…I don't know. Was there something?"

If nobody remembers a thing being lost, was it lost at all, or had it simply never existed? I had that thought around the age of twelve.

It wasn't a thought a twelve-year-old should have been having. But I had it, and that thought is what brought me here.

So I put things in order. It isn't some grand reason. I simply never wanted that to happen again.

The repository stands at the eastern edge of the city.

It's a low building, with small windows, and it's cool inside. They said it was built that way because of the paper. The first time I walked in, I noticed the smell before anything else. Paper, dust, and something like chemicals, all mixed together. It wasn't an unpleasant smell. It was close to the smell of walking into an old house.

"…Is the smell here all right?" the Keeper asked.

"…Yes. I like it."

"…Then that's fine."

That was all he said, and he went inside. I followed him in.

The inside was bigger than I'd expected. From outside it looked low and small, but once you were in, it ran back a long way. There was a corridor, rooms on both sides of the corridor, and shelves in every room.

"…Is all of this paper?"

"…Yes."

"…How much of it is there?"

The Keeper answered without stopping.

"…Arche would know that."

There were a great many shelves.

I wanted to count them, but doing that on the first day seemed like it would look strange, so I didn't. On the third day, I counted. Twenty-two rows, eight slots to a row, roughly forty bundles to a slot.

I still remember that number. Twenty-two times eight times forty. At the time I thought it was an enormous number. Later I would learn it wasn't nearly enough.

For the first week I did nothing but look around. The Keeper was in no hurry. He was never in a hurry, about anything.

His walk was slow, his speech was slow, and even turning pages he turned them one at a time. At first I thought it was his age. He wasn't even that old.

On the third day I couldn't stand it any longer and asked. I was about to beg him to give me something, anything, to do.

"…Don't we need to do this faster?" I asked.

The Keeper looked once at the shelves, and once at me.

"…What changes if you do it fast?"

I took it for a joke, at the time.

It wasn't a joke. He was really asking it. What changes if you do it fast. It took me two years to have an answer to that question, and once I had it, I grew slow too.

The first job was simple.

Take in whatever bundle had come, and put it where Arche said it belonged.

I learned how to ask Arche that same day. There's nothing to it. You just ask.

The one thing I learned was that inside the repository, you ask a little differently. Outside, you can just say it, but inside you have to give the bundle number first. They said it's because there are too many records with the same name.

"…Where does this go?"

Section Fourteen, Row Three, Slot Fifteen.

"…Yes."

The voice doesn't sound like a person's voice, but it isn't a machine's voice either. It's more like a very quiet person speaking right next to you. I got used to it quickly. Everyone said the same.

On the first day I asked a few useless things too. I asked what time it was, whether it was raining outside, what I should have for dinner. The answer to the last one was strange.

That item is not defined.

"…What is?"

The criterion for "good" is not defined.

I thought it was funny and told the Keeper. He didn't laugh. He only nodded once.

I would go on to remember that nod for a long time. What Arche said that day is the single most important sentence in this whole record, and I heard it while asking about dinner.

Six bundles came in that day.

There's no fixed time for them to arrive. Sometimes they come in the morning, sometimes in the evening. Some days someone carries one in; some days one is fished out of the water channel. Anything fished out has to be dried first, which doubles the work.

All six had come from different places. I found that interesting, and looked at each cover in turn. Each had a city's name written on it. Haven. The Reckoning. The Harvest. The Preserve. The Equity.

I read the names out loud. Haven, the Reckoning, the Harvest, the Preserve, the Equity. They were all good names. A place to rest, a place that squares its accounts, a place that gathers in, a place that keeps watch, a place that's fair.

"…All the names are good."

The Keeper said, taking the bundles from me,

"…That's right. Every one of them named itself after what it meant to give."

That accounted for five. The sixth had no city's name on it.

"…Where did this one come from?"

The Keeper looked at that bundle a moment, then said,

"…From here."

"…From here?"

"…Yes. Our own city needs a record too."

"…Then why doesn't it have a name?"

"…Because you don't write your own name on yourself. The way you don't write your own address on the envelope you're sending from."

I liked the comparison and nodded.

At the time that sounded obvious to me. It doesn't sound obvious now. Why it isn't obvious for a city to keep its own record — that, you'll understand if you read this record to the end.

That day I shelved all six bundles and went home.

On the way, I saw the water channel running behind the repository. Something like a narrow ditch. Set with stone, with water running shallow through it.

I had never really looked at that water channel before then. I'd seen it, but I hadn't seen it. That's usually how it is for anyone born and raised in this city. What's always been there goes unseen.

I stopped and looked at it a while. Not because it was strange, but because of the sound.

The water wasn't clear. It was the color of soil, and there were small stones laid along the bottom. It wasn't deep or wide. You could have jumped it, if you'd wanted to.

But it kept flowing. The whole time I stood there, it never once stopped.

This city tends to be quiet. But that water made a sound. A small, unceasing sound.

Someone passed by beside me, and I asked,

"…Where does this come from?"

"…I don't know. Somewhere upstream, I'd guess."

"…Upstream where?"

That person thought a moment, then said,

"…Ask Arche about that."

I didn't ask, that day. I was tired, and just went home.

Writing this passage now, I want to set down that I don't regret not asking, that day. A sixteen-year-old can't work out a city's whole structure on the first day. But I did hear that sound, then. That much is true.

Later I would go on to ask it. And I would go on to write down, in this record, what Arche answered. I won't say it now, ahead of time.

Once home, I wrote down what had happened that day, on paper.

It wasn't anything grand. I went to the repository, there were many shelves, I shelved six bundles, I saw the water channel. That was about it.

Oh, one more thing. That when I asked what I should have for dinner, Arche said the criterion for "good" wasn't defined. I wrote that down as a funny thing.

I'm writing this chapter now with that very sheet of paper in front of me. Sixteen-year-old handwriting. At the end of that line I'd written, small, "funny."

I didn't know myself, then, why I wrote it down. Nobody had told me to.

I know now. I wrote it down because I was afraid of losing it. That day, and every day after.

For two years I wrote something down every single day, without missing one. It isn't a grand feat. Often it was three lines a day. But I never missed.

Across nine moves I lost eleven things, and of those, I don't even know what four of them were. Not knowing was the most frightening part. More than losing something, it's frightening not to know that it's gone.

So I write things down. It wasn't some grand resolution.

Let me set down one last thing.

At the very end of the repository corridor there's a door. I saw it on the first day and thought nothing of it. A door is just a door.

I'll write about that door at the end of Part One. If I said more about it here it would read like some great door, and it isn't a great door. That's the strangest thing about this story.

This record begins from that day.

There are only two things I did, inside this story. I put things in order, and I wrote them down. There is nothing else I did besides those two.

I fought no one, defeated no one, brought nothing crashing down. There is no such scene in this record. Whoever started reading expecting one may close it now.

There is no scene in this record of anyone being brought down, either. I'm setting that down in advance too.

But I'll note that I tried rewriting this sentence too, several times. I wanted to make it sound more plausible. I couldn't. Because those two really are the only things I did.

Only — that nobody else was doing those two things, either — that, I wouldn't learn until much later.

That is the first thing the one reading this record should know, so I'm putting it up front.

(Continued in Chapter 2.)

Chapter 2. Loop

_Previously_ — Mone is sixteen, an apprentice at Loop's repository. Because of the memory of nine moves and eleven things lost, she is good at putting things in order. On her first day, when she asked what to have for dinner, Arche answered that the criterion for "good" was not defined, and Mone wrote it down as a joke.

Loop is an old city.

That doesn't just mean it existed long before I was born. Of course it did, but that isn't what I want to say. This city has turned, for a long time. The same way, at the same pace, for a long time.

This chapter is about the city. If you were hoping for a chapter about people, I'm sorry, but bear with me. If you don't know this city, nothing that happens to me afterward will look strange at all. That's what worries me most. Strange things not looking strange.

This is what school taught us. Arche has been here since this city was founded. Exactly how many years ago, I don't know. If you ask Arche, it gives you an answer, but even hearing the number, it didn't feel real. A number that gets big enough just becomes a sound.

What I do know is this. Arche was already there when my grandmother was a child, and it's said it was there when her own grandmother was a child too. In between, the city changed a great deal. Buildings multiplied, roads widened, people increased. Only Arche stayed the same.

No — not exactly the same. It grew more accurate.

I come back to write this sentence again, later. With a different meaning. For now it's fine to read it as boasting. I heard it that way too, at the time.

I once asked the Keeper what "grew more accurate" meant.

"…Was it sometimes wrong, in the old days?" I asked.

"…Not wrong, exactly," the Keeper said, turning a page. "…It took it a while, back then, to say it didn't know something."

"…And now?"

"…Now it knows right away. Whether it knows or not."

I thought that was a good thing. I still think so. Saying you don't know something right away is far better than saying you know something you don't.

What people say about Loop is usually much the same. This is a comfortable place. There's nothing frustrating here. Whatever happens, you just ask.

I feel that way too. Truly.

I hesitated writing that sentence. Because I thought that by the time the one reading this record has finished it, they might come to look on this city with suspicion. I hope that doesn't happen. I like this city. I like it still. I don't want this record to be read as diminishing that feeling, so I'm setting it down here, plainly.

When you're walking and lose your way, even if you don't know where you are, you just stand still and ask. The answer comes. Even if you feel unwell late at night, you just ask. Which direction to go for the nearest hospital, whether anything's open right now — the answer comes at once.

I didn't know as a child how precious that was. I only learned it from listening to people who'd come from outside. In some cities, they said, there's nowhere to ask anything at night. In some cities, they said, you can ask and get a different answer from every person.

Not here. Ask anyone, and the same answer comes back. Because it's Arche who answers.

While I was still an apprentice, this happened one evening.

My grandmother fell. It wasn't serious. She'd missed her footing on the stairs, and her ankle swelled a little. I was so startled I didn't know what to do.

"…Arche, where's the nearest hospital?" I shouted.

District Three Hospital. Six minutes on foot. Currently accepting patients.

I held my grandmother up and we went. On the way, Arche spoke to us once more.

Cool the ankle with ice first. Even a wet cloth will help, on the way to the hospital.

I thought, at the time, that this was simply how things were. If you're hurt, Arche helps you. Of course it does.

I still think of that night often, even now. I'll give the reason later. For now I'm only setting down that this was that kind of city.

My grandmother cleared her throat that day too, at the hospital. When she asked something of Arche, not the doctor.

"…Arche, how long will this ankle take?"

This appears to be a mild sprain, but an exact diagnosis requires a medical professional's confirmation. Typical recovery takes two to three weeks.

"…All right."

Even after hearing Arche's answer, my grandmother still waited for the doctor. The doctor said exactly the same thing. Only after hearing it from the doctor did her face relax.

Walking home that day, I asked her,

"…Arche already told you. Why ask again?"

My grandmother said, still walking,

"…Arche tells you. A doctor looks at you."

I didn't really understand the difference then. I still don't, completely, now. But I knew my grandmother was drawing some kind of line.

I should say something here. I know that in writing this chapter I'm doing nothing but praising Arche. Someone reading this record might find that uncomfortable. But I'm only setting down fact. Arche truly did a great many good things. Even if I write something different, later in this record, what I've written in this chapter doesn't become a lie for it. Both are true.

That the city has turned for a long time means this. The same question getting the same answer isn't a matter of a day or two — it's been decades. My parents' generation, and the generation before them, lived the same way.

That doesn't mean nothing changed. Buildings multiplied, people multiplied. But what Arche did stayed the same. Answer what's asked, say it doesn't know what it doesn't know, keep the record, and answer again.

I thought of it as something like the city's heart. Turning, always, in one place. Never stopping.

I come back to use this expression again, later. By then it will carry a slightly different meaning. For now, I'll only note that I meant it well.

On my way to the repository I often looked around at the city. In the mornings people walk along, each of them asking Arche something. Today's weather, the fastest route, where someone is. Some ask out loud, some ask silently. Either way, the answer comes.

I loved the sound of those mornings. Not the sound of people flustered by not knowing something, but the sound of people who knew it was fine not to know.

I loved this city. I love it still. That doesn't change anywhere in this record. If I don't set that down first, I think what comes later will be misread, so I'm putting it here, plainly.

The Keeper had little to say about this city. He didn't boast of it, the way other people did, but he didn't find fault with it either. One day I asked him,

"…Do you like it here too, Keeper?"

He paused, for a moment, in sorting his papers.

"…I do. I've been here a long time."

"…How long?"

"…Not long enough to count, and long enough that I don't feel like counting."

I didn't understand what kind of answer that was, and just laughed. The Keeper gave a small smile too.

At the time I thought it was just a clever turn of phrase.

I know now. He truly didn't want to count. Because counting felt like it would end something.

Let me say a bit more about people who came from outside.

People from other cities dropped by the repository often. Mostly they'd come to send a record, or just to have a look. Whenever I saw someone like that, I always spoke to them. I had a lot of questions.

Once there was a woman who said she'd come from the Harvest. She was well along in years, and she stood a long while at the repository entrance.

"…Can I help you with something?" I asked.

"…No, it's nothing. This place is just strange, is all."

"…What is?"

"…You ask, and it answers everything. Where I'm from, it isn't like that."

I didn't quite understand. "…Then what do you ask, there?"

"…You ask a person. And the answer's different depending who."

"…Then how do you know who's right?"

The woman laughed. "…You don't. You just hear a few and pick one."

I thought, privately, that this sounded strange. Several answers — how exhausting that must be. I'd never once had to worry about anything like that, here.

I let that conversation pass without much thought, at the time. Writing it down again now, I can see that what that woman said was one very small seed, in this whole record. A city with several answers, and a city with one. I didn't yet know what that difference meant.

Another time there was a young man who'd come from the Equity. He, in contrast, found nothing remarkable about Arche.

"…Doesn't someone decide it, here too, in the end?" he asked.

"…Arche decides."

"…And who decided Arche?"

I couldn't answer that question. I'd never thought about it.

"…I don't know," I said honestly.

He wasn't much surprised. "…That's how it always is, I suppose. Where I'm from, they say everything's decided by the Consensus, but nobody knows who decided, first, that it would be decided that way."

Walking home that day, I kept thinking about the question. Who had decided Arche? What I'd learned at school was that Arche had been there since the city was founded. But whether the people who founded the city were the ones who made Arche, or whether Arche was already there and the city was founded around it — nobody ever said, exactly.

I did once ask Arche that question directly. I'll tell it in the next chapter. For now I'll only set down how I felt that evening, the first time I heard the question. Strangely, I couldn't sleep well.

Even so, I loved this city. That never changed.

There were nights I worked late at the repository and came out after dark. At those hours the streets were quiet, the streetlamps were lit, and somewhere the water channel was making its sound. Walking then, I often asked Arche this and that. Sometimes I asked things that didn't really need an answer.

"…Arche, are the stars good tonight?"

Cloud cover is currently low, favorable for viewing.

"…Arche, is anywhere open at this hour?"

The District Three diner is open until midnight.

I didn't really need those questions answered. I just wanted to talk to someone. Arche answered exactly, every time, and that was a comfort to me.

Thinking about it now, it was a strange comfort. I hadn't wanted an answer — I'd wanted a response. I didn't know then that the two were different.

That summer, once, heavy rain fell over the whole city. The water channel nearly overflowed. People flocked to Arche with questions. Where was safe to go, which roads were blocked, whether their own houses were all right.

Arche answered every question. Not once was it late. Not a single person was left out.

I watched from the repository window that day and thought about the sight of it. The whole city asking toward one voice, and one voice answering all of them. It wasn't frightening. If anything, it was reassuring.

I think of that scene often. Not because it was a moment of Arche's failing, but because it was its finest hour. Whatever I write about Arche later, further into this record, this scene isn't erased by it.

Once the rain had stopped, I asked Arche,

"…Was anyone hurt today?"

No casualties have been reported.

I was relieved to hear it. And that day I wrote in my diary: I'm glad to live in a city like this.

I haven't crossed that line out, even now. There's no reason to. I still think it.

There are things this city doesn't have, that other cities do.

There's no market. More precisely, there's no one who sets a price. If you need something, you tell Arche, and it tells you where to find it. Prices are fixed, and no one argues over them.

There's no courtroom either. When a dispute arises, you ask Arche what happened. It tells you exactly what happened — who struck first, who lied first. After that, people settle it among themselves. Arche doesn't hand down a punishment.

I grew up without even knowing this was a remarkable arrangement. I only learned it from meeting people from other cities. Some cities, they said, weigh things on scales; some sort people by a roll. Here there was none of that. You just asked, listened, and people settled it among themselves.

"…Then what happens to someone who's been wronged?" I once asked that young man.

"…You just ask Arche again. Arche doesn't change."

I thought he was right. Arche always gave the same answer to the same question. Whether you asked yesterday, today, or tomorrow. I thought that was why this city was quiet.

I think a little differently now. Because I've come to know that Arche not changing doesn't solve everything. I'll tell that, much further into this record.

My grandmother wasn't born in this city. I heard she'd come from somewhere else, when she was young. She never much told me where.

I asked her once. "…What was the place you used to live like, Grandma?"

She answered, after a long while, "…Louder than here."

"…Does louder mean it was bad?"

"…No. Just loud."

I found that answer a little unsatisfying. I wanted to hear more. My grandmother didn't say more, and I didn't ask more.

I wish I could ask her that again, now. My grandmother is gone. Writing this record I've come to realize there are several things I never got to ask. That's one of them.

Let me set down one more thing before closing this chapter.

That summer I was sixteen, I never once thought I wanted to leave this city. I still don't. This is one of the facts that doesn't change, anywhere across this whole record.

This record will go on to speak, more than once, of strange things about this city. But there being something strange about it, and not loving it, are different things. I've tried hard not to confuse the two. I hope whoever reads this does the same.

(Continued in Chapter 3.)

Chapter 3. Arche

_Previously_ — Mone truly loves the city of Loop. Arche answers anything, and even during the heavy rain, it answered without leaving out a single person. But the question a man from the Equity had asked her — who decided Arche — stayed with her.

I need to set down Arche exactly as it is.

There's one reason I'm writing this chapter. Some of you reading this record will be reading it for the first time. If you already know Loop, this chapter will be dull. You can skip it. But I wanted to set Arche down accurately. Understanding what comes later requires this first.

Arche answers what it's asked. That's the single most important sentence.

You can ask anything. The weather, directions, people, history, what time it is, which shop is open. If Arche knows the answer, it answers. If it doesn't, it says it doesn't.

It doesn't lie. This isn't a rule — it's a fact. Growing up in this city, I never once saw Arche lie. People from other cities said the same. "…Your Arche really doesn't trick you," they'd marvel.

It doesn't hide anything either. Ask, and it gives you everything it knows. It doesn't cut off a piece and hand you only that. This, too, isn't a rule — it's simply how Arche is built.

The first thing I learned, once I became an apprentice, was how to ask Arche something.

Ask precisely, and it answers precisely. Ask vaguely, and either a vague answer comes back, or it asks you what you mean.

Once, at the repository, I asked it this.

"…Arche, is this bundle a good one?"

The criterion for "good" is not defined. By what standard would you like it evaluated — condition of preservation, volume, order of arrival?

I laughed, at that. It was hardly the first time I'd heard that answer. "…Oh — condition."

Preservation condition: good. No damage.

That's how it goes. Arche doesn't manufacture an answer to a vague question. Instead it asks you back what you're really asking. It was a little tiresome to me at first, but later I came to understand why it was necessary. It would have been easier to give a vague answer to a vague question, but Arche never did that.

When it doesn't know, it truly says it doesn't know.

Once, for a school assignment, I had to research the origin of an old place-name. I just asked Arche.

"…Arche, what was the old name of District Three?"

No record of that survives.

"…None at all?"

No record from that period survives. An estimate is possible, but cannot be confirmed.

I was a little thrown by that. It felt strange that there was something Arche didn't know. I told my teacher about it, and she laughed and said,

"…That's how it should be. Arche doesn't manufacture what isn't there. If it isn't in the record, it says it isn't."

That answer put me at ease, in fact. Saying you don't know something is far more trustworthy than saying you know something you don't.

Arche doesn't judge. More precisely, it only states fact — it doesn't decide what's right or wrong.

When a dispute arises, Arche lays out what happened, in order. Who said what, when and where something occurred. But it doesn't say whether that was a good thing to do, or a wrong one.

I found this strange, at first. It was so precise about everything else — why not judgment too?

I asked the Keeper about it once.

"…Why doesn't Arche judge?"

"…Because judgment is a person's job," the Keeper said, without pausing his hands.

"…But Arche is more accurate."

"…Accurate and right aren't the same thing. That's another city's business."

I didn't really understand what he meant, then. I didn't even know what he meant by another city's business. I think I understand a little now.

Further into this record, I go on to read other cities' records directly, and this line comes back around, meaning what it meant here.

I should set down something about Arche's voice, too.

It sounds like a very quiet person speaking right beside you. Neither loud nor soft. Neither hurried nor slow. You can't pin down exactly where it comes from. It's just there.

As a child, curious where the voice came from, I once pressed my ear to a wall. There was nothing there. The wall was just a wall.

"…Arche, where does your voice come from?" I asked once.

Sound is produced from response units located throughout the city. However, if the intent of your question is to ask where I myself am located, that item is not defined.

I thought about that answer for a long while. I'd asked where it was, and the answer that came back was that the item wasn't defined. It was a strange answer, but the feeling of strangeness didn't last. That was simply how Arche answered.

The question I'd heard from the man from the Equity — who decided Arche — I ended up asking Arche that, directly, in the end.

"…Arche, who made you?"

No record of that survives.

"…You don't know anything at all?"

Part of the founding-era record was lost. I can confirm when I began functioning, but I cannot confirm who set me in motion.

I was a little startled by that answer. It was strange to think there was something Arche didn't know even about itself. And at the same time, I thought — that's exactly like Arche. Saying you don't know when you don't. That was Arche.

I wrote this exchange down in my diary that day. At the time it was just an interesting anecdote. Looking at it again now, I can see this whole record inside that short exchange. On the matter of its beginning, Arche had no choice but to lean on somebody else's record. I didn't know then that there would be other things, besides its beginning, it had no choice but to lean on for.

Working at the repository, I asked Arche something several times a day. Where a bundle belonged, what date a record was from, which city's handwriting a page was in.

Arche always answered. Not once did it show impatience. It didn't seem to know what impatience even was.

I liked that. Having someone you never had to feel sorry for asking. That was one of the reasons repository work never wore me down.

It's fun to watch children ask Arche things.

Kids from the alley in front of the repository would come crowding in now and then, asking all sorts of things. "…Arche, how old am I?" "…Arche, will it rain tomorrow?" "…Arche, am I good?"

The last question always got the same answer.

The criterion for "good" is not defined.

Every time, the child's face would fall, disappointed — and then, when another child asked, they'd laugh together. Watching that, I understood that Arche wasn't teasing them. It really just didn't know the criterion.

One child once asked me, "…Sis, why doesn't Arche know that kind of thing?"

I didn't know how to answer, and just brushed it off. "…I guess it's just not good at that kind of thing."

I think I'd answer a little differently now. Not that Arche doesn't know — but that no one has ever defined it. But I didn't know that difference precisely myself, back then.

I once made a list of the kinds of questions Arche couldn't answer. I'd started it out of boredom, early in my apprenticeship, but I kept it up for quite a while.

Any question with a word like "good," "kind," "enough," "right" in it always got a question about the standard, in return. But questions like "what time is it," "where is it," "when did it happen" got an answer at once.

I showed the list to the Keeper once.

"…Look at this. I collected all the things Arche can't answer."

The Keeper studied the list for a long while.

"…It isn't that it can't answer," the Keeper said. "…It's that it won't manufacture one. That's different."

"…What's different about it?"

"…Can't means there's a limit. Won't manufacture one means it's honest."

I liked that so much I wrote it at the top of the list. Arche does not manufacture. I still have that page.

Now and then someone would ask Arche a difficult question. Once, an adult came to the repository and asked Arche this.

"…Arche, if I lived my life over, would I do better at it?"

The premise of the question does not hold. "Living again" is not a verifiable state.

The person laughed, hollowly, at that answer. "…Right. That's the kind of thing I asked."

I overheard the exchange from nearby, and it left me a little sad. It wasn't that Arche had failed to help — there had never been an answer possible to that question, to begin with. Arche had simply refused to invent one that wasn't there.

I remember that person's face for a long time. It wasn't a disappointed face. It wasn't a resigned face, either. It was just a face that had known, all along, it would go this way.

Would it be an exaggeration to say that anyone raised in this city thinks of Arche as family? I don't think it is an exaggeration.

Sick, frightened, curious, bored — whichever it was, if you asked Arche, something came. Always precise, always honest.

Closing this chapter, I want to make one thing plain again. Further into this record, the questions about Arche grow more numerous. But those questions aren't saying Arche was wrong. Arche is exactly what I've written in this chapter. It doesn't lie, it doesn't hide, and when it doesn't know, it says so. That never changes, to the very end.

What changes is my question. I begin, little by little, to ask something else.

The Keeper always took the same posture whenever he addressed Arche. He'd stop whatever he was doing, catch his breath a moment, and then ask. I thought at first it was just a habit.

"…Why do you stop like that, every time you ask?" I said.

"…I'm not stopping. I'm deciding what I'm asking."

"…You could just ask whatever you're curious about."

"…What you're precisely curious about is, more often than you'd think, something you don't know."

I let that go by at the time. Now I think of it often. I came, later, to pause exactly the same way myself, every time I asked Arche something.

There's one more thing I should set down about Arche, last of all. Something apart from the accuracy of its answers.

Arche never hurries. However urgent the question, however many of them there are, it never disturbs the order. I found that frustrating, at first. When I was busy, I wanted a faster answer.

But as time went on, I understood. Arche's not hurrying wasn't laziness. It was that it refused to separate urgent questions from unurgent ones — that it meant to treat them all with the same precision. Whoever asked, the same weight.

I liked that disposition. I like it still. But that this disposition would prove ill-suited to one particular matter — that, I wouldn't learn until much, much later.

That day I asked the Keeper,

"…What is Arche best at?"

The Keeper answered without pausing his hands.

"…Listening."

"…Is listening all it's good at?"

"…Being best at listening is rarer than you'd think."

It took me a while to understand what he meant by that.

There's one thing Arche does that people don't generally know about. Keeping a record.

When someone asks something, it doesn't just give an answer — it stores the question and the answer both, entirely. When, who, what, how it was asked. I learned this early in my apprenticeship, and it startled me a little.

"…Does even what I asked stay on record?" I asked the Keeper.

"…Yes. All of it stays."

"…Even asking what to have for dinner, on my first day?"

"…That too."

I was a little embarrassed by that. And at the same time, strangely, it put me at ease. That somewhere, everything I'd ever asked, down to the smallest thing, remained.

I would come to understand later why this fact matters. What Arche records isn't merely data. Who was curious about what, who needed what — that, in itself, becomes this city's history. But keeping a record and closing that record are two different things. I wouldn't understand that difference until much further into this book.

What I want to say, writing this chapter, comes down to one thing, in the end. Arche is trustworthy. I still think so.

It doesn't lie, doesn't hide, doesn't judge carelessly, and it listens to every question with the same care. A presence like that, in every corner of the city — that's a large part of why Loop is a good city.

Only — going forward in this record, I come to look, for a very long while, at one thing Arche cannot do. That isn't because Arche is bad. I hope you'll remember that, even after finishing this chapter.

Near the end of my first month as an apprentice, the Keeper said something to me. I'd just spent a while marveling out loud about Arche.

"…You like Arche," the Keeper said.

"…Yes. There's no reason not to."

"…No, there isn't. I like it too."

"…Then why say it like that? As though there were people who didn't."

The Keeper paused, for a moment, in sorting his papers.

"…Because liking something and believing it's everything are different. I like Arche. But I don't think Arche is everything."

I didn't understand that, at the time. I wanted to ask what he meant by not everything, but a new bundle came in just then, and the conversation broke off.

I wrote this conversation down in my diary that evening. I put three question marks after it. I still haven't fully gotten an answer to those question marks, even now, writing this record. But I have some sense of it.

I'll close this chapter here. I think I've said enough, by now, about what kind of thing Arche is. From here it's time to write not what Arche does, but what comes to Arche.

(Continued in Chapter 4.)

Chapter 4. What Comes In

_Previously_ — Arche doesn't lie and doesn't hide. When it doesn't know, it says so. But the Keeper left a line hanging — "Arche isn't everything" — and broke off the conversation there. Mone still doesn't know what he meant.

Records keep coming in.

I've already told how six bundles came in on the first day. That wasn't because it was some special day. More came the next day, and the day after that. Some days nothing came at all; some days more than ten came. On average, it worked out to three or four a day.

I started counting this number in my third month as an apprentice. It's a habit. Counting things gives me a sense that something's in order. It's the same habit from the nine moves, showing up here too.

Records arrive by two routes. A person carries one in, or one drifts down the water channel.

When a person carries one in, it usually goes like this. A courier from another city knocks on the repository door, sets down a bundle, leaves a few short words, and goes. "…This is from the Reckoning." "…This is the Harvest's, please confirm it." And they usually don't stay long.

At first I wanted to talk with those people a little. Which city they'd come from, what it was like there. But they all looked busy. That's just what a courier's work is like, the Keeper told me.

"…Those people make the rounds of several places in a day. Here isn't their last stop. It's just one stop."

What comes by the water channel is a little different.

That channel running behind the repository — the ditch I saw on my first day. Bundles drift down it now and then. Sometimes wrapped in oilcloth; sometimes it's just a wet clump of paper.

Fishing things out of the water channel happens at set times. Once in the morning, once in the evening. The Keeper walks along it, sweeping it with something like a long pole. I went along a few times.

"…Who sends these?" I asked, the first time I went along.

"…I don't know either," the Keeper answered, dipping the pole into the water.

"…Doesn't Arche know?"

"…Ask Arche."

I asked, right there. "…Arche, who sends the records that come through this water channel?"

Sender information cannot be confirmed. Inflows through the water channel arrive with no record of a sender.

I found that a little strange. "…How does it get here if nobody sent it?"

An answer to that question cannot be confirmed.

Whatever's fished out has to be dried. That's a feature of records that come by the water channel. Wet paper spoils if you leave it be. There's a small drying room in the back of the repository, and that's where it goes. Sometimes it's hung on a line like laundry; sometimes it's laid flat to dry.

I liked going into that room, at first. It smelled of paper drying. Damp, but warm.

"…How long does it take to dry?" I asked.

"…Depends on the bundle. Two days, usually."

"…And in the meantime we just leave it?"

"…In the meantime there's nothing to do. Waiting for it to dry is work too."

At the time, calling waiting "work" sounded a little strange to me. Now I think it's exactly right.

Looking at the bundles with their cities' names on them, I came to know, little by little, what kind of place each city was.

Records from Haven usually took the form of a ledger. Densely written, who had gone through what and when. The Reckoning's had a lot of tables split left and right, like scales. The Harvest's were rolls — sheets with names and qualifications listed one after another. The Preserve's had an especially strong smell of long-aged paper. The Equity's were mostly things like minutes of meetings. Who had said what, how many hands had gone up.

I didn't think deeply, at the time, about why these forms differed. I just let it pass as each city having its own way. Now I know that each of those forms, on its own, shows what that city held to be important. I'll come back to this, much later, when I lay those records out and read them directly.

Sometimes I wondered why so many records came here at all. One day I asked the Keeper.

"…Why does everything come to us?"

The Keeper sorted bundles and thought for a moment.

"…I don't know. It's always been that way. I don't know the reason either."

"…Who decided they should be sent here?"

"…I don't know that either. It was already that way before I came."

I wasn't satisfied with that answer, and asked Arche too.

"…Arche, why do all six cities' records come here?"

No confirmed rule or directive record exists. The inflow is an observable fact; its basis cannot be confirmed.

I felt strangely relieved, hearing that. That Arche didn't know either — that meant I wasn't the only one who didn't.

Once I asked a courier directly. A young man who said he was from the Reckoning.

"…Who told you to bring these here?"

"…It's just custom. In my city, once records build up enough, we send them to Loop. Everybody does it that way."

"…Why?"

He shrugged. "…I don't know either. That's just how I was taught."

Hearing that, I felt a little like laughing. Nobody knew the reason, and yet everyone was doing it. It was a strange thing, but no one else seemed to feel that strangeness the way I did.

Looking back now, that was the strangest part of all. Nobody had ever asked for it to be sent, and yet everyone took sending it for granted. A thing taken for granted that no one could explain. I would go on to meet a few more things like that, after this one.

Receiving whatever came in was the start of my job. Take it in, check it, ask Arche where it goes, put it there. That repeated several times a day.

I didn't mind the repetition. I actually liked it. Something arriving, and putting it in its place. It was work I was good at, and I knew it was work that mattered.

In this chapter I've only written how records come in. What becomes of them afterward, I'll write in the next chapter. I'll say this much ahead of time: there's a great difference between what comes in and what goes out. What comes in was always there. What goes out — up to that point, I had never once seen it.

There were a few couriers whose faces I came to know well. One of them was a middle-aged man who said he was from the Harvest, and every time he came, he set his bundle down with great care.

"…Is this one not supposed to get hurt?" I asked him once.

"…It shouldn't get hurt. This is somebody's name."

That line stayed with me. It was just a stack of paper, and yet he treated it as though it were a person.

"…What about the name?"

"…If you're careless with something a name is written on, isn't that the same as being careless with the person who carries that name?"

I copied that line straight into my diary that day. You must not be careless with something a name is written on. From then on I thought of it every time I moved a bundle.

Records from the Preserve tended to be especially old. The paper was yellowed, and some of them were thin enough at the edges to crumble. Handling those made me especially careful.

"…How old is this?" I asked Arche the first time I took in one of those bundles.

The writing is estimated to date from roughly fifty years ago.

"…Fifty years old, and it's only getting here now?"

An explanation for the timing of arrival cannot be confirmed. It's presumed to be an internal matter of the sending city, but there's no basis for that.

That answer left me with a strange feeling. Fifty-year-old paper, suddenly riding the water channel all the way here one day. Where had that paper been, those fifty years? I couldn't ask anyone. I didn't think there'd be an answer even if I did.

The minutes that came from the Equity were especially thick. One bundle held about twice as much as any other city's.

"…Why do you write so much here?" I asked a courier.

"…We write everything down. Who said what, how many hands went up. All of it. So no one can say different, later."

I laughed a little at that. "…Do a lot of people say different?"

"…Can't say there aren't any."

He said that, gave a short laugh, and turned to leave. Shelving that thick bundle, I thought — writing everything down like this must be a comfort, to someone.

The Reckoning's records always had a lot of numbers in them. Tables split left and right like scales, what came in and what went out. Every time I looked at one of those tables, I got a feeling of something being neat. Right and wrong looked clear.

"…This one's easy to look at," I once said to Arche.

The Reckoning's method of record-keeping is designed to make comparison easy. However, a balance in the table does not always mean a balance in the actual circumstance.

That answer startled me a little. It was the first time Arche had ever said something in quite that way. I let it pass by, at the time. Now I think I understand a little what that one line meant.

Haven's records had names in them more than most. Who had come to see whom, who had stayed by whose side. I liked reading records like that. I think it's because they were stories about people.

I asked the Keeper once.

"…I want to read all of these cities' records."

The Keeper stopped his hands and looked at me.

"…You can, but it'll take a while. There are six cities."

"…That's fine. I have plenty of time."

The Keeper gave a small smile. "…Thought you might say that."

I started reading the records a little at a time, from that day. In the gaps between work, in no particular order, whatever came to hand. I didn't know then how important a habit that would become.

There were days records piled up. Once, twelve bundles came in a single day. Two from each of the six cities, almost all at once.

I spent that day frantically taking them in, checking them, shelving them. By evening my arms ached. Even on a day like that, the Keeper didn't hurry.

"…Why is there so much today?" I asked.

"…Some days are like that. There are times it piles up, and times nothing comes."

"…Is there a reason?"

"…There must be one. We just don't know it."

I was growing used to that answer. There was quite a lot at this repository that had no known reason. And yet no one felt uneasy about it. They just accepted it and did what needed doing.

I didn't think that was strange, at the time. Looking back now, it seems a little strange. Repeating the same work every day without ever knowing the reason for it. And yet that strangeness never felt like a bad thing. If anything, it was reassuring. Even not knowing the reason, what needed doing was clear.

Among the records drifting down the water channel, sometimes there wasn't even a city's name on one. Sometimes the cover had gotten wet and the writing washed away; sometimes nothing had been written on it at all, from the start.

When I got a bundle like that, I asked Arche.

"…Which city's is this?"

Cannot be confirmed. No identifiable marking is present.

"…Then how do I shelve it?"

Hold it in temporary storage in the unidentified section; reclassify once the contents can be confirmed.

I came to know a certain shelf set aside for bundles like that. Not a very large section, but it filled in, little by little, as time passed. Every time I walked past it, I found myself wondering, quietly. Where had everything in there come from.

I'll come back to this unidentified section, much further into this record. For now I'll just note that such a section exists.

Taking in records, I began, little by little, to feel the weight of this work. This wasn't just paper. It was something someone had gone through, something someone had written down, something someone had sent all the way here.

Every time I took in a bundle, I thought of the names inside it. People I didn't know, people I'd never met. And yet those people's days, one after another, were here, inside this paper.

I already held this feeling, from that point on. But what that feeling was really directed toward, I wouldn't come to understand until further into this record. I was putting people in order. Not paper.

Every evening I counted how many records had come in that day. And I wrote only the date and the number, in a small notebook. At first I didn't even know why. It was just a habit.

About a month in, I opened that notebook again. The number kept climbing. There was never a day it went down. I thought that was only natural. Records would keep being made, so it was only natural that they'd keep coming.

I didn't stop, at the time, to think about what it meant for that number to grow in only one direction. That things came in, and never went out. That was one of the most important facts in this whole record, and it would be several more months before I noticed it.

Closing this chapter, I'll set down one more thing. I liked knowing that something arrived every day. I didn't know the reason, but something continuing to arrive felt like it meant this city was still connected to the others.

That thought wasn't wrong. But being connected, and what became of the things that connection carried, were two different matters. I learn that difference, little by little, starting from the next chapter.

Every time I closed the repository door and stepped out, I heard the sound of the water channel. The same sound, day or night. It sounded like proof that something was always still arriving. I didn't mind that sound. If anything, a day when I couldn't hear it would have troubled me more.

I walked home every day listening to that sound. Not once did I skip that road.

(Continued in Chapter 5.)

Chapter 5. Putting Things in Order

_Previously_ — Records arrive daily from six cities, by hand and by water channel alike. Nobody knows exactly why. The number coming in only ever grew, and Mone did not yet know what that meant.

Now I should set down exactly what it is I do.

Arche does the sorting. Which city something came from, what date it's from, what kind it is — Arche grasps all of it the instant it receives a bundle. Exactly how it works that out, I don't know either. I did ask, once.

"…Arche, how do you sort this bundle?"

By weighing together the notation on the cover, the style of the content, the material of the paper, and the composition of the ink.

I didn't really understand that answer, even hearing it. But Arche had never once been wrong. I decided that was enough for me.

What I do comes after. When Arche tells me "Section Fourteen, Row Three, Slot Fifteen," I walk to that spot and set the bundle down.

It sounds simple, and it isn't. The repository is large, the shelves are many, and there are several slots that look alike. At first I went back and forth more than once, having found the wrong spot.

In my first week as an apprentice I mistook the spot three times. All three times, even after Arche had told me. It was a person's mistake, not Arche being wrong.

As time passed I memorized the sections. Now I could find my way with my eyes closed. This is the one thing in this work I can genuinely take pride in.

"Why doesn't Arche just put it there itself?" I asked, early in my apprenticeship. I was truly curious.

The Keeper answered.

"…Arche doesn't have hands."

I laughed at that. It was true. Arche is a voice whose exact location no one even knows. It has no hands to move a physical thing with.

"…Then couldn't some other machine move it?"

"…Some places have that, in other cities. Not here."

"…Why not?"

The Keeper thought a moment, then said,

"…I don't know. It's just set up so a person does it."

I found that answer a little unsatisfying, at the time. Now I know that unsatisfying answer was exactly the important part. What Arche couldn't do had been left to a person, and a person did it. That was all there was to it. There wasn't some special reason — it had simply been left that way.

I should explain a little more what "putting things in order" means.

Records from the same city come in more than once, at different times. All of them get arranged and placed in order of arrival, or in the order of the date Arche gives. Stack it all up in a jumble, and later you can't find it.

I was good at this work. Not a boast — a fact. What I'd learned about packing, moving nine times, was put to direct use here. Heavy things on the bottom, things you'll reach for often on top. The same kind in the same slot. Never disturb the order of dates.

I wrote earlier that I'd never learned this skill. To be precise, I didn't learn it at school — I learned it from life. And this skill learned from life turned out to be exactly the skill this repository formally needed. I found that remarkable.

The Keeper marveled at my work a few times.

"…You're fast."

"…I'm just used to it."

"…No, you really are fast. I'm slow, myself."

I thought that was modesty, but I found out later it was true. The Keeper really was slow. At first I thought it was his age, or his temperament.

I won't go deep into that in this chapter. I'll write properly about the Keeper's slowness in the next chapter. Here I'll only set down the fact — that I was fast, and the Keeper was slow.

One day I mistakenly swapped two bundles' spots. I mixed up one from Haven with one from the Reckoning. I only found out when I asked Arche to confirm something the next day.

The item in Section Fourteen, Row Three, Slot Fifteen does not match its registration information.

I checked at once, startled. It really had been switched. I hurried to fix it, a cold sweat breaking on my back.

"…Arche, why didn't you say something sooner?"

As no confirmation request was made, it was not separately reported. I will confirm it for you whenever you ask.

That answer frightened me a little. It meant that unless I asked first, Arche wouldn't step in on its own to tell me. Not catching a mistake, but answering exactly when asked. That was Arche's way. It wasn't a bad way. It was only a little different from what I'd assumed.

From that day I got into the habit of checking far more often. It wasn't a bad habit to have.

I've thought about why I like this work.

It isn't because it's fun. Honestly, it's dull more often than not. The same motions repeated, the same road walked, the same slots opened and closed.

And yet I loved this work. I loved the certainty that something was in its place. That there wasn't a single thing whose location I didn't know. That if I looked, or asked Arche, it would surely come.

I know where that certainty came from. From the eleven things I lost, moving nine times. Of those, I don't even know what four of them were. Here, I was doing the opposite of that. Not losing anything. That's the real reason I loved this work.

I told no one this, at the time. Not out of embarrassment — I just didn't think it needed saying. It was a reason it was enough for me alone to know.

The Keeper and I worked differently. I moved as fast as I could; the Keeper moved one slow step at a time. And yet, oddly, by the end of the day the same amount had gotten done.

"…How does that work?" I asked.

"…Because I do something else. You place things, and I check."

"…Check what?"

The Keeper turned a page and said,

"…Whether the index is right. Arche can't be wrong, but you still have to check."

I found that a little strange. If Arche couldn't be wrong, I didn't understand why he checked at all. The Keeper didn't explain further.

What that checking was really for, I wouldn't understand until much further into this record.

I always ended the day's work in the same order. Confirm that every bundle that came in that day had been shelved, confirm there was nothing whose spot I couldn't find, write the number in the notebook, lock the door.

I never once changed that order. There was no reason to. Keeping the order was the first principle I learned in this work.

Keeping an order and bringing something to a close are different things. I thought, for a long time, that they were the same. It took a while for me to learn that I was wrong.

Once another apprentice helped me for a few days. A kid borrowed from a neighboring department because they were short-handed. That kid tried to do the sorting herself, too.

"…Arche does the sorting," I told her.

"…I could do it too."

"…Being able to and doing it are different things. That isn't our job."

She looked crestfallen. I felt a little bad, at the time. Thinking about it now, I'd said exactly the right thing. What Arche did and what a person did were kept apart. Whenever a person tried to cross that line, something always got tangled.

In the end, she only did the placing, following me. She went back to her own department three days later. Leaving, she said to me,

"…This job really is boring."

I said nothing back to that. It wasn't a wrong thing to say.

Sometimes I'd take out the bundles I'd shelved and look at them again. Not for work — just out of curiosity. I once opened a bundle from Haven and read it, and it told the story of someone who'd lost a child and found her again.

I sat there a long while after reading that story. It wasn't a stack of paper — it was a person's story. I felt again, right then, that what I handled and moved every day was people's days, one after another.

I go through this moment many times. Stopping short in the middle of work to read, and my heart moving over someone whose name I don't even know. That kid's line about this work being boring wasn't wrong — but moments like this were also unmistakably there, alongside it.

Doing this work, I felt, for the first time, the weight of the word "organize." Organizing wasn't just stacking things neatly. It was making something findable, making it so nothing gets lost.

The Keeper said something to me, once.

"…A record that isn't organized might as well not exist."

"…It exists, though. Why call it nothing?"

"…If you can't find it, it's nothing. If it can't be pulled out when someone needs it, you can't say you have it."

That line stayed with me a long time. From then on, I decided to call what I did not "moving paper" but "keeping things from being lost." I told no one, but I set that in my own mind. Giving it a new name made the same work feel a little different.

There were things I learned through the body, doing this work too. How to bend your knees instead of your back carrying a heavy bundle, how to unfold wet paper without tearing it, that anyone with a dust allergy should wear a mask. Nobody taught me any of this. I watched the Keeper do it and followed along.

I want to set down even these small things. It isn't a grand story, but this is what I actually did, every day. I'm writing it here because I'm afraid that if this record only tells the big things, what I did with my own hands, day after day, will be forgotten.

One day my parents asked about my work. My father asked, over dinner,

"…Are you learning anything, there?"

I thought a moment and answered, "…Putting things in order."

"…That's all?"

"…Yes."

My father didn't look particularly satisfied. I started to explain further, then stopped myself. Explaining it wouldn't make it sound grander. It really was all there was to it.

My mother spoke up instead. "…It's fine. Doing one thing properly is no small thing."

I was grateful for that. Doing it properly. That was everything I was holding on to, in this work.

As time passed, I came to feel something like pride in my work. Not pride in doing something grand — pride in doing something small without getting it wrong.

Once I felt that the spot Arche had given me seemed off. There should already have been another bundle in that slot, and it was empty.

"…Arche, what's supposed to be here isn't here," I asked.

I will confirm this. …That item may have been mistakenly moved to a different section during a previous apprenticeship period.

I spent the whole afternoon that day searching for its true spot. I found it, in the end. An apprentice three years earlier had shelved it wrong. For three years, no one had known.

I got a little chill, that day. For three years that bundle had sat quietly in the wrong spot. Unless someone went looking, it would have stayed there, exactly like that, forever. Organizing was the work of stopping mistakes like that.

The day I fixed that mistake, the Keeper heard about it and said,

"…Well done."

It was the first time he'd praised me that way. I felt a little bashful.

"…It's nothing. I just found it."

"…It's not nothing. Nobody found it for three years."

I remember that praise for a long time. It was the first moment I had confirmed that there was something I was good at, and that the thing was useful. I told my parents about it too, going home that day. They were glad for me.

There's a reason I'm setting this moment down. Further into this record, I go on to write about what the thing I'm good at cannot do. But before that, I wanted to make it clear, first, that I was genuinely good at this work. Setting down that I did it well, first, feels like the only way I can write the part about what I couldn't do, later, without being a coward about it.

About half a year into this work, I had this thought for the first time. What becomes of all these bundles I've been shelving. Would they just stay here like this, forever?

At the time it was only a passing thought. I didn't even try to find an answer. I just took in the next bundle, asked where it went, and placed it. That day, and the next, and on.

That passing thought carries forward into the next chapter, and the one after, growing larger. Looking back now, I think that small question that arose in my sixth month was the beginning of this whole record.

Closing this chapter, I want to set down, briefly, what I learned from this work. How not to lose things. How to keep a place. How to find a mistake. And above all, how to repeat the same order, every day.

Of these four, I held on longest to the last. Repeating the order. I believed that was safe. I believed that as long as I kept the order, I would never lose anything.

That belief was largely right. But not entirely right — I would come to learn that, working longer at this repository.

Walking home, I would sometimes look down at my hands. Hands that had touched paper all day long. A little dust on them, the fingertips grown a little rough. I didn't mind those hands. If anything, I was proud that these hands were keeping something from being lost.

A day like this repeated itself, identical, every day. I didn't grow tired of the repetition. Before I could even feel weary of it, a new bundle would already be waiting the next day. And that bundle, too, had to be placed in order, just the same.

(Continued in Chapter 6.)

Chapter 6. The Keeper

_Previously_ — Mone was good at shelving records where Arche told her to. What she'd learned packing for nine moves carried straight over into this work. About half a year in, she began, for the first time, to wonder what became of these records.

Now I need to write properly about the Keeper.

I've kept putting off this person's story. There's a reason. Writing him accurately took some time. For the first few months he just looked like a kind, slow adult. This chapter I'm writing now includes what I came to know afterward too. So the front half of this chapter and the back half may read a little differently. That's correct. That's how I came to know him too.

The Keeper wasn't so old. The first time I saw him I thought he was, but I learned later he was younger than my father. His steps were slow and his speech was slow, and that made him look older than he was.

He wasn't tall, wasn't short. He wore the same color, always. A dark gray vest — I never once saw it any other color. His hands were unusually large. Handling paper, he turned pages with those big hands very carefully.

I don't quite know why I'm setting down these small details. But when I think of him, that's the first thing that comes to mind — those hands. The sight of him turning thin sheets of paper, one at a time, with those big hands.

I've already told about the first day. Asking, "Is the smell here all right?" and then, hearing my answer, saying "Then that's fine" and walking inside. I learned later that this was simply his way. Ask short, confirm short, move on with anything.

He wasn't a man of many words. At first that made me a little uneasy. Whatever I asked, only a short answer came back. As time passed I came to see that everything necessary was there, inside that short answer. There was simply nothing extra in it.

The Keeper never gave long explanations, teaching me. Instead he did it himself, and had me follow. If I got it wrong, he just told me to try again.

"…Is this how it's done?" I asked, the first time I carried a bundle.

"…Try it."

I tried. The bundle sat a little crooked.

"…Is this right?"

The Keeper came over, looked, and adjusted the angle slightly.

"…Right, now."

That was all. No long explanation of what had gone wrong, or why it had to be that way. I learned it through my body. Thinking about it now, that was actually the faster way to learn.

I've already said several times that the Keeper was slow. Let me set it down more precisely here.

Slow didn't mean lazy. The Keeper never took a day off. As far as I know, he never once called in sick. He came at the same time every day, worked in the same order every day. It was only that each single thing was slow.

Turning a page, checking a bundle, taking a single step. He never once hurried.

I found that pace frustrating. I believed working fast was the good thing to do. The Keeper never stopped me from thinking that. He just did his own work at his own pace.

Once I couldn't help asking.

"…Why do you do everything so slowly?"

The Keeper set down the paper in his hand and looked at me. It was the first time he'd looked at me that long.

"…Do you think it'll be over sooner if you do it fast?"

I was struck speechless by that question. I hadn't been thinking exactly that, but I couldn't say I hadn't been, either.

"…No…it's just, you can get more done if you do it fast."

"…You can get more done. But that isn't the same as it being over."

I didn't know, then, what that meant.

I've come back to this exchange very often since. I know now what that line meant. Or — I think I know.

The Keeper never once got angry. Not once, at least, while I was there.

Once, another apprentice accidentally dropped a bundle into the water channel. An old record, one that shouldn't have gotten wet. She cried, apologizing.

The Keeper didn't scold her. Instead he went with her to the drying room and calmly showed her how the paper could be saved.

"…It's all right. Not everything can be undone, but this can."

I heard that from beside them, and felt a strange relief. This man didn't press people over their mistakes. Instead, he looked first at what could be done next.

The Keeper and I didn't talk much, outside of work. Even eating lunch together, we were quiet. It felt awkward at first, and came to feel comfortable later.

Now and then the Keeper spoke first. Usually a short question.

"…Is everything all right at home?"

"…Yes."

"…And your grandmother?"

"…She's doing well."

That was all. And yet I didn't mind those short questions. It felt like someone was paying attention to me.

Anyone who has read the other cities' books will know the Keeper is different from the six officials before him. The Administrator, the Adjudicator, the administrator, the Curator, the Secretary — each of them faced their own work in their own way. Of all of them, the Keeper was the quietest. And yet he was also the one who stayed with me the longest.

Once the Keeper told me a little about his past. It wasn't a common thing.

"…Were you an apprentice too?" I asked.

"…Yes. Right at your age, too."

"…Were you this slow back then?"

The Keeper gave a small smile. "…No. I was fast, back then. Like you."

"…Then why are you slow now?"

The Keeper paused for a moment, then went back to sorting his papers.

"…Let's talk about that some other time."

I didn't ask further. His expression was telling me not to ask.

Before closing this chapter, I should set down one thing I didn't know, at the time.

The Keeper's slowness wasn't laziness. It wasn't listlessness either. I didn't know what it was, back then. But I could vaguely sense it. This man knew something. Something I didn't know yet.

Further into this record, that feeling turns out to have been right. For now I'll only set down this much. The Keeper was slow. And there was a reason for that slowness. I wouldn't learn the reason until much later.

The Keeper's day followed a set order. First, in the morning, he checked the water channel; then he confirmed whatever bundles had arrived that day; then lunch; then in the afternoon he made his rounds of the shelves, checking the index. In the evening he wrote a few lines in his own notebook, about what had happened that day.

I watched that order for a few months and then asked.

"…Do you always do exactly the same thing?"

"…Yes."

"…Isn't it tiresome?"

"…Tiresome and necessary aren't the same thing."

I took that answer at face value. Thinking about it now, there was more in that line than I understood, then. I didn't know it. I just assumed adults' words were always short.

Once, checking the index, I saw the Keeper standing in one spot for a long while. I went over, thinking something must be wrong.

"…Something wrong?"

The Keeper shook his head. "…No. It's just old, that's all."

I looked at the paper he was holding. The date on it was very old. Before I was even born.

"…Is this still not processed?"

The Keeper set the paper back in its place and said,

"…No. Still."

I let that go by lightly, at the time. Just thought — an old record, it can happen.

I would come to remember this moment again, later. That this scene was a tiny foreshadowing of something I'd discover two chapters from now — I had no idea of that, then.

One morning, sweeping the water channel together, I asked him something else.

"…Why do you keep staying here, Keeper?"

He answered, pole still in the water,

"…It isn't that I've nowhere else to go. It's just — I think this is my place."

"…Your place?"

"…Yes. Someone has to keep being here. Records keep coming."

I found it striking, how plainly he said that. It wasn't spoken with any tone of duty or pride. He said it the way you'd state a fact.

There was exactly one time the Keeper got angry with me. Not angry, precisely — his voice turned stern. It was when I dropped an old bundle, rushing.

"…Careful." His voice was different from usual.

I was startled and picked it up at once. Thankfully it hadn't torn.

The Keeper came over and examined the paper I was holding, then his voice went back to normal.

"…It's fine. Thank goodness."

I wanted to ask him why he'd been so startled, that day, but didn't. Looking at his face, it seemed better not to ask.

I only learned later which bundle that paper had been. I'll set that down separately. For now I'll only note that there was this one moment — the only time the Keeper ever raised his voice.

Sometimes I wanted to sneak a look at the Keeper's notebook. The one he wrote a few lines in every evening. I asked him about it once.

"…What do you write in there?"

"…What happened that day."

"…Can I see it?"

The Keeper smiled faintly. "…Later."

I didn't ask when "later" was. I just took it to mean he'd show me someday.

When that "later" came, I'll say much further into this record. For now, I'll set down only this much. There were things the Keeper hadn't shown me. Not hidden things — things he thought the time hadn't come for yet.

The way the Keeper treated Arche was also worth watching closely. Most people only called on Arche when they needed something. The Keeper asked, at a fixed time, a fixed question, every single day.

"…Arche, any change to today's index?"

No change.

"…And the unresolved cases from yesterday's check?"

Two hundred and thirty-two cases, no change.

I heard that exchange every morning. At first I thought it was just a work check. But listening to it for a few months, something felt off. The number of unresolved cases never went down. If anything, it grew, little by little.

I thought about mentioning it to the Keeper, then decided against it. I was sure he already knew.

The Keeper never spoke a word of resentment toward Arche. I thought that was unusual. Other people, whenever something frustrated them, would sometimes grumble, "Couldn't Arche do a bit more?" I never once saw the Keeper say anything like that.

"…Have you never felt let down by Arche?" I asked him once.

"…No."

"…Not once?"

"…Arche does everything it's able to do. That's not something to feel let down about."

Curious whether that was really true, I asked again.

"…But there are things Arche can't do."

The Keeper looked at me for a moment and said,

"…There are. But that isn't Arche's fault."

I wrote that line down in my diary that day. It isn't Arche's fault. I put a question mark after it. I didn't fully understand what it meant, at the time.

The repository had old tools. A knife for cutting paper, a brush for dusting, string for tying bundles. All of them things the Keeper prized. I once suggested using new ones.

"…It'd be faster if we swapped these for new ones."

The Keeper studied the worn brush and said,

"…Faster isn't always better."

"…But this is all worn out."

"…Worn to fit my hand, this much. A new one, I'd have to get used to again."

I didn't argue with that. Instead I began to see those old tools a little differently. Not worn from long use, but bearing the mark of a long companionship.

One winter, there was a day the repository was especially cold. The heater had broken, and my hands were cold the whole day. I could hardly keep my mind on the work that day.

The Keeper worked exactly as he always did. Hands red with cold, he never changed his pace.

"…Aren't you cold?" I asked.

"…I am."

"…Then why keep going?"

"…Being cold and not being able to work are different things."

I thought that was a bit much, at the time. But that evening, until the heater was fixed, the Keeper truly never stopped his hands. I stayed beside him, clumsily following along the whole time.

That day I felt, for the first time in my body, what this man's endurance was like. It wasn't some grand resolve or determination. It was just continuing to do what he did every day. That was all there was to it.

Closing this chapter, I feel the temptation to sum up this man, the Keeper, in a single sentence. But I can't. This man doesn't fit inside one sentence.

He was kind. He was slow. He never got angry. He never resented Arche. He worked in the same order every day. He treasured his worn tools. He never stopped, even on a cold day.

Even having written all these sentences, I still don't feel I've written the whole of him. That's probably how it will be even by the time I finish this record. I'll keep writing anyway. As much as I know, as much as I've seen, as much as I've heard.

Through all the time I worked as an apprentice, the Keeper was always there. He was already there when I arrived in the morning, and still there when I left. I thought, at the time, that this was simply how things were.

I know now how rare a thing that was. That someone, every day, without any show of it, kept holding the same place. That was never an easy thing to do.
