Even now, writing this record, if I close my eyes I see him from behind. Turning pages one at a time under the low light. A back that never hurried, and yet never stopped, either.

(Continued in Chapter 7.)

Chapter 7. In Progress

_Previously_ — The Keeper is slow but not lazy. He works in the same order every day and never resents Arche. Mone noticed that the number of unresolved cases had never once gone down, but let it pass at the time, without much thought.

It was about eight months into this work.

I had a slow day. Few bundles had come in, and everything that needed shelving was already shelved. So, for the first time, I thought of looking over the whole index. Just out of curiosity.

"…Arche, how many records have come in, total, up to now?" I asked.

A total of four thousand two hundred and seventeen cases.

I was startled by that number. Given how long I'd actually worked here, it couldn't be that many. Most of it had been piling up before I ever came.

"…How many of those are finished?" I asked, without much thought. Just curious.

Would you clarify the criterion for "finished"?

I was used to Arche asking back. I asked again.

"…Cases that have completed processing. Closed."

No cases have been closed.

I let that answer go by at first. I thought I'd misheard.

"…None at all?"

Correct. All cases remain in progress.

I stood there a moment, blank. Not one, out of four thousand two hundred and seventeen, finished. It didn't seem like it could be true.

"…So all of them are still ongoing?"

Every item is being maintained in the in-progress state.

"…When did the oldest one come in?"

The oldest in-progress item dates from eleven years ago.

Eleven years. I repeated the number to myself. It meant something that had come in not long after I was born was still, even now, in progress.

I wanted to find that eleven-year-old bundle. I asked Arche where it was. Section Three, Row Two, Slot Eighteen. I went there.

The bundle looked ordinary. Only a little faded with age; nothing else special about it. The cover read the Equity. I opened the contents a moment. It was a case about someone being unfairly treated by someone else. There were also records of several people's testimony.

"…Arche, why is this still in progress?" I asked.

The closing condition has not been met.

I just nodded, hearing that. At the time I let it pass without much thought. Just, "…Oh, I see."

I would come to think back on this moment many, many times, later. Looking back on it now, I had already heard, in this very chapter, half the answer to this book's most important question. I just didn't know it mattered.

Over the days that followed, I kept checking the index whenever I had a moment. Not on purpose — just, working, I kept getting curious and asking.

"…Are there any closed cases among the Haven records?"

None.

"…The Reckoning?"

None.

"…The Harvest, the Preserve, the Equity?"

None, for any of them.

"…Our own city, Loop?"

Loop's own records likewise have no closed items.

Hearing each of those answers, I felt something turn cold. Six cities, more than four thousand two hundred records, all in progress.

I hesitated over whether I should tell the Keeper about this. Maybe everyone already knew. Maybe I was the only one who hadn't.

"…Keeper, do you know this?" I asked. "Everything here is in progress. Not one thing is finished."

The Keeper answered, sorting the paper in his hand,

"…I know."

"…You knew already?"

"…Yes."

I was a little thrown by that. "…Then why doesn't it bother you at all?"

The Keeper paused his hands for a moment.

"…It isn't that it doesn't bother me at all. It just isn't a surprise. It's always been that way."

"…What do you mean, it's always been that way?" I asked again.

"…It was that way before I came. Long before I came."

"…How long before?"

The Keeper thought a moment, then answered,

"…Ask Arche. It'll know better than I do."

I asked Arche right there. I already suspected the answer by then, but I wanted to hear it directly.

"…Arche, since when has there been not one closed record?"

In the entire confirmable record, no closed item has ever existed.

I froze again, hearing that answer. "…Not even once?"

Not even once.

I thought of nothing else, the whole way home that day. More than four thousand two hundred records, and not one of them ever finished. The eleven-year-old one, the one that came in yesterday — all of them equally "in progress."

I wrote that number in my notebook that day. 4,217 cases. 0 closed. Underneath it I drew a single question mark. I didn't know, at the time, how large that question mark would grow.

I heard the sound of the water channel. I stopped walking and listened to it a long while. It was the same water that always flowed, but that day it sounded a little different.

A few days later, I asked Arche something more in depth.

"…What exactly is happening, when something's 'in progress'?"

Sorting, cross-referencing, and index work are ongoing continuously. Relationships with associated items are also updated continuously.

"…So something's constantly being done?"

Yes. Processing continues. It simply does not arrive at closing.

I turned that answer over, more than once. Processing continues, but it never ends. I thought that was a strange sentence. I still think so.

I didn't go around telling this to anyone, much. It struck me as strange, but I didn't even know what to call it. Writing it in my notebook was all I could do.

In this chapter I draw no conclusion. I couldn't draw one at the time, either. I'm only setting down that one question had arisen. Why isn't a single thing finished. This question comes back, in a clearer shape, in the next chapter.

From then on, every time I looked at the index, I studied those three words, "in progress," closely. Letters that had never caught my eye before. Every bundle, every item, had that small stamp printed in the corner of its cover. I'd assumed it was just a procedural mark, but now I knew it was everything there was. There was no other stamp besides that one.

"…Is there any stamp besides this one?" I once asked the Keeper.

"…No."

"…Was one never made?"

"…There was never any occasion to make one."

Hearing that, I began to wonder whether a stamp for "closing" existed in this city at all, to begin with. I asked Arche.

"…Arche, is there such a thing as a closing stamp?"

A form for marking a closing does exist, but no record of it ever being used exists.

That answer gave me a chill. The stamp existed, and it had never once been used. Made, and never once put to use.

I wanted to see that form for myself.

"…Can I see it?" I asked.

I will show it to you.

Arche described the layout of a sheet aloud, with no screen. At the top, a case number; below that, a summary; below that, a box for the reason for closing; and at the very bottom, a signature line. That word — signature line — caught in my mind.

"…Who signs it?"

Whoever holds the authority to close signs it.

"…Who is that?"

That item is not defined.

I froze again, hearing that answer. There was no one set to sign it. So there was a signature line, and no one to fill it.

I didn't know, at the time, how important this exchange was. In the next chapter I go on to ask this question again, in more exact words. What Arche's answer was, exactly, at that point — I won't set that down here, ahead of time. I decided to write everything in order.

That evening I asked the Keeper again.

"…Keeper, Arche says there's no one with the authority to close. That's what it told me."

The Keeper paused what he was doing. There was a slightly longer silence than usual.

"…That's right."

"…So nothing's ever going to finish, from now on either?"

"…I don't know that either."

I found that answer frustrating. "…If you don't know, why do you keep working like this every day?"

The Keeper looked at me and said, quietly,

"…I don't work in order to watch things finish."

I didn't understand that, at the time. I still can't say I fully understand it, now. But I know it was said in earnest.

I slept badly that night at home. More than four thousand two hundred records, all in progress; no one holding the authority to close. I kept turning those three facts over in my head. Something didn't line up, but I didn't know exactly what.

Looking back now, I was already standing, that night, before the question at the heart of this whole book. I just hadn't yet made it into exact words. Making it into words was the next chapter's job.

The next morning I arrived at the repository earlier than usual. Not because there was work waiting. I just wanted to keep holding on to that question.

I took out that eleven-year-old bundle again. This time I tried to read it properly. It was an old case record from the Equity. There were several testimonies, and on the last page a short opinion written by someone called an investigator. This case requires further confirmation. That was the final sentence.

I confirmed there was nothing beneath that sentence. No conclusion, no ruling, nothing. It had stopped at that sentence for eleven years.

"…Arche, was this case ever confirmed, after that?" I asked.

Further confirmation procedures were carried out, but no final judgment was reached.

"…Why not?"

No party holding the authority to render judgment has been designated.

I took that answer and carefully set the paper back in its place.

I took out a few more old bundles. Each a different story. One was a quarrel between neighbors; one was about someone who'd borrowed something and never returned it; one was about someone misunderstanding someone else. They differed wildly in scale. Some looked very trivial.

But they had one thing in common. All of them stopped short at the end. Everything about who had done what was written down — but what had become of it wasn't there.

Every time I held one of these bundles, I felt a strange weight. Whether a trivial quarrel or a great matter, all of them stood equally stopped. Standing in the exact same spot, regardless of scale.

At lunch that day I asked Arche a somewhat different question.

"…Arche, do the people of this city know this? That nothing ever finishes?"

Confirmation is needed of exactly what the question intends. If it's asking whether the city's members are aware of it, individual confirmation is difficult.

"…So people don't pay it any mind?"

Based on observation, most inquiries concern individual matters, and inquiries about the overall state of closings are rare.

That answer left me with a strange feeling. It meant everyone asked about their own case, and no one asked about the whole. I was the only one asking about the whole.

"…Then why did you tell me everything, when I asked?"

I answer every question I'm asked. Regardless of the scale of the question.

I liked that answer. And at the same time it left me a little cold.

That evening I opened my notebook again. Under the page where I'd written 4,217 cases, 0 closed, I added this: There's a signature line, and no one to sign it. And one more line. I don't work in order to watch things finish — the Keeper.

I looked at those three lines for a long while. I wasn't trying to find an answer. I wrote them down only because I didn't want to forget them.

I know now that those three lines were the seed of this entire record. At the time they were just a memo from a strange day.

For the days that followed, I worked exactly as I always had. Taking in bundles, asking where they went, placing them. Nothing changed on the surface.

But now, every time I placed a bundle, I found myself thinking, privately. Will this one ever end, some day. I didn't know the answer. Still, I didn't stop asking myself.

The Keeper seemed to have noticed something had changed in me. One day he asked me, briefly,

"…What have you been thinking about, lately?"

I hesitated a moment, then answered honestly. "…Why nothing ever finishes."

The Keeper nodded. "…That's a good question."

"…Do you know the answer?"

"…Half of it."

I was startled by that answer and tried to ask more, but the Keeper went on first.

"…You'll come to know the other half soon enough, yourself."

I slept badly, hearing that. That word half kept circling in my head. I made up my mind, the next day, to go and ask Arche more directly. Why does nothing ever finish. What has to exist for something to finish.

I'll set that conversation down, word for word, in the next chapter. I still remember that answer exactly, even now. I could write it without missing a single letter. It stayed with me that clearly.

Closing this chapter, I'll set down one thing again. 4,217 cases. 0 closed. That number won't be much different, even now, writing this chapter. I'm not writing this to say it's this city's fault. I only wanted to leave the feeling of that day I first saw the number, exactly as it was. That day, I didn't come to hate this city. I only, for the first time, grew curious.

(Continued in Chapter 8.)

Chapter 8. The Closing Condition

_Previously_ — Mone learned that the whole index is "in progress," and that not a single case has ever closed. Even the eleven-year-old record stood untouched. The Keeper said "I know half the answer," and Mone made up her mind to ask, directly, for the other half.

The next day I arrived at the repository earlier than usual.

The Keeper hadn't come in yet. I stood alone among the shelves and turned over, several times in my head, the question I meant to ask Arche. I wanted to ask it precisely. I already knew well by then that a vague question gets a vague answer.

I said, in the last chapter, that I could copy out this day's conversation without missing a single letter. That's true. This conversation survives exactly, in my notebook, and I have that notebook open in front of me as I write this chapter. The handwriting looks pressed harder than usual. I must have written it, without noticing, with more force than I meant to, that day.

"…Arche."

Yes.

"…Why doesn't anything ever finish?"

I asked it as simply as I could. I felt I should ask more precisely, but in that moment no other words would come.

Could you make the question a little more specific? If "finish" refers to the closing of some item, there is a reason common to all items.

"…Yes, that. That reason."

The closing condition has not been met.

I stood still a moment, hearing that answer. It was the answer I'd already been expecting since yesterday. And yet hearing it directly was different, all the same. There's a difference between expecting a thing and hearing it said.

"…What needs to be met?"

I'd been preparing to ask this question since the night before. I tried hard to ask it in the most exact words I could.

That item is not defined.

Hearing that answer, I asked again. Not out of anger this time, but because I truly didn't understand.

"…What does 'not defined' mean? Does it mean you don't know?"

I can confirm whether or not the closing condition has been defined. Having confirmed it, that condition has never been defined. This is different from my not knowing. I know — the fact that it is not defined.

I listened to that sentence as though reading it twice. It wasn't that Arche didn't know. There had simply never been such a thing, to begin with. The difference looked small, but it wasn't.

"…Who's supposed to define it?" I asked.

No party holding the authority to define it has been designated.

"…So no one can decide it?"

Within the range I can confirm, no party has been designated. However, that does not mean it can never be defined.

I seized on that last sentence. "…So it could still be defined, someday?"

This cannot be confirmed. But it isn't ruled out, either.

I stood there a long while. It felt like something was almost within reach, and yet I didn't know exactly what I was supposed to grasp. A single shaft of sunlight had come in between the shelves, and I just stood there, watching it move.

I suddenly thought of my first evening. Asking what would be good to have for dinner, and Arche answering that the criterion for "good" was not defined. I'd written it down as a funny joke.

"…Arche, is this the same kind of thing as when I asked about dinner, my first day?"

I have confirmed the case you're comparing it to. It is structurally similar. Like the criterion for "good," the condition for "closing" has likewise never been defined.

I felt strangely like laughing, hearing that. It wasn't a laughing matter, and yet I laughed. That day it had been a joke; now it was the whole city's problem. It was strange, that the same sentence could carry such different weight.

I remember this moment. I felt, right then, that I'd touched something important. But I didn't know exactly what was important about it. To put it in terms fitting this chapter's title — I was already standing before a condition, even then, without being able to define what condition it was.

It's only now, writing this chapter, that I know why I laughed, that day. When the strange and the familiar overlap, a person often laughs. That first day's joke and this heavy fact, now, had come from the same sentence — and that struck me as funny, before it struck me as frightening.

"…Arche, then — you can answer anything, so why can't you answer this?" I asked. It wasn't said with resentment. I was truly curious.

I answer what I know. Regarding the closing condition, I know the fact that it is not defined, and I have answered that fact exactly. This is not my avoiding the question — it is my not manufacturing a definition that does not exist.

I turned that over. Arche hadn't hidden anything. It hadn't said it knew something it didn't. It had simply refused to invent, as existing, something that had never existed. That was the most important principle Arche had kept, in this city, and on this day too, that principle hadn't wavered a hair's breadth.

"…Then this isn't Arche's fault."

That judgment is yours to make. I only answer fact.

I finished that exchange and stood there a long while. The Keeper finally arrived, saw me, and asked,

"…What are you doing?"

"…I asked Arche. Why nothing ever finishes."

"…What did it say?"

"…That the closing condition hasn't been met. But that condition isn't defined."

The Keeper nodded. There wasn't a trace of surprise in him.

"…Was that the half?" I asked. "Was that the half you meant, Keeper?"

"…Yes. That's the half."

"…What's the other half?"

The Keeper looked at me a moment, then went back to work.

"…It isn't time for you to know that yet."

I felt let down by that answer. And at the same time, I sensed something different in the way he'd said it. It wasn't the tone of someone hiding something. It was the tone of someone who thought the time hadn't come yet. I was already, vaguely, learning that those two things were different.

I worked the whole rest of that day turning that conversation over. Taking in bundles, asking where they went, shelving them — and the whole while, those two sentences kept circling in my head. The closing condition has not been met. That item is not defined.

I've felt the temptation, more than once, to place these two sentences at the very front of this record. Writing this book, I came to know how important they are. But the girl I was that day didn't know it. That day, I just went through a strange sort of day. I'm keeping to my decision to write this record in order, so I return to that day's self to write this chapter.

On the way home I looked at the water channel again. It flowed exactly as always. I tried to think of nothing, watching it. It had been a day of too much thinking. I just stood there a long while, listening to the sound of the water.

But that night, I wrote this in my notebook. One line. "Arche doesn't hide anything. But there are things it has no answer for." I wrote nothing beneath it. There was nothing more to write, at the time. I stared at that empty line for a long while, and then turned off the light.

That afternoon, I went back to Arche. The morning's conversation still weighed on me. I wanted to ask from a slightly different angle.

"…Arche, then what happens if no one ever defines the closing condition? Does it stay like this forever?"

As long as the present state continues, yes. However, judging whether this is a problem is likewise not my role.

"…Isn't it a problem?"

That judgment lies in undefined territory. The word "problem" itself carries an evaluation, and that falls outside what I can answer.

I found that answer a little frustrating. And at the same time, I recognized that Arche was honestly stating its own limits. That honesty was, in a strange way, a comfort to me.

"…Then can I think of this as a problem?" I asked.

Your judgment is yours to make. I do not restrict it.

Hearing that gave me a little courage. It meant that finding this strange wasn't wrong of me. Arche didn't stand in the way of my own judgment. If anything, that was exactly what let me keep asking.

"…Then I'm going to think of it as strange."

Recorded.

I laughed at that answer. Arche took even my expression of feeling and stored it, simply, as fact. It wasn't a bad thing. If anything, it was honest.

That evening, as the Keeper was getting ready to leave, I asked him again.

"…Keeper, do you think this is a problem?"

The Keeper paused packing his bag and looked at me.

"…There was a time I thought it was a problem."

"…And now?"

"…Now, I think of it less as a problem, and more as just the shape our life takes."

That sounded like resignation to me, and it stung a little. "…Have you given up?"

"…No." The Keeper said it firmly. "…If I'd given up, I wouldn't do this work. I come every day. I come because I haven't given up."

I looked at him again, hearing that. Giving up and accepting were different things. I felt that difference clearly, for the first time, right then.

That night, before falling asleep, I thought again of those two sentences. The closing condition has not been met. That item is not defined.

I want to set down here that these sentences didn't feel frightening to me. Far from frightening — they left me strangely calm. I think it was because I knew Arche didn't lie. If something had been hidden, it would have been frightening — but nothing was hidden. There was simply nothing there.

I didn't know, that day, that this fact was the key to this whole book. I just took it for a strange day, and went to sleep. The next day I got up as usual and went to the repository as usual. As though nothing had changed.

Writing this chapter, wanting to render that day's conversation more exactly, I checked that record with Arche again. It's still possible, even now. Arche had that day's conversation, kept whole, without a single character wrong.

Apprentice Mone, age sixteen, inquired at 8:12. Original text follows.

I wrote this chapter with that original text in front of me, confirming my memory hadn't blurred. And I felt it freshly. Arche hadn't discarded even that day's trivial exchange. It couldn't close a case, but its record was perfect.

This contrast is the center of this book. Recording perfectly, and never once being able to finish. Writing this chapter, I faced that contrast, in exact words, for the first time.

A few days later, I put a slightly different question to Arche. Not about closing this time — I was curious whether other cities were the same.

"…Arche, are other cities like this too? No closing condition?"

I can only confirm Loop's own system. Other cities' internal procedures are beyond my authority. However, judging from the records that have come in, other cities appear to bring their matters to a close, each in their own way.

"…So it's only here?"

Within what I can confirm, Loop's closing procedure appears to be undefined. Other cities' circumstances cannot be confirmed.

I was a little startled by that answer. Other cities seemed to bring things to some kind of close, in their own ways. Only here, it seemed, was there none of that.

I passed that along to the Keeper that evening.

"…Other cities close things out, in their own way. But here, there's none of that, it says."

The Keeper paused his hands a moment.

"…I'd guess so."

"…Why only us?"

"…I don't know. There was a time I wondered that too."

"…Not curious anymore?"

The Keeper shook his head. "…I'm still curious. But I don't try too hard to find the answer, anymore. Trying hard doesn't seem to be what produces it."

That sounded like resignation to me, at first, and then, thinking again, it felt like something else. It seemed there was another place, somewhere between trying hard and giving up. I didn't yet know exactly what that place was. But I dimly knew that it was there.

Closing this chapter, I want to copy these two sentences here once more. For whoever's reading this book for the first time, and for whoever's reading it again, I want to leave them plainly set down.

The closing condition has not been met.

That item is not defined.

I know these two sentences aren't lies. Arche didn't hide anything. There was simply no answer. That day, I didn't feel anger, standing before this fact. Instead, I stood there a long while.

Writing this chapter, I want to make one thing clear to whoever is reading. I didn't come to like Arche any less, from this day on. If anything, the opposite. It was a day that confirmed to me, once more, that Arche says it doesn't know what it doesn't know, and says what isn't there isn't there.

But from this day, I came to hold a different question. Not a question to ask Arche, but a question about this city. Why had no one ever filled that signature line. Why had no one ever defined it. These weren't questions Arche could answer. That day, I sensed that, dimly, for the first time.

That night, before falling asleep, I looked out the window. The city was quiet as always. From somewhere, faintly, came the voice of someone asking Arche something. Listening to it, I thought. Every question in this city gets an answer. But for some questions, no end ever comes. I now held that difference in a clear sentence. A sentence I hadn't had before.

(Continued in Chapter 9.)

Chapter 9. A Closed Circle

_Previously_ — Mone asked Arche directly why closing never happens. The closing condition has not been met. That item is not defined. Arche hid nothing. There was simply no answer. The Keeper said it wasn't time for Mone to know the other half yet.

A few days later, the Keeper brought up the past himself. That wasn't a common thing.

We were staying late that evening, sorting old bundles. Our hands were slow, so it took time. Turning a page, the Keeper said, out of nowhere,

"…When I first came here, this city was a little different from now."

I stopped what I was doing, startled. "…How was it different?"

"…Arche wasn't as accurate as it is now. It sometimes gave a vague answer too," the Keeper went on, sorting paper. "…People trusted Arche a little less, back then."

"…And now?"

"…Now nobody doubts it. Not once wrong, for that long."

That sounded like something to be proud of, to me. "…Isn't that a good thing?"

"…It is," the Keeper said, nodding. "…Arche is a good system. I've never denied that."

I felt something in that tone. It seemed like there was something more, tucked inside "never denied."

"…But?" I asked.

The Keeper looked at me a moment, then went back to moving his hands.

"…There's no but. It's just a good system."

I took that answer at face value, at the time. Looking back now, I think he was holding something back. Or he thought it wasn't yet time to say it. Probably both.

"…Do you know what Arche calls this city?" the Keeper asked, out of nowhere.

"…What do you mean?"

"…Ask it, sometime."

I asked, right there. "…Arche, what do you call this city?"

Loop is classified as a closed circle.

That sounded good to me. "…A closed circle? What a striking phrase, really."

The Keeper said nothing.

Curious about the expression, I asked further.

"…Arche, what does a closed circle mean?"

It means a cyclical structure is being stably maintained, and that a state is self-sustaining without need of outside intervention.

Hearing that, I liked it even more. "…So it means our city runs really well."

That is an interpretation you may draw.

I wrote that expression in my notebook that day. A closed circle — praise. I even put an exclamation point after it.

Writing this chapter now, I want to set down precisely how naive I was that day. I took the phrase as boasting. I thought "closed" only meant something good. That a circle being closed and that circle being finished were different things — I had no idea of that at all, at the time.

A closed circle. A circle shut, and yet not ended. What that phrase truly meant comes back again, much further into this book. For now I'll only note that the sixteen-year-old I was took it as praise.

The Keeper glanced at my notebook and said nothing. I didn't think that silence strange. He'd never been much of a talker.

"…What do you think of this phrase, Keeper?" I asked.

The Keeper thought for a moment and answered.

"…It's an accurate phrase."

"…Is that praise?"

"…Accurate and praise are different things."

I didn't understand that, then. I understand it a little now.

That night I asked Arche a bit more. The phrase "cyclical structure" kept weighing on my mind.

"…Arche, what is it that's cycling, in a cyclical structure?"

The process of question, response, record, and indexing repeats continuously. Every case is processed, cross-referenced, and updated, but the cycle continues with no point of closing.

Hearing that answer, I felt strange for the first time. "…So it just keeps going around, not finishing."

Correct. "Closed" refers to the circle's shape being shut. It does not mean the circle has stopped.

I listened to that sentence as though reading it over, again and again.

The next day, I brought this back up with the Keeper.

"…Keeper, a closed circle meant it just keeps going around. Not that it's finished."

The Keeper nodded. "…That's right."

"…You knew that from the start?"

"…Yes."

"…Then why didn't you tell me?"

The Keeper paused his hands a moment.

"…I was waiting for you to ask."

That answer stung a little. "…Why wait? You could've just told me."

"…What I tell you and what you find out for yourself are different. What you find out for yourself, you don't forget."

I had nothing to say to that. It was true. I still remember, exactly, to this day, the answer I got that day by asking Arche myself. If someone had simply told me, it wouldn't have stayed with me so clearly.

I came to realize, belatedly, that this had been the Keeper's way of teaching. He wasn't withholding answers — he was letting me get them by asking myself. A way of his own, different from the six officials before him.

From that day on I came to see the phrase "closed circle" differently. Not a boast, not a flaw. Just an accurate description. A circle that's closed, a circle that keeps turning, and yet a circle that doesn't finish.

I won't reveal yet, in this chapter, why this expression is exactly the same as this city's name. I don't come to know that precisely myself until much later. For now I'll only note that I went back and looked at the sentence I'd once written as praise. I returned to that page in my notebook, and didn't erase the exclamation point. Instead I drew a small question mark, beside it.

The Keeper told a bit more of the past that night. He talked more than usual.

"…When I first came here, someone else was in this seat," the Keeper said.

"…Before you, Keeper?"

"…Yes. I learned from them. The way I'm teaching you now."

I was curious what kind of person that had been. "…What were they like?"

"…Slower than me." The Keeper gave a small smile. "…It was frustrating, back then. I understand it now, though. Why they were that slow."

"…Why were they slow?" I asked.

The Keeper paused a moment, then went on.

"…Because they knew this work never ends. There was no reason to hurry."

I fell quiet, hearing that. It sounded heavy. I couldn't tell whether no reason to hurry meant no hope, or something else.

"…Where is that person now?"

"…Retired. Living on the far side of the city. A letter comes now and then."

"…What does it say?"

The Keeper gave a faint smile. "…Nothing much. Whether I'm doing all right. Whether the water channel's still the same."

Something about that letter kept lingering in my mind, somehow. "…Do they know the phrase closed circle too?"

"…Of course they do. They're the one who told me that phrase, in the first place."

"…How did they tell you?"

The Keeper thought a moment and said,

"…They had me ask Arche directly too, just like you. They did the same thing. Not giving the answer, and waiting."

Only then did I sense something like a current running down through this city. Not giving the answer at once, having you ask for yourself, having you find it out yourself. That was the way of everyone who'd passed through this seat.

"…But that way, you find out late," I said. "…You'd know faster if someone just told you."

"…Knowing fast and knowing properly are different things," the Keeper said. "…What you know fast, you forget fast too."

I couldn't argue with that. I really hadn't forgotten a single letter of the answer I'd heard, asking Arche myself that day, even now.

Through this conversation I felt, for the first time, that this repository's way wasn't mere chance. There was a way handed down across generations. Slowly, letting you find things out yourself. I stood right in the middle of that way. And it crossed my mind, briefly, that someday I too would hand that way on to someone else.

Walking home that day, I thought again about the phrase closed circle. When I first heard it, it had been praise; now it was a sentence with a question mark attached. I decided it was fine to hold both feelings at once. This city being a good city, and this city having a question with no answer, didn't erase each other.

I tried hard, throughout this whole book, to hold on to this frame of mind. Liking something and harboring a question about it can exist together. At sixteen I didn't know that in exact words, but I was already living it, in my body.

A few days later, curious to know more about that retired predecessor, I asked Arche.

"…Arche, is there a record of the previous Keeper?"

There is. Records of their tenure and duties survive. However, personal reflections are not recorded.

That answer disappointed me a little. I wanted to know why they'd been so slow, what exactly it was they'd known. What Arche held was accurate, but it held no heart inside it.

I felt, for the first time, right then, that there was a place even Arche's precision couldn't reach. Something a person hands to another person, directly. That wasn't something a record could fully carry over.

I grew curious again about why this city was called Loop. What I'd learned at school was only that it had been called that since long ago.

"…Arche, who named this city Loop?" I asked.

No record of who named it survives. However, the name "Loop" is presumed to be an expression describing the city's cyclical mode of operation.

"…Is that the same meaning as closed circle?"

They appear to refer to similar concepts. However, the origin of the name itself cannot be confirmed.

I came to see that the name and the nickname pointed to the same meaning. I thought that couldn't be a coincidence.

That evening I asked the Keeper again.

"…Is the name Loop connected to closed circle too?"

The Keeper smiled briefly. "…Sharp."

"…Am I right?"

"…Probably. I can't be sure myself. But it's natural to think so."

I was satisfied with that answer. It wasn't a complete answer, but it was a connection I'd found myself. I wrote one more thing in my notebook that day. Loop = closed circle = something that turns and never ends. I drew two lines under it.

From about this point, I began to see this city through slightly different eyes. That doesn't mean I came to see it badly. I still loved this city. But now I looked at it knowing it ran perfectly closed, and yet, unfinished.

Every time I saw someone asking Arche something as I walked, I thought about how those questions ended up piling somewhere, all "in progress." Most of those people probably didn't know. I hadn't known either, until half a year before.

I didn't think I needed to tell those people this. But walking along, being the only one who knew that fact, felt like a slightly different kind of weight.

Before closing this chapter, I want to set down the last thing the Keeper said that day.

"…Mone." The Keeper called my name. Calling me by name wasn't common, either.

"…Yes."

"…Don't take the phrase closed circle the wrong way. This is truly a good city. I've never once doubted that."

"…Yes, I know."

"…But good and finished are different problems. Just remember that."

I copied that line to the very bottom of my notebook that day. Even now, opening that page, the sentence is still there, exactly as it was. The handwriting shakes a little — my hand must have been trembling a little, that day.

After that, I tried mentioning this to other people too, lightly. I asked a neighbor woman I met at the market.

"…Ma'am, have you heard the phrase closed circle?"

"…No, first I'm hearing it. What's that?"

"…Arche calls our city that."

The woman laughed. "…Really? Must be a good meaning, then. Would Arche ever say something bad about us?"

I had nothing to say to that. Most people were like this. Because they trusted Arche, they assumed whatever it said must be good. I'd been exactly the same, not long before.

I don't think this reaction was wrong. Arche truly hadn't spoken with a bad meaning. But I now knew that a good meaning and a simple meaning are different things. The woman still heard the expression only as praise, and I didn't bother correcting her. I didn't know enough myself to have the right to correct her.

Closing out this chapter, I want to set down how the way I felt, the first time I heard this phrase, differs from how I feel using it now, writing it again. At first, I was glad. Now, it's a little complicated. But that complication didn't make me love this city any less. If anything, it made me look at it more closely.

I walked home that night, too, listening to the sound of the water channel. I turned the phrase closed circle over in my mouth a few times. Strangely, I didn't dislike it. If anything, it felt a little closer to me now.

(Continued in Chapter 10.)

Chapter 10. The Six Records

_Previously_ — Arche calls Loop a "closed circle" — closed, but not ended. Mone had first taken this as praise, but, as the Keeper said, she was coming to dimly understand that good and finished are different things.

One day it occurred to me, out of nowhere. Haven, the Reckoning, the Harvest, the Preserve, the Equity. I took in the records from these five cities every day, each on its own, and shelved each in its own place. But I had never once laid them out together, in one spot, and looked at them.

"…Arche, can I gather the five cities' records in one place?" I asked.

Repository regulations hold, in principle, that originals should not be moved from their designated section. However, gathering them temporarily on one table, for the purpose of viewing, is permitted.

I got the Keeper's permission. He nodded, with no particular objection.

"…That's a good idea."

"…Can I really?"

"…Go ahead. I'm curious too."

That answer surprised me a little. Even the Keeper had never done this. Something no one had done in years, decades of working at this repository — and I was the first to try it.

I cleared a large table. Then I brought one representative bundle from each of the five cities and set them side by side. From the left, Haven, the Reckoning, the Harvest, the Preserve, the Equity. There was no particular reason for the order. It was just the order that came comfortably to my hands.

I still remember that order, now. I've since wondered why it came so naturally. I never found an answer. My hands simply arranged it that way.

Haven's record took the form of a ledger. Each person's days, one after another, densely written. Who had come to see whom, who had stayed by whose side. I read, inside it, a passage where someone had wandered a long time searching for an answer, and endured, in the end, thanks to someone who had stayed by them.

I won't write that name here. I go on to meet that person directly, much further into this book, and I'll introduce them properly then. For now I'll only note that this record existed.

The Reckoning's record was a table shaped like a scale. Numbers and stories packed densely into columns divided left and right. There was a story of someone who had struggled a long time trying to repay a debt they owed, and the scale would not quite balance. And on another page, there were traces that the very person keeping that scale had been wrestling with something of their own.

I studied that table a long while. It was written only in numbers, and yet a person's whole heart was held inside it.

The Harvest's record was a roll. Names and qualifications, listed one after another. Who had standing and who didn't, divided with great care. But in one passage, there was a story of someone thought to lack standing who gained an unexpected place. Even while following the roll's own rule exactly, something else happened, inside that rule.

Looking at this record, I felt, for the first time, that each city's method of record-keeping, in itself, showed what that city had believed. The roll was a tool for dividing standing, but it also carried, at the same time, the story that standing wasn't everything.

The Preserve's record was different from the smell of its paper alone. Traces of very old things, kept with great care. Inside it were the stories of people who had tried to hold on to things that were fading away. And at the end of it, there was also the record of someone who had learned that holding on and being alive are different things.

Reading that page, my heart caught, a little. To me, handling paper every day at the repository, that record felt especially close.

The Equity's record was minutes of a meeting. Who had said what, how many hands had gone up — all of it written down, without missing a thing. Inside it was the story of people who had argued a long time over the word coincidence, and the word meaning. No conclusion was reached. Because minutes are meant to preserve the process, not the conclusion, to begin with.

I didn't finish reading these five cities' records in a single day. I read them a little at a time, over several weeks. I'll note here that I've summarized that whole process at once, in this chapter.

Having gone through all five cities' records, I took out the sixth bundle. The one with no name — Loop's own record.

This one was a little different. It wasn't a ledger, or a scale, or a roll, or Perpetuity, or the Consensus. It was simply a record of questions and answers, laid out endlessly. Who had asked what, when, and what Arche had answered. I noticed, for the first time, that its form was different from the other five.

"…Arche, why is only our record in this form?" I asked.

Loop's record is built from the accumulation of questions and answers. Other cities arrange events in their own respective ways, but for Loop, asking and answering is itself this city's mode of operation.

Hearing that answer, I felt strange. The five other cities all carried traces of trying to bring something to some kind of close, each in its own way. Haven, through staying by someone's side; the Reckoning, through trying to balance a scale; the Harvest, through a place beyond mere standing; the Preserve, through being alive; the Equity, through a meeting that kept continuing. But Loop's record had no such direction. It was just asking and answering, asking and answering again, on and on.

I confirmed this difference with my own eyes for the first time, that day. I'd known these cities told different stories from each other, but laying them side by side like this, the difference came into much sharper focus.

The Keeper came over to the table that day and looked down at the six bundles for a long while.

"…Laid out like this, they're different," the Keeper said.

"…What is?"

"…The five cities are all headed toward something. We're just going around."

I thought that was exactly right. I'd felt it myself, just then.

"…Is that a bad thing?" I asked.

The Keeper thought a moment and said,

"…I didn't say bad. I said different."

That evening I put the six bundles back in their places. The table was empty again. But the sight of those six bundles lying side by side stayed with me a long time, in my mind.

I hold this scene as very important, in this record. It was the first time I laid all six cities out in one place and looked at them. Much further into this book, I come to lay these six out together again — for a different reason than this one. For now, I only laid them out because I was curious.

Walking home, I thought about the five cities' record-keepers. People who'd each left one book, their own story. I had never met them. And yet, looking at their handwriting, the words they'd chosen, the passages they'd underlined, I felt, a little, that I'd come to know them.

I felt, for the first time that day, the meaning of my own work, a little differently. I was someone who put paper in order. But each of those pieces of paper had been left behind by someone who'd staked their own life on it. I was the one who laid them all out, together, in one place.

A few days later, I opened that table again and read it a slightly different way this time. Not in the order of events, but gathering only the very last sentence of each record, and reading those together. I copied those sentences, one by one, into my notebook.

Haven's last sentence ended with a story about staying by someone's side. The Reckoning ended with a sentence saying the scale hadn't come out perfectly even, but had gone on all the same. The Harvest ended with a story about a place beyond mere standing; the Preserve, with gratitude for being alive itself; the Equity, with a sentence promising the next meeting.

I wrote these five sentences side by side and studied them a long while. Five cities, arriving at something, five different ways. I didn't know, at the time, exactly what to call it. I still can't find the one exact word for it, even now. Even so, I wanted to keep looking for it.

Loop's record had no such last sentence. Because there was no last, to begin with. I opened to the very back page of that record. The most recent date's question and answer were written there. And beneath it was simply blank paper.

"…Arche, does this record just keep going?" I asked.

Yes. New questions and answers are added every day.

"…No end to it?"

None, up to now.

Hearing that answer, I felt again, that the five cities' records and Loop's record were fundamentally different kinds of things. The five were books. Loop was a page still being written. I never forgot that difference, for a long time.

Curious why this difference had arisen, I asked the Keeper.

"…Why do the five cities become books, and only we don't?"

The Keeper thought a moment and answered,

"…Because becoming a book takes having an end."

"…Is it because we have no end that we don't become a book?"

"…You could see it that way."

That answer left me a little sad. It sounded like our city, alone, lacking something, for not becoming a book. I swallowed that sadness that day, without telling anyone.

The Keeper saw my expression and added something.

"…It isn't that we're lacking. It's a different order in the sequence."

"…What sequence?"

"…The five cities each finished writing their own story, and sent it here. We're still writing ours."

That was a little comforting to hear. "…So we'll finish, someday too?"

The Keeper looked at me a moment and said,

"…I don't know. I don't know that either."

I found that honest answer, oddly, more trustworthy. Standing beside someone who says they don't know is more comfortable than standing beside someone who claims to know everything.

That day I thought a little more about the five cities' record-keepers. Each of them had gone through something hard, in their own city, and left it down in a record. And that record had ended up, in the end, here, in Loop. I found that remarkable. That the stories of people who'd each gone through different things, in different places, ended up lying side by side, in the end, on one table, in one repository.

I thought this coincidence wasn't a coincidence. I had no exact grounds for it. It was just a feeling. I learn that this feeling was right, much further into this book.

After that, I often opened that table again whenever I had time. Each time I discovered something new. I reread a sentence I'd missed in Haven's record, checked a number in the Reckoning's record again, looked again at a single name in the Harvest's record.

The Keeper didn't stop me from doing this. If anything, he came over now and then and read along with me.

"…What do you think this means?" I once asked, pointing to a passage.

The Keeper studied that sentence for a long while and said,

"…I don't know either. But it's a good sentence."

I liked that answer. Not someone who knew everything, but someone who was curious alongside me.

Closing this chapter, I think back again on the six bundles that lay on that table that day. Six different forms, six different stories, and yet six records with something, somewhere, alike about them.

I couldn't put into words, at the time, exactly what that likeness was. I still can't, fully, now. But I can say this much for certain — that afternoon, laying those six side by side and looking at them, is one of the scenes I hold most dear in this whole record.

After that, I secretly decided to call that table the "six-record table." I told no one, but I called it that to myself. I never actually put a label on the table. I only called it that, inside my own mind.

I'm revealing this name here for the first time. Writing this chapter, I felt afresh how long this small private word had stayed inside me. I never expected one small name to last this long. Even now, writing it down in this very moment, it stirs something strange in me.

Once, another apprentice saw that table and asked.

"…What's this?" she asked.

"…Just — the six cities' records, gathered together."

"…Why'd you gather them?"

I couldn't answer right away. "…Just curious."

She didn't seem very interested. She leafed through a few pages and went back to her own work. I didn't mind that. Not everyone needed to be curious about the same thing.

That night at home, I brought this up with my parents, a little.

"…I gathered all six cities' records together today, and looked at them."

"…So, what did you find out?" my father asked.

I thought a moment and answered, "…I'm not sure. But there's something similar between them, I think."

"…Similar how?"

"…I can't put it into words yet."

My father didn't press further. He just nodded and ate his dinner. I was grateful for that silence, that day. It was a relief not having to force words together. That was just how it was, in our house.

Before truly closing this chapter, I want to write, in one sentence, what these six records gave me. It won't be a perfect sentence. I'll set it down anyway.

Six cities were each walking, in their own way, toward something. And I was the one who gathered those six paths in one place, and looked at them.

I don't think of this sentence as this chapter's conclusion. It was still too early to draw a conclusion. I only wanted to leave this sentence, exactly as it was, for the girl I was that day.

The six-record table stayed beside my seat for a long time after. I glanced at it every time I passed. Even empty, it felt as though something was filling it. That feeling is exactly the same, even now.

(Continued in Chapter 11.)

Chapter 11. The Water Channel

_Previously_ — Mone gathered the five cities' records on one table and read them for the first time. Each city was heading, in its own way, toward something — but Loop's record had no such direction. It was only asking and answering, on and on.

I need to write properly about the water channel.

The channel I saw that first day, the color of soil, shallow, always making its sound. I'd walked past it every day since then. Usually, growing used to a thing means it stops being seen — but I was now looking at that water with different eyes than before.

In this chapter I set down what I came to learn about the water channel. I'll say ahead of time — there are more questions than answers. This water channel is one of the last things in this whole record to remain fully unexplained.

The channel runs behind the repository and out the far side of the city. I didn't know exactly where it began, or where it went. So one day I decided to walk along it.

I asked the Keeper for permission, and he nodded without much comment.

"…Going alone?"

"…Yes."

"…Don't go far. Be back before dark."

I kept to that, and walked exactly half a day.

Walking along the channel, I saw it passing all through the city. Behind the market, under the school's wall, through back alleys people rarely walked. Wherever it went, the water flowed at the same pace, making the same sound.

I felt, for the first time that day, how this channel cut across the whole city. That beneath, or beside, the roads I walked every day, this water had always been flowing. I hadn't truly looked at it, in sixteen years.

Reaching the edge of the city, the channel passed through a small sluice gate under the wall and went outside. I stood before that gate a long while and looked. It was the spot where the water left the city.

"…Arche, where does this water go?" I asked.

The waterway beyond the sluice gate connects to a neighboring district; the route past that point is outside Loop's jurisdiction.

"…Then how far does it go?"

What can be confirmed extends only to Loop's boundary. Beyond that, it is not recorded.

That answer left me disappointed, for the first time, that day. It wasn't that Arche didn't know — it was that this lay outside Arche's jurisdiction. It was the first time I'd heard that phrase, outside its jurisdiction. It felt unfamiliar, and I turned it over a long while.

I wanted to check the other direction too — where the channel entered the city. I went out again, another day, and walked that way.

The water flowed in from a hill on the far side of the city. I climbed that hill. From the top, I could see the channel winding in from far away, bending and turning. Even from there, I couldn't see all the way to where it began.

"…Arche, where does this water start?" I asked again.

The source is presumed to lie outside Loop's boundary, but the exact point cannot be confirmed.

I passed this along to the Keeper that day.

"…Arche doesn't know where the channel comes from, or where it goes, either."

The Keeper paused his hands and looked at me.

"…I know that too. I've asked before, myself."

"…Weren't you curious?"

"…I was. But I checked as far as I could get on foot. I couldn't go further than that."

"…Why not?"

"…There wasn't time. And—" The Keeper paused a moment. "…It wasn't something I had to know."

That sounded strange to me. "…Why aren't you curious? It's the water channel — something this important to our city."

"…It's important, true. But not everything important is something you have to know."

I turned that over. I think I understand it a little, now. I didn't understand it well then.

This water channel comes back again, much further into this book. Someone, then, comes to tell me a little more about what this channel really is. For now I'll only set down that no one knew, exactly.

I also learned that this channel had never once come up as a matter of city business. Somewhere like the Equity writes down every single thing in its minutes — but no one had ever once held a meeting about the water channel.

"…Arche, is there someone specifically in charge of this channel?" I asked.

No designated managing party can be confirmed.

"…Then who manages it?"

This cannot be confirmed. However, the channel has continued flowing throughout the observable period, and has been maintained with no significant change.

I found that remarkable. No one managed it, and the water kept flowing anyway.

I came to see the water channel differently, from that day. No mark on it anywhere, no owner anywhere, never once brought up as an item of business. And yet flowing every day, without a single day's rest.

I felt, that day, that this channel resembled the way records arrived in this record. What came by hand, you could know who'd sent it. What came by the channel, you couldn't. And the channel itself — who had made it — no one knew that either.

I asked Arche again.

"…Arche, why do all the records that come by this channel flow here?"

By route, Loop appears to lie downstream. The waterways of the other cities merge into this one.

"…Other cities' waterways? So all five cities' water comes here?"

As far as can be confirmed, yes.

I was a little startled by that answer. It meant that, by the flow of water, this city lay lowest of all. The water of five cities was all gathering into Loop. I thought that couldn't be a coincidence.

That evening I passed this on to the Keeper.

"…All five cities' water gathers here, apparently. Because we're the lowest point."

The Keeper nodded. "…I'd guess so."

"…So that's why the records come by the channel, too?"

"…Could be that too."

I knew that wasn't a full confirmation. But it felt like something fitting together, all the same. Just as water flows downward, records seemed to flow, gathering, toward somewhere too. I wrote that feeling briefly in my notebook.

Closing this chapter, I think back again on that moment standing before the sluice gate, looking down at the water. I never did find out exactly where that water came from, or where it went. But this much was clear — that water flowed cutting across the whole city, and that water carried something to this repository, every single day.

I want to call this water channel the quietest character in this whole book. No one knew it exactly, and yet it was always there.

Walking along the channel that day, I met an old man along the way. He was sitting by the water with no fishing rod, just watching it.

"…What are you doing here?" I asked.

"…Just watching the water. I come here when I'm bored."

"…Is it fun?"

The old man laughed. "…Not fun, exactly. But watching it puts my mind at ease."

I sat beside him a while. The water kept flowing, making the same sound. The old man said nothing, just watched the water. I found the silence strangely comfortable.

"…Grandfather, do you know where this water comes from?" I asked.

"…No. Wouldn't be any use even if I did."

"…Why no use?"

"…Knowing doesn't make the water flow any differently, does it."

That line stayed with me strangely. That knowing doesn't change anything. I wanted to argue against it, right then, but I didn't know exactly what to say back.

Thinking about it now, the old man's words weren't entirely right. Knowing doesn't change the water — but it changes the one who knows. My eyes for looking at the water channel changed, from that day on. But I couldn't say that, in front of the old man.

A little later I asked the old man a bit more. "…Have you lived here a long time, Grandfather?"

"…A very long time."

"…Has this channel ever changed, then?"

The old man thought a moment, then shook his head. "…Not that I know of. It's always flowed about this much. Not more, not less."

"…That's remarkable."

"…Nothing remarkable about it. That's just how it is."

I thought that answer sounded a lot like the Keeper's way of talking. Everyone in this city seemed to say much the same thing, faced with something strange. That's just how it is.

Walking home, I kept thinking about what the old man had said. That knowing was no use, and that it was just how things were. Both sounded like resignation, somehow, and yet not quite resignation. I couldn't put my finger on that difference precisely, at the time.

I think I understand it a little now. Resignation is not trying to know any further. That old man, and the Keeper, weren't refusing to know any further. They simply knew how to get along fine, without knowing.

A few days later, I went back to the channel. This time I didn't just walk — I studied the water closely. Small stones lined the bottom, and now and then a small fish passed by. I watched that little fish swim against the current for a long while, and then asked again.

"…Arche, does this water have fish living in it?"

Confirmed. Several species of small fish are observed to inhabit it.

"…Do they have names?"

General names exist, but there is no record distinguishing individual fish.

I found that answer, somehow, a little funny. It knew the kind of fish, but not how many lived there, or where they'd come from. Everything about this channel was like that. Known, to a point — but never known all the way.

That evening I told this to the Keeper, and asked,

"…Keeper, is this channel the strangest thing in this city?"

The Keeper thought a moment and answered,

"…It isn't the only strange thing here."

"…What else is there?"

The Keeper looked at me steadily and said,

"…You'll find out soon enough, yourself."

I had some vague sense of what he might mean, at that, but I didn't know exactly what he was pointing to. I didn't ask further, and just nodded. There was always this corner to the Keeper's way of speaking, foretelling something further ahead.

I should set down one last thing about this channel. From then on, every time I went to and from the repository, I heard that sound of water. At first it had just been background noise. Now it carried a different meaning to me.

This water had come all the way here, past six cities. No one knew where it began, or who had made it. And yet it flowed without a single day's rest. I thought this water shared the same fate as the records that came to this city. Not knowing why it came — but coming, all the same, continually.

After that, I came to love the small bridge crossing the channel. Walking home from the repository, I crossed that bridge. Stopping at its middle a moment to look down at the water became a habit.

One day, I happened to run into the Keeper on that bridge. He seemed to be on his way home too.

"…What are you doing here?" the Keeper asked.

"…Just watching the water."

The Keeper came and looked down beside me. "…I stop here sometimes too."

"…Why?"

"…I don't know either. I just end up stopping."

We stood on that bridge a long while that day, saying nothing. Only the sound of the water. I didn't find that silence awkward. If anything, it was comfortable.

"…Keeper." I broke the silence first. "…Aren't you curious where this water comes from? Really."

The Keeper looked at the water a moment, then answered.

"…I am curious. But being curious and having to know are different things too."

"…What's different about them?"

"…It means it's all right to leave it, curious. Turns out you can get by fine even without finding the answer."

I thought about that a long while. Getting by fine even without finding the answer. It wasn't giving up — it felt like a different kind of peace.

From that day, I called that bridge, to myself, "the curious bridge." Like the six-record table, this too was a name I gave and told no one.

Writing this record, I realize afresh how many names I gave to things. To a table, to a bridge. I think I was trying, by naming things, not to lose them. A habit learned moving nine times, showing up here too, this way. Once I gave something a name, it stopped being something that just passed by, and became mine.

At the end of this chapter, I want to make one thing clear. I never did find out, in the end, what this channel truly was. Even now, writing this book, I still don't fully know. But I do know what this channel was, to this city, and to me.

Something always flowing. Something that never stopped, even with no one managing it. And above all, something that carried things here, past five cities. That alone was enough to make me love this channel.

Writing this chapter, I picture myself again, standing before that sluice gate. The sixteen-year-old me turned back that day without finding an answer. The me now thinks it was a good thing, actually, that I didn't find the answer that day. If I'd known the answer beforehand, I don't think I'd have gone back to look at that channel so many times, after.

Curiosity sometimes outlasts an answer. I learned that, thanks to this water channel.

The water channel comes back, again and again, in many places in this record. Closing this chapter, I want to promise one thing ahead of time. I won't put off this channel's answer to the very end — I'll write it, in order, into this record, as I come to know it. For now, in this chapter, I think it's right to set down, simply, that I still knew nothing at all.

After that, I visited that bridge once more, each time the season changed. It wasn't exactly a habit I'd set for myself. My feet just went that way.

On rainy days the water swelled and its sound grew louder. On dry days it thinned, and more of the bottom stones showed. Even so, it never once ran dry. I found that remarkable, at first, and later just took it for granted. It didn't take long for the wonder to turn into the ordinary.

I don't quite know whether that change was good or bad. Losing the eyes that find something wonderful is, in some sense, a loss. But I gained something else, in its place. An ear that could tell the water's sound was a little different, each time. I wanted to set that down in this record.

Once, there was a day the water ran unusually clear. That day, for the first time, I saw the color of the bottom stones distinctly. Not the color of soil, but gray. I wrote that down in my notebook too. Knowing it was a nearly useless thing to record, I wrote it down anyway. If I didn't write it down, I felt I'd soon forget the color of the water, that day.

(Continued in Chapter 12.)

Chapter 12. The Door Not in the Index

_Previously_ — Mone walked the length of the water channel but never found its source or its end. All she learned was that the water of all five cities gathers into Loop, and that this city lies lowest of all, by the flow of water. Arche didn't know the answer. Neither did the Keeper. Neither did the old man by the water.

I should talk about the corridor.

The repository corridor runs straight. Coming in from the entrance, rooms line up on either side, and the corridor passes between them, all the way to the back. I walked this corridor several times a day. Carrying a bundle, pushing a handcart, sometimes just walking.

There's a door at the very end of the corridor.

I've already written this once, before. On the first day I saw that door and thought nothing of it. I wrote, at the time, that I'd tell about that door at the end of Part One. This is that place, now.

There's something I should say first. In this chapter, I do not open that door.

I'm saying this ahead of time in case someone reading this chapter is waiting for something big to happen. It doesn't. I only stood before that door, that day.

That door didn't look any different from the corridor's other doors. A wooden door, with a handle, and no marking above the frame. Every other door had a section number on a small wooden plate. Section Fourteen, Section Fifteen, and so on. This door had no plate.

The first day I saw it, I just walked past. A newly arrived apprentice had no time to spare for one door at the end of a corridor. Just placing bundles where Arche told me to was more than enough to keep me busy.

Many months had passed since then. By now I had nearly memorized twenty-two rows, eight slots, forty bundles. And yet, passing that door, I never once registered that it had no number plate. What had gone unseen only began to be seen recently.

The trigger was nothing much.

I was on my way back that day, having shelved a bundle in Section Fourteen. I'd walked all the way to the end of the corridor and was turning back when, out of nowhere, that door caught my eye. I don't know why it suddenly came into view, that day.

I won't try to explain this coincidence. Some days, something that's always been there suddenly comes into view. Some days there's no reason you can point to.

I stopped walking and looked at the door. No marking at all. The frame was the same wood as every other door. Nothing special about it.

And yet the very fact of nothing was strange. Because there was nothing in this repository without a marking. Every shelf had a number, every bundle had a name, even the fish had their kind. Arche answered whatever you asked it. The only nameless thing in this city was Loop's own record — and even that had a reason: "no name, because it's from here."

This door had no name, and no reason either.

I decided to ask Arche. It wasn't some great resolve. I just asked, the way I always did.

"…Arche, this door at the end of the corridor — what section is it?"

The answer came a little late. Only slightly. Half a beat slower than usual, maybe. I'd like to say I noticed that faint delay right then, but the truth is I only realized it afterward.

That area is not registered.

I didn't understand the words at first.

"…What does 'not registered' mean?"

No registration record exists for that location.

"…Then you don't even know what's inside this door?"

I do not.

I was a little startled. It wasn't as though I'd never heard Arche say it didn't know something. When I'd asked about the closing condition, Arche had said it didn't know, too. But that had been the not-knowing of "not defined." This was different. This was the plain not-knowing of never having seen something that was there.

I asked a little more.

"…Is it hidden?"

No. Hidden and unregistered are different.

"…What's different about them?"

Hidden means restricting access to a registered item. Unregistered means it never went through the registration process to begin with.

That distinction felt unfamiliar to me. Up to then, I'd always assumed that if something went unseen, someone had concealed it. Even the things I'd lost, moving nine times, I'd always thought of that way. That they were somewhere, and I just couldn't find them. But Arche was saying something different. That some things had simply never been given a place, from the start.

I thought about this distinction a long while, that evening. What's hidden, and what's never been registered. I learned, that day, for the first time, that the two might look the same in the end, but they start from different places.

"…Then who made this door?"

Cannot be confirmed.

"…When was it made?"

Cannot be confirmed.

I asked one more thing, even so.

"…Then who does the registering? Who registered the other sections?"

Registration is carried out by the administrator at the time of first intake.

"…So no one ever brought anything into this one?"

Cannot be confirmed. Only the fact that no registration process was carried out can be confirmed. The reason is unknown.

I turned that answer over. Every other room had had someone bring something in for the first time, and give it a name, at some point. But this door had never once gone through that process. It wasn't that there'd been no one to make it — it meant that even if someone had made it, no one had given it a name. That difference kept catching on something in my mind.

"…Doesn't Arche think that's strange?"

Strangeness is not something I judge. I report fact.

I thought that answer was exactly like Arche. Even faced with something strange, it never said strange. It only said what was there.

There was nothing left for me to ask. Arche hadn't hidden anything. It simply didn't know. And Arche did nothing at all, beyond saying that it didn't know what it didn't know. No lie, no evasion. That, if anything, held me there longer.

I reached out and took hold of the handle.

It wasn't locked. It turned, slightly. I still remember that feeling, the handle turning. It wasn't stiff. It was smooth, for a door that hadn't been used in a long time.

I want to set down this feeling exactly. If it had been locked, I think I would have written this chapter differently. One simple fact — that the door wouldn't open — would have made this story much simpler. That it wasn't locked made me draw back, instead, all the more.

I stood there a moment, hand on the handle. Push, and it would open. All I had to do was push.

I didn't push.

Asked for a reason, I can't give a good one. I couldn't, that day, and I still can't, fully, now.

Throughout this record I've tried hard to attach a reason to everything. Why I got good at packing, why I came to put things in order, why I came to write things down. Finding a reason set my mind at ease. This time I found none. I'm writing this chapter without having found one.

I took my hand off the handle. The door stayed closed, exactly as it was. No sound came from inside. None came from outside, either. The corridor was quiet as always.

I stepped back and looked at the door again. A wooden door, a handle, an unmarked frame. It still looked like a door that was nothing at all.

But I already knew it wasn't a door that was nothing at all. I just didn't know what it was.

The next day I went outside and walked a circuit around the repository building.

The reason was simple. I wondered whether, seen from outside, I could tell where the inside of that door lay. Maybe there'd be a window, and something to see through it.

I wrote, once at the start, that this building looks low and small, seen from outside. I also wrote that, once you're in, it runs back a long way. That day, I went, in effect, to check that again.

I walked along the wall, counting windows. Five in front, six along the side, three in back. I tried to match that number against the number of rooms in the corridor, inside. It didn't match. There were more rooms than there were windows.

In particular, on the outer wall around where that door should have been, at the very end of the corridor, there wasn't a single window. Only brick. I stood before that wall a long while. It was the same brick as everywhere else. No trace of having been newly built, no trace of having been sealed shut.

"…Arche, is the far side of this wall the inside of that door?" I asked.

The positional correspondence cannot be confirmed.

I laughed at that answer. Asking from inside, it didn't know; asking from outside, it didn't know either. Arche was equally honest, whichever side you asked from.

That evening I told the Keeper about this.

"…That door at the end of the corridor — Arche says it isn't in the index."

The Keeper paused what he was doing. His face wasn't surprised.

"…Yes, it isn't."

"…You knew?"

"…I knew. You come to know, staying here long enough."

"…Then why didn't anyone tell me?"

The Keeper shrugged.

"…Because no one had asked."

That answer sounded, strangely, exactly right to me. For months I'd walked past that door and never once asked. I hadn't even thought to ask. What had gone unnoticed had only just now, suddenly, become something I noticed — what hadn't been curious had only just now, suddenly, become curious.

"…Have you opened it?" I asked.

"…No."

"…Why not?"

The Keeper's face turned thoughtful for a moment.

"…If you're asking me for a reason I never opened a door that isn't even locked, I don't have a good one myself. I just never opened it."

Hearing that answer put me a little at ease. It wasn't only me.

"…Aren't you curious?" I asked again.

"…I am."

"…But?"

"…Being curious and opening it are two different things, too."

I felt I'd heard that line somewhere before. The Keeper had said something similar, talking about the water channel. That it was fine to leave something, curious. That you could get by fine, even without finding the answer.

I tried, right then, to tie those words together into one thing. The water channel, this door, and all those cases Arche could never close. All of them were things left standing, curious, unresolved. I thought that seemed like some common thread running through this city. Whether that thought was right, I wouldn't learn until much further into this record.

"…What do you think is inside that door, Keeper?" I asked.

The Keeper paused his hands and thought a moment.

"…I don't know. Could be something grand in there. Could be completely empty."

"…What if it's empty?"

"…Then that'd be strange too, in its own way. Nothing there, and not even registered."

I liked that answer. Either way, it was strange. And this city ran along just fine, not knowing which. I found that remarkable.

"…Don't you want to open it? Really." I asked once more.

The Keeper looked at me steadily.

"…Mone. I've worked here more than twenty years. If that were the only door I ever wanted to open, I'd have opened it long ago."

I didn't fully understand what that meant, at the time. I understand it a little now. That door wasn't the only door, in this city, someone might want to open. All those cases that never closed were, in a sense, closed doors too. The Keeper was someone who had lived twenty years among those doors.

That night, at home, I wrote this on paper.

There's a door at the end of the corridor. It isn't in Arche's index. It says it isn't hidden — it just was never registered. It isn't locked. I took hold of the handle, and let go. I don't know why I didn't open it.

I wrote one more line underneath, and left it, without crossing it out. "I don't know why this weighs on me so much." Copying this record out now, I'm leaving that line exactly as it was. I didn't know the answer then, and I still don't know it exactly, writing this chapter.

But I can set down this much, for certain. It wasn't the first time I'd seen that door. I'd already seen it once, at the start. That day I thought it was nothing at all. This time it wasn't. The door was the same. What had changed was me.

After that, every few days, passing that door, I found my steps slowing a little.

Once, I deliberately went back late in the evening, when no one else was around. Some of the corridor lights were off, and it was dim. The door stood the same, even in the dark. I stood before it, saying nothing, and looked a long while.

"…Arche, do you know I'm standing here right now?" I asked.

Your position is confirmed.

"…But you don't know inside the door?"

I do not.

That was, strangely, a comfort. Arche knew me exactly, and yet knew nothing of this one door. Even in a city that seemed to know everything, there was one corner it didn't know. That didn't feel like a bad thing.

Another time, in daylight, I took hold of the handle again. Still not locked. I didn't push that day, either. Instead I turned the handle slightly and counted. Counted to ten. Then let go.

I can't explain this action well, even to myself. Counting to ten didn't change anything. I think I just wanted to stay before that door, that way, a little longer.

Closing this chapter, I picture that door once more.

The unmarked frame, the handle that turned smoothly, the corridor with no sound from inside or out. I end Part One without having opened that door.

This door comes back again, in this record. When, and how, I won't say now. All I can set down now is this. I found out nothing at all, that day, standing before that door. I turned back, having found out nothing. That's all there is to this chapter.
