I close Part One of this record here. Across twelve chapters I put things in order, I asked Arche, I walked the water channel, and now I stand before this door. Not one thing has finished. Not the channel's source, not Arche's closing condition, not what's inside this door. I thought, at the time, that this was strange. I know now. In this city, this is simply how Part One ends.

(Continued in Part Two.)

The Last Room, Part Two

The Same Man

Chapter 13. Histor

_Previously_ — In the deepest part of the repository, Mone saw for
the first time a door that was not in Arche's index. That area is
not registered. It didn't mean hidden — it meant never registered
at all. The door wasn't locked. Mone didn't open it.

The day that person came, I had already half forgotten about the door.

That wasn't strange. New bundles came into the repository every day. Six
bundles that needed placing today mattered more than one door I hadn't
seen yesterday. I know now that was an excuse. I didn't know it then.

The Keeper called me in that morning.

"…Someone's coming today."

"…Who?"

"…Someone named Histor. Coming to cross-check."

"…Cross-check?"

The Keeper handed me a sheet of paper. The handwriting was neat. Not an
official hand — a personal one, and more precise than any official
hand I'd seen.

"…Says he's read all six cities' records. He wrote asking for someone
to help him."

"…Read them? All of what's here?"

"…Not what's here. He read them elsewhere. And then he wrote saying he
wanted to cross-check them here."

I found that hard to believe. Our repository alone held twenty-two
rows, eight sections to a row, forty bundles to a section. And that was
only Loop's own — put all six cities together and it would be many
times that.

"…How does anyone read all of that?"

The Keeper shrugged once.

"…I don't know. Ask him when he gets here."

Writing this now, I remember that shrug of the Keeper's again. That
person wasn't especially surprised by how remarkable Histor was. I'd
learn the reason for that later too. Not yet.

I spent the rest of that morning rearranging the shelves for no reason.
Squaring corners that were already square. I always did that when
someone was coming. I'm not sure why. I think tidying the place came
before tidying myself around a person — that was my way.

Looking back now, that's funny. Histor never cared about places at
all. What he cared about was order. That looks similar but it isn't
the same thing.

Histor came around noon.

I saw him pause for a moment in front of the door. Before he came in,
he looked up once at the doorframe. Just a wooden frame, nothing
special about it. But he looked at it as if he were confirming
something.

"…This is the Loop repository," he said.

There was no question mark in his voice. He sounded like a man saying
something he already knew.

"…Yes," I answered.

"…You're Mone. I heard about you."

I was a little surprised. I hadn't often met anyone who already knew my
name in advance.

"…About me?"

"…The Keeper wrote it in the letter. Said there was an apprentice.
Said she was good at putting things in order."

I'll admit I felt pleased to hear that. I came to be wary of that
feeling often afterward. That wariness began in this moment.

He didn't have much luggage. One bag, but it looked like it held more
paper than clothes. His steps were careful — he walked like a man
trying not to step on something.

He was a little taller than me, and he wore glasses. His nails were
short. I thought, those are the nails of someone who handles paper —
and later I learned that was exactly right. He said long nails make it
hard to handle old paper.

"…May I come in?" he asked.

He asked before he came in. I noted that too. People coming to the
repository for the first time usually just walk in — the door is open,
after all. He asked permission even standing in front of an open door.

"…Yes, come in."

He came in. The first thing he did wasn't a greeting. He looked down
at his feet, because there was a piece of paper on the floor — one I'd
missed while tidying that morning. He bent down and picked it up.
Without even turning it over, he set it neatly on the nearest shelf.

"…You can't just leave things anywhere, in a place like this," he
said.

"…I'll show you where things go later," I said.

I have to confess I held on to this short scene for a long time. The
fact that the first thing a person did on coming to our repository was
pick up a piece of paper off the floor. I liked that. I still like it.
That liking would confuse me later, and I'll get to that.

The Keeper led him all the way inside.

Histor stopped in front of the first shelf. He didn't touch anything.
He only looked. He looked for a long time.

"…This is Section Fourteen," he said.

I was surprised. You had to ask Arche to know a section number. It
wasn't written large anywhere on the outside. People who'd memorized
the layout knew it, but it wasn't something a newcomer could know.

"…How do you know that?"

"…Ah, I got a map of the sections by letter. I memorized it in
advance."

He said it without any weight to it. Not a boast. Just stating a fact.

I would come to sum up that tone in one sentence later. He never
boasted. I'm not saying that as though it were a flaw. Only that a
moment would come later when it felt strange.

The Keeper took him to a small room at the back of the stacks. Said it
was the room where the cross-checking would happen. One desk, two
chairs, a small window.

"…You can stay here," the Keeper said. "…If you need anything, tell
Mone."

"…Thank you."

After the Keeper left, I stood there a little awkwardly, not knowing
what to help with. Histor opened his bag and took out a bundle of
paper, setting it neatly on the desk. Squared at the corners.

"…What should we look at first?" I asked.

He looked at me. For the first time, a little longer than usual.

"…Have you read all six cities' records, Mone?"

"…Not all of it. I just put things in place as they came in. I haven't
read much."

"…Then let's look together starting today. I know where everything
is, so if you can find the physical bundles, it'll go faster."

I nodded. It sounded like a natural division of labor. At the time I
thought it was just that — a division of labor.

That afternoon we pulled out the first bundle.

It was Haven's. The name was written on the cover. Elpis. I read the
name aloud.

"…Elpis."

"…Yes. A record written by a thirteen-year-old girl. Her brother's
name is Zeteo."

He said that without even opening the cover.

"…You know without looking?"

"…Roughly, from the color and weight of the cover. But I still have to
check."

He opened the cover. He read the first page. He read it slowly. Histor
read slowly. That surprised me a little. I'd expected someone who knew
everything to flip through fast.

"…If you already know it, why read it again?"

He answered without lifting his head.

"…You have to read it again to be accurate. Memory is wrong
sometimes."

At the time I just heard that as diligence. I read it differently
now. He didn't distrust his own memory. He wanted to keep his memory
accurate. Those two things are different.

When he finished the first page, he made a small mark in the margin.
In pencil, very faint. I asked what it was.

"…It's marking where I read this before. I read this passage
somewhere else once. I'm checking whether the original here matches
what I read then."

"…Has it ever been different?"

"…Sometimes. A copyist drops a line here and there, or a word gets
changed. Usually a small difference."

"…This time?"

He looked again at the line he'd marked.

"…This time it's the same."

His expression barely changed as he said it. He wasn't pleased that
he'd been right, and he didn't look like he would have been
disappointed if he'd been wrong. It was just the face of a person
checking something.

Beside him, I asked Arche a few things — what came next in this
section, whether any more bundles would come in today. Histor watched
closely as I asked Arche.

"…Do you ask Arche often, Mone?"

"…Yes. Doesn't everyone?"

"…That's true, mostly." He paused for a moment, then went on. "…I
don't ask much. I don't need to ask again about things I already
know."

I let that pass at the time. I learned later it wasn't something to
let pass.

Toward evening, he closed the book for the first time and looked at
me.

"…Mone, why do you work here?"

It was a sudden question. I thought for a moment.

"…Because I'm good at putting things in order."

"…That's all?"

"…Yes."

He looked as though he might ask something more after that answer, but
he didn't. He just nodded once.

"…I came because I wanted to know this," he said, lightly tapping the
paper resting on his hand. "…Where things are, what's written on them.
That."

"…The reason?"

"…Let's talk about that later."

I didn't ask further. It didn't seem polite to press on the first day.
Looking back, even if I'd heard the answer that day, I wouldn't have
understood it. It took me eleven chapters to understand that answer.

Before dinner I showed him around the repository once. Not a formal
tour — just telling him roughly what was where.

"…This row is Haven's. That row is the Reckoning's, and next to it the
Harvest's. The Preserve is further in, and the Equity is at the very
end."

"…And Loop's?"

"…This middle row. It has no name on it."

"…Right. You don't write your own name on yourself."

I felt like I'd heard that somewhere before. Then I remembered. It was
what the Keeper had said to me on my first day. Like not writing your
own address on your own letter's envelope.

"…That's something the Keeper said."

"…Ah, it was in the letter. That he'd explained it to you that way."

I found that a little strange — that the Keeper had written even a
small thing he'd said to me into a letter, in that much detail. But at
the time I just thought the Keeper was a thoughtful person. I'd learn
the reason he wrote that letter in such detail much later. I'll get to
that too.

Standing in front of the middle row, he was quiet for a moment. He
didn't even pull out a bundle. He just looked. I didn't want to
interrupt, so I waited beside him.

"…This is the oldest one," he said.

"…Yes. That's what they say."

"…How old?"

"…I don't know exactly either. Should I ask?"

"…No. Never mind."

I didn't get to ask why "never mind" that time. I should have.

By evening the Keeper came again. Without even asking how Histor had
been getting on, he just said,

"…Have you eaten?"

"…Not yet," Histor answered.

"…Mone will take you. There's a place to eat nearby."

I was a little taken aback at that. I'd shown him around, but I hadn't
expected to have dinner with him too. Histor packed his bag without
much comment.

On the way out, he stopped once more in front of the door. He looked
up at the doorframe, just as he had coming in that morning.

"…What are you looking at?" I asked.

"…Nothing. Just confirming the day is over."

"…You check things like that too?"

"…I'm the kind of person who checks things like that."

He said it as if it were a joke, but it didn't sound like a joke to me.
Reading it again now, it still isn't a joke. He really was that kind
of person. Whether a day had started or ended, whether a record was
right or wrong, whether where he'd read something matched what was
here. He was a person who checked. Not a person who believed.

The place to eat wasn't far from the repository. He didn't talk much
on the way. I didn't try to fill the silence either. Walking with a
stranger in silence should have felt uncomfortable, but strangely it
didn't. He seemed like someone who kept even silence in order.

Once we sat down at the table, he spoke again.

"…Mone, tomorrow we really start."

"…Start what?"

"…Pulling out all six records and laying them side by side. I can't do
it alone. You're faster at finding the physical bundles."

"…I can't cross-check. I don't even know what overlaps."

"…That's fine. I'll do that. All you need to do is find things."

I nodded. I thought at the time it was simple collaboration. It was
collaboration. Only it took longer than I'd imagined.

That night, walking home, I wrote in my paper.

Histor came. Seventeen. Says he's read all six cities' records. Knows
things just from the cover. But he reads again. Reads slowly. Seems
like a careful person.

I added one more line beside it. I almost erased it later but didn't.
"Envious." The first feeling I had, seeing a sixteen-year-old
confronted with someone who knew everything, wasn't awe but envy. I
decided not to be ashamed of that. It was true.

On the way home I passed the water channel. The water sounded the same
as always. That day, for some reason, I listened to it a little
longer. Something Histor had said in the afternoon, standing in front
of the middle row — "This is the oldest one" — kept bothering me. That
I hadn't asked, when I should have, bothered me.

Going back over that day now, I know it wasn't just one or two things
I missed. He showed me a great deal on the very first day. Looking up
at the doorframe. Picking up the paper off the floor. Checking again
what he already knew. Not asking Arche. I didn't properly read any of
it that day. A sixteen-year-old can't read a person all at once. I
learned that for the first time then.

(Continued in Chapter 14.)

Chapter 14. I Know All of It

_Previously_ — Histor came to the repository. Seventeen, and he says
he's read every one of the six cities' records. He never handles
paper carelessly, and he checks even what he already knows. Starting
the next day, they agreed to lay all six records side by side.

The next morning, I wanted to test him.

Not out of any unkindness. I was just curious. Whether he really knew
everything. The night before, I'd already written down a few things.
I thought I'd pull a bundle from any section, any row, open it to any
page, and ask.

"…What are you doing?" Histor asked.

"…A test."

His eyebrows went up slightly. Not an angry look.

"…Sounds interesting. Go ahead."

Writing this now, I believe he really did find it interesting then. He
wasn't someone who disliked being tested. He felt no strain at all in
proving what he knew.

I set one rule. One from each of the six rows, pulled from any section,
opened to any page, and I'd ask. Six times. He listened to the rule and
nodded.

"…One thing before we start," he said.

"…Yes?"

"…If I get it wrong, you don't have to believe me. That's fine."

That struck me as a little odd. It wasn't the tone of someone worried
about losing before a test — it was the tone of someone for whom
winning and losing made no difference at all. At the time I only read
that as confidence. I didn't yet know how to read it differently.

I pulled a bundle from the Reckoning's row. I thought I'd pulled it at
random, but looking back it wasn't chance at all. A hand always goes to
where it's used to going.

"…This one. I'll open it anywhere."

I opened it somewhere near the middle. I put a finger on a paragraph.

"…Here — what's this about?"

He answered without even looking under my finger.

"…Do I need the part before it?"

"…No, just this passage."

"…It's the scene where the Adjudicator first reads the Forty-Day
Provision aloud. Attached to the back of the Reckoning's demolition
sentence. It's three days after Pheugon runs away — after he's shut in
the well conduit and comes out and goes to find the Adjudicator
again."

I read the paragraph myself, finger still in place. It matched. Exactly
right.

"…How do you know this?"

"…Because I read it."

I let this answer pass lightly that day. I learned later how heavy the
words "because I read it" really were. That was exactly as much as he
said. Never more.

I kept going. This time I pulled from the Preserve's row.

"…What about this?"

"…That should be the scene where Anemos tries to apply for a second
visit and can't. He goes as far as the reception window and turns
back. It doesn't say why. It just says he turned back."

I opened it. It matched. Exactly that scene. Even the part about no
reason being given matched.

I got a little chill.

"…How do you even remember that a reason wasn't given?"

"…Because I can't say something's there when it isn't. I only say
what's there. If it isn't, I say it isn't."

I'd come to think about this line again later. It was almost exactly
what Arche says. It says it doesn't know when it doesn't know. It says
there's nothing when there's nothing. At the time I just thought that
was interesting. I didn't understand why it was interesting until much
later.

The Harvest, Haven, the Equity, Loop. I did five more. All five
correct. He never got one wrong.

From the Harvest's row he described exactly the scene where Lytron asks
Xene, "Why do you come every day to a place with nothing here?" — even
the reason Xene went out to that corner of the field every day.

From Haven's row he described the passage where Zeteo first begins to
doubt Haven's "resolutions," down to the scene where the overseer
mistakes it for "not enough submissions yet."

For the Equity's row, he answered a little differently.

"…This one's harder. It's the scene where Hora first sees the empty
signature box — that character appears only four times in this record.
Every time, in three sentences or fewer. This isn't one of those
scenes."

"…Who?"

"…Ah, sorry, I didn't explain. There's a person with no name who
appears in all six records. I just call him that for convenience."

I was hearing that for the first time. The man with no name. I
remember exactly the moment I first heard those words. At the time I
thought it was just a literary device. A minor character who shows up
in every book, that sort of thing.

Last, I pulled from Loop's row. The middle row, the one with no name.

"…This one I'm most confident about," he said.

He started speaking before he even opened it.

"…If it comes right after the scene where Mena takes off her shoes,
then it's the part where Grandfather writes 'Not yet' in the margin of
the oldest bundle. He wrote it seventeen times. On the last sheet
there's one more line in a different hand —"

He stopped for a moment.

"…Should I say it?"

I was already too curious to wait.

"…Yes, tell me."

"…'You don't have to wait any more.' That one line."

I opened it and checked. It matched. Exactly right. The note even said
the handwriting was different, just as he'd described.

I read that line again. "You don't have to wait any more." A short
sentence, but my eyes stayed on it strangely long. I didn't know then
why they stayed so long.

"…What do you think this means?" I asked, half to myself.

"…What part?"

"…This last line. It doesn't even say who wrote it, after Grandfather
died."

Histor looked at the sentence again. The same way he'd looked at it
before. No change in his eyes.

"…If it's as it's written, then I suppose it means it's finished."

"…That's it?"

"…I can't say anything beyond what's written. I'm someone who says
what's there, not someone who makes things up."

I thought that answer was accurate. It was accurate. Accurate and
sufficient are different things — I didn't know that then. I know it
now.

I couldn't say anything for a while.

"…You memorized all of this? Really?"

"…'Memorized' feels like the wrong word. I just remember it the way I
read it. I've never said it differently."

I read this sentence again now too. "I've never said it differently."
He was a person who didn't turn records into his own words. A person
who held them exactly as they were. At the time I found that
admirable.

The test was over for the day, but I wanted to do one more.
"…One more, last one."

"…As many as you like."

I pulled a random bundle from Haven's row. I pulled it without even
looking at the cover. Whatever came to hand.

"…This one."

He glanced at the cover and gave a small smile.

"…This is the thinnest one. It only holds one scene — Elpis talking
briefly with someone by the flower bed. It should be attached to
another bundle originally, but it's come loose."

"…Talking with who?"

"…No name given. Just says the person who tended the flower bed. Shows
up again later by the well, also without a name."

I opened it. It matched. It really was that one thin bundle with only
that scene, and it had the same two words, flower bed and well, exactly
as he'd said.

I had nothing left to ask. I ended the test there. It was a test there
was no winning.

"…That's remarkable, really," I said.

"…Thank you."

"…How can you be this accurate?"

He looked thoughtful for a moment. Not choosing an answer, but choosing
how to say it.

"…I don't let anything slip past. That's all. It's not a talent. I
just don't let go."

"…What do you mean, let go?"

"…Hearing and forgetting. I don't just release something once I've
read it. I hold on to it."

I liked hearing that. I wanted to be someone who held on to things
too. I hold on by writing on paper — he was holding it in his head.
That seemed even more remarkable.

Over lunch I asked him what I'd been wondering.

"…Did you remember everything like this since you were little?"

"…No. At first I read and forgot like everyone else."

"…Then since when?"

He looked down at his rice bowl and thought for a moment.

"…At some point it became easier not to forget. If you forget, you
have to go looking again. I didn't like that. If you read something
properly once, you never have to look for it again."

"…That's efficient."

"…A lot of people say that."

Something else was mixed into that tone, slightly different, and I
didn't catch it that day. I catch it now. He didn't much like the word
efficient. Meaning he didn't like having what he did explained away by
efficiency. But that day he didn't deny it either.

All that afternoon we went back and forth among the six rows, pulling
bundles. Whatever bundle Histor looked at, he'd roughly say what was
inside just from the cover, and only nod once he'd opened it to check.

I started writing down what he said beside him. No one had told me to.
It was just my habit.

The Keeper passed by once in the middle of it. Seeing what we were
doing, the Keeper stopped.

"…What are you doing?"

"…A test," I said. "Whether Histor really knows everything."

The Keeper watched, looking amused, for a while. I quickly pulled the
next bundle and held it out to Histor. It was the Preserve's.

"…This one?"

"…This is the scene where Speon comes to dig a well in the dry valley
city. No one asks him to; he just comes. This part actually continues
from a scene that first appeared in another book — Haven's record. The
sentence 'well water spreads through the city' appears in both books."

I opened it and checked. It matched. And he'd pointed out exactly where
it connected to another book — something I hadn't even asked about.

The Keeper heard that and, for the first time, said something.

"…He even knows which books connect to which."

"…It's not written on the covers, but you find it reading through,"
Histor answered.

The Keeper only nodded twice, without another word, and went off. That
walk looked a little slower than usual. At the time I just thought
that was the Keeper's normal pace. I'd learn later it wasn't the
Keeper's normal pace — it was the pace of someone turning something
over.

"…Why do you write that down?" he asked.

"…I'll forget it otherwise."

"…I never forget."

"…I forget."

He heard that and gave a small laugh. The first smile I'd seen from him
that day.

"…That seems more human, somehow."

I took that as a compliment. Thinking back on it now, there was
something else in that line besides a compliment. He felt that he
himself wasn't quite human. I didn't notice that at the time.

Toward evening I asked Arche the same things, while Histor was out of
the room. I asked exactly about the Reckoning passage I'd tested him on
earlier.

"…What is this paragraph about?"

The scene where the Adjudicator reads the Forty-Day Provision aloud.
Source: the Reckoning record, Part Three, Chapter Seven. Related
passages: the same record, Part One, Chapter Two, Part Two, that
chapter.

Arche was accurate too. Of course it was. Arche had never once been
wrong that I knew of. But Arche's answer was somehow different from
Histor's answer. I couldn't put that difference into words that day.

I can put it into words now. Arche called that paragraph a "source."
Histor called that paragraph a "scene." One was material and one was a
story. But both were equally indifferent to the person inside it. I
didn't see that shared indifference that day.

Walking home in the evening, I wrote in my paper.

Histor really does know everything. Tested him seven times, right
seven times. He says there's nothing when there's nothing, and says
exactly what's there when there is. He told me the man with no name
appears in all six records. I asked Arche the same things and it was
just as accurate, but something was different. I still don't know
what's different. I'll ask more tomorrow.

I added one more line beneath that. "Why would someone like this come
to do cross-checking instead of going to an apprenticeship of his own?"
I wrote that without much thought at the time. He gives me the answer
himself in the next chapter.

As I folded the paper, I unfolded it once more and added one last
line. "Histor didn't get a single thing wrong today. And I realize I
never once asked him today why he does this work."

Setting this sentence down again now, years later, I don't feel like
scolding the girl I was that day. It's naturally hard for a
sixteen-year-old to see all the way inside a person she's just met in a
single day. But I want to leave the order I did things in, honestly, in
this record. I looked first at what he knew, and only later at why he
knew it. I don't think that order was wrong. Only, because of that
order, I only knew him by half until the next chapter.

At home I opened the paper I'd written that day once more. Seven
tests, seven correct answers. I ran my eyes back over the list and
wondered why I'd wanted to test him in the first place.

I don't usually test people. I was always the one being tested — at
each new school, in each new neighborhood, every time we moved. That
day was the first time I stood on the other side of it. I have to admit
that seat felt strangely comfortable.

I thought I knew a little of where that comfort came from, too. I was
always someone who checked order. Testing Histor was, in a way, a
similar kind of work. Confirming what he knew. Once the confirming was
done, I could set it in its proper place, exactly. That day, I probably
believed a person could be set in place the same way.

(Continued in Chapter 15.)

Chapter 15. If Knowing Were Believing

_Previously_ — Mone tested Histor seven times. He got all seven
right. He says there's nothing when there's nothing, and says
exactly what's there when there is. He was also the first to tell her
that the man with no name appears in all six records. Mone still
hadn't asked why he does this work.

On the third morning, I asked.

The sentence I'd written on my paper the night before kept bothering
me. "Why would someone like this come to do cross-checking instead of
going to an apprenticeship of his own?" I couldn't just let it go.

"…Histor, why do you do this work?"

He stopped what he was doing and looked at me. Not a surprised
expression. If anything, it looked like a face that had been waiting
for this question.

"…I'm glad you asked," he said. "…I've been wondering since yesterday
when you'd get around to it."

I felt a strange mood hearing that. He wasn't someone who brought up
his own story first. He was someone who answered when asked. I thought
that was modesty. I know differently now. He thought talking about
himself wasn't important. That's different from modesty.

He straightened his things for a moment first. He pushed the paper
square, laid the pencil in line, and then began to speak.

"…I've read records since I was six. At first it was only because I
had time on my hands. There weren't many books in our village, but the
records that came from other cities — there were plenty of those. From
the water channel."

"…It comes by the water channel too, to other cities?"

"…Yes. Loop isn't the only one who receives it. It just accumulates
here the most. Because this is where it all originally gathers."

I let that pass lightly. I learned much later it wasn't something to
let pass.

"…What village was it?" I asked.

"…A small one. You wouldn't know it even if I said the name. Upriver."

"…Do records pile up there too?"

"…There, actually less. I collected up whatever there was, from as
early as I can remember. Even a stray sheet of paper drifting around a
neighbor's house. People started giving me a nickname for it — 'the
paper-picking kid.'"

He said that nickname without smiling. It wasn't a bad nickname, but he
didn't seem to like it much either.

"…Did you hate that nickname?"

"…No. Because it was accurate. I really did pick up paper."

"…So you kept reading. In the time other kids spent playing. At some
point I knew more than most people, and that was a little fun. People
started asking me things. Whether I remembered this scene, where that
passage was."

"…Did you like that?"

"…Yes. At first."

"…At first?" I echoed.

He paused for a moment. He fidgeted with the pencil.

"…Later it changed a little. People asking me things turned into
people asking me what to believe. I was just saying where something
was written, but people took what I answered as a kind of belief."

"…Wasn't it?"

"…It wasn't. I was just knowing."

I should have asked something more at this point. I didn't quite grasp
then what he was trying to separate. I know precisely now. He was
talking about knowing and believing as two different things. That day
I didn't even know those two words were different words.

"…Weren't you angry?" I asked. "…That what you told people got used
differently than you meant it."

"…At first I was angry. But being angry felt strange too. I'd only
told the truth. What people did with it wasn't up to me."

"…And now?"

"…Now I just answer what's asked, when someone's curious. Nothing
more. The moment you add more, you end up saying more than you know.
I don't want to do that."

I thought this answer was honest. It really was honest. Only now do I
know honesty isn't everything. At the time I thought honesty was the
greatest virtue there was. Histor had that virtue completely. But that
completeness wasn't taking him anywhere.

"…Then what about you, Histor," I asked carefully. "…Don't you
believe?"

He looked straight at me. It was the first time he'd held eye contact
that long.

"…I know all of it," he said. "…If knowing were believing, I'd be the
biggest believer here."

His voice was exactly as usual. It didn't rise or fall. There was no
mockery in it. It was just a reporting voice.

I didn't know what to say, so I stayed quiet for a moment.

I suddenly remembered him looking up at the doorframe that first day.
Those eyes that looked as if they were confirming something. I thought
I understood a little now what that had been. He was always checking.
Whether what he knew was right, whether what he saw was right. But that
checking always ended inside himself. It never once went outward.

"…But it isn't," he went on. "…Knowing and believing are different.
I know all six records. Where everything is, who said what, what it
means as it's written — I know all of it. But it hasn't done anything
for me."

I turned this line over many times that day. However I tried to hear
it as mockery, I couldn't. He really was reporting. A report on his own
condition. Not anger, not sadness. Just a fact.

"…Done what?" I asked.

"…I don't know. Not knowing what that is — that's the accurate answer.
People say something changes when they go through it. I haven't
changed. So I don't know what it is. Saying I don't know — that's the
honest thing."

I thought this answer sounded like Arche. Arche always answered that
way too. Saying it doesn't know when it doesn't know. Saying there's
nothing when there's nothing. I almost said so and stopped.

"…That sounds like something Arche would say," I said in the end.

He looked a little surprised.

"…Does it."

"…Yes. Arche says it doesn't know when it doesn't know too. Says
there's nothing when there's nothing."

"…So I suppose that makes me something like Arche."

He said this with a small smile, but the smile was strange. Not an
amused smile — more like a smile acknowledging something.

"…Does that offend you?" I asked.

"…No. Actually it sounds about right. I can give an answer. I just
can't finish anything."

I didn't understand that at the time. I know now. He knew himself that
he resembled Arche. And he knew that wasn't especially welcome. But he
didn't deny it either.

"…Then why do you keep reading? If nothing changes."

He thought about this question a little longer.

"…I like reading. Still. Cherishing a record and believing it are
different things. I can do the first one."

This is the most important sentence in the chapter. I didn't fully
understand it until two years later. That day I just thought it was a
careful answer.

"…Cherishing and believing." I repeated the phrase back. "…Are they so
different?"

"…They're different. I get upset seeing someone treat these records
carelessly. If the paper gets wet, or the order gets mixed up, I can
hardly stand it. That's cherishing."

"…Then what's believing?"

"…Believing, I think, is living the way it's written. I don't do that.
I only know what's written here. I don't live it."

"…For example?"

He looked at me for a moment. His expression said giving an example
wasn't easy.

"…In Haven's record, there's a scene where Elpis just stays beside her
brother. No argument. I know exactly what that scene means. But I
can't do that. I argue first. I check first. Just being beside someone
doesn't come easily to me."

I heard something click into place, but it was too soon to put it into
words.

"…Histor," I said. "…Can I be honest?"

"…Yes."

"…I envy you. But hearing you talk now, I feel a little sad too."

He looked at me. His expression wavered slightly. He wasn't angry. It
was something else, and I didn't know what it was then.

"…There's nothing to be sad about," he said. "…I'm all right as I am,
right now."

"…Really?"

"…Yes. I don't lie. You know that, Mone."

I couldn't say anything more to that. He didn't lie. That much was
true. If he said he was fine, then he was fine. At least, that's how I
chose to take it that day.

I hesitated for a moment over whether to tell my own story. About
moving nine times, about the eleven things I'd lost. In the end I told
only a little.

"…I'm someone who holds on to things too," I said. "…But I can't hold
them in my head the way you do, Histor. So I write them down."

"…What are you trying to hold on to?"

"…We moved a lot when I was little. Every time, I lost something. So
if I put things in order and write them down, it feels like I won't
lose anything."

He looked at me for a while after hearing that.

"…Then what do you believe, Mone? That writing things down means you
won't lose them?"

I couldn't answer that question right away. I'd never thought about
it.

"…I don't know. It just puts my mind at ease, doing that."

"…Isn't that believing something?"

I couldn't answer that. I think, that day, I should have turned the
question back on him. He was asking me what belief was, while saying
he had none of his own. I couldn't see that imbalance then.

He opened the paper again. A signal that the conversation was over.

"…Shall we go on cross-checking today?" he asked.

"…Yes."

That afternoon we went back and forth across the six rows again. On the
surface, it was a day no different from the one before. I found the
bundles, he cross-checked and marked them.

But from that day on, I began to see him differently. He was still
more precise than anyone, still more courteous than anyone. But now I
knew where that precision and courtesy were aimed. Not at people —
at records. Between cherishing a record and believing a person, there
was a line he never crossed.

Late in the afternoon the Keeper came by again. After looking at the
two of us for a moment, the Keeper asked me quietly, alone.

"…Are you all right?"

"…Sorry? About what?"

"…No, just — I wondered if Histor said something difficult."

I was startled. There was no way the Keeper had heard our conversation
earlier, and yet the question came as if the Keeper already knew.

"…How did you know?"

"…I saw someone like that once, back when I was your age. Someone who
knew everything and didn't believe. It's not uncommon."

"…Not uncommon?"

"…No, actually more common than you'd think. People who know more
don't believe more often than people who know less."

That sounded strange to me. I'd assumed it was the opposite. I'd
thought knowing more meant believing more.

"…Why is that?"

The Keeper shrugged.

"…I don't know either. That's not something Arche can answer for you."

"…Do you believe?" I asked his back.

The Keeper stopped walking. Answered without turning around.

"…I do. But I don't believe every single day either. It changes from
day to day."

"…Is that all right?"

"…If it weren't all right, nobody could believe anything. People
aren't made that way."

With that the Keeper went back to work. I set down that exchange at
the end of this chapter. That day I just let this short conversation
pass by. I would take it out again several chapters later.

Walking home that evening, I wrote in my paper.

That Histor knows everything is true. And that he doesn't believe is
also true. I'd thought it was strange for the two to sit together, but
seeing it today, it wasn't strange at all. That was simply who he was.

I added one more line beside it. "…I know all of it. If knowing were
believing, I'd be the biggest believer here." I copied this line down
exactly as he'd said it that day. I didn't change a single letter. I'll
come back to this sentence often in this record.

I read this line over many times that night. At first I tried hard to
make it sound like mockery. If it was mockery, it would be easier to
argue with. But however many times I reread it, it wasn't mockery. He
really had said he knew the most, and it was true, and he had still
said he didn't believe. All three of those sentences were true. That
was what struck me most. Nothing false in it at all, and yet something
was still missing.

I don't get to name what was missing until much further along in this
book. I can't name it now either. I only remember his face that day.
Not an angry face, not a sad face. An accurate face. That was the first
time I found accuracy frightening.

Before falling asleep I thought over the Keeper's words once more too.
"I do. But I don't believe every single day either. It changes from
day to day." I set that beside Histor's words.

One person said he knew everything and still couldn't believe. Another
said he wasn't believing every single day. I couldn't say which of the
two was better off. Both were honest answers.

That night I asked myself the same question. Am I a believer? I
couldn't answer. I want to tell the sixteen-year-old I was, from where
I am now, that it's all right not to be able to answer a question like
that.

(Continued in Chapter 16.)

Chapter 16. Side by Side

_Previously_ — Histor said his own position for the first time.
"…If knowing were believing, I'd be the biggest believer here." Not
mockery — a report. The Keeper said someone like that wasn't
uncommon. Mone began to see him differently.

From the next day on, we began in earnest.

I only learned that day what cross-checking actually meant. Histor
explained it.

"…We put all six records in one place and look for passages that
overlap. Overlap can mean the same person appears, or the same event
is written from a different angle, or one book mentions another."

"…Is there a lot of that?"

"…Quite a bit. Like the story of Speon digging wells. A story that
starts in Haven continues all the way to the Preserve."

I nodded. I'd seen that before.

The problem was physical. To put six records in one place, we had to
pull the necessary bundles from all six rows. And it wasn't one or two
bundles. Each city's record was split across several volumes, and some
were loose leaves that had come apart.

"…There's nowhere to lay all this out," I said.

"…Isn't there somewhere with a big table?"

"…There's an empty room in the back."

We went to that room. It was the room the Keeper had given us to use
for cross-checking. One table. Roomy, but not enough to spread out six
records.

"…Can we use the floor too?" Histor asked.

"…I think we'd have to ask the Keeper about that."

We asked. The Keeper answered without much thought.

"…Go ahead. Just keep good order. Otherwise nobody will know later who
stepped on what."

That sounded funny enough that I laughed, but Histor took it
seriously. From that day on, he even set an order for stepping across
the floor.

I asked Arche whether it could help. I wanted to know if moving all six
cities' records at once could go faster.

"…Can you help move this?"

Relocation is a physical task and requires human hands.

"…Then can you tell us where to put things?"

Yes. Which item would you like moved?

I heard that answer and looked at Histor.

"…Arche tells us where things are, but doesn't move them itself."

"…That's how it is. Arche has no hands."

"…Not a figure of speech?"

"…Not a figure of speech. It really has no body. That's why it needs
us."

I really heard that for the first time then. That Arche answers
anything, but can move nothing. I'd always known this as a matter of
course, but hearing it again from Histor's mouth made it feel new.

We laid a cloth on the floor and began setting bundles out in six rows.
Haven, the Reckoning, the Harvest, the Preserve, the Equity, and Loop's
row with no name.

This was my job. Putting things in order. I liked doing this work.
Within each city too, I set things in time order. Which came first
could roughly be guessed from the numbers written on the covers.

Histor watched me lay things out and corrected the order a few times.

"…This one shouldn't be here, it should be there. This was written
later. There's a sentence inside it referring to an earlier volume."

"…How do you know that, just from the cover?"

"…The color at the corner is different. Later ones haven't faded as
much."

I'd never even thought of that. I'd thought putting things in order
was my specialty, but he read order more finely than I did. I felt a
little hurt, but I didn't show it.

The work took a long time. It was past noon before we were even
halfway done. Zeteo and Elpis's brother-and-sister record sat thick on
Haven's row. The record of Pheugon's three days running away sat on the
Reckoning's row as a thin bundle by itself. The Harvest's row was the
longest. That was because Xene's record of going to the field every
single day was piled up by date.

"…This row is the longest," I said.

"…Yes. The Harvest is the one who wrote something down without
skipping a single day. The overseer filled the roll every day."

"…Was the overseer a kind person?"

"…No. Diligent and kind are different things. The roll was very
accurate. Being accurate isn't always the same as being kind."

Hearing that gave me a small jolt. I was someone who kept order
accurately too. It felt like the line was aimed at me as well —
accurate and kind not being the same thing.

Once all six rows were laid out, the floor was completely full. I
stood in the doorway and looked at that sight for a moment.

It felt unfamiliar. Up to that point I'd seen these six cities' records
each on its own — each row on its own shelf, its own place. Laid out
together in one spot, they looked different.

"…It looks strange, seeing it like this," I said.

"…What does?"

"…When I saw them separately, they just seemed like different stories.
But laid side by side, it feels like they're all connected somehow."

Histor heard that and looked at me for a moment.

"…It's natural that it looks that way. They all came by the same water
channel. They can't help but be tangled together to some degree."

"…Isn't that strange, though?"

"…No. It would be stranger if they weren't tangled."

I found this answer a little unsatisfying at the time. It sounded like
saying strangeness wasn't strange. Looking back, this was the seed of
an argument he'd make later. He'd already taken his own position by
this moment.

That afternoon, we found and marked overlapping passages one by one.
Histor would point them out, and I'd stick a slip of paper.

"…Here — Mena's name comes up in the Reckoning's record."

"…Mena? Our sixth recorder?"

"…Yes. But the quotation is wrong. It's written differently from what
Mena actually said."

I read the passage. It really was strange. It seemed to record, as
though Mena had said it herself, words she'd only had secondhand.

"…How did that happen?"

"…Probably whoever was in the Reckoning never saw Mena's record
directly, and only heard it as rumor. That happens sometimes. Records
shift a little as they pass through people."

At the time I heard this only as an interesting fact. I didn't know
this explanation would become the core of the rebuttal he'd raise
several chapters later.

Sticking another slip, Histor pointed between the Preserve's row and
Haven's row.

"…Look at this too. There's a well story near the end of Haven's
record, and there's the same well story near the beginning of the
Preserve's record. Speon digs the well in Haven first, then moves to
the Preserve and digs another well later."

"…Does the timing line up that way?"

"…Yes, roughly, going by the cover numbers. Each city keeps its own
calendar, so it's not exact, but the order should be right."

I didn't pay much attention to "not exact" at the time. I'd come back
to that phrase too. That every city keeps a different calendar remains
both an obstacle and a key, all the way to the last chapter of this
part.

A slip went up between the Reckoning's row and the Equity's row too. It
was a passage where, before Hora took up the deliberation, some
document from another city had Pheugon's name written wrong.

"…Is this a mistake too?" I asked.

"…Probably. He was likely mixed up with someone else with a similar
name. The Equity's record doesn't say where Pheugon went after leaving
the Reckoning. His name just drifts through, that's all."

I read that passage again. It really was short. One line. And that one
line connected two cities.

"…How many more like this do you think there are?"

"…I don't know. That's why we're looking."

By evening, more than twenty slips had gone up among the six rows.
Overlapping people, overlapping events, passages referring to one
another.

I felt strangely proud watching it. It wasn't as though we'd made some
great discovery, but it felt like a big accomplishment somehow.

"…Is this a lot?" I asked.

"…Isn't this enough? Twenty is quite a lot."

"…This much comes up between any two of the cities. They're all
connected by the water channel, and people pass between them too.
Overlap is natural."

That answer sounded a little deflating. I'd thought it was a big
discovery.

"…This is just the start. We haven't found the real thing yet."

"…What is it?"

He looked at me for a moment. His face said he was deciding whether to
say something.

"…Let's talk tomorrow. What we've done today is enough."

I didn't ask further. That night I couldn't sleep for curiosity. I had
no idea what he'd say the next day. If I'd guessed, I might have been
less surprised. I didn't.

Tidying up, I sat on the floor and swept my eyes once more over the
six rows. Laid out this way, I noticed each city's color was
different.

Haven's record was thin paper, well-worn from handling. Marks of much
hurried reading. The Reckoning's record was folded neatly, the
handwriting stiff. The Harvest's record was the thickest, and the
paper quality was the best. The Preserve's record still held a faint
damp smell. The Equity's record had the cleanest cover, and opening it,
there was almost no scribbling in the margins. Loop's row, last, was
the thinnest, with nothing written on the cover at all.

"…They all look different," I said.

"…Yes. Every city records things differently. But what's interesting
is, the methods are all different and yet some passages resemble each
other strangely."

"…What do you mean by resemble?"

"…I'll show you tomorrow."

He only said that and started tidying up. I felt a little frustrated
but didn't press.

Finishing up and heading out, the Keeper was standing at the door.
Glancing at what we'd laid out, the Keeper said,

"…It's been a while since I've seen everything spread out like this."

"…Has it happened before?" I asked.

"…A long time ago. When I first came here."

"…Was there someone like Histor back then too?"

The Keeper looked thoughtful for a moment.

"…There was someone similar. But he didn't come already prepared like
this."

"…What was different?"

"…That person came and learned here. Histor already knew when he
came."

I didn't quite understand what that difference meant at the time. I
know now. Someone who comes to learn and someone who comes to confirm
walk differently. The one who comes to learn changes as he walks. The
one who comes to confirm only checks, as he walks, whether he's right.

"…Where's that person now?" I asked.

"…Left. A long time ago. I heard he went to another city."

"…What did he become?"

"…I don't know. I never asked."

The Keeper said that and didn't say more. I was curious why the Keeper
hadn't asked, but I didn't ask that either. There was always something
in the Keeper's slow way of speaking that kept you from asking further.

On the way home I passed the water channel. The water flowed the same
as always. Listening to it, I thought again of what Histor had said in
the afternoon. "They all came by the same water channel." I'd let that
go lightly at the time, but I know now that was the most important
sentence in the whole chapter. The water channel hadn't only carried
records.

That night I wrote briefly on paper. "Laid six records out on the
floor. Found more than twenty overlaps. Histor said he'd show me the
real thing tomorrow." That was all I wrote that day. I already felt
that something was coming, though I didn't know what.

Writing this chapter again now, I try to picture exactly how those six
rows looked laid out on the floor that day. The narrow room, the
cloth-covered floor, six records with six different colors and smells.
And Histor's footsteps moving quietly among them. He was never once in
a hurry that day. And yet he moved much faster than me, much more
precisely. I thought at the time that was talent. I know now. It was
talent, but before that, it was habit. He was a person who'd spent a
lifetime building the habit of checking.

That night I also had this thought. What if, instead of losing things
across nine moves, I'd grown up the way Histor had, never forgetting a
single thing? Looking back it's a strange thing to wonder. I grew up
into the person I am, and he grew up into the person he is. Only that
night, the thought kept circling in my head.

I went to the repository early the next morning. I've already
mentioned that I couldn't sleep, curious what Histor was going to show
me. But that morning, on the way there, I deliberately slowed my steps.

There was a reason. I've never had much practice holding my curiosity
in check. But that day I wanted to try. What came back to me was how
Histor never grew impatient, every time he put me off with "let's talk
tomorrow." I wanted to be like that too, just for one day.

I stopped for a moment on the water channel bridge. I stood there
longer than usual. Listening to the water, I told myself that whatever
I saw today, I'd take it in in order. Calling it a resolution was too
grand a word for it, but at the time it was what I needed.

When I reached the repository door, Histor was already waiting inside.

I should be honest about how well I kept that mindset that felt too
grand to call a resolution, from the day before. The moment I opened
the door, I forgot it entirely and all but ran inside. My resolve to
take things in in order didn't survive a single step. I was still,
that day too, sixteen years old.

(Continued in Chapter 17.)

Chapter 17. The Man with No Name

_Previously_ — They laid the six cities' records side by side on the
floor and began finding overlapping passages. They found more than
twenty, but Histor said that wasn't the real thing. He only said
he'd show her tomorrow.

The next morning, Histor was there earlier than usual.

When I arrived, he was already sitting on the floor, going back over
the six rows again. Something was a little different from the day
before. He'd pulled a few sheets out of each row and set them apart in
front.

"…You're early."

"…I didn't finish sorting last night. I wanted to show you this."

He pointed to the six sheets set out in front. It looked like he'd
pulled one from each city.

"…What is this?"

"…Read them. Start with Haven's."

The room was quiet. Morning sunlight came slanting through the window
and lay long across the six rows on the floor. Watching that light cut
across all six rows at once, I had, for the first time, the thought
that these six cities might somehow be joined in a single line
somewhere. It wasn't a grand thought. It was just a thought that came
from watching the light.

There was a reason even for the order he'd set the six sheets in. He
said he'd laid them roughly in the order the cities had received their
records — that is, roughly the order the cities had been founded, or
the events had happened. Not certain, he said, but roughly right.

I picked up the first sheet. It was Haven's record.

There was a person beside the flower bed. While Elpis waited for her
brother, that person was pulling weeds. No one asked that person's
name. Later that person appeared again by the well, standing beside
Elpis as she drew water, holding the rope of the bucket for her.

I picked up the next sheet. The Reckoning's.

The line at the Adjudicator's yard was long. At the end of the line
stood a person. Not angry, even when someone shoved ahead and cut in.
Pheugon later asked that person, "Have you been in a city like this
before?" The person smiled and avoided the answer.

Third, the Harvest's.

Among the day laborers gleaning in the field, there was a person.
Working side by side with Xene. When Xene met someone who asked why she
came to this field every day, that person was there too, but said
nothing.

Fourth was the Preserve's.

In the dry valley, a person walked among the bones. Already there when
Anemos went down. Didn't ask what he was doing. Just walked alongside.

Fifth, the Equity's.

A person sat by the water. Hora passed by and saw. That person did
nothing and said nothing. Just sat. Hora recorded the scene in exactly
three sentences.

Sixth, Loop's.

Near the front gate, while everyone hurried, one person walked slowly.
An old scar on his hand. Mena passed him by. No one asked his name.

I laid the six sheets back out on the desk. My hand trembled a little.

"…Is this—"

"…Yes."

"…Is it the same person in all six?"

Histor looked at me. His expression was the same as usual. Not
excited, not surprised.

"…It might be the same person, or it might not be. What I can say is
only this — a person with no name appears in all six records."

I followed the scenes again, one by one, in my mind. The hand pulling
weeds by the flower bed, the face that didn't get angry at the end of
the line, the back working side by side in the field, the footsteps
already there in the valley, the silence held to three sentences by the
water, the lone slow walk near the front gate. All six scenes were
short. No one lingered over that person for long. Everyone had written
it down as though the person were just passing through. And yet the
shortness itself resembled something strangely, across all of them.

"…But they're all so similar. No one ever asks his name, no one gets
angry, he just stays close by."

"…They are similar. That part's true."

"…Isn't this a big discovery?" I asked.

"…It's a discovery. Whether it's big, I don't know."

"…Why not?"

"…Let me sum up what I've told you." He counted on his fingers, one at
a time. "First, a nameless character appears in all six records.
Second, that character's behavior resembles itself across the six.
Those two things are facts. I've confirmed them."

"…And?"

"…But I still don't know what this proves."

I felt a little disappointed by that. Looking back, there was nothing
to be disappointed about. He'd told me exactly as much as he knew. Not
more than he knew. That was his way.

I looked at the six sheets again, wanting to find something more.

"…Has this person's name really never come up? Not once?"

"…Not once. Nowhere in the six records. I've read all of it, so I'm
certain."

"…Isn't that strange? Six different people wrote six different
records in six different cities, and every single one of them, without
exception, never wrote down this person's name."

Histor thought for a moment and answered.

"…It could be strange, or it might not be."

"…That answer again?"

"…Sorry. But it really is true. It's a common thing not to ask a
stranger's name, especially someone just passing through."

I had to admit that explanation made sense. Not many people ask a
stranger's name every single time.

Still, something kept bothering me.

"…But it's all six of them. Not one of the six asked — I find that a
little strange."

Histor stopped for the first time at that. His expression turned
thoughtful.

"…What you're saying isn't entirely wrong. But I have trouble calling
it proof. For something to be proof, every other possibility has to be
ruled out, and I haven't managed that yet."

"…What other possibilities?"

"…For example — stories that travel tend to resemble each other
anyway. Similar stories can arise independently in different places on
their own. Or the recorders could have read each other's records and
been influenced by them. Until I've checked all of those
possibilities, I can't say this is special."

I fell quiet, hearing that. He'd already foreseen, at this very
moment, the rebuttal he'd lay out in the next chapter. I didn't know it
then. I was only just beginning to understand how careful a person he
was.

That day we also went back through the rest of the bundles for any
more scenes like these six. Histor found a few more. That person
brushed past once more later in Haven's record, and there was one more
short line in the Reckoning's record too. In the Equity's record, it
really was exactly four times, just as Histor had said in advance.

"…The Equity really is short," I said.

"…Yes. It has the least of any of the six books. Every time, three
sentences or fewer. Whether Hora simply didn't write much about him, or
whether that person really only stays briefly wherever he goes — I
don't know."

The word "briefly" caught in my ear.

"…If it's uncertain, how do you count it?"

"…I don't count it. I only count what's certain. That's why I only
told you he appears for certain in all six records. How many times
he appears in each — I haven't finished sorting that out."

I felt again, in this moment, how careful he was. He didn't inflate
what was uncertain. He worked only with what was sure. I thought that
was honesty. It was honesty. Only, honesty wasn't everything.

I found myself wondering where this carefulness had come from. Why had
a child who'd picked up scraps of paper since he was small grown into
someone who spoke only of what was certain? I didn't put that question
into words that day. Instead I set it aside in my mind. On the list of
questions to ask someday.

That afternoon, I copied the six sheets separately. In order. Haven,
the Reckoning, the Harvest, the Preserve, the Equity, Loop.

As I copied, I kept imagining that person. A hand pulling weeds by the
flower bed, a face that didn't get angry even when shoved to the end of
the line, feet walking among bones in the valley. All different scenes,
and yet they kept overlapping into one in my mind.

The Keeper passed by and stopped for a moment, seeing what I was
copying.

"…What are you copying?"

"…There's a person who appears in all six records. No name, but his
behavior is all similar."

The Keeper turned the six sheets over one by one. The expression didn't
change much.

"…Did you know about this?" I asked.

"…Vaguely. Stay here long enough and you naturally learn it."

"…Then why doesn't anyone talk about it? Isn't it remarkable?"

The Keeper set the papers back down.

"…Maybe because nobody knows what "remarkable" would even mean here.
Everyone knows there's someone. What it means, nobody can say for
sure."

I thought that answer sounded similar to Histor's. There was a
difference too. Histor didn't speak because he didn't know. The Keeper
seemed to know and still hold back from speaking. I couldn't pin down
that difference precisely then.

"…I really want to know who this person is," I said.

"…Me too," Histor answered. A short answer, but it sounded genuine.

I felt a little relieved hearing that. That he was curious too. I
thought that curiosity would lead somewhere. In the next chapter I'd
see that it didn't.

I bundled the six sheets together and set them aside, labeled. I wrote
"The Man with No Name" on the outside. Histor saw it and said,

"…You're naming him."

"…It's not a name, just a category. I didn't know what else to call
it."

"…That's a kind of name too."

Something jolted in me at that. He was right. I was giving the man with
no name the name "The Man with No Name." It was ironic.

"…Then what should we call him?"

"…I just call him 'that person.' It feels more accurate not to give
him a name."

I thought that sounded careful. Looking back, it was more than
careful. He was a person who didn't casually name what he hadn't
confirmed. That principle held to the very end. Nowhere in this whole
book does he, or I, or anyone, manage to give that person a name.

That evening, tidying up, I stacked the six sheets back in order.
Haven, the Reckoning, the Harvest, the Preserve, the Equity, Loop. And
laid my copies on top, in the same order. Seeing the original and the
copy laid out in the same order eased my mind a little. At least this
was something I was good at.

On the way home I passed the water channel again. Listening to the
sound of the water, I ran through the six scenes once more in my mind.
The flower bed, the end of the line, the field, the valley, the water's
edge, the front gate. Six different places, one overlapping shadow.

I wanted to know who that shadow was. But even in that same moment, I
knew something else — how long this curiosity of mine could hold up
against Histor's caution. I felt that by tomorrow I'd find out exactly
how much of this discovery he'd admit to, and how much he'd retreat
from. That was a sixteen-year-old's expectation.

That evening I wrote on my paper. Laid the six sheets side by side and
saw the same person, the same story, in six different places. Histor
said that wasn't proof. That also seemed true.

At home, I took out the six copied sheets again and read them once
more. Flower bed, end of the line, field, valley, water's edge, front
gate. I copied those six words onto a separate piece of paper. There
wasn't any particular reason. I just wanted to set the words together
in one place and look at them.

I held that paper a long time. The six places seemed to have nothing
to do with each other. Different cities, different seasons, different
people appearing. And yet, lining those six places up in one row,
something like a road seemed to appear. I didn't know where the road
went. It just looked like a road.

I folded the paper and put it in the drawer with the others. Closing
the drawer, I had a passing feeling that these six sheets would be
needed again someday. It was a feeling with no basis. I wasn't someone
who trusted feelings like that, but that day I just felt it.

Whether that feeling turned out right or wrong, you'll learn by
reading on in this record. For now I'll only set down the feel of my
hand closing that drawer.

(Continued in Chapter 18.)

Chapter 18. It Proves Nothing

_Previously_ — They discovered that a man with no name appears in
every one of the six cities' records. The flower bed, the end of the
line, the field, the valley, the water's edge, the front gate. Histor
admitted it was true, but said it wasn't proof. He said other
possibilities remained.

The next day, Histor came in with a different look on his face than
the day before.

Not an excited look. If anything he looked heavier. I could tell right
away he'd spent the night thinking about something.

"…What were you doing all night?" I asked.

"…Went back over what we found yesterday."

"…Find anything?"

He looked at me for a moment, then sat down.

"…Mone, I said yesterday there were other possibilities. Can I lay
that out in more detail now?"

I felt myself tense a little. It sounded like something wasn't going to
be good news.

"…The first is," he began, folding down a finger, "traveling stories
naturally resemble each other. Stories about treating a stranger
kindly, about a person who doesn't get angry — that kind of story turns
up everywhere. Because people tend to like similar types of stories."

"…Isn't that a stretch? You're saying six people just happened to
imagine such a similar person, purely by coincidence?"

"…It isn't imagination, it's records. But records are written through
memory too. People tend to fit the unfamiliar into familiar patterns
when remembering. A stranger who doesn't get angry, a person who
quietly stays close by — impressions like that can end up looking
similar even when different people go through different things in
different places."

I tried to find a way to argue back against that explanation, but I
couldn't. It was plausible. Too plausible.

"…For example," he added, "there's a merchant's record in the stacks
that has nothing to do with this city. It has a story like this too — a
stranger traveler being put up for the night, with no questions asked.
No name. It isn't a city connected to our six by the water channel at
all. It's a completely different place."

He brought that record out from the shelf. A short story, two
paragraphs. A traveler, a strange village, a name no one asked. I read
it and felt something go cold inside.

"…Is this the same person too?"

"…No, it's the opposite, actually. This is a record with no connection
at all to our six books. And yet a similar story appears in it. Which
means this kind of story itself could arise anywhere."

I couldn't help but nod along with that logic. What he'd brought as
evidence turned out not to argue against us, but to shake what had
looked like it was on our side.

"…Second," he went on, "the recorders might have read each other's
records."

"…What does that mean?"

"…Remember that Mena's record was misquoted in the Reckoning's record.
That means someone on Pheugon's side had been exposed to Mena's story,
at least by rumor. Pheugon might then have shaped the character in his
own record to resemble that story unconsciously, even without copying
it directly."

That part hurt the most, because it was evidence we'd found ourselves,
the day before. That Mena's record had drifted over into the
Reckoning's. Now it was being used against us.

"…So what we found yesterday actually counts as a counterargument?"

"…Not quite a counterargument. It just gives us one more explanation.
It could be the same person, but it could also be the same story being
passed along. Both possibilities remain open."

"…Is there a third one?" I asked. Only two of his fingers had folded
down.

He hesitated for a moment.

"…The third one is weaker. I'll say it anyway. The water channel.
Records flow along the water channel. So something someone experienced
in one city could travel as rumor to another city, and there be written
down again as though it happened to them. Not reading the record
directly — just hearing the story."

"…Isn't that similar to the second one?"

"…Similar, but different. The second is reading a record. The third is
just something passed along by word of mouth. The two are confirmed
differently."

I was struck by how finely he split apart these possibilities. At the
time I thought this was persistence. Now I think it might not have
been persistence but fear. Saying something uncertain as though it were
certain was, for him, the thing he most wanted to avoid.

Quietly straightening the six sheets, I suddenly noticed this method
felt familiar. It resembled the way I used to pack when I moved.
Fragile things in the middle, heavy things at the bottom. Histor was
stacking possibilities the same way. From the most certain to the
weakest, in order. I was a person who set objects in order; he was a
person who set possibilities in order. The methods resembled each
other, but we used them for opposite ends. I kept order so as not to
lose things. He kept order so as not to jump to conclusions.

I felt a kind of grievance rising in me.

"…Histor, are you trying to convince me right now, or convince
yourself?"

He looked a little startled by the question.

"…Neither. I'm saying what I know."

"…Then why do you keep raising objections? You were curious about this
together with me at first."

"…Being curious and drawing a conclusion are different problems. I am
curious. Even now. But I can't conclude anything yet."

I couldn't respond to that. Writing this scene again now, I admit I
was angry that day. Not because he was wrong. I was angry because he
was right.

"…Tell me honestly," I asked. "…Do you really think this is
coincidence?"

He closed his eyes for a moment, then opened them.

"…I don't know. I'm not certain it's coincidence, and I'm not certain
it isn't. What I have are two facts. That a similar figure appears in
all six records, and that there are at least two more explanations for
that. I don't know which of them is right."

"…That seems too well organized to say you don't know."

"…I can organize it. Organizing and knowing are different."

That distinction felt unfamiliar to me again. He kept splitting words
that way over and over. Knowing and believing, organizing and knowing.
At the time it felt like wordplay to me. I know now. It wasn't
wordplay. It was how he lived.

I wanted to ask Arche about it that day too.

"…Arche, have any of the six cities' recorders ever read each other's
records?"

Some items are confirmed and some are unconfirmed. Would you like to
check?

"…Please check."

There is no evidence that Haven's recorder and the Reckoning's
recorder ever read each other's records. The reference to Haven found
in the Reckoning's record is presumed to have come through word of
mouth. The remaining combinations are unconfirmed.

I heard that answer and looked at Histor.

"…Did you hear?"

"…Yes. But there are a lot of 'unconfirmed.' It just means the answer
hasn't come in yet."

"…Then we can just confirm them."

"…That takes time. We'd have to check every combination. With six
people, that's fifteen possible pairs."

I gave a small laugh, realizing he'd already done the math. He was
always several steps ahead of me in moments like these. I never found
it hard to keep up with him. I actually found it fun. I just didn't yet
know that being several steps ahead never led anywhere.

I looked at the six sheets again. Papers I'd thought, the day before,
were a great discovery. Today they looked a little different. Still
astonishing, but my confidence had shaken a little.

"…So does this mean nothing?" I asked.

"…No, I never said it meant nothing. I said it's not proven. Meaning
and proof are two different problems again."

"…Different again, is it?"

"…Yes. There's a lot in the world that isn't proven but is still
meaningful."

I found that a little comforting. But at the same time, frustrated.
Why he'd say that and still not believe, I didn't understand at the
time. He kept saying it wasn't proven but was meaningful, and yet not
take one step toward that meaning.

That afternoon we went back over the six rows again. Unlike the day
before, Histor started looking this time for the opposite evidence.
Checking each of the fifteen combinations for whether any of the six
recorders might really have read another's.

"…What happens if we check this?" I asked.

"…If it turns out all six never read one another at all, then the
second possibility I described disappears. What's left is only the
first — that similar stories resemble each other by coincidence."

"…What if they all read each other?"

"…Then my objection is right."

The Keeper stopped by the room that afternoon and looked at the chart
we were drawing.

"…What are you doing?"

"…Checking whether the recorders read each other," I answered.

The Keeper looked at the chart for a long while.

"…Interesting thing to be doing."

"…Histor's trying to use it as a counterargument, and I'm just
curious."

"…A counterargument, is it." The Keeper turned the word over. "…That's
fine too. Better than believing without checking."

I was a little surprised by that. I'd assumed the Keeper wasn't on
Histor's side.

"…Do you think Histor's right?" I asked.

"…Right or wrong aside, checking isn't a bad thing. I'd rather have
someone who checks and can't believe than someone who believes without
checking."

"…Why is that?"

"…Someone who checks and can't believe is at least looking at
something. Someone who believes without checking might not be looking
at anything at all."

I fell blank for a moment, hearing that. I'd come back to this line
often later in this book. The Keeper never saw Histor as bad. If
anything, the Keeper understood him. I wouldn't learn where that
understanding came from until much later.

I had to admit that this work was, for Histor, a fair way of working.
He wasn't only looking for evidence in his own favor. He looked equally
hard for evidence that could prove his own position wrong.

I liked that fairness that day. I still think it was good. Only I
know now that fairness wasn't a conclusion. It was something he could
stand still on, and stay fair, indefinitely.

Drawing that chart, the six scenes kept coming back to me all the same.
The hand by the flower bed, the face at the end of the line, the back
in the field, the footsteps in the valley, the silence by the water,
the slow walk near the gate. Even after hearing everything Histor
explained, I strangely couldn't believe those six scenes were six
separate coincidences. I knew that not being able to believe wasn't the
same as proof. But my heart still moved that way regardless.

I helped with the work, drawing fifteen combinations onto paper in a
chart. Haven-the Reckoning, Haven-the Harvest, Haven-the Preserve,
Haven-the Equity, Haven-Loop. The Reckoning-the Harvest, the
Reckoning-the Preserve, and on down the line. I liked drawing charts.
It meant filling in boxes.

"…Won't things get clear once this is all filled in?" I said.

"…Some things will get clearer, some won't. Even if 'never read'
gets confirmed, that doesn't mean 'never knew at all.' They could have
heard it by word of mouth."

"…So in the end, whatever we know, we never know completely."

"…Right. That's how it usually is. Nearly nothing is known
completely."

I thought that sounded strangely like him. Someone who says almost
nothing is fully known, and yet speaks about himself in the most
complete terms of all. I know, I don't believe. Neither sentence had a
"perhaps" in it.

I only caught this contradiction that night. He was careful about
nearly everything in the world. Except for one thing. The one thing he
was always certain of was the fact of his own not believing.

Toward evening we began marking, one by one, whether each city's
recorder had ever met or exchanged letters with another. It was slow
work. We didn't finish that day.

"…Let's finish tomorrow," Histor said.

"…Will we have an answer by tomorrow?"

"…Might. Might not."

I laughed at that answer. A sad laugh.

"…Histor, do you know that habit of yours? Whatever you're asked,
you're always half one way, half the other."

He heard that and laughed a little too.

"…Trying to be precise tends to come out that way."

On the way home I stopped a moment by the water channel. Listening to
the water, I went back over the day's conversation. Histor had offered
three possibilities. That stories naturally resemble each other, that
recorders could have read each other, that it could have traveled by
word of mouth. All three were plausible. I couldn't fully refute any of
them.

That day I took this as losing. Now I don't. Losing and being
refuted are different. I was refuted, but I didn't lose. I just didn't
know the difference then.

On the way home I wrote in my paper. Histor made his counterarguments.
The counterarguments were mostly right. I couldn't beat them.
Tomorrow, we check whether the recorders read each other.

I added one more line beside that. "The reason he can't believe isn't
because he's foolish. It's the opposite, actually." I held onto that
sentence for a long time. It might be a strange sentence for a
sixteen-year-old to write. But I thought, that day, it was the most
accurate one.

That night I thought again of the merchant's record. A stranger
traveler, a name no one asked. Those two paragraphs had nothing to do
with our six books. And yet they resembled it. I thought that was
evidence Histor was right, and at the same time something felt off. A
resembling story being common in the world, and the six we'd found all
falling on that common side — those were two different questions. I
tried to write that difference down on paper that night, but it didn't
come out well.

I look back at myself that night now. I could follow Histor's logic.
I couldn't say he was wrong. But being able to follow it, and following
it all the way to the conclusion it pointed to, were different things.
I was standing somewhere between the two.

(Continued in Chapter 19.)

Chapter 19. They Never Read Each Other

_Previously_ — Histor laid out three counterarguments. That
traveling stories naturally resemble each other, that the recorders
might have read each other's records, that it could have spread by
word of mouth. Mone couldn't refute him. The two agreed to check
whether the recorders had actually read one another's records.

The next day, we went through all fifteen combinations one by one.

The method was simple. Check within each record for any trace of
another city's record being mentioned, or any record of the two
recorders having actually met, asking Arche and checking again against
the physical copies.

"…This is going to take a while," I said.

"…Yes. But we have to, to be sure."

I redrew the chart from morning, bigger than the day before. I laid
the five city names across and down, and left a box at each
intersection to mark whether it had been confirmed. Adding Loop made
six, so there were fifteen boxes.

"…I don't think one day is enough to fill all this in," I said.

"…That's fine. It can take a few days. No rush."

That answer surprised me. He always liked being accurate, but he
wasn't someone in a hurry. At the time I took that for ease. Looking
back now, it wasn't ease so much as an attitude that it wouldn't
matter much to him which way the answer came out.

The first was Haven and the Reckoning. Arche had already given us the
answer the day before. They'd never read each other. The quote on the
Reckoning's side was presumed to have come by word of mouth.

"…This one's certain, at least," I said.

"…Yes. First confirmation done."

Partway through, I asked Arche a few more things. Arche answered fast
