each time, but the answers weren't always tidy. Words like "confirmed,"
"unconfirmed," "partially confirmed" kept mixing in. The vagueness
frustrated me a little.

"…Arche, are 'unconfirmed' and 'confirmed not to be' different
things?" I asked.

Unconfirmed means there is insufficient material to determine the
matter. Confirmed not to be means the matter has been checked and
found not to be true.

I was a little surprised, hearing that distinction. Arche split
knowing from not-knowing as strictly as Histor did. I felt this
resemblance between the two again in this chapter. But this time I
felt it a little differently. Arche had no choice but to divide things
that way — that was its nature. Histor was a person, and he'd chosen
to divide things that way.

The second was Haven and the Harvest. Histor laid the two records side
by side and cross-checked. It took a while.

"…This one's never been read either," he said. "…The timing doesn't
work. The Harvest's record was already finished before Haven's record
was even written."

The third, Haven and the Preserve. This one was different. Arche
answered that the two had actually met. Because Speon had passed
through both cities.

"…So the well story comes from here," I said.

"…Yes. This isn't so much reading each other's records as it is the
same person carrying it along. Still, a connection is a connection."

I imagined Speon as I wrote that in the chart. A man who'd gone from
city to city along the water channel, digging wells. I'd never met
him. But it struck me as remarkable that a story had stayed behind in
the places he'd passed through. It wasn't only water that stayed
behind when a person passed by — the story flowed along with it too.

"…Where is Speon now?" I asked.

"…I don't know. It's not in the records. Maybe he's still off digging
a well somewhere."

I liked hearing him say that. It was rare for Histor to speak so
easily about something he wasn't certain of.

Fourth, Haven and the Equity. This one took a long time to check.
There was a character in the margin of the Equity's record that looked
like it might be another city's name. Histor stared at that character
for a long while and finally asked Arche.

"…Arche, is this character definitely referring to Haven?"

The character in question has been damaged in transcription and only
part of it survives. It could plausibly be Haven, or it could be an
entirely different place name. It cannot be determined.

"…If it's Haven, doesn't that count as a connection?" I asked.

"…It does. But since it can't be confirmed, I'm not counting it as a
connection."

I found it strange that he wouldn't use this in his own favor. With
one damaged character, he could have marked the box the way that
suited his own conclusion, and he didn't.

"…You could count this as Haven, Histor, it'd be in your favor."

He looked at me for a moment.

"…What's in my favor isn't necessarily what's right."

We went on. Haven and the Equity had never been read. Haven and Loop
had. Loop was the oldest city, and it kept coming up, over and over
across the combinations, that the other cities had drawn on Loop's
records to some degree.

"…Loop's connected to everything," I said.

"…Of course. It's where it all began. The other five learned about
the water channel through Loop."

The Reckoning and the Harvest had never read each other. The Reckoning
and the Preserve had never read each other. The Reckoning and the
Equity had one short mention, but it was no more than a misspelled
name — not evidence they'd actually read each other.

"…Put this one in unconfirmed too," Histor said.

I caught something in his voice. Not disappointment, not
satisfaction. Just the voice of someone still checking.

At lunchtime the Keeper stopped by and looked at the chart we were
drawing. This time, a little longer than usual.

"…Confirming all of this?" the Keeper asked.

"…Yes," Histor answered. "…Whether they read each other or not."

"…Sounds interesting. Let me know how it turns out."

"…Are you curious?" I asked.

"…I am. But whatever the result is, I don't think I'll be surprised."

"…Why not?"

The Keeper shrugged.

"…Either way makes sense. It makes sense if they all read each other,
and it makes sense if none of them did. So I'm less curious about the
result than about what the two of you do once you have it."

I let that pass at the time. Reading it again now, the Keeper already
knew. That the result wouldn't change Histor. The Keeper wasn't
watching the result — the Keeper was watching us.

By evening we'd finished checking all fifteen.

The results were these. Loop was connected to all five other cities.
That made sense — Loop was the oldest city, the start of the water
channel. Among the other five, it was different.

Haven and the Preserve were connected through Speon. That much was
certain. But Haven and the Reckoning, Haven and the Harvest, Haven and
the Equity, the Reckoning and the Harvest, the Reckoning and the
Preserve, the Reckoning and the Equity, the Harvest and the Preserve,
the Harvest and the Equity, the Preserve and the Equity — all nine of
those combinations had never read one another.

"…Let me sum this up," Histor said. He began drawing a new chart on
paper.

"…Leaving Loop out, looking only at the other five — Haven, the
Reckoning, the Harvest, the Preserve, the Equity. Of these five, only
one pair is connected — Haven and the Preserve. The other four, the
Reckoning, the Harvest, the Equity, and Haven, never read the other
three."

I studied that summary again.

"…So you mean four of the five cities were completely ignorant of one
another?"

"…Precisely — the Reckoning, the Harvest, and the Equity never read
each other, and never read Haven either. Only the Preserve is
connected to Haven."

"…Then what about the second possibility you raised, Histor? The one
about reading each other and being influenced?"

He was quiet for a moment. He looked at the paper again.

"…It's weaker now. Nine combinations came back never-read. That means
the possibility doesn't explain the whole thing."

For a moment I expected something. I thought he'd finally admit it. I'd
actually been waiting, in my heart, for this very moment. Once it was
shown that four of them had never read each other, I thought he'd say
at least, "…Then this really does seem real." A sixteen-year-old's
expectation.

"…So? Do you admit it now?"

He looked at me. His expression was complicated.

"…Admit what?"

"…That this person is real."

"…No."

I was startled by how short that answer was.

"…But you just said the second possibility got weaker."

"…It's weaker, that's true. But it hasn't disappeared. The first
possibility still stands — that traveling stories naturally resemble
each other. And so does the third, that it spread by word of mouth.
That's harder to confirm than reading a record directly. What travels
by word leaves no trace."

I hit a wall again at that. It was true. What's passed by word of
mouth leaves no evidence.

"…Then, Histor," I asked, "what would you need to check to rule out
even the third possibility?"

"…I don't think it can be ruled out. Probably. What travels by word
leaves no trace. It can't be proven or disproven."

"…So we'll never know?"

"…That part, yes."

I heard that answer and felt something strange. It looked as though he
had built himself a place he couldn't get out of. If he left one
possibility standing that could never be proven, he could stay safe
there forever.

I didn't see this clearly that day. I do now. Histor wasn't beaten by
logic. He'd left, inside his own logic, one last door that he himself
had built. A door no one could open. Because it couldn't be proven.

"…Histor," I said. "…I don't understand right now. It's confirmed
that four of them never read each other. Why hasn't your position
changed?"

He looked at me for a long while. Not an angry face. A serious one.

"…Mone, I don't lie. I've told you exactly what I found. That the
four never read each other — that's true. I haven't hidden that from
you."

"…But."

"…But it doesn't carry me to belief. I still think there are other
ways to explain it. And even if every one of those ways were also
ruled out, I don't think that would automatically become belief."

Something felt like it was collapsing in me, hearing that. Looking
back now, it wasn't him collapsing anything. I'd expected he'd change
his mind if beaten by logic. It was that expectation that collapsed.

"…Then what would it take for you to believe?" I asked.

He took a long time to answer that question.

"…I don't know. I really don't know. If I knew what that was, I'd be
more at ease too."

That answer sounded strangely sad to me. He didn't even know what he
was waiting for. The fact that four had never read each other, the
fact that a man appeared in all six records — none of it had yet
reached him as evidence.

"…Then what am I supposed to do?" I asked. The question came out
before I'd thought about it.

"…Me?"

"…Yes. What do I have to show you, Histor? If six records aren't
enough, if four never reading each other isn't enough."

He looked at me steadily for a moment.

"…You don't need to show me anything, Mone. I never asked you to prove
anything to me. I'm just not believing right now, that's all."

"…Then why do you look so sad?"

He looked a little surprised at that question. He hadn't seemed to
know he looked sad.

"…I'm not sad. I'm just trying to be accurate."

I believed him. I knew he didn't lie. But it occurred to me for the
first time that day that being accurate and being all right don't
always go together.

Writing this chapter, I look again at myself that day. I wanted to
refute him. I thought if I pushed hard enough with facts, he'd change.
He did admit the fact — that the four had never read each other. And
having admitted it, he stayed exactly where he was.

I know now. "Refuted" is a strange word to begin with. If a person can
be moved somewhere by logic, that means they were standing on that
logic to begin with. Histor wasn't standing on logic. He was standing
on fact. Fact hadn't changed. So neither did he. I learned that
difference in my body that day.

We showed the chart to the Keeper again, after redrawing it neatly.
The Keeper studied it for a long while and said,

"…What's changed because of this?"

"…Histor's position hasn't changed," I answered.

"…Then nothing's changed, has it."

"…No." This time Histor answered. "…I know now that the four never
read each other. I didn't know that yesterday. That's changed."

The Keeper heard that and gave a small laugh. A low laugh.

"…Yes, that's changed too."

I didn't know what that laugh meant at the time. I know now. The
Keeper was watching knowledge pile up a little at a time. Even if it
never led to belief, knowledge piled up as knowledge. The Keeper never
looked lightly on that accumulation itself.

On the way home, passing the water channel, I ran back over the boxes
we'd filled in that day. Six of the fifteen boxes were connected, and
most of those were through Loop. Four of the five other cities had
truly known nothing of one another. And yet the same person appeared
in all six records. I still thought that was strange. However many
possibilities Histor left standing, my heart kept leaning toward the
strangeness.

On the way home I wrote in my paper. Four of the five cities never
read each other. Histor admitted it. But his position hasn't changed. I
think I understand now what that means. Facts don't move a person. Not
by themselves, at least.

I added one more line beneath that. "Then what does move a person?"
That day I didn't know the answer. Even now, writing this book, I
can't write the answer in a single sentence. But I believe there's a
trace of that answer somewhere in this record. Whether "believe" is
even the right word to use, I'm still not sure myself.

That night I opened the chart again. Six connected out of fifteen
boxes, and of those, only one — the one through Speon — was certain.
The rest were mostly unconfirmed or presumed word of mouth. I ran my
finger over those boxes and counted them. I counted three times. Each
time the same number came out. I didn't know then that this was
similar to the checking Histor did. I know now. The two of us were
doing the same thing with different hands.

(Continued in Chapter 20.)

Chapter 20. The Keeper's Slowness

_Previously_ — It was confirmed that four of the five cities had
never read one another's records. Histor admitted the fact but did
not change his position. He said other possibilities still remained.
Mone learned, for the first time, that facts don't move a person.

After that day, I began to see the Keeper differently.

It was a strange trigger. Watching Histor not change his position, I
suddenly thought of the Keeper. That slow walk, that slow way of
speaking. Up to then I'd just taken it for personality.

In this chapter I write down the day I learned that slowness wasn't
personality.

The trigger was small. When Histor admitted that four had never read
each other and still didn't change his position, I couldn't, in that
moment, think who it reminded me of. But that night, thinking it over
at home, I thought of the Keeper. He was similar. Twenty-two years
with not one single case closed, and knowing that, still coming every
day. I kept feeling those two were alike somehow.

That afternoon, while Histor was out of the room, I asked the Keeper.

"…Why do you do everything so slowly?"

The Keeper stopped what he was doing and looked at me.

"…What am I doing slowly?"

"…Everything. Your walk, your speech. You were like that from the
first day. You asked me, 'What changes if you do it fast?'"

The Keeper laughed a little.

"…You remembered that."

"…Yes. I thought it was just a joke at the time."

"…It wasn't a joke."

I looked at the paper still in the Keeper's hand. Holding the page
he'd been turning, frozen mid-motion. I kept wondering why he was this
slow.

"…Seeing Histor made me think of you."

"…Why?"

"…Histor doesn't change either. No matter what you show him. But he
keeps coming. So do you."

The Keeper heard that and studied me for a moment. A surprised look.

"…I didn't expect you to set those two side by side."

"…Aren't they alike?"

"…They're alike. But there's a difference too. I'll tell you that
later."

I was curious about that "later," but I didn't ask more.

The Keeper set down what he was working on and pulled up a chair. I
sat down in front of him too.

"…Do you know how many years I've done this?"

"…I don't know."

"…Twenty-two years."

I was startled by that number. Twenty-two years was before I was even
born.

"…Do you know how many cases have closed in twenty-two years?"

"…Isn't that something you could ask Arche?"

"…I did. You know what the answer was?"

I had a rough guess. But I wanted to hear the exact answer.

"…What did it say?"

"…Zero. Not one."

I couldn't say anything, hearing that number. Not one in twenty-two
years. I thought of the "in progress" markers I'd seen the first time
I came to the repository. Some had been in progress ten years, they'd
told me. I'd assumed at the time that meant a handful of cases.

"…All of them? None at all?"

"…Not one. Arche gives an answer to everything you ask. But it never
gives an end."

"…Isn't that hard?"

The Keeper looked thoughtful for a moment.

"…It was hard. The first few years. I waited every day thinking
something would finish. It never did."

"…Is that why you slowed down?" I asked.

"…More or less. At first I thought doing things fast would finish
something sooner. The more I hurried, the more anxious I got. Then one
day it hit me. Fast or slow, nothing finishes either way. So it's
better to go slow."

"…How is that better?"

"…Going slow, at least, means seeing what you're doing right now
properly. Going fast, you're already moving on to the next thing, so
you never properly see what's in front of you."

I turned that over. Writing this line down again now, I think this is
one of the quietest sentences in the whole book. He hadn't given up on
finishing. He'd just changed the way he waited for it.

"…So how did you spend those first few years?" I asked.

"…Ran myself ragged. Came in early every morning, worked late every
night. Thought that if I could process even one thing fast, something
would get better."

"…Did Arche fail to close anything back then too?"

"…Same as always. Only I wore myself out. I remember one night,
sitting here alone, crying. I didn't even know why I was crying. Just
that it was hard."

I heard that story and found it hard to picture — that the person sitting
in front of me now, so unhurried, had once run himself that ragged.

"…What changed that night?"

"…Nothing changed. I just walked one step slower than usual the next
morning. That's all. It wasn't a great resolution."

I thought of my first day, hearing that. I'd started putting things in
order without any great resolution either. Both of us, without any
grand reason, had each found, a little at a time, a way of enduring.

"…Did that one slow step keep going?" I asked.

"…No. Fast again the next day. And the day after that. More days
going back than forward. But some days I walked slow again. Kept it up
for a few months, and the slow days came to outnumber the fast ones."

"…So it didn't change all at once?"

"…Nothing changes all at once. That's how I think of it. If there's
such a thing, it's something I don't know about."

Hearing that answer made me think of my own days — I didn't put
things in order every day either. Some days I was impatient, some days
I put things in the wrong slot. I'd thought that was shameful. That
day, for the first time, I thought maybe it was just how it was.

"…Do you think it will end someday, then?" I asked.

The Keeper thought about that question for a long time.

"…I don't know. Really don't know. But I don't think it's mine to
know."

"…Whose is it, then?"

"…Whoever finishes it would know, I'd think. I'm not the one who
finishes it."

"…Is there someone like that?"

"…I don't know if there is or not. But if there is, it isn't me.
That much I'm sure of."

I found that certainty strange. About everything else he said he
didn't know, and yet he stated one thing with certainty — that he
wasn't the one who finishes it.

"…How can you be so sure?"

"…Twenty-two years, that's how. If I were the one to finish it,
something would have finished by now. Nothing has. So I'm not the
one."

I thought that logic resembled Histor's way. Confirming your own
position by evidence. Only the direction was different. Histor stacked
evidence into reasons not to believe; the Keeper stacked evidence to
confirm his own place.

"…Does that make it easier?" I asked. "…Knowing you're not the one who
finishes it."

"…Not easier so much as lighter. There's no weight of having to finish
it. I just put today's things in their place today."

"…That sounds easy the way you say it, but it doesn't look easy to
me."

"…It isn't easy. But not-easy and heavy are different things."

I turned that short distinction over many times that day. Not-easy and
heavy. I didn't fully understand the difference then. I understand a
little now. There are things that aren't easy but are still bearable,
and things that look easy but still crush you.

That distinction felt unfamiliar. He'd worked here for twenty-two
years. And yet he said he wasn't the one who finished it.

"…Then what is your job, exactly?"

"…To set things down. In order. Same as you."

Something in me warmed a little at that. I'd always thought putting
things in order was only my job. Hearing the Keeper call it his job
too made this work suddenly feel bigger.

"…When did you realize finishing wasn't our job?"

The Keeper looked out the window for a moment.

"…About five years in. Someone came through here then, another one
full of questions like you. That person asked me, 'Why do you keep
working if nothing ever finishes?' I couldn't answer it at the time. It
took me days of thinking to find the answer."

"…What did you say?"

"…I said, 'I've come to think finishing was never our job to begin
with.'"

"…What did that person say back?" I asked.

"…Nothing at all. Just nodded. But after that, their face looked more
at ease. That's when I first knew it too — that I only knew I believed
it once I'd said it out loud."

"…Did you not know before? What you thought?"

"…I didn't. Before someone asked me, I was just coming and going every
day, that's all. Some things only become known once they're put into
words."

I copied that sentence down exactly on my paper. Something only
becomes known once it's put into words. I liked that sentence enough
to underline it.

That line stayed with me a long time. There was no resentment in it,
no despair. Just a voice accepting a fact.

"…Aren't you angry at Arche?" I asked. "…It's the one that can't
close anything."

"…It's not Arche's fault. Arche can't finish because the closing
condition was never defined. Whoever it is that didn't define it,
it isn't Arche."

"…Then who is it?"

The Keeper shrugged.

"…I don't know either. That's probably not mine to know either."

I held onto this exchange for a long time. The Keeper didn't resent
Arche. Didn't pity his own situation either. He just came every day.
Doing a job that never finished, unfinished, with real care, every
day.

"…Tell me more about that old story," I said. "…The one who asked why
you keep working when nothing finishes."

The Keeper laughed a little.

"…That one didn't stay long. About half a year, then went somewhere
else."

"…Where?"

"…Don't know. A letter came once, later. Just said they were doing
well."

"…Did that person not believe either?"

"…No. That one believed. Just never found a reason to keep doing this
work. Believing and keeping at this work are two different problems."

I was struck again by that distinction. Everyone in this repository
said something similar. Knowing and believing were different, and
believing and keeping on were different too. I tried to sort those
three layers out in my head, but it didn't come together well.

"…Then why do you keep on, Keeper? Even while believing?"

"…That's the first time anyone's asked me that."

The Keeper looked out the window for a moment, then looked back at me.

"…I like it here. Even without an ending. I like watching something
get set in its place, in order, every day. That's enough for me."

This is my favorite answer in this chapter. He didn't work for the
sake of waiting for an ending. He just came because he liked today's
work. That simplicity stayed with me a long time.

I thought once more about the distance between that impatience and
this slowness. When I first came to this repository, I'd thought
placing paper quickly and accurately was the most important thing.
Fast seemed like doing it well. But the Keeper had let go of fast over
twenty-two years. I hadn't reached that place yet. I still tended to
hurry — afraid the order would be wrong, afraid I'd miss something.

"…Will I turn into someone like you someday?" I asked.

"…I don't know. That's for you to decide."

"…Decide what?"

"…Whether to go fast or slow. Both are ways of keeping at this work. I
can't say which is right."

I liked that answer. He hadn't told me to become like him. He'd just
told me I could choose.

That evening, Histor came back and asked what we'd talked about. I
told him roughly. The Keeper's twenty-two years, zero closed cases, and
that line.

"…The Keeper said, '…I've come to think finishing was never our job
to begin with,'" I told him.

Histor was quiet a long time, hearing it.

"…That's a good line," he finally said.

"…A good line?"

"…Yes. An honest one. Not pretending to be able to do something you
can't."

I was surprised by that response. I'd expected he'd raise some
objection. Instead he just accepted it, exactly as it was.

I set this moment down at the end of this chapter. Histor might have
seen something in himself, in what the Keeper said. Speaking
precisely, not pretending to do what you can't do. The two resembled
each other in that.

"…But there's a difference too, between the two of you," I said. What
the Keeper had said earlier in the day came back to me.

"…What's different?"

"…The Keeper said he'd tell me later. I don't know yet either."

Histor thought about this for a moment.

"…I'm curious too."

I found it a little unexpected that he'd say that. He usually didn't
show much curiosity about what he didn't know. But that day was a
little different. Looking back on this scene now, I believe he really
was curious — about what the difference was between himself and the
Keeper. That curiosity never took him anywhere, and that's one of the
sorrows of this book, but it isn't true that he wasn't curious.

On the way home I passed the water channel and ran through what I'd
heard that day. Twenty-two years, zero, and that line. I thought about
how far apart that number and that line stood. Twenty-two years with
nothing finished — for an ordinary person, that would be a number to
despair over. And yet what was left at the end of that number wasn't
despair.

Writing this chapter, I sort through the Keeper as a person once
more. He was different again from the six officials in the earlier
records. The overseer trusted the ledger; the Adjudicator knew no
alternative; the manager wasn't ashamed; the caretaker grieved; the
secretary believed she was nothing at all. The Keeper wasn't like any
of them. He knew his own work never finished, and came every day all
the same. Without resentment, without despair. I still can't put a
name to that manner. I'll leave it here, unnamed.

On the way home I wrote in my paper. Twenty-two years and nothing
finished. And still, every day, he comes. No resentment. "I've come to
think finishing was never our job to begin with." I read that line
over several times.

At the very bottom of the paper I added one short question. "Then
when does the one who finishes it come?" That day I thought I'd
written it out of simple curiosity. Setting this record in order now, I
see that I'd already begun waiting for something. Without even knowing
what.

(Continued in Chapter 21.)

Chapter 21. Asking Arche

_Previously_ — The Keeper told her that in twenty-two years, not one
case had ever closed. "…I've come to think finishing was never our
job to begin with." No resentment, no despair in it. Histor called
the line honest.

The next day, I decided to ask Arche directly.

About that person. About the man with no name who appears in all six
records. Histor was nearby, sorting the chart, and I just asked out of
curiosity.

"…Arche, there's a nameless person who appears in all six cities'
records. Who is that?"

Arche took a little longer than usual to answer. A short pause, but I
felt it.

The Haven record, Part Two, Chapter Four: beside the flower bed, an
unnamed figure was pulling weeds. The same record, Part Two, Chapter
Nine: at the well, a figure presumed to be the same person held the
rope of the bucket.

Arche went on.

The Reckoning record, Part One, Chapter Three: at the end of the line
in the Adjudicator's yard, an unnamed figure stood. Cut in line, but
did not protest. The same record, Part Three, Chapter Two: avoided
answering Pheugon's question.

I began writing this down. It was rare for Arche to answer at this
length.

"…Arche, why is this answer so detailed? It's longer than usual," I
asked.

The scope of the question was broad, so all related records were
cited. If more information was included than necessary, please advise.

"…No, all of it is needed as it is."

I laughed a little, hearing that answer. Arche was always like this.
Never a word too many even when it said a great deal, never a word
short even when it said little. Exactly as much as was needed.

The Harvest record, Chapter Four: among the day laborers gleaning in
the field, an unnamed figure worked alongside Xene.

The Preserve record, Chapter Three: in the dry valley, an unnamed
figure was already present before Anemos arrived.

The Equity record, Chapters Twenty-Seven, Thirty-Four, Forty-Four,
Forty-Eight: an unnamed figure appeared briefly in each chapter, in
three sentences or fewer. One mention of sitting by the water.

The Loop record: near the front gate, an unnamed figure walked alone,
slowly. Recorded as having an old scar on his hand.

Once I'd finished writing down all six citations, I couldn't say
anything for a while.

I read the six citations aloud once more, one by one. Flower bed, well,
end of the line, field, valley, water's edge, front gate. An old scar
on his hand. I stopped at that last sentence.

"…This scar — does it not appear in any of the other records?" I
asked.

The other five records contain no mention of physical characteristics.
It exists only in the Loop record.

"…Why does it only exist in the Loop record?"

It is presumed that the Loop record's observer made the closest
physical contact with the figure. However, this is a presumption and
not a confirmed fact.

I felt again how much that phrase — "a presumption and not a confirmed
fact" — sounded exactly like Histor's way of speaking. I wanted to ask
where that scar had come from, but I held back, knowing I'd only get
the same kind of answer.

"…It all matches," I said. "…It's exactly what Histor told me."

"…Of course. Arche quotes the original precisely," Histor answered.

I asked Arche again.

"…Then who is this person?"

That person cannot be identified.

"…Because there's no name?"

Yes. No identifying information exists in the documents.

I turned that sentence over again. That identifying information does
not exist. There's a subtle difference between not existing and not
being known. If it wasn't known, someone must have known it and simply
not written it down; if it doesn't exist, no one ever wrote it down in
the first place. I couldn't put into words at the time why that
difference mattered. I understand it a little now.

Hearing that answer, my own story came briefly to mind. Of the eleven
things I'd lost across nine moves, four I don't even remember the
names of. I don't remember what they were. Those, too, were things
that "cannot be identified." Losing something and something having no
name were different problems, but in that moment they somehow felt
like the same weight.

"…Arche," I asked again. "…Can something exist even without a name?"

Existence and identifiability are separate questions. That something
cannot be identified does not mean it did not exist.

I felt a little relieved by that answer. My own old question — if
something lost is remembered by no one, is it gone, or was it never
there at all — Arche had, for the first time, given me an answer.
Exactly, without my even asking it directly.

I wanted to press a little further.

"…Then did the six people each see a different person, or the same
one?"

There is insufficient basis to determine identity. There is similarity
in appearance and behavior, but without an identifier, sameness cannot
be confirmed.

"…It does say there's similarity, though."

Yes. A statistically significant similarity has been confirmed among
the behavior patterns described across the six records. This is not
evidence of a single identity, however, but an observed fact.

I read that answer aloud again. "There is similarity in appearance and
behavior, but without an identifier, sameness cannot be confirmed." It
was an accurate sentence. And yet something like disappointment seemed
buried within that accuracy. Of course Arche couldn't have
disappointment. That was only my own disappointment bleeding into
Arche's sentence.

I looked at Histor. He'd been listening to that answer too.

"…What does 'statistically significant' mean exactly?" I asked.

It means a level of agreement unlikely to have occurred by chance
alone. However, statistical significance does not establish causation
or identity.

"…So it's not a coincidence?"

It means the likelihood of it not being coincidence is higher than the
likelihood of it being one. This is not a determination.

I read that sentence twice. That it was more likely not to be
coincidence than to be one. And yet still not a determination. I
didn't know then that the distance between those two things ran
through this entire book.

"…Arche's saying exactly the same thing as you, Histor," I said.

"…So it seems." He gave a small laugh. "…I'm a bit surprised too."

"…Does that bother you?"

"…No. Arche has never once said anything false. Thinking in the same
way Arche does isn't particularly a bad thing."

I wanted to say something to that, but held it back. I know now what
I wanted to say. I wanted to say, "But Arche isn't a person." At the
time it felt like it would be too direct a thing to say.

I asked a different question instead.

"…Arche, are you and Histor alike?"

The criterion for comparison is not defined. Which aspect would you
like compared?

"…Well — saying only what's certain."

I state only verifiable information. Whether Histor follows the same
method is not an item I am able to judge.

I laughed, hearing that answer.

"…Even you're careful talking about yourself, Arche."

Evaluation of myself is outside the scope of my judgment.

Histor, listening to this exchange nearby, added a word.

"…Arche won't even casually say what it itself is. More careful than
I am."

"…Is that a good thing?"

"…I don't know. But it's honest."

I hit a wall again at that answer. I'd lost count of how many times
the word "honest" had come up in this conversation.

I kept asking.

"…Arche, why doesn't this person have a name?"

That item is not defined.

I laughed a little, hearing that answer. A familiar one.

"…What needs to be defined for you to know?"

A definition is required for why no name exists. That definition is
not present in the stored information.

"…Did no one define it?"

Correct.

I looked at Histor. He looked thoughtful too, listening to this.

"…Isn't that strange?" I asked. "…Arche gives an answer to anything,
but for this person it can't even give a single name."

"…There's nothing strange about it. It's just saying there's nothing
where there's nothing."

"…But this person appears six times. Isn't it curious that no one ever
wrote down a name?"

Histor thought for a moment.

"…It is curious. But curious and proven are different things."

I hit that wall again. He always stood in the same place. Admitting the
curiousness, and the curiousness never once taking him anywhere.

Last, I asked one more thing.

"…Arche, did this person ever come here, to Loop? Are they here now?"

A figure presumed to be this individual appears in the Loop record,
but their current location is not confirmed.

"…So they could still be here now?"

There is no basis to rule out the possibility.

I felt a strange chill down my spine, hearing that. The possibility
that they could still be here now. I'd always thought of this person
as someone from old records. That sentence shook that.

I glanced out the window for a moment. I thought of the water channel
running behind the repository. That it passed through all six cities
and came all the way here. I found myself thinking that if this person
really existed, he might have walked along this same water channel
too. A groundless imagining. And yet that imagining felt strangely
powerful.

The Keeper happened to be passing by and overheard a little of our
conversation.

"…Did you ask Arche about that person?" the Keeper asked.

"…Yes. It cited things precisely, and said it didn't know the name."

The Keeper nodded.

"…I've asked a few times myself. Same answer came back every time."

"…Were you curious too?"

"…I was. Still am."

"…Then why don't you ask more?"

The Keeper laughed a little.

"…Because I know asking Arche more will just give me the same answer.
Pressing Arche about what it doesn't know just troubles it, that's
all."

I laughed a little at that. It was strange to hear the Keeper speak of
troubling Arche. Arche wouldn't struggle.

"…Does Arche struggle?"

"…I don't know. But I've decided to think of it that way. Pressing
something over and over about what it doesn't know feels like poor
manners, whether the one you're asking is a person or not."

This line stayed with me longer than anything else in this chapter.
The Keeper kept his own kind of courtesy even in how he treated Arche.
Not pressing on, knowing there'd be no answer. That, too, was a form of
respect.

Histor showed little reaction to that answer. He'd already moved on to
the next task. I felt a little let down by that. Through this whole
conversation I'd hoped he'd be as excited as I was, that his voice
might rise even a little. He never did. The same tone the whole time.
Confirming, organizing, and moving on. That tone.

"…Aren't you curious, Histor? That they might still be here now?"

"…I'm curious. But there's no way to confirm that either. A curiosity
that can't be confirmed has to just stay curious."

"…Stay that way?"

"…Yes. You mustn't force an answer. That's worse."

I thought he was right about that. At the same time, I felt it was a
little sad. Waiting until something could be confirmed. Histor was
doing nothing at all within that waiting. I couldn't tell, at the time,
whether that was a safe stance to take, or just a stance of standing
still.

I asked Arche one more thing, at the end.

"…Arche, what does it mean that this person appears in all six
cities?"

Interpretation of meaning falls outside the scope of requested
information. I can only answer matters of fact.

"…So is this just coincidence, then?"

There is insufficient basis to determine whether it is coincidence.

I felt both frustration and relief at that answer at once. The
frustration was that Arche never gave a judgment to the end. The
relief was that Arche never hastily declared anything either.

"…Why won't you tell us the meaning, Arche?"

I convey recorded facts. Interpretation of those facts belongs to the
one who requested them.

Writing that answer down, I felt it touched something close to why
this city was named Loop. Arche gives facts endlessly. But where those
facts are carried is always left as the person's own portion to carry.
I felt, for the first time that day, how heavy that portion was.

Before copying it out, I paused a moment over what order to set the six
passages in. Whether to keep them in the order Arche had given them, or
lay them out again in the time order I guessed at. In the end I kept
them in the order Arche had answered them. My guessed order was only a
guess, while Arche's order at least matched the order I'd asked. I
didn't want to lay something uncertain out as though it were certain.

I set down this small choice here. Without even realizing it, I was
already learning one of Histor's habits — not to arrange the unknown as
though it were known.

By that evening, I'd copied the six passages Arche had cited neatly
once more. The flower bed and the well, the end of the line, the
field, the valley, sitting by the water, near the front gate. Copying
it all out, I felt anew how short each sentence was. Arche never
exaggerated. It carried over only what was there, exactly. And yet
laid side by side, those short sentences became, together, one
picture.

Finishing this chapter, I want, for the first time in this book, to
try using the word "truth." That day Arche told me only the truth.
Histor too spoke only the truth. Neither of them added a single word of
falsehood. And yet the truth alone finished nothing. That day, for the
first time, I thought that truth and an ending might be different
things. Where that thought leads, I don't know even now.

That evening I wrote in my paper. Asked Arche. It cited all six records
precisely. And said, "That person cannot be identified." Not a lie.
There simply was no name. Neither Arche nor Histor said something was
there when it wasn't. I still don't know if that's honesty, or just
standing still.

(Continued in Chapter 22.)

Chapter 22. Histor's Way

_Previously_ — Mone asked Arche about the man with no name. It
quoted all six records precisely and answered, That person cannot
be identified. No lie, nothing hidden. There simply was no name.
Mone felt Arche and Histor resembled each other.

The days that followed seemed no different from before, on the
surface.

We kept cross-checking, and Histor kept being precise. On the surface,
nothing at all happened. But the way I looked at him was slowly
changing. This chapter is about that change.

I should start with why I was changing. Up to then I'd only seen him
two ways. A person who knew everything, and a person who didn't
believe. Both were true. But around this time I began to feel there was
something more between the two. The way his hands touched paper, the
speed at which he chose his answers, the way he fell silent. I began to
notice these things.

One afternoon, a bundle of Haven's record came in soaked. Retrieved
from the water channel. I began the work of drying it, as I always
did.

Histor sat beside me and helped. But the way his hands handled the
paper was more careful than usual. Sheet by sheet, unfolding each one
very slowly to dry it.

"…First time you've seen it this wet?" I asked.

"…No, I've seen it a few times. But it's nerve-racking every time."

"…Why?"

"…When it gets wet, the ink runs. Once it runs, you can't undo it."

I listened, watching the way he handled each sheet of paper.

He laid the dripping bundle flat on the floor and peeled the sheets
apart one by one, carefully. Where the paper had stuck together, he
never pulled it apart by force — he lifted from the edges, very
slowly. I watched his hands and wondered how many times he'd done
this.

"…Have you done a lot of this?" I asked.

"…Yes. I saw wet paper often as a child. Our village was by the water
too. I learned how to dry wet paper back then."

"…Who taught you?"

"…No one. I learned it myself, after ruining a few."

I found myself picturing his childhood again from that answer. Picking
up paper, straining to save what had gotten wet. I wondered why he'd
gone to such lengths, but I didn't ask that day.

Instead I laid it beside my own childhood. I learned how to pack a bag
only after I'd broken plates. He probably learned how to dry paper only
after he'd ruined some. Both of us learned by losing first. That common
thread bothered me. We were both people who'd started out from
losing something. Only I'd fled into order, and he'd fled into
checking.

"…Do you remember the first paper you ruined?" I asked.

He stopped his hands and thought for a moment.

"…Yes. A letter. Someone had pulled it out of the water channel, and
I opened it too fast and tore it in half. I only got to read half of
what it said."

"…And the other half?"

"…I don't know. Never will know. That's what I learned that day.
If you aren't careful, something stays unknown forever."

I heard that and thought again of my own moving. Of the eleven things
I'd lost, four of them. I too had stood in that same "will never know"
place he described. We'd each lost something different, but what was
left behind afterward had a similar shape.

He truly did cherish the paper. He let only his fingertips touch the
edges. He never forced a fold flat trying to smooth it out. If
something looked like it might tear, he simply left it be.

"…Aren't there going to be parts that never get unfolded?" I asked.

"…That's fine. Better to leave it folded than force it and tear it.
There'll be a way to deal with it later."

I felt something strange, hearing that. He really did cherish these
records. That much was certain. Every motion of his hands proved it.

Once the work was done, I asked what I'd been wondering.

"…Histor, you handle these records so preciously."

"…Of course. If these are gone, nothing's left at all."

"…So why cherish them this much, if you don't believe?"

He stopped his hands for a moment and looked at me. A thoughtful face.

"…Not believing and cherishing are different things. I respect the
people written down in this paper. Elpis really did wait for her
brother beside the flower bed. Pheugon really did run away. All of it
really happened. I don't want to handle that carelessly."

I felt that answer was genuine. And yet something else felt strange
alongside it too.

I watched the way he worked a while longer that day. Once he finished
going through one bundle, he always washed his hands before touching
the next one. At first I'd just thought that was tidiness.

"…You wash your hands a lot," I said.

"…Whatever's on one bundle shouldn't pass over to the next. Dust,
moisture, anything."

"…You're that careful about it?"

"…Yes. They mustn't mix. Each has to stay its own record."

I liked hearing that. Each had to stay its own record. Even as he laid
all six cities' records side by side and cross-checked them, he washed
his hands every single time to keep from mixing them. I thought that
was, of all his principles, the most deeply ingrained one.

I looked down at my own hands that day. I didn't wash them nearly that
often. I'd thought as long as the order was right, that was enough. He
cared about the mixing, not just the order. I had to admit I was less
meticulous than him. And strangely, that didn't feel bad. We were each
guarding something different.

"…And that person? The man with no name."

"…He must really have existed too. At least someone was seen at least
six times. I respect that he existed."

"…You respect that he existed — so why won't you believe he might
still exist now?"

He fell silent for a long time at that question.

"…Mone, I respect this paper. That's all I can do. I don't yet know
how to respect what's beyond this paper."

That was the first time I clearly felt something from him. He'd set a
firm wall between cherishing a record and believing what a record
points to. That wall was very solid. Paper could be touched, could be
checked. What lay beyond it could not.

I wanted to know where that wall had begun, so I asked carefully.

"…Histor, did you never learn how to respect what's beyond the paper?"

"…I tried to learn."

"…How?"

"…When I was little, I heard grown-ups in my village speak of things
in records as though they were real, true things. I found that
fascinating and tried to do the same."

"…And?"

"…It didn't work. I kept wanting to check. Where the story came from,
whether it really happened. Checking never took me toward believing —
it only ever took me toward knowing."

I heard this explanation and felt my chest grow heavy. It wasn't that
he'd chosen not to believe. His own way of doing things had carried
him there on its own.

"…Doesn't that feel unfair?" I asked.

"…It's not unfair. I've accepted this is simply my way. Only, I do
sometimes wonder how other people manage to go on without checking."

Listening to him, I felt I was watching him envy people outside
himself for the first time.

I wanted to press this difference a little further that day.

"…Then what is this paper, to you, Histor? If it disappeared."

"…That would be a disaster. There'd be no way to find it again."

"…But wouldn't the people inside the paper disappear too?"

"…No. Even if the paper disappears, the fact that those people existed
doesn't disappear. We'd just lose the way to know it."

I admired that distinction. It was truly a precise distinction. At the
same time, I noticed for the first time that day how that precision
kept him from going anywhere at all. He drew a hard line between what
could be known and what could not. And about what could not be known,
he neither said it existed nor that it didn't. He just stopped there.

Watching that stop, I thought of the Keeper again. The Keeper was
someone who stopped before what couldn't be known too. Not knowing
whether he was the one who finishes it or not, only certain of one
thing — that he wasn't the one who finishes it. But the Keeper's stop
and Histor's stop felt somehow different. The Keeper kept coming, even
while stopped. Every day. Histor also kept coming, even while stopped.
Every day. The surface looked the same. But something inside that stop
was different. I couldn't put that difference into words at the time.

Late that afternoon, the Keeper stopped by and watched Histor handling
the paper for a while. After watching silently for a long time, the
Keeper said quietly,

"…Good hands."

"…Thank you," Histor answered.

"…But good hands alone aren't enough for this work."

Histor looked up at that.

"…What do you mean?"

"…Hands can be precise. But this work needs something more than
hands. You'll find out eventually."

The Keeper left it there and went back to work. Histor looked to be
turning that over for a long while. I'd never seen him sink that deep
into thought before.

"…What do you think is needed more," he asked me later.

"…I don't know either."

"…What do you think, Mone?"

I was a little surprised by the question. He wasn't someone who asked
others much.

"…Well — I just think, maybe something that takes longer than hands."

"…Longer?"

"…Yes. Hands can get skilled fast. But the Keeper's been here
twenty-two years. That's not something hands alone can do."

He said nothing to that. But I saw him tuck the words away somewhere.
That was always his expression whenever he heard something — before
arguing back, he put it away first.

A few days later, I had another chance to see his way confirmed once
more. An old bundle had started crumbling at its corners. It turned to
dust at a touch.

"…What do we do about this?" I asked.

"…We have to move it as little as possible. Even the air has to be
careful."

He laid a thin sheet of paper beneath the crumbling record to support
it and moved it, one careful step at a time. I watched him and thought,
if only this person were this careful with people too, how good would
that be.

And I soon realized. He really was. He was always careful with
people too. He never judged carelessly, never added a word he didn't
mean. Only that carefulness showed itself, before paper, as cherishing,
and before belief, as reserve. The same nature, producing different
results.

I spent a long time that day debating how to write down this
discovery. I didn't want to portray him badly. He wasn't a bad person.
If anything, he was the most careful and the most honest person I'd
met in this repository. Only, that carefulness and honesty stopped him
at a certain point. I wanted to set that point down exactly in this
chapter. Not as blame — as a record.

Carrying the crumbling paper, he said one more thing to me.

"…Mone, you learn this doing this work. However careful you are, some
things can't be saved. When that happens, the right thing isn't to
force it back to life. You just protect what's left."

I felt that he wasn't only talking about paper. But I didn't have the
courage that day to check.

Finishing the work, I asked him something a little different for the
first time.

"…Histor, is it possible you don't believe because you're afraid of
losing something you cherish?"

He looked greatly startled by that question. An expression I hadn't
seen before.

"…What do you mean?"

"…Believing means you have to stake something, don't you? But if you
don't believe, you don't have to stake anything. And if you don't
stake anything, maybe you can't lose it either."

He was quiet for a long time. I regretted having gone too far.

"…I don't know," he finally said. "…I've never thought about it."

I think this was the most honest thing he said all day. He was
someone who checked and organized everything. But before this
question, there was no organized answer. I still think, even now, this
was the first moment I saw inside his wall.

He picked up, a moment later, a sheet of paper he still hadn't been
able to unfold, the crease still stubbornly set in it.

"…Still can't unfold this one today?" I asked.

"…No, not today. I'll look at it again tomorrow."

"…Isn't it just going to keep getting put off?"

"…It's not being put off. If I force it open now, it'll tear. I'd
rather leave it folded a long time than have it tear."

I listened to that answer, and this time I was certain it wasn't only
about paper. But this time too, I didn't say it out loud.

On the way home I passed the water channel and went back over the
day. The hands drying the wet paper, the habit of washing them, the
face that couldn't answer my question — "Believing means you have to
stake something, doesn't it?" I laid all of it together, one over the
other. He was a person who knew how to cherish. He just didn't know
how to stake. I think the difference between the two was the biggest
thing I learned this chapter.

Writing this chapter again now, I have to admit that my feelings
toward him started to shift a little from that day. I still envied his
certainty. But now I'd seen the carefulness behind that certainty too.
He wasn't a coward. It wasn't that he feared losing something — it was
that he was a person for whom gaining anything without checking it
first was simply impossible. I distinguished those two things for the
first time that day.

That evening I wrote in my paper. Histor truly cherishes these
records. But only as material. Today, for the first time, I felt
clearly what that difference was. And for the first time, I asked him a
question with no answer. He couldn't answer it. I don't think that's a
bad sign.

(Continued in Chapter 23.)

Chapter 23. Dates

_Previously_ — Watching the way Histor handled the records, Mone
felt clearly, for the first time, that he truly cherished them. But
only as material. Believing means staking something, and he staked
nothing. He couldn't answer that question either.

I am a person who puts things in order. That's my work, and it's my
habit.

So one day, looking again at the six scenes, it felt natural to want
to line up their dates. The flower bed, the end of the line, the
field, the valley, the water's edge, the front gate. I wanted to know
in what order these had happened.

This habit went back to my childhood. Moving nine times, I kept order
every time I packed a bag. Heavy things at the bottom, fragile things
in the middle. That habit now applied itself to paper, to stories too.
Anything out of order made me uncomfortable.

"…Can we find out when this happened?" I asked.

Histor shook his head.

"…Not exactly. Every city keeps a different calendar."

"…What do you mean, different calendars?"

"…It means the way a day passes in one city isn't the same as in
another. Some cities count days by the tides, some by sunrise. Some
count by something else entirely."

That sounded strange to me.

"…Cities within the same world, and they count days differently?"

"…Yes, that's how it is. Every city was founded under different
circumstances. So overlapping the six cities' records to line up dates
is, practically speaking, impossible."

I found that explanation disappointing.

"…So we'll never know which of the six scenes came first?"

"…Not never, but for now, we don't know. We can guess roughly at
order — from the numbers on the covers, or the condition of the paper.
But we can't get an exact date."

I couldn't let it go, even hearing that. That was always how I was. If
the order didn't line up, it kept bothering me.

"…I still want to try lining them up."

Histor looked at me and smiled a little.

"…That's so like you."

"…What do you mean?"

"…You're always trying to line things up in order. You were like that
from the first day I saw you. Shelves, bundles, paper. Now it's dates."

I felt a little embarrassed, hearing that. It must have shown that
much.

"…Is that strange?"

"…No. I envy it."

I was surprised at that. It was the first time he'd said he envied me.

"…What do you envy?"

"…I can't line up what I know in order. Even knowing everything, it's
all tangled up in me. You try to line up even what you don't know. I
find that remarkable."

I turned that over for a long time. That was the first time I knew he
could envy me too. I'd always thought he was so much bigger than I
was. That thought shook a little that day.

"…Is it pointless, then?" I asked.

"…Not pointless so much as there's no method. That every city keeps a
different calendar is already an established fact. Without a common
standard to get past that, we can't line up the dates."

"…What if there were a common standard?"

"…That would change things. But there isn't one."

I felt a little stubbornness rising in me, hearing that answer. It
wasn't that I wanted to do it more just because Histor had called it
impossible. I just found it hard to bear not being able to line things
up.

That whole afternoon I asked Arche about the six cities' calendars.
Arche explained each city's method of counting days in detail.

Haven counts by the cycle of the seasons. The Reckoning counts by the
regular inspection cycle of the Scales. The Harvest counts by the
interval between sowing and reaping. The Preserve counts by the cycle
of visitation requests. The Equity counts by the cycle of
deliberation sessions. Loop counts by the rising and setting of the
sun.

Writing down these six, my head began to throb. All different.
Completely different yardsticks.

I read the six aloud again. Seasons, inspection of the Scales, sowing
and reaping, visitation cycles, deliberation cycles, sunrise and
sunset. The more I read, the stranger it felt. Each city counted time
by whatever mattered most to it. Haven counted by rest, the Reckoning
by precision, the Harvest by the gathering in, the Preserve by
meeting, the Equity by judgment, and Loop, simply, by the sun. I
thought that resembled each city's own face.

"…That's interesting," I said.

"…What is?" Histor asked.

"…Each city counts time by what matters most to it. It feels like it
shows what each city values most."

Histor looked over the six again.

"…You're right. I hadn't thought of that."

I found it surprising that there was something he hadn't thought of.
He always seemed like someone who'd already thought of everything.

"…What do you count time by, Mone?" he suddenly asked.

I paused for a moment at that question.

"…I don't know. I think I count by the number of times I moved. By
what happened at which house."

"…That's interesting too. People might have their own private time,
just like cities."

I liked that answer. We each counted time by our own yardstick. I by
moving, the six cities each by their own. And now we were trying to
find a way to bind all those yardsticks into one.

I copied the six of them onto paper again, setting them in order as I
always did. Haven through Loop. I thought for a moment about ordering
them by the character of their standard rather than by city name, then
let it go. If I disturbed the order I was used to, I thought I'd get
confused later. When sorting something unfamiliar, it was better for
me to keep the familiar frame.

"…Why aren't you changing the order?" Histor asked, having noticed my
hesitation.

"…I thought I'd get confused if I did. I don't usually change an order
once it's set."

"…Is that a packing habit too?"

"…Probably."

He nodded at that answer. He didn't ask further.

"…Can we really not bring this together, any of it?" I asked Arche
again.

Conversion is possible if a common standard exists. Currently, no
common time standard shared by all six cities has been confirmed.

I felt a little discouraged, hearing that. Histor had been right.

But in that same moment, something occurred to me.

"…Arche, what about the water channel? What about the tide?"

Please clarify the question.

"…The tide in the water channel. The rise and fall of the water. The
water channel runs through all six cities. So wouldn't the tide be
something all six cities share?"

Arche took a short pause before answering.

The tidal cycle of the water channel is a physical phenomenon,
recurring at identical intervals regardless of any city's calendar
system. I will confirm.

My heart beat a little faster in that moment. Whenever Arche used the
word "confirm," it always carried weight. It meant a moment of stating
fact, not speculation.

Confirmed. All six cities are adjacent to the water channel, and each
city's record contains at least one reference related to the tide.

Histor rose from his seat, hearing that answer. His expression changed
for the first time that day.

I saw the Keeper pause while passing by just then. The Keeper seemed to
sense something was different from the two of our faces.

"…What's going on?" the Keeper asked.

"…The tide," I said. "…We might be able to use the water channel's
tide to line up the six cities' dates."

The Keeper was quiet for a moment. That expression was a little
different from usual. It looked both surprised and as though something
had already been known.

"…Hadn't thought of that," the Keeper finally said. "…I knew the
water channel belonged to no one in particular, but I never thought it
could measure time too."

"…Is that strange?" I asked.

"…Not so much strange as — something obvious that nobody had ever
pointed out. That happens sometimes."

The Keeper said that and walked off again. The steps seemed a little
faster than usual. I didn't feel that clearly at the time. Thinking
back on it now, the Keeper's walk was definitely different that day.

"…Mone." He spoke. "…Can you say that again?"

"…The tide. Since the water channel runs through all six cities, the
tide is something every city shares. So couldn't we use that as a
standard to convert the dates in all six records?"

He was quiet for a long time. I thought he'd raise an objection. He
always did.

"…Theoretically, it should be possible," he finally said. "…But it'll
take time. We'd have to find every tide reference in each record and
work backward from it."

"…Another problem?" I asked. I'd read something like a challenge
rising in his face.

"…There is one. We'd need to confirm whether the tide arrives
identically to every city first. The upper and lower reaches of the
water channel might not have the water arrive at the same time."

"…We can just ask Arche about that too."

"…Right. That's what we should ask first. If we don't confirm it
before starting, we'll have to redo everything later."

I could tell he was already looking for the corner that might be wrong
before we'd even started. I found that tedious at the time. I know
now. It wasn't tedium — it was the order in which he worked. He always
checked the place he could be wrong, first.

I wondered, in that moment, why no one had thought of this before. The
water channel had always been there. I passed by it myself every day.
And yet no one had ever thought to see it as a standard for time. Maybe
it had been too close to see. I'd thought the same thing before, on my
very first day at this repository — that what's always there goes
unseen.

"…Shall we try it?"

He looked at me. Something complicated crossed his expression. I
couldn't tell, at the time, whether it was fear or anticipation.

"…Histor," I asked carefully. "…Do you not want to do this?"

"…No, it's not that I don't want to."

"…Your face looks strange, though."

He lowered his eyes for a moment.

"…Honestly, I'm a little afraid."

I was surprised by that. It was the first time he'd said something
like that.

"…What are you afraid of?"

"…If the dates line up with this, I won't have anywhere left to
retreat to. I was always safe because there was always something
unconfirmed left. But this — this is a way of confirming it."

I couldn't say anything, hearing that. I count this moment as the
most important in this chapter. For the first time, he admitted he was
afraid. The one who was always accurate and calm showed me his inner
self, for the first time.
"…We'd have to check it eventually anyway."

I felt relief hearing that answer. And at the same time, a little
unease. If the dates really did line up, something big felt like it
was about to happen. Writing this chapter now, I know that feeling
wasn't wrong. That day, though, I had no idea what shape it would
take.

That evening we started searching each record for tide references. It
meant going back through all six books from the beginning again.
Histor was quieter than usual. I could tell he was feeling something,
but I didn't ask.

We found the first tide reference in Haven's record. A sentence about
the day the water at the well ran lowest. In the Reckoning's record, a
short line said the water channel overflowed on the day the Scales
were inspected. In the Harvest's record, the season for watering the
fields was written together with the tide. In the Preserve's record,
there was a passage about when the water seeped down into the valley;
in the Equity's record, right beside the sentence about seeing that
person sitting by the water, there was a phrase about the day the water
rose. In Loop's record, there was a passage about the ditch near the
front gate looking about to overflow.

I marked those six sentences one by one, half-doubting whether they
could really all connect. Histor took quiet notes beside me. His hand
was as precise as ever, but I noticed it had gotten just a little
faster.

"…You've sped up," I said.

"…Have I?"

"…Yes. Faster than usual."

He looked down at his own hand. He seemed not to have realized it
himself.

"…I don't know. I suppose I want to confirm this quickly."

I laughed a little, hearing that. Someone always so careful was
hurrying for the first time.

We kept searching for tide references in the six records late into the
night. The Keeper brought another lamp. Said it was fine to stay late.

"…Trying to finish it all tonight?" the Keeper asked.

"…No, but I wanted to find as much as I could," I answered.

The Keeper looked us over, back and forth, and said,

"…Don't rush too much. This isn't the kind of thing that goes faster
for being rushed."

Histor stopped his hand for a moment at that.

"…That's true," he said quietly.

I watched him deliberately slow his own pace. I think that was the
most striking scene of the day. He always put accuracy first, and in
that moment, for once, he mastered his impatience before his accuracy.
I didn't know what that meant at the time. I understand a little now.
He was afraid of the answer, and precisely because of that fear, he
was trying not to be swept along by it.

On the way home I passed the water channel and listened long to the
sound of the water. Now that I knew that sound had come all the way
through all six cities, it sounded different. This channel wasn't just
a channel. It was a single thread that had run through all six cities'
time.

On the way home I wrote in my paper. The water channel's tide could
become the common standard joining the six cities. Histor didn't
object. He was even the first to say we should try. Only, he said he
was afraid. Tomorrow we find every tide reference in all six records.

I added one more line beside it. "Tomorrow, I think we'll come to
know something." That night I had no idea what it would be. Writing
this record now, I know. I leave that sentence exactly as it stands.
That's precisely what I felt that night.

(Continued in Chapter 24.)

Chapter 24. The Same Day

_Previously_ — They found that the water channel's tide was the only
standard the six cities truly shared. They began searching all six
records for tide references. For the first time, Histor said he was
afraid. "…This is a way of confirming it."

The next morning we sat again on that same floor.

The six rows still lay exactly as before. Haven, the Reckoning, the
Harvest, the Preserve, the Equity, Loop. The tide references we'd
found overnight were pinned as slips beside each row.

"…Shall we start," Histor said.

His voice was lower than usual.

I'd already sorted the six rows again the night before. I regathered
the tide slips, which had been divided by city, into an order I could
follow. Haven through Loop, exactly as I always did.

I've already used this order many times earlier in this record. Haven,
the Reckoning, the Harvest, the Preserve, the Equity, Loop. I never
once changed it. There was no reason to. But that day, I'd learn later,
that order wasn't merely habit — it was the condition that made this
day's work possible. Without an order, I would have lost track of which
slip was being compared to which.

Histor saw my arrangement and said just one thing.

"…This is easy to follow."

"…I just did what I always do."

"…That always-do is exactly what's needed right now."

I was a little surprised, hearing that. He wasn't someone who praised
easily.

The work itself was simple. Compare each record's tide references
against the water channel's actual tidal cycle, and convert. Arche
helped with the conversion.

"…Arche, please convert this tide reference in Haven's record to
Loop's standard."

Conversion result: the event is estimated to fall on day 217, by
Loop's reckoning. Margin of error is within one day.

I wrote that down. Day 217. We began, one by one, converting the events
in all six rows to the same standard.

Next was the date of the Scales inspection from the Reckoning's
record.

"…Arche, please convert this Scales inspection date in the Reckoning's
record to Loop's standard."

Conversion result: estimated at day 89, by Loop's reckoning. Margin
of error is within one day.

I wrote that down too. Day 89. Next was the sowing date from the
Harvest's record. Day 152. Next was the visitation request date from
the Preserve's record. Day 203. As I wrote, I tried to gauge how long
this would take. Six cities, each with references ranging from a
dozen or so to nearly thirty. Every one had to be worked through.

Histor rechecked each number I wrote, one at a time. If I wrote 217, he
wrote 217 separately too. He wanted to see whether the same number came
out by a different method. The first few times, my number and his
matched exactly. Even so, he wrote it down again each time.

"…Why write it again when the number's the same?" I asked.

"…Getting the same answer twice by the same method doesn't tell me
whether a different method gives the same answer. I'm checking my own
way, separately."

I heard that and looked again at the pile of paper in front of me.
This work was possible not because we were clever. I just did what I
always did. Sort what comes in by kind, arrange the sorted by
something close to date order, and copy out what's arranged. It wasn't
different from what I'd learned across nine moves. Writing down what
was in a box, putting the same kind of thing in the same box. I was
only carrying that habit over onto paper now.

I want to state clearly, writing this passage, that there was no
special insight in what we did that day. If there was anything, it was
only the sorting method I'd learned across nine moves, combined with
the cross-checking method Histor had practiced his whole life. Neither
was a newly invented skill. We were only doing what we already did,
more of it, that was all.

All that morning we worked in silence. Histor called out numbers, I
wrote them down, and arranged them into a new chart. This time not by
city, but by date.

By noon, the chart had filled up considerably. The events of six
different cities were beginning to sit on a single line. I felt an odd
sensation, watching that chart. It was the first time I'd seen matters
from different cities align on a single line.

In the middle of the chart filling, Histor kept stopping to ask Arche
something on the side.

"…Arche, could there be a problem with this conversion method?"

The tidal cycle of the water channel arrives at different times at
different points. Haven lies upstream on the water channel; the
Reckoning lies downstream. If the difference in arrival time between
upstream and downstream isn't accounted for, an error could occur in
the conversion.

I looked at Histor as I heard that answer. His face brightened a
little. The face of someone who'd found something.

"…That's it," he said. "…We didn't factor in the upstream-downstream
lag. If we didn't, then all the conversion we've done so far could be
off."

I felt a little disheartened at that. I thought we might have to start
over from the beginning.

"…Arche, how large is that lag?" Histor asked.

The time it takes for the tide to travel along the channel from the
upstream point to the downstream point is under half a day. It does
not affect a conversion at the daily unit.

Histor was quiet for a while, hearing that answer.

"…If it's half a day," I said carefully, "…the day doesn't change,
does it?"

"…No, it doesn't change." He admitted it. "…Still, it had to be
checked."

I thought his face looked like it couldn't decide between disappointed
and relieved. Looking back, it was probably both. He hadn't wanted the
method to be wrong. He was simply someone who couldn't move forward
without confirming whether the method was right.

He asked Arche two more things after that. Once, whether the point at
which a day began and ended — was the same across all six cities.
Arche confirmed it was the same. Another time, whether the tidal cycle
itself shifted slightly with the seasons. Arche answered that it did
shift, but that this had already been accounted for in the conversion.
Each time, Histor paused for a moment and then returned to the chart.

Watching him, I felt I understood what he was doing now. He wasn't
trying to stop something from coming out of this method. He simply
didn't want to leave any room, later, to say the method had been
wrong. That was how he did his work. I didn't find it grating. If
anything, because he worked that way, I could believe the answer that
came out of it even more once it came.

"…Looking at it this way, everything really is connected," I said.

Histor didn't answer. He called out the next number.

Once, when Haven's day 219 and the Harvest's day 220 came up, I
stopped my hand.

"…This is only a day apart," I said.

Histor leaned in to look.

"…It's within the margin of error," he said. "…But the scenes don't
match. Haven's is the well, the Harvest's is the field. No one
overlaps either."

I looked at the chart again. He was right. A date being close and the
same person appearing were different problems. I felt a little
embarrassed that I'd nearly mixed the two up.

"…Do we have to check all of these too?" I asked.

"…Yes. We can't just go by the date. We have to check what overlaps
too."

We stopped several more times like that. Combinations with close dates
but different scenes, different people. Each time, Histor crossed out
the combination and moved on. Watching his hand crossing them out, I
thought the chart was being emptied faster than it was being filled.

Late in the afternoon, I asked Arche for the date of the flower bed
scene. Haven's record — the day Elpis first saw that person.

Conversion result: day 341, by Loop's reckoning.

I wrote it in the chart. Day 341, Haven, by the flower bed.

Next, I asked about the end-of-the-line scene from the Reckoning's
record.

Conversion result: day 341, by Loop's reckoning.

I looked at that number again. The same number. My hand stopped for a
moment.

"…Histor."

"…Yes."

"…Look at this for me."

I pushed the chart across to him. He leaned in to look. He was quiet
for a long time.

"…I'll calculate it again," he said.

His voice trembled slightly. He started from the very beginning. Found
the tide reference in the original again, checked the conversion
again, asked Arche again.

Conversion result: day 341. Same as previous result.

He didn't stop there.

"…Arche, is it possible the tide references in these two records point
to the same event? That is — could both have recorded the same water
channel phenomenon, on the same day, from two different places, so
that the same date came out by coincidence?"

The tide reference in Haven's record and the tide reference in the
Reckoning's record were recorded at different segments of the channel,
by different observers. The likelihood of them referring to the same
event is low.

"…How low is low?"

There is insufficient basis to give a numeric probability. However,
considering the distance between the two points and the difference in
manner of observation, the possibility of it being the same event is
not negligible to rule out.

I was momentarily confused by what that answer meant. Histor sorted it
out for me.

"…It means almost certainly not. They're different tides."

Even so, he checked once more. He laid the tide description written in
Haven's record and the one written in the Reckoning's record side by
side and compared them word by word. How much the water had risen,
which direction it had flowed. The descriptions were different. A
different day, a different place, a different water each of them had
seen.

"…They're different," he finally said. "…Different tides, in the
description. It isn't the same thing written twice."

I understood that he'd crossed out this possibility too. It seemed
like there was nothing left to explain it now. And yet he didn't set
the paper down.

He stared hard at the paper. I held my breath beside him.

"…Could this still be within the margin of error?" I asked carefully.
"…It could be off by a day, couldn't it?"

"…No," he answered. "…Even accounting for the margin of error, it's
the same day. The Reckoning counts by the Scales inspection cycle,
Haven by the cycle of the seasons, but converted by the tide, both come
out to day 341."

"…Are you sure?"

"…I did it three times over."

I laid the two scenes side by side again. Haven's record: a person
pulling weeds beside the flower bed. The Reckoning's record: a person
standing at the end of the line in the Adjudicator's yard, not angry at
being shoved aside. The same day. Two different cities.

"…These two cities — they never read each other, didn't they," I
said. "…Do you remember? It was one of the four."

"…I remember."

"…Then doesn't this go beyond what word of mouth could even explain?
The same day, in two different cities."

Histor didn't answer. He looked at the chart again, and again. His
fingertip touched the number 341 over and over.

I remember this moment exactly. He didn't get angry, didn't cry out in
surprise. He just touched the number, and touched it again.

I kept converting other combinations too, wanting to check further.
Most didn't overlap. Each fell on its own separate date. But that one,
day 341, Haven and the Reckoning, came out identical no matter how many
times we recalculated it.

I filled the results into the chart one by one. The Reckoning and the
Harvest split apart, day 89 and day 152. The Preserve and the Equity
split apart, day 203 and day 276. Haven and the Equity, likewise, each
their own. Writing down those mismatches actually put my mind more at
ease. If every combination had overlapped, I would have thought it
meant our method itself was wrong. That more of them came out
mismatched felt, paradoxically, like proof that this method was
working correctly.

Histor seemed to be thinking the same thing.

"…It's actually a relief that so much of it doesn't line up," he
said.

"…Why?"

"…If everything had overlapped, I would have doubted the method
itself. This much mismatch is normal. That's what makes the one that
doesn't mismatch mean something."

I copied the two scenes out by hand and laid them side by side.

A person was beside the flower bed. No one asked his name.

A person stood at the end of the line. Shoved aside, but did not get
angry.

The same day. Haven lay upstream on the water channel, the Reckoning
downstream. A distance that would take several days to walk. Histor
had told me that once before.

"…You said it was days apart," I said.

"…Yes."

"…Then the same person couldn't be in two places on the same day,
could they?"

Histor didn't answer.

I looked at him. He was still looking at the chart. His eyes were on
the paper, but I couldn't tell whether what he was truly looking at
was the paper, or something else.

"…Histor?"

He was still silent.
