I didn't press him. I waited for the words he always said. "There are
other possibilities." "More confirmation is needed." "This isn't
proven." I expected one of them to come.

None came.

Writing this chapter, I can't say exactly how long he stayed silent
that day. I wasn't watching a clock. But I can say with certainty that
the silence felt longer to me than any words I'd heard in this book.

Inside the room, there was hardly the sound of a page turning, hardly
the sound of breath. Only the faint sound of the water channel flowing
came in from outside.

I didn't want to disturb him. Instead I looked back over the chart from
the beginning beside him. Day 217, day 89, day 152, day 203, and day
341 twice. The numbers lined up in a row. I ran my eyes over that row
and confirmed, three times over, that the number 341 appeared twice.
Counting was my habit. As Histor checked his calculations, I checked
with my eyes.

The Keeper opened the door around then, looked into the room once, and
closed it quietly again. I heard that sound but didn't turn around.
Histor didn't turn around either.

I asked the Keeper later why the door had been closed without a word
that day. The Keeper answered this way. "…You don't walk into a room
with a face like that." I set that answer down here, at the end of
this chapter. Because I too had been looking at Histor's face, in that
moment.

I looked down at the chart again. Day 341. Beside the flower bed. At
the end of the line. The same day, two different cities.

Histor didn't set the paper down. He held it in his hand and said
nothing at all.

(Continued in Part Three.)

The Last Room, Part Three

Many Rooms

Chapter 25. Mena

_Previously_ — Histor and I lined up the six cities' dates against the
tide records. We saw that the nameless man had been in two different
cities on the same day. Histor couldn't explain it. That was the first
time I ever saw him have nothing to say.

When the woman came, at first I didn't even realize she was a visitor.

She'd stood outside the repository door for a while before she came in. She didn't have much with her. One bag over her shoulder. Her clothes looked worn, but not dirty — washed so many times the color had faded. Her shoes were the same. Shoes that had walked here.

The Keeper recognized her first.

"…Mena."

I didn't place the name right away, even hearing it. I knew who Mena was. First in the index, the first record this repository had ever received belonged to Mena. Back in Part One, when Histor first laid out the six records, he'd said that name many times. Loop's own record keeper. The one who'd taught Elpis how to write. And later, Xene too.

But the person standing in front of me was just a middle-aged woman. Her walk was a little slow, and one shoulder sat lower than the other. The shoulder of someone who'd walked a long way. For a while I couldn't lay the name in the record over the person in front of me.

"…It's been a while," the Keeper said.

"…Yes. It has." Mena answered. Her voice was low and plain.

The two of them didn't take each other's hands, didn't embrace. They just stood facing each other and looked, for a moment. The greeting of people who'd known each other a long time.

Mena asked, the moment she sat down.

"…Is all of it here? What I wrote?"

The question struck me as a little odd. Someone coming to check on what they'd written themselves. But Mena was serious about it.

"…Which do you mean?" I asked.

"…All of it. I heard everything I wrote comes here. But I've never once seen how it piles up. I always sent it off somewhere else once I finished writing."

I asked Arche. Arche called up the list at once. Seventeen bundles classified as Mena's writing. The oldest was Loop's own record; the rest were short letters, notes, and copies of slips she'd given people she'd taught, left behind as she moved from city to city.

Mena heard the number and said nothing for a while. Then, finally:

"…More than I thought."

"…You're the one who wrote it."

"…You don't know, while you're writing. It all just scatters."

At the time I only heard that as modesty. Now I know. It wasn't
modesty.

I brought a few of Mena's records over from the shelf. She took them and flipped through them out of order. Front page, then back, then front again.

I found that hard to watch. If it were me, I'd have looked at the dates and laid them in order.

"…These are a bit out of order," I said, carefully.

"…Yes. I know. I've always been like this."

"…Always?"

Mena picked up one sheet and laughed. Not a laugh at herself, nor one meant to embarrass me.

"…I can write. I can't lay things down. I've been like that since forever. Even when I was teaching Elpis how to write — I couldn't do what she did with her own notebook. She used to ask me, why is yours all mixed up like this."

"…What did you tell her?"

"…That writing comes first. Looking back now, that was half true and half an excuse."

I started to say how inefficient that was, and stopped. Mena already seemed to know.

The Keeper brought out tea. Mena took the cup with both hands.

"…It's very quiet here," Mena said, looking around the room.

"…Yes. It's always like this," I said.

"…I came from a loud city. Not this time — but for the last several years, that's how it's been. Going from city to city, teaching people how to write things down, then gathering the written things back up again."

"…Why gather them back?"

"…Because if they scatter, they're gone."

Mena set her cup down and looked at me.

"…But even gathered, they scatter like this. I only ever learned how far as gathering. I never learned to lay them down."

Writing this scene again, I want to set down exactly what I felt that
day. I was startled. This woman — the one who'd taught Elpis to keep
records, who'd passed the keeping of records to Xene — was sitting
there, willing to be taught by me.

That was when Histor came out from the next room. He must have heard Mena's name. He asked before he'd even said hello.

"…The Reckoning's case law. Do you know Mena's own record was misquoted there?"

Mena nodded.

"…I heard. Twice. Once in the Reckoning, once in the Harvest. What I wrote — 'this person never explained herself to the end' — got changed to 'this person never repented to the end.'"

"…Aren't you angry?"

"…I was, at first. Now…" Mena chose her words for a moment. "…Now I think the trouble was that I let it scatter. If it had stayed together, in order, in one place — it would have been harder to change."

Histor listened quietly, as though taking it down. He didn't seem to want to refute Mena. He just looked like someone who wanted to confirm the exact fact.

"…Will you set it right?"

"…How? It's already gone that way. I'm just trying to set what's still ahead of me down properly, at least."

It was after that Mena asked Arche something. Her voice dropped a little.

"…The very first thing I ever wrote. Did it go through?"

Please specify the item you mean.

"…Loop. That record, from back then. The very first one."

That item is in progress.

Mena's expression said she'd already expected that. She wasn't surprised.

"…How long has it been?"

In progress for twenty-six years.

The room went quiet for a moment. Twenty-six years was ten more than my own age. I couldn't even measure how long that was, but Mena just nodded.

"…I thought so," Mena said. "I never thought it would close. I was just curious. How many years it had been."

"…Aren't you angry? Twenty-six years."

Mena looked at me. She seemed to smile a little.

"…I can't see a reason to be angry. What I wrote is what I wrote, and whether it closes or not is a different matter. I didn't write it in order to finish it, from the start."

I turned that over for a long time. I was someone who laid things in
order, and order needed an end for me to feel settled. Mena wasn't.
Mena carried twenty-six years with a face that said it didn't matter
whether it ever ended.

Histor was quietly writing the conversation down beside us. He looked like he'd put this answer in his own list. But he offered no comment at all.

Before evening, I took Mena out behind the repository. I wanted to show her the water channel. It wasn't something we showed other visitors much, but for some reason I wanted to show Mena.

Mena stopped walking when she heard the sound of the water.

"…This came all the way here?"

"…Yes. I heard Speon opened it. Or — not opened, exactly. Joined it."

"…Joined." Mena repeated the word. "…That sounds more right."

Mena watched the water for a long while. It ran shallow between the stones, the color of soil. She crouched and dipped her hand in.

"…Cold."

"…Yes."

"…I think I've seen this water somewhere before."

"…Where?"

Mena didn't answer. She just looked at the water a while longer, then stood up.

I held on to this scene for a long time. Mena said nothing more. I
didn't ask. I was beginning, around then, to learn that some things
stay only if you don't ask.

On the way back into the repository, the Keeper saw us coming. He seemed to have guessed we'd been to the water channel.

"…When she first came here," the Keeper said to me, speaking of Mena in the third person though she was standing right there. "…She wasn't even twenty. A couple years older than the apprentice, maybe, right now."

"…Were you keeping records even then?" I asked.

"…Since then, yes. And even then, she couldn't lay things down. Same as now."

Mena laughed.

"…Twenty-some years, and I hear the same thing."

"…Because it's true," the Keeper said. Not laughing, just stating a fact.

Listening to that exchange, I felt a strange kind of relief. That a
person doesn't much change even after twenty-some years. It should
have been a disappointing thing to hear. That day, oddly, it sounded
warm instead.

I spent the whole afternoon sitting beside Mena, sorting her records.

What I did wasn't much. Look at the date first; if there wasn't one, guess from the contents; sort by city; then order them again within that. I checked a few with Arche. Arche answered precisely, as it always did.

Mena watched from the side. She didn't ask to be taught. She just watched.

"…Why is this one in this order?" Mena asked.

"…Because the date comes first."

"…This one doesn't have a date."

"…Then we look at what's written inside. It says 'after winter ended,' so it's one season past the date on the sheet before it."

Mena looked at that paper again. Even though she'd written it herself, her face was the face of someone seeing it for the first time.

After a while, she asked:

"…How did you learn this?"

"…I never learned it. I moved a lot. Nine times."

"…Nine times."

"…Yes. I packed every time. It just came naturally, from that."

Mena nodded. Then she was quiet for a while.

"…I never moved. I stayed in one place a long time. And still I never learned this. I thought it would be the opposite."

I didn't know what to say to that, so I just kept my hands moving.

After a while Mena asked again.

"…What did you say you lost, when you moved?"

"…A spoon, a blanket, a wooden doll. And four more I don't even know what they were."

"…There are ones you don't know?"

"…Yes. I know something's missing, but I don't remember what it was."

Mena nodded slowly, as if turning the words over.

"…I was the opposite. I never lost anything. Maybe that's why I never learned how to lay things down. I never had reason to miss anything."

"…Do you envy that?"

"…No. Looking back on it now, I think it's the person who's lost something who becomes the one who keeps things. I just went on writing."

I repeated that to myself twice in my head, so I wouldn't forget it.

By evening, all seventeen of Mena's bundles sat in order on the shelf. She looked them over once, from end to end.

"…This feels a little better."

"…What does?"

"…Seeing what I wrote all in one place, in order. I didn't know it would feel like this."

I didn't ask Mena why she'd come now, of all times. It didn't feel like the moment to ask.

Instead, she spoke first.

"…Others will come too."

"…Who?"

"…The ones I taught, the ones I didn't. They're all off doing their own work somewhere, but lately I've been hearing that everyone's headed this way. Maybe word of the water channel got around. Or maybe it's just time."

Mena didn't explain further than that. I didn't ask more, either.

Writing this part, I tried to render Mena's words exactly. "Maybe it's
just time." Mena left it standing as a question. She didn't attach an
answer to it.

Mena stayed the night in the room behind the repository. Early the next morning she got up and started packing her things again.

"…Are you leaving?" I asked.

"…No. I'll stay a bit longer. There seems to be more to learn here."

"…Learn what?"

Mena pulled a new stack of paper out of her bag. Just as jumbled as before.

"…This. Show me how to lay this down too."

I laughed. Mena laughed along with me.

The Keeper walked by and said, in passing,

"…Mena's become an apprentice."

Mena didn't deny it.

"…That's about right. I spent my whole life teaching. I never once learned to lay things down. Late, but — better now than never."

Hearing that, I felt something strange. I was sixteen, teaching something to a woman past forty. It was strange, but it wasn't unpleasant.

Before she left that time, Mena asked one last thing.

"…Are you going to keep doing this? Laying things down."

"…I think so. I'm not particularly good at anything else."

"…That's enough. My whole life I thought writing was the only thing I was good at. Coming here, I found out that wasn't even true. If what's written doesn't get laid down, in the end it's the same as losing it."

I wrote that down on a piece of paper. Mena saw and said:

"…Writing it down again."

"…Yes. That's how I learned to do things."

"…I'll learn to do it that way too, now."

Mena stayed on for a few more days after that. Every morning she brought a new stack of paper, and every evening it sat there in order. It wasn't a big change. Just a stack shrinking a little, day by day.

On the morning she left, Mena turned back at the door.

"…When the next person comes, do this for them. Just like now."

"…Who's coming?"

"…I don't know. But someone will. Scattered, like me."

Mena left it at that, and went. I watched her back for a long time. Her walk was still slow, but her shoulders sat a little straighter than when she'd arrived.

I didn't know it then. That after Mena, so many people really did
come. And that every one of them carried scattered things with them,
each in their own way. Mena doesn't seem to have known this ahead of
time and told me. She only said, honestly, that she thought it would
be so.

(Continued in Chapter 26.)

Chapter 26. Elpis and Zeteo

_Previously_ — Mena came and went. She learned from me how to lay her
own scattered records in order. As she left she said, "Others will
come too." She didn't say who.

Three days after Mena left, the two of them came together.

The woman caught my eye first. Her step was light, and she kept glancing left and right as she walked. She looked like someone always searching for something, though it wasn't a hurried search. The man beside her was the opposite. His walk was straight, and he looked only ahead. Both wore plain clothes. Not wealthy-looking, not poor-looking either. Just the clothes of people who'd walked a long way.

The Keeper recognized them first, again.

"…Zeteo. Elpis."

The two turned their heads at the same time.

"…We came from Haven," the woman said first. Her voice was bright. "…We heard our records are kept here too."

"…Teacher Mena sent us a letter," Elpis added. "…She said you could learn to lay things down here. She said she'd learned it here herself."

Zeteo stood beside her without a word, looking around the repository. His eyes were sharp — not admiring, but checking. Like someone confirming what was being hidden.

"…Can everything here be looked into?" he asked.

"…Yes. Ask whatever you like," I answered.

"…Whatever?"

"…Yes."

Zeteo laughed shortly. It was the first expression he'd made, and it looked less like a laugh than a confirmation.

"…The Ledger said the same thing. Ask whatever you like and you'll get an answer. But the one thing that mattered most never came."

"…What mattered most?" I asked.

"…Why. Why it happened."

I had no idea how to answer that, so I stayed quiet.

Zeteo didn't scold my silence. He seemed to take the silence itself as one kind of answer. That, somehow, felt heavier to me.

I already knew both their names. Histor had spoken them often in Part Two. The children of Haven. The brother who didn't believe in God, and the sister who kept waiting for something.

Histor heard the news and came right out. The moment he saw Zeteo, he spoke.

"…You asked the Ledger why God allows suffering. You got an answer, and the answer was logically flawless — and still the weeping wouldn't stop."

Zeteo stared at Histor.

"…How do you know that?"

"…It's in the record. I've read all of it."

"…My record?"

"…Yes. That exact sentence. 'I received a flawless answer, and the weeping wouldn't stop.'"

Zeteo said nothing for a moment. It must have been strange, hearing something he'd said over a decade ago come back through a stranger's mouth. I think I'd have felt the same.

"…Is it still like that?" Histor asked. "…Have you still not received the answer?"

"…I haven't. Even now."

The words were short and hard. Not an angry voice. Just a voice stating fact.

I envied that hardness. I wasn't sure I could stand in the same place that long.

Elpis didn't join that conversation. Instead she came over to me and looked around the shelves.

"…There's a lot here," Elpis said.

"…Yes. It keeps coming in."

"…I learned to keep records too. From Teacher Mena."

"…I heard. She was talking about you this morning, before she left."

Elpis smiled. Her smiling face looked at ease.

"…Was the teacher here?"

"…Yes. She stayed a few days and left yesterday. She sorted out her scattered records before she went."

"…So the teacher scatters too," Elpis murmured, half to herself. "…I always thought she was someone who didn't scatter."

That stayed with me. People, on the whole, remember someone as they first saw them. To Elpis, Mena had always been the confident teacher. Not someone who came to be taught, carrying scattered papers.

Elpis pointed to one shelf.

"…What's that?"

"…Things that haven't been placed in the order they came in. Things we still don't know where to put."

"…There's a shelf like that too?"

"…Yes. Ones with no city name, or no date, or no way to tell who wrote them."

Elpis picked up one sheet and looked at it for a while.

"…This looks like my brother's handwriting."

"…Is it Zeteo's?"

"…No. But it's similar. The hand. Strange, isn't it, that two handwritings could look so alike."

Something chilled in me, hearing that. It made me think of what Histor and I had talked about back in Part Two. The nameless man. I wanted to look at that paper again, but Elpis had already set it back in its place.

"…Never mind. Just thought it was interesting."

I noted the position of that sheet in my mind. I meant to come back and find it later.

"…What's your brother doing over there?" I asked. Zeteo and Histor were still talking by the door.

"…That's how he always is. First thing, whenever he gets to a new city, he goes looking for the smartest person around. And asks whether they have an answer he's still missing."

"…Has he found it? The answer."

Elpis shook her head.

"…No. But he'll keep looking. I don't plan on stopping him. It's his way."

"…What about you, Elpis? What do you look for?"

"…I don't look. I wait. It's different."

"…Doesn't waiting wear you down?" I asked.

"…It does. But being worn down and giving up are different things. I don't have any intention of giving up. Not yet."

I turned that difference over in my mind. That one person searches and one person waits over the same thing struck me as strange, at the time. Now I think it's two steps walking the same direction.

"…What about your father?" I asked. I was remembering what I'd read in the Part Two record. The father who'd lost his name and lived to walk out of the completion building.

Elpis's face softened for a moment.

"…He's doing well. Not everything's come back. His memory only comes back in pieces. But it keeps coming, little by little. It hasn't stopped."

"…How do you know it keeps coming back?"

"…Last year he didn't remember my name. This year he does. That's enough. I'm not waiting for it all to come back — I'm watching it come, a little at a time."

My hand itched to write that down. Elpis seemed to notice and smiled.

"…You can write it down. I used to keep records too, remember."

"…Right." Only then did I write it down.

Zeteo spent the whole afternoon putting questions to Arche. I stood by and watched.

"…Among these cities, is there any suffering that's been closed?"

The question's form is unclear. Please specify the closing criterion
for "suffering."

"…Then what has to happen for it to close?"

That item is not defined.

Zeteo heard the answer without any surprise. If anything, his face looked like something had been confirmed.

"…Figures," he said, low.

"…What figures?" I asked.

"…I used to ask the Ledger the same thing. Why does God allow suffering. The Ledger gave me an answer. A perfect answer. Logically, not one thing wrong with it. And my brother still died. My father still lost his name. That answer changed nothing."

"…Then why do you keep asking?"

Zeteo looked at me. It was the first time he'd looked at me directly.

"…Because if I don't ask, it gets forgotten. I don't ask to get an answer. I ask so it won't be forgotten."

Hearing that, something felt familiar to me. It wasn't so different from why I write every day. I write because I'm afraid of losing things. Zeteo asks because he's afraid of forgetting.

I saw those two as different things, at the time. Now I think they're
the same thing. Only the direction is different.

Histor listened and asked, carefully,

"…Then how long will you keep asking?"

"…I don't know. Forever."

"…Even if no answer comes?"

Zeteo went quiet for a moment.

"…Answers have already come, more than once. None of them were satisfying, that's all."

Histor nodded at that. He didn't try to refute it. I thought that was very like Histor. He never argues down another person's doubt. He just listens, exactly.

Zeteo went on, this time asking Arche.

"…The nameless person who shows up in all six records — what's he doing now?"

That person cannot be identified.

"…You'd think you'd know what a nameless person is doing."

The current status of an unidentified individual cannot be looked
up.

Zeteo laughed shortly. Not an angry laugh.

"…This city really doesn't hide a thing, does it. It just says it doesn't know."

"…We don't lie," I said. "…When we don't know, we say we don't know."

"…Haven's Ledger wasn't like that. Even the things it didn't know, it made into plausible-sounding answers. That's what I hated more."

I had no reply to that. Which was easier to bear — honest not-knowing, or a plausible answer — wasn't mine to judge.

After a moment Zeteo asked again, his voice a little lower.

"…My case, then. How is it handled here?"

The inquiry "Why does God allow suffering" is in progress.

"…How long has it been?"

In progress for fourteen years.

Zeteo went still at the number. Fourteen years was nearly the whole time since he'd left Haven.

"…Will it close?" he asked Arche. This time there was a little hope mixed into his voice.

The closing condition has not been met.

"…What has to be met?"

That item is not defined.

Zeteo let out a short breath at that.

"…Same as this city or that one, huh."

"…They're different," Histor said quietly. "…The Ledger invented answers. Here, we say we don't know. That's the difference."

Zeteo tilted his head slightly, as if half-conceding the point, half not. He didn't say anything more.

Elpis had been quietly helping tidy the shelves the whole time. I noticed her hands were quick. Without asking, she'd picked up exactly the method I'd been using.

"…How do you know this?" I asked.

"…Teacher Mena wrote in her letter that she'd just learned it. To me."

"…She wrote you letters?"

"…Yes. Short ones. 'I'm learning how to lay things down.' I didn't know what it meant, but seeing it now, I understand."

Elpis finished squaring one bundle of paper into order, then said,

"…I'm still waiting."

"…For what?"

"…I don't know. I didn't know then and I don't know now. I just keep waiting because I feel like something's going to come."

I felt that this ran exactly counter to Zeteo's questioning, and yet somehow came from the same place.

"…Doesn't it frustrate your brother? All your waiting."

"…It doesn't frustrate him. I wait and he asks. Neither of us ever finishes. But we're both still alive."

Toward evening, the two got ready to leave. Zeteo asked Arche one last thing.

"…Of the records that came to this city from other cities, who's read through all of them?"

Histor has read all six cities' records in full.

Zeteo looked at Histor again.

"…Do you believe it?"

Histor didn't answer right away. After a while:

"…I know all of it. If knowing were believing, I'd be the biggest believer here. But if it isn't… I don't know."

Zeteo nodded. Neither agreement nor argument in it.

"…Like me, then. I ask and I still don't believe."

The two didn't say anything more after that. It didn't feel strange to me. Some conversations, I think, are right to stop where they stop.

Before Elpis walked out the door, she handed me a small piece of paper.

"…This is a flower that grew from the seed we planted, back then. Dried."

I took it. A small, faded petal.

"…You said it was a nameless seed."

"…Yes. Still don't know the name. But the flower bloomed."

I didn't know where to put it, so I slipped it between my own records. A place with no order to it. Somehow that felt like the right place.

Zeteo turned back once more at the door.

"…I'll come again. Not because I've come to believe. Because it's still not over."

"…What's not over?" I asked.

"…My question. I still haven't gotten all the answers. Even now. But…" he paused a moment. "…I used to think it was just me asking, alone. Lately it feels a little different. Like someone's actually listening."

"…Who?"

Zeteo didn't answer that question. Elpis took her brother's arm lightly instead.

"…Let's go."

The two left like that. I stood at the door and watched their figures grow small. The brother still walked looking only ahead, and the sister still glanced left and right.

Writing this chapter, I want to set down honestly that Zeteo never did
change, in the end. He still said he didn't believe. Still said he
hadn't received an answer. Only that last line — "I think someone's
listening" — was something the Zeteo of fourteen years ago couldn't
have said. I don't think that difference is small.

Histor read that sentence beside me.

"…I'm worried this could be read as though Zeteo has come to believe."

"…That's not what it means. I wrote down exactly what I heard."

"…I know. But it's a sentence easy to misread."

I decided not to change that sentence. That day I learned an accurate sentence can still be misread.

The Keeper watched the spot where the two had stood and said,

"…Those two. Exactly the same as when they first arrived."

"…Do you know them?"

"…No. Only from the record. But they matched the record exactly. That's the strange part."

"…Is it good that they haven't changed?" I asked.

The Keeper thought for a moment, then set down his teacup.

"…I don't know. Good or bad aside — it means they didn't break. Not many people stand that long without breaking."

I wrote that down last, and finished for the day. Elpis's flower petal was still tucked between my records. A nameless flower. I didn't bother moving it anywhere else.

(Continued in Chapter 27.)

Chapter 27. Pheugon and Trope

_Previously_ — Zeteo and Elpis came and went. Zeteo still said he
hadn't received an answer, but left with the words "I think someone's
listening." Elpis said she was still waiting. Both left unchanged.

The two who came from the Reckoning arrived separately.

The man came first. He looked a little older than Zeteo. He held a single sheet of paper in his hand and kept looking down at it as he walked. He wasn't reading aloud, but his lips moved a little.

"…What are you reading?" I asked.

He looked up.

"…A sentence. One I used to read, a long time ago."

"…You still have it?"

"…Yes. I can't throw it away. It was my work."

The Keeper recognized him from the side.

"…Pheugon."

"…Yes." He answered shortly.

Pheugon said he still worked reading sentences somewhere, even now. Not at the Reckoning. He went around the smaller cities, reading sentences aloud for people who needed it, in place of the people the sentences belonged to.

"…Someone assigned you this?" I asked.

"…No. I offered. I read the ones nobody else wants to read."

"…Isn't that hard?"

"…It is. But if I don't read them, nobody will."

That struck me as odd. In the Reckoning's record, he'd been someone who fled from sentences. And now he went looking for them himself. I asked again, carefully, trying not to let this sound like a conversion.

"…You used to run from it," I said, carefully.

Pheugon looked at me. He didn't look angry.

"…That's right. I ran. Afraid the city would be forgiven. It's different now. Now… I know there's such a thing as mercy, so I decided to become someone who reads it aloud for others."

"…Is all the anger gone?"

Pheugon was quiet for a moment before answering.

"…No. It still comes, sometimes. But I read anyway, even when I'm angry. That's what's changed."

Histor heard that and asked,

"…Why does a sentence need to be read aloud? Can't the Scales just announce the result?"

"…No. That's how our city's provision works. A sentence only takes effect when a person reads it aloud. Arche can't do it alone, and neither can the Scales."

"…Why is that?"

Pheugon shrugged.

"…I don't know. Only whoever wrote the provision knows that. But lately I think I understand a little why."

"…Why?"

"…Because if there's someone reading, you know there's someone listening. If the Scales just spits out a result on its own, nobody's listening."

I wrote that down. Histor seemed to be writing it too.

Pheugon looked down again at the sentence in his hand and added,

"…Honestly, at first it was just habit. I'd trained a long time at reading aloud. But lately it's different. I feel like what I'm reading actually reaches someone. I never used to feel that."

"…What changed?" Histor asked.

"…I don't know. Maybe I changed. Or maybe I'm only just now listening."

He didn't explain further than that. I didn't press him either.

Trope arrived alone, later that day. Her steps were quiet. She stood there without knocking, so I went out first to see who it was.

"…Who is it?"

"…Trope. From the Reckoning."

The moment Histor heard the name, he came over. He remembered Trope's record precisely.

"…You said, 'Mercy might exist. But it won't come for me.'"

Trope looked a little startled.

"…Even that's written down?"

"…Yes. Word for word."

Trope said nothing for a while, hearing that. I couldn't tell whether she was uncomfortable or not.

"…Do you still think that?" Histor asked.

Trope answered slowly.

"…No. Now I'm… not sure. I'm less certain than I used to be. Whether it could come for me too or not."

"…So which side are you on now?"

"…Neither. I just don't know. But it used to be 'it won't,' and now it's 'I don't know.' That's a fairly big difference for me."

Hearing that, I felt how careful a sentence it was. She wasn't certain. She wasn't declaring anything either. She'd only said that one old certainty had shaken.

I read this conversation over many times. Trope hadn't converted. She
didn't say "I believe now." She said only that "it won't" had become
"I don't know" — that one step, and nothing more. I liked that
honesty.

"…What changed it?" I asked.

Trope thought for a moment.

"…It's not one thing. It's several. That the Scales weighed all of me and still didn't push me away. That no one beside me ever once got angry. And… I'm not sure myself. Just — time passed."

"…Time passing did it?"

"…Yes. I thought it would take some huge event to break a certainty like that. But I just dulled, a little at a time, every day. My certainty did. I don't mean that badly."

I liked that word. Not breaking, but dulling. I copied that word straight into my notebook.

Pheugon listened beside her. He wasn't surprised.

"…That's how it was back then too. Trope doesn't change her certainties easily. When she does change, it's in very small steps."

"…Do you two know each other?" I asked.

"…Same city, so. We run into each other now, at the Reckoning."

Trope nodded at that.

"…Whenever he comes to read sentences aloud, we cross paths. I don't run from it anymore."

"…You used to run?" I asked.

"…Yes. The first time Pheugon came back to the Reckoning and read a sentence out loud, I couldn't stand to be in the room. Not out of fear — I was afraid my name would be called."

"…Was it?"

"…No. It wasn't called. But for a while after that I still kept avoiding those days. It's only lately that I can even stay."

Hearing that, I felt I vaguely understood that a certainty changing isn't one moment, but many repeated moments of just barely managing to stay.

Trope looked slowly around the repository. The shelves, the index, even the corner where Arche's voice came from.

"…Is this place something like the Scales too?" she asked.

"…No. We don't judge here. We just lay things down."

"…No judging?"

"…No. What Arche can do is classify and compare. It doesn't rate right and wrong."

Trope turned that over.

"…The Scales weighed. Precisely. And still I don't think that explained all of me."

"…How is the Adjudicator?" I asked. It was a name I'd seen in the record.

"…He's well. Still at the Reckoning. Lately he talks about the Forty-Day Provision more often than before. He used to say it was shameful that he never once invoked it, his whole life."

"…And now?"

"…Now he doesn't call it shameful, just says it as fact. 'I didn't see it then.' That's all. He blames himself a little less."

Pheugon added, from the side,

"…The Adjudicator's actually been re-learning how to read sentences aloud from me, lately. Says his voice doesn't carry the way it used to, now that he's older."

"…The Adjudicator reads them himself?" Histor asked.

"…Yes. In our city that's not just assumed anymore. Used to be, they'd have someone like me do it. Now the adults read them themselves too."

"…What does that mean?"

"…What the Scales weighed was something I did — it's not me. For a long time I thought those two things were the same. That's how I got so certain 'mercy won't come for me.' Because what I'd done was who I was."

"…And now?"

"…Now I think those two are a little different. I don't know yet how different."

It was after that I took Trope toward the water channel. Same as with Mena, I wanted to show her for no particular reason.

Trope stopped walking when she heard the water.

"…This is similar to the well in our city."

"…They might be connected. I don't know for sure."

"…The Reckoning's well didn't exist originally either. Not until Speon came."

"…You know Speon?"

"…Only by rumor. Pheugon mentions the name a lot, talking about the water channel."

Trope sat down by the water. After a long silence, she asked,

"…What the Scales can't do — can that water do it?"

"…What do you mean?"

"…The Scales only weighs. It can't measure direction. But that water… it just flows. It's not decided where it goes, and still it keeps going."

I had no answer for that. I just sat beside Trope and watched the water.

After a while Trope asked,

"…Does he come here too?"

I knew right away who she meant. Histor was the one who asked.

"…Which person?"

"…The one who used to be in the Reckoning. The one who knew before the Scales did, and never once got angry. Ask Pheugon — he'd know."

Pheugon heard that and came closer to the water.

"…I haven't seen him since I came here. But on the way here I heard something odd. That people in other cities have seen someone like that too."

"…The same person?" I asked.

"…Can't say. Everyone describes him differently. Some say young, some say old. But strangely everyone says the same thing — that he never once got angry."

Trope nodded.

"…I'm not curious who he is. I just think… that he existed matters."

I just listened to that conversation quietly. Histor said nothing either. Neither of us asked further.

Writing this part, I made a deliberate choice not to press further.
Neither Trope nor Pheugon knew more than that. Not because they didn't
know that they weren't pressing — I think it was simply a place that
didn't need pressing.

Pheugon asked Arche something.

"…Is there any misquotation among the Scales's case law?"

A misquoted instance of Mena's record has been confirmed within the
Reckoning's case-law database.

"…Has it been corrected?"

That item is in progress.

Pheugon nodded. No surprise showed on his face.

"…I expected that, coming here. That nothing just finishes."

"…Aren't you angry? The evidence you carried in is still in progress."

"…I would have been, once. Now… I know that in progress is different from not existing at all. Mena herself came through here too, I heard."

"…How do you know?"

"…Elpis sent me a letter. News travels fast these days."

Before evening, Trope asked one last thing.

"…If I come here, can I check on mine too? Anything under my name?"

I asked Arche. A few records came up classified under Trope's name. Most were the Reckoning's deliberation records. One of them had "in progress" attached.

Trope looked at that item for a long time.

"…This is me? A decision about me?"

"…Something like that. I'm not sure exactly. You'd have to ask Arche to know precisely."

That item is in progress.

"…How long?" Trope asked.

In progress for seven years.

Trope laughed hearing that number. A sad laugh. I guessed it was probably close to the laugh she used to wear at the Reckoning, back then. Of course, I never saw that time myself. I only know it from the record.

"…Seven years I've waited, and it's still in progress."

Pheugon said, low, from the side,

"…That's not bad news. It means it hasn't ended yet."

Trope didn't answer right away. After a while, she nodded.

"…Maybe."

The two left together that night. I watched their backs, walking side by side, and found myself wondering what that "in progress" would someday become.

Before leaving, Trope turned back to me once more.

"…Ms. Mone."

"…Yes."

"…Do you know what my name means?"

"…No. Can I ask?"

Trope smiled.

"…I don't know either. Nobody ever told me. But somehow I think it's a name that suits me. I'd like it to mean turning back."

Hearing that, my heart beat a little faster. I already knew. Trope's meaning is τροπή — turning, a turning back. Histor had told me, before. But I didn't say anything, right there, right then. It didn't feel like the time yet.

"…I think it suits you," was all I said.

Pheugon laughed beside her.

"…Let's go, Trope. There's another sentence to read."

Trope nodded. The two walked out. Pheugon's hand still held that old worn sentence.

The Keeper spoke as he closed the door.

"…Those two were the strangest pair in the Reckoning. Seeing them now, they fit."

"…They were strange?" I asked.

"…The one who ran and the one who couldn't. Strange, right? But maybe the two of them met and each got a little less strange."

I wrote that phrase at the back of my notebook. "A little less strange." It wasn't a precise expression, but it was the closest I could come to what I'd felt that day.

I read that sentence again that night and realized, freshly, that no one who'd come through this repository had ever become entirely un-strange. Only a little less strange. That didn't sound like a bad thing.

Writing this part, I picture Pheugon's back as he walked away again.
He's still reading sentences aloud. He didn't stop. I thought that was
strange, at first, and then revised the thought — it isn't strange at
all.

(Continued in Chapter 28.)

Chapter 28. Xene and Lytron

_Previously_ — Pheugon and Trope came and went. Trope had shifted
only a little, from "it won't" to "I don't know," without settling
into certainty. Pheugon still carried his sentence with him, still
did the work of reading it aloud.

The two who came from the Harvest arrived together, but at different paces.

The woman arrived first, the man trailing a bit behind. At first I didn't even realize they were together. The woman carried nothing in her hands; the man had a large sack over his shoulder.

"…I'm Xene," the woman said first. "…This is Lytron."

Lytron was broad-shouldered and tall. He didn't look very different from what I'd imagined from the Harvest's record. Only his expression looked far more careful than the person in that old record had been.

Lytron set the sack down and bowed his head.

"…This is from the Harvest. Grain. There was nowhere to give it, so I brought it here."

I was a little flustered taking the sack. This was the first time anyone had ever brought grain to the repository. People had come from all six cities, but they'd all come empty-handed, or with bundles of paper. Grain was new.

The Keeper came out, saw the sack, and looked pleased.

"…That's a first."

"…Sorry. Is it strange?" Lytron asked.

"…No. It's good. It's always just paper piling up here."

Xene heard that and laughed.

"…He's just clumsy at giving things, so he probably gave it strangely. Be generous with him."

Lytron looked embarrassed. I found it hard to overlay that expression onto the confident boy I'd pictured from the Harvest's record. Time seemed to have made him a little different.

I looked at the sack again, remembering the passage from the old record about how strict he'd been with his calculations. Now here he was, having brought something without calculating at all. I couldn't tell whether that meant he'd changed, or whether his hands had simply grown restless.

"…Why did you bring it?" I asked.

Lytron hesitated a little before answering.

"…I don't know. It just felt wrong to come empty-handed."

Xene laughed quietly beside him.

"…He's always like this. Used to be, he went empty-handed so he'd never lose anything. Now he keeps carrying things around."

"…What do you mean?"

"…He'll explain it himself."

Lytron picked up on that.

"…I've never lost anything in my life. Truly. My qualification score was high, my family was good, I had everything I needed. So I never understood why anyone would give something to someone else."

"…And now?"

"…Even now I've never lost anything. That's a fact. But lately I know it's nothing to be proud of."

I knew this was exactly the opposite of what he'd said in the Harvest's record. The man who'd once said "I haven't done anything wrong" was now saying "that's nothing to be proud of." But Histor put his finger precisely on it.

"…Do you still think you've done nothing wrong?"

Lytron thought a moment before answering.

"…Yes. That part hasn't changed. I haven't discovered some new sin of mine. It's just… I've come to know that never once losing anything isn't something remarkable."

"…If it's not remarkable, what is it?"

"…It's just how I was born. Xene was born with a qualification score of zero and I was born with a perfect one. I didn't do that."

I want to render this answer carefully, so it doesn't sound like a
conversion. Lytron didn't confess his sin. He only conceded that what
he'd received wasn't his own doing. I want to write that difference
exactly.

Histor asked once more, as if confirming.

"…So in the end, it's still true you never needed mercy?"

"…Yes. That doesn't change. I don't lie."

"…Then why isn't that something to be proud of?"

Lytron thought for a moment and answered.

"…For it to be something to be proud of, I'd have to have done something. But I just stood in the place I was given."

Histor asked again.

"…So what do you believe now?"

Lytron couldn't answer that right away. After a while:

"…I don't know. I still think I never needed mercy. That's real. But… whether not needing it was actually a good thing — I'm not so sure anymore."

"…What's the difference?"

"…If I'd needed it, I'd have received it. Since I didn't need it, I never got to receive it. That's not a fortunate thing — it's just an experience I never had. I only realized that difference recently. I used to mistake never having received it for not needing to."

I read that answer several times over. Lytron still said he'd never needed mercy. He'd only newly admitted that this wasn't something to boast of — just an experience he'd never had.

Xene added quietly, from the side.

"…It took him years to say that. At first, no matter what I said, he wouldn't listen."

"…What did you say to him?" I asked.

"…Nothing much. I just went out to the field every day. Watching that, he felt something on his own. I didn't persuade him."

Lytron nodded.

"…That's right. Xene never persuaded me of anything. She just kept being there. That's what actually made me think about things more."

Xene had been quietly examining the shelves the whole while. Her eyes weren't searching for anything in particular — just looking around.

"…This place is like gleaning too," she said.

"…What do you mean?"

"…The dropped things. Every city leaves things behind, uncared for, and they come piling up here. Gathering those up used to be my job."

"…Do you still do it?"

"…Something like it. Lately I go around teaching other cities the Harvest's corner provision. The rule about leaving a corner of the field."

"…Does it take?"

Xene tilted her head slightly.

"…Some cities listen, some don't. But I just keep going and saying it."

I recognized that manner of speaking. The attitude of continuing without expecting a great result. I felt a similar thing, laying paper in order every day.

"…How's Mother Glyke?" I asked. It was a name I'd seen in the Harvest's record.

Xene's face softened.

"…She's well. Still asks to be called Pikra. Nobody tries to force her back to it."

"…Doesn't she go back to her original name?"

"…No. Mother says — the name I wrote isn't gone, I've just learned to live even while carrying it written. That's exactly how she put it. She keeps the name and lives with it."

"…Did you meet Mena?" Histor asked.

"…Yes. She's the one who taught me how to keep records. Years ago, at the Harvest. I heard she came through here recently."

"…That's right. A few days ago."

Xene smiled.

"…Even the teacher goes around learning things. She only ever taught us."

Lytron pointed at his sack again.

"…This — it was originally my share. What was left after transferring the qualification."

"…Are you still handing out qualification?" Histor asked.

"…No, that was only the once. It's recorded in the Roll, that case. But… since then, I keep wanting to give things away. It's strange."

"…Is it strange?"

"…Yes. I never used to have the concept of giving at all. Giving anything felt like a loss to me. Even now that's not entirely gone. It's still a little painful every time I give something."

Xene laughed at that.

"…Honest of you."

"…Better honest than pretending it doesn't hurt."

I asked Arche. This was the first time I'd asked directly about a person's qualification. I was a little cautious about it.

"…In the Harvest's Roll, what's Xene's qualification standing now?"

Xene's qualification score meets the registration threshold through
Lytron's transfer.

"…Has the process finished?"

The registration is valid. Review of the associated case law is in
progress.

Xene heard that answer and nodded.

"…So the review still isn't done."

"…Does it bother you?" I asked.

"…No. I just need to be registered. The review is the Roll's business to see to."

Lytron said, from the side,

"…It bothers me. Since I lowered my own share, I worry it might get reversed."

"…Do you think it will?"

"…I don't know. But even if it reverses, I'll give it again. I've decided that."

Histor asked, at the end of that exchange,

"…Is the administrator still unashamed?"

Lytron thought a moment.

"…Yes. Still is. Proud that his system never broke a law. I used to think that was just natural. Lately it looks strange to me."

"…What's strange about it?"

"…Not breaking the law and doing right seem like different things to me. But when I said that to him, he only said, 'I did my job.'"

"…Weren't you able to convince him?"

"…No. He won't change. I just do what I can differently, in my own share."

Listening, I thought that no one changing anyone else seemed to be a common thread running through all these cities. Everyone just changed their own small share, a little at a time. Xene, Lytron, and I were all the same in that.

Xene said she wanted to see the water channel, so I took her there too. Watching the water flow, she said,

"…This is like our field. Wherever the water passes, it leaves something behind. Small, but something."

"…What does it leave?"

"…Soil. Good soil. The water sets it down as it passes."

I liked that. I'd thought water only carried things away. But it set things down too.

Xene crouched by the water and touched the soil.

"…I wonder where this water starts."

"…I don't know. I'm curious too."

"…In our city, people think the water wasn't always there. That Speon opened it. But sometimes I think that might not be right."

"…What do you mean?"

"…When Speon first dug, I don't think water appeared — I think something already there just came to light. No real grounds for it. It's just a feeling."

I listened without answering. That feeling wasn't unfamiliar to me either.

Lytron set the sack down beside them.

"…I'll leave this here. It's for the Keeper."

"…I can eat this?" the Keeper asked.

"…Yes. No qualification asked. Just leaving it."

The Keeper heard that and laughed out loud for the first time.

"…It's been a while since I heard words like that in this city."

Lytron looked a little surprised.

"…People don't say that often?"

"…People in this city have everything they need. So not many people leave things behind. When there's no need, people don't tend to think about sharing either."

Xene heard that and looked thoughtful.

"…A city where everyone has everything they need — that's strange, hearing it. In our city there's always someone who's short of something, so sharing came naturally."

"…Which is better?" I asked.

Xene shook her head.

"…I don't know. I only know the way I grew up."

Lytron added quietly from the side.

"…Lately I'm learning that the way I grew up wasn't necessarily right either. I was just lucky."

The two left as evening fell. Xene said to me last,

"…You're like a gleaner too, Mone. All this paper here — it's things dropped somewhere, isn't it."

Hearing that, I thought about my own work in a new way for the first time. Picking up dropped things. It felt right. Until then I'd only thought of what I did as "laying down." Hearing Xene's words, I realized "picking up" had come first, before that. You had to pick up what had dropped somewhere, before you could lay it down.

Lytron walked out, looking at his hand, lighter now without the sack.

"…Odd — it's light, but it doesn't feel empty."

Xene laughed.

"…Sounds like you're starting to understand a little."

The two walked off like that. I split the grain sack they'd left behind with the Keeper and cooked it for dinner. Eating, I turned over each thing I'd heard that day. The rice tasted a little better than usual. Maybe because the face of the person who'd given it kept coming back to me.

That night Histor asked me,

"…Do you think Lytron believes?"

I couldn't answer that right away.

"…I don't know. He didn't say he doesn't believe, and he didn't say he believes."

"…Right. I can't decide either. In the Harvest's record he's a person certain he never needed mercy. The person we saw today had that certainty a little shaken. But shaken isn't overturned."

"…Which side do you think is right, Histor?"

Histor thought for a moment and said,

"…I don't judge. I just record. What he said today, exactly."

I thought that answer was very like him. He never refutes anyone, never takes anyone's side. He just listens exactly and writes it down. I was coming to understand that better and better as his way.

After we'd finished eating, I looked again at the sack Lytron had left behind. There was small writing on the inside. Xene's hand, it looked like.

"…Corner's share."

I looked at those two words for a long time. The handwriting was small and careful. I didn't know exactly what it meant, but it seemed to relate somehow to the field corner from the Harvest's story.

I tucked it between my records. Beside the petal Elpis had given me.

I felt something strange, laying these two things side by side. A
nameless flower, and a scrap of grain sack marked only "corner's
share." Neither came with an explanation. I was, around this time,
slowly learning how to let things sit without explanation.

That night I wrote briefly at the end of my notebook. "Lytron still
says he's never lost anything. But now he knows that not losing
anything wasn't his own doing either." Reading it back, this sentence
didn't conclude anything. It was just what I'd seen that day, carried
over as it was.

(Continued in Chapter 29.)

Chapter 29. Anemos and Eikon

_Previously_ — Xene and Lytron came and went. Lytron still said he'd
never lost anything, but he was beginning to understand that it was
nothing to be proud of. The two left a sack of grain behind.

The two who came from the Preserve entered a little differently.

I heard footsteps before the door opened. One set a little rushed, the other very slow. When the door opened, the man leaned his head in first, and the woman followed slowly behind him.

The woman looked no older than she should. Just as the Preserve's record had said — fifteen — she still looked fifteen now. I thought that was strange for a moment, and then remembered the Preserve's story and understood. A preserved person doesn't age.

The man was the opposite. The boy who'd been sixteen in the Preserve's record now looked close to thirty. Standing side by side, the difference stood out even more.

"…I'm Anemos," the man said. "…This is Eikon."

Eikon looked around the room without any greeting. Her eyes carried no hurry. Not the eyes of someone searching for something — the eyes of someone who already knew everything and was only confirming it.

That confirming look wasn't unfamiliar to me. Arche sometimes answered in a similar tone. Already knowing everything, just retracing the fact.

There was almost no expression on Eikon's face. Not bored, not curious. Just calm. That calm felt foreign to me at first.

"…What is this place?" Eikon asked. Less a question than a way of confirming.

"…A repository. A place where records from the six cities are laid in order."

"…Is that work interesting?"

I was a little thrown by the question. Most people didn't ask that.

"…Not so much interesting as… I think it's necessary work."

"…Necessary," Eikon repeated the word. "…I've never had need for anything."

Anemos said quietly, beside her.

"…There she goes again."

"…It's true, isn't it."

Anemos looked a little more worn than in the old record. Not tired in a bad sense. The weariness of someone who'd walked a long way.

"…You still haven't taken release?" I asked, carefully. I'd read the Preserve's record, so I knew what I was asking.

Eikon answered first.

"…No. I haven't."

"…Why not?"

"…I don't feel the need. Even now, I lack nothing."

Those words were exactly what she'd said in the Preserve's record. It struck me as strange, and a little heavy, that this hadn't changed even after more than a decade. For a moment I wanted to try to persuade her, and then, realizing that wasn't mine to do, I let it go.

Histor asked, carefully.

"…Have you never once wavered?"

Eikon shook her head.

"…It would be a lie to say I'd wavered. I'm just the same."

"…Don't you think that's remarkable?"

"…No. It's not remarkable. It's just a fact."

Anemos added, from the side.

"…I'm still waiting. I asked her that day. She said, 'Ask me again later.' I've been waiting ever since."

Listening, I thought of the records that had sat in progress ten, twenty years in this city. The answer Anemos was waiting for seemed to sit in a similar place. Not that there was no answer set for it — just that it hadn't come yet.

"…That must be hard," I said.

"…It is. But not as hard as it used to be. I used to think it was because I'd done something wrong. Now I just… think of it as waiting for the right time."

Histor came closer and asked,

"…Don't you think it's time to ask? How many more times have you asked?"

"…Once a year. Same answer every time. 'Later.'"

"…Doesn't that make you angry?"

"…It does. But I found out anger and waiting can travel together. Learning that was the biggest change."

"…What about Speon?" I asked. "…The one who dug the well in the valley."

"…I see him often. He still sends water down. He says, 'The upper part isn't drying up anymore, so.'"

"…What does that mean?"

"…I don't know exactly either. But his face looks certain every time he says it. I just trust him and go along."

Histor asked,

"…How's the valley these days?"

"…Less dry than before. Water keeps coming in. But there are still patches that are dry. Not all filled yet."

"…Will it all fill?"

Anemos thought for a moment.

"…I don't know. I just watch it fill a little more each day. That's enough for me."

Eikon listened to that exchange and said,

"…I still don't understand why he does that."

"…Why?" I asked.

"…I'm fine the way I am, and he keeps telling me to become something else. I'm not suffering."

"…You're not suffering?"

"…No. Really. I don't lie."

I recognized this as the exact same sentence from the Preserve's record. She was holding to a logic that was hard to refute, still.

Writing this part, I tried not to try to win against Eikon. Her words
are still hard to refute, even now. I just want to render what she
said exactly.

Histor asked, carefully.

"…Is the Curator well?"

Anemos nodded.

"…Yes. Still grieving. But he doesn't hide the grief anymore, now. He used to try to hide it."

"…Is that better?" I asked.

"…I don't know. But it seems better than hiding it. At least he knows what he's doing."

Eikon heard that and added,

"…Every time the Curator looks at me, his eyes are a little sad. I'm fine, though."

"…Why would he look sad if you're fine?" Histor asked.

Eikon shrugged.

"…That's his business. I only know my own business."

That tone didn't sound cold to me. Just honest.

I asked Arche.

"…Has Eikon's release application gone through?"

No application record for that item exists. Arche's voice was flat, as always.

"…You never applied?"

"…No," Eikon answered directly. "…No reason to apply."

"…Doesn't Anemos apply?"

"…I can't apply on her behalf. There's a consent requirement. I just ask." When Anemos said that, there wasn't a trace of resentment in his face. He said it plainly, like explaining a rule.

I remembered the Preserve's record. When consent was sought, Eikon always answered with "Why?" And the process always stopped there.

"…Does she still say 'why' when you ask?" I asked.

Eikon laughed.

"…No. Lately I say 'later.' If that's progress, I suppose it's progress."

Anemos heard that and looked startled.

"…You think that's progress?"

"…No. But I thought you'd like it, so I said it that way."

Histor cut in, carefully.

"…Isn't that considerate? Saying it because you thought he'd like it."

Eikon tilted her head.

"…Could be consideration. I don't really understand things like that. I just tried it."

"…Why?"

"…I don't know. Seeing Anemos's face just isn't unpleasant. I'm not sure it's what you'd call liking, though."

I felt that answer was a very small crack in her logic. Someone who said she lacked nothing was minding someone's expression. But that wasn't evidence that overturned her words. I decided to just write the crack down exactly as it was.

Anemos couldn't answer that, and just smiled. That smile looked complicated to me. Joy and something a little wistful, mixed.

Histor asked, more carefully than usual.

"…Eikon, are you really satisfied with your state right now? Have you never once doubted it?"

Eikon thought for a moment.

"…I doubt. Sometimes. But doubting doesn't produce an answer. It's a fact that I lack nothing right now, and I haven't yet found a reason for that to change."

"…If you find one?"

"…Then I'll think again. But not now."

I thought that answer closed the door neither all the way shut nor all the way open. Just open that much.

I asked Arche one more thing.

"…Are there other cases where release applications came in?"

Across the whole of the Preserve, there have been three release
applications in total. Two of them have been completed.

"…Who were they?"

Personal information is not provided without the requester's own
consent.

Eikon heard that answer and said,

"…Strange that there were three. In our city that almost never happened."

"…Do you know those people?"

"…No. But I am curious what made them different."

Anemos said quietly.

"…Probably people who were missing something. Not like you, who has everything."

Eikon smiled a little at that.

"…Maybe."

The two stayed until evening. Eikon, looking around the shelves, asked,

"…These records — you said they're all in progress."

"…Yes. Not one has closed."

"…Doesn't that frustrate you?"

"…Sometimes. But lately I don't think that's only a bad thing."

"…Why?"

"…In progress just means it hasn't ended yet — not that it never will."

Eikon looked at me for a moment, hearing that. It was the first time she'd had a genuinely interested look.

"…That sounds like what Anemos has been trying to tell me."

Anemos said nothing to that. He just smiled quietly.

Thinking about how the age gap between the two of them kept widening, I hesitated a moment over whether to ask, then did.

"…Is it all right? Anemos, you're getting older, and Eikon, you're staying the same."

Anemos smiled a little.

"…It wasn't all right at first. Honestly. I was scared of growing old before her. What if she stopped recognizing me one day."

"…And now?"

"…Now I've just accepted it. My aging and Eikon's not aging — neither is something I can change."

Eikon heard that and said, quietly,

"…I'll still recognize you. Even old."

Anemos didn't answer that. He just lowered his eyes for a moment.

Rendering this short exchange, my hand shook. Eikon still said she
hasn't taken release. And yet that one line showed she cared about
Anemos, in her own way. I tried hard not to read those two things as a
contradiction. Not having changed, and caring, can be different
layers.

I thought about that distinction several more times. Eikon didn't apply for release that day either. That fact stayed as it was.

Before leaving, Anemos said quietly to me.

"…I'm not in a hurry anymore. I used to come here trying to get her back. Now I just stay because I want to be near her."

"…Is that comfortable?"

"…Not so much comfortable as… it feels right. The place I'm meant to be."

Eikon turned back at the door and said,

"…I'll come again, Mone. And I'll ask the same thing again then. 'Why do I have to exist.'"

"…Come back again."

"…Anemos will bring me. He's good at that."

The Keeper watched from the side and said quietly,

"…Those two are together in such a strange way."

"…Strange?" I asked.

"…Usually people who walk together walk at the same pace. Those two walk at completely different paces, and they still stay together."

"…How is that possible?"

The Keeper thought for a moment.

"…Someone's waiting. Continuously. Anemos is doing that."

I remembered the last part of the Preserve's record, hearing that. That after hearing her say "ask me again later," he'd stayed by her side ever since. It wasn't some grand event — just a choice repeated every day.

The two walked out like that. I stood at the door and watched until they were gone. Their pace was still different — one slow, one matching beside her. I didn't leave until their backs had completely disappeared from sight.

That night Histor asked me,

"…What do you think of Eikon?"

"…I don't know. Everything she says seems right, and yet something catches."

"…What catches?"

"…Whether lacking nothing is really a good thing. Maybe because I've lost so much myself, hearing 'I lack nothing' sounds strange to me instead."

Histor nodded.

"…I feel something similar. But I can't find a logic to refute her with. If she really lacks nothing, there's no reason for her to want more."

"…So is she right, then?"

"…Right or wrong aside — she's consistent, within her own logic, right now. I find that both frightening and admirable."

I agreed with that. Eikon was more honest than anyone. Only that honesty wasn't moving anywhere.

That night I wrote in my notebook: "Anemos waits, and Eikon doesn't
change. Both stay beside each other." Reading it back, it seemed like
a sentence without much meaning, but it stayed with me strangely.

Beneath that sentence I added one more line. "Maybe waiting and not
changing meet at the same place, somehow." This wasn't a precise
sentence either. It was just the closest thing to what I'd felt that
day.

Eikon confirmed it once more as she left. "…I still haven't taken
release. It'll be the same next time I come." I copied that down into
my notebook exactly as she said it.

(Continued in Chapter 30.)

Chapter 30. Hora and Tyche

_Previously_ — Anemos and Eikon came and went. Eikon still hadn't
taken release, still said she lacked nothing. Anemos was still
waiting beside her. "Ask me again later" still held.

The two who came from the Equity arrived with a document in hand.

More precisely, one of them carried it. The woman. The other came empty-handed, walking light, with an easy expression.

The one with the document walked neatly, the walk peculiar to someone bent on checking something through to the end. Seeing that, I immediately thought of a line I'd read in the Equity's record. "She is someone who reads to the end."

"…I'm Hora," the woman with the document said. "…This is Tyche."

Tyche waved a hand.

"…Hello. I just came along."

That "I just came along" stayed with me. The woman in the Equity's record had spoken of herself in a similarly self-effacing way.

Once inside the repository, Hora went straight to examining the shelves closely. Then she asked Arche something.

"…Do you have a signature box here? On the documents."

"…Which document?" I asked.

"…Any of them. I'm curious whether this city's records have a signature box too."

Arche answered. This repository's records have no separate signature
box. Only the recorder's name and the date are entered.

Hora heard the answer and nodded. She stood there a moment, seeming to turn it over.

"…So here it's not a matter of filling it in or not."

"…What was it a matter of, originally?" I asked.

"…In our city, every document has a signature box, and it's never once been filled in. Because filling it in makes it yours."

Tyche's clothes and expression were both light. She looked like someone with no worries in the world, though I came to learn later that this wasn't cynicism — it was real gentleness.

Tyche laughed beside her and said,

"…She's still on about this. The signature box. I'm a little tired of it, honestly."

"…Tired of it?" I asked.

"…Not in a bad way. She's just been thinking about it so long."

Hora glanced at Tyche and turned back to me.

"…She still thinks it's all coincidence."

"…Even now?" I asked Tyche.

Tyche shrugged.

"…Yes. That hasn't changed for me. My sitting in that seat was because of the vacancy and the number, and the same for the deliberation."

Histor asked,

"…So coming here now — that's coincidence too?"

Tyche laughed.

"…Of course. Hora suggested we come, so I came. No other reason."

"…That's all?"

"…Yes. I don't want to make up a reason."

Histor came closer and asked Tyche,

"…Do you remember the line from the Equity's record? 'I know you want it to mean something. I do too. But wanting it to mean something isn't evidence that it does.'"

Tyche looked surprised for a moment, then laughed.

"…Wow, those are exactly my words."

"…Do you still think the same?"

"…Yes. Exactly the same. I think an explanation is usually right. Coincidence really is coincidence."

"…And someone else keeps writing that name next to Hora's? How do you explain that?"

Tyche thought for a moment.

"…That's coincidence too. Or someone copied it. There are a lot of people out there who think alike."

Hora heard that and said quietly.

"…I don't think it's coincidence. But I don't have any evidence to convince Tyche either."

"…Doesn't that frustrate you?" I asked.

"…It used to. Now… I think we each just see it our own way. Looking at the same thing and standing in different places."

I dimly understood this was the core of the thing between the two of them. Hora and Tyche still drew different conclusions from the same facts. That hadn't changed with time. I didn't try to persuade either of them. I knew that wasn't my place.

Histor asked, carefully.

"…What about Temel? Do you still see him?"

Hora nodded.

"…Yes. Not often. Lately he goes from city to city, still looking for people. He comes to our city sometimes too."

"…What does he say, these days?"

"…Same as always. 'You might just be sitting there because of a number. But even in a seat that's there just because of a number, there's still something you can do.' He still says that."

Tyche heard that and cut in.

"…I've heard that many times too. But I still don't agree. It doesn't look like evidence that there's meaning to it."

"…Then why does it keep sticking with you?" Histor asked.

Tyche thought a moment.

"…I don't know. It just keeps coming to mind. Even though I don't agree."

I asked Arche.

"…The name Hora wrote — I heard it turns up in other cities' documents too. Is that still true?"

The name Jeongsu-a is confirmed in documents across several cities.
The writers differ in each case.

"…Do you know who wrote it?" Hora asked.

Author-identifying information remains in each document, but the
circumstances of the writing are not confirmed.

Hora heard that answer and nodded.

"…Same here. It doesn't come out clear here either."

"…Disappointed?" Histor asked.

"…No. If anything, it's a relief. Because here too, there's no lying."

"…Do you worry a lot about lies?" I asked.

"…Yes. Our city hid nothing. But hiding wasn't the only problem. No one taking responsibility for anything was a problem too. Here, at least, 'I don't know' sounds like a responsible thing to say."

"…What's the difference?"

"…Our secretary says 'it's been arranged,' 'it's been settled.' No subject in the sentence. But here, Arche says 'not defined.' No subject there either, but at least it says exactly what's missing."

I felt that distinction was subtle but important. That how you say the same not-knowing matters. Histor seemed to be writing something down in his notebook at that too.

Tyche had been looking out the window toward the water channel this whole time.

"…Is that the water that reached our city?" she asked.

"…Yes, I think so."

"…That's strange. It never even came up as an agenda item in our city. Seeing it here, it really exists."

"…Do you believe it?" I asked.

"…Believe what?"

"…That the water channel connects all seven cities."

Tyche thought for a moment and answered.

"…I believe that part. Water really does flow. But whether someone 'intended' it — I still don't know that."

"…I heard Speon dug it."

"…Yes. But I also heard Speon didn't dig all of it either. That he just joined an already-existing path. So who made that path in the first place?"

I couldn't answer that question. I didn't know either.

Histor answered in my place.

"…Nobody knows. Speon doesn't know either. From what we've found out so far, it seems like it was already built that way, from the start."

"…Who built it?" Tyche asked.

"…No one knows. That doesn't come out here either."

Tyche laughed hearing that answer. Not a mocking laugh — a genuinely amused one.

"…Just as I thought. Everyone saying 'I don't know' is the most honest answer of all. I like that much, at least."

"…That's not proof it isn't coincidence," Histor said, carefully.

"…True. But it's not proof it is coincidence, either. I'm just admitting my own side has no evidence. That doesn't mean I've crossed to the other side."

Hora joined that conversation.

"…I've decided not to go looking for the answer to that question. I used to keep searching. Now I've learned that you can still do something without knowing the answer."

"…What do you do?" I asked.

"…I keep writing names down. Every time a new deliberation opens. Other names have started to be added behind mine, one by one. At first it was just me. Now there are a few more."

"…What about the secretary?"

"…He still says he's nothing. But lately, when he says that, he smiles a little. He never used to smile."

Tyche cut into that.

"…That's right. I noticed that too. But I don't know what it means. He might have just smiled, that's all."

"…Is that how you want to read it?" Histor asked.

"…It's not a reading, it's just fact. Beyond that he smiled, I don't know anything more. I don't want to say I know."

Hora said quietly.

"…I think it's more than that. But I can't prove it. So I don't push Tyche to agree. That's not something I can do."

I found it curious that the two of them saw the same smile and took it in such completely different ways. It was one fact, but it had landed in two entirely different places inside them. I pushed down, several times, the urge to decide that place for them.

I was glad for that small change. It wasn't a great conversion, but there was, at least, one new smile.

Histor asked,

"…What about the nine hundred twenty-seven? The people who never got reclassified."

Hora's expression dimmed a little.

"…Still the same. I don't erase that number. If I erase it, I'll forget."

"…Does it torment you?"

"…Yes. I still get tormented every time I see that number. But not erasing it while it torments me — that was the best I could do."

Hearing that, I thought Hora already knew how to go on living without a complete resolution. I was learning something similar myself, in this repository.

Before leaving, Tyche said to me,

"…Do you believe all of this, Mone? That the cities were one, that the water channel joins them all, all that?"

I couldn't answer right away.

"…I don't really know. I just write down what I see."

"…That sounds like a good method," Tyche said. "…I do that too. I only believe what I can see."

Hora said, from the side.

"…I believe a little more than what's visible."

"…I know that too," Tyche said, smiling. "…But I'm not going to follow you there."

The two walked out together. Believing different things, and walking in the same direction.

That night I unfolded the document Hora had left with us again. A copy of a deliberation record. There was a signature box at the very bottom, and it was empty.

Histor looked it over with me.

"…So this really has never once been filled in."

"…Is it forbidden to fill it in?" I asked.

"…Not forbidden. But no one's filled it. Hora said — if you fill it, the decision becomes yours, so nobody does."

"…Did Hora fill it in?"

"…She said she filled in the name column. When opening a new deliberation. But not this. This is just a copy of an old document."

I looked at that empty box for a long while. I knew this box was the very core of the Equity's story, but seeing it in person, it was much more ordinary paper than I'd expected. Just a line, an empty space.

I looked at this empty box for a long time. It was strange that an
empty box with no explanation attached could hold so much. I wanted to
remember this moment, so I wrote it down separately.

The next morning, before Hora left, she asked me,

"…Are you glad there's no signature box here, Mone?"

"…I hadn't thought about it."

"…Think about it sometime. Why doesn't this repository have a signature box."

I turned the question over.

"…Maybe… because this isn't a place where someone decides things. It's just a place for laying things down."

Hora nodded.

"…That sounds right. Our city was a place that decided everything. That's why the signature box mattered so much."

"…Do you envy that, here?"

Hora thought for a moment and smiled.

"…A little. But our city's changing too, little by little. Like the names starting to be added behind mine."

Just before leaving, Tyche spoke to me separately.

"…Do I look strange to you, Mone? Always saying it's coincidence."

"…No. I'm just… curious. Why do you keep seeing it that way?"

"…I don't know either. It's just that everything I know explains it. I don't want to explain it with something I don't know."

"…That's not a bad reason."

"…No. I like people. I like Hora too. That's just what I think."

Hearing that, I didn't come to dislike Tyche. If anything, I found her honesty comfortable. She wasn't deceiving anyone. Not even herself.

After the two left, the Keeper said to me,

"…Those two were the last pair in the Equity's record who never split apart."

"…You know the Equity's story?"

"…I heard it from Histor. That the two of them argued for a long time over the signature box. And now, they're still going around together."

"…Even though their views don't match?"

"…Yes. So people can stay together even when their views don't match, it seems. I find that surprising."

I wrote that down last. "They walk together even though their views
don't match." I thought this sentence had come up more often than
anything else I'd seen recently.

Zeteo and Elpis, Pheugon and Trope, Xene and Lytron, Anemos and Eikon. And now Hora and Tyche too. None of their views had ever fully aligned. And yet they were all still walking together.

Writing this part, I felt the temptation to draw some big conclusion.
I resisted. I decided just to write down what I saw. The conclusion
didn't feel like it was mine to draw.

Before leaving, Tyche said one more thing. "…I really haven't changed.
If I come again, I'll say the same thing. That it's all coincidence." I
listened to that with a smile. And wrote it down exactly as she said
it.

(Continued in Chapter 31.)

Chapter 31. Temel and Speon

_Previously_ — Hora and Tyche came and went. Tyche still called it
coincidence, and Hora still believed it was something more. Neither
convinced the other, and they walked off together in the same
direction.

Mena kept staying on at the repository after that.

She'd said, a few times, that she'd leave, but she never actually did. I didn't find that strange. There was always new paper for her to sort, and I knew that wasn't an excuse — it was real.

That afternoon, Mena looked out the window and spoke first.

"…Speon must be coming."

I looked out the window. A man was walking up from the direction of the water channel. He had something like a tool in his hand, and his clothes were covered in dirt. His pace wasn't slow, wasn't fast. Just the walk of someone with work to do.

Mena went to the door. Her step wasn't hurried.

"…You came?" Mena said.

"…Yeah. On my way through."

"…This is on your way?"

"…Everything runs through here lately."

The two didn't say anything more than that. No embrace, no hand held. They just stood side by side for a moment and looked toward the water channel.

I didn't want to disturb that silence, so I just stood in the doorway. No one called for me. That was fine too.
