That greeting felt strange to me. It was too short for people who hadn't seen each other in a long time. But looking at their faces, the shortness wasn't a lack of something. It just wasn't necessary.

Histor whispered to me, beside me.

"…It was in Haven's record. That there was something unfinished between the two of them. 'You said you'd still be waiting.'"

"…Is that still true?"

"…I don't know. Watching just now, it doesn't quite look that way, and it also kind of does."

I didn't know how to write down that subtlety, so I decided to just write what I could see. The two stood with the ease particular to people who'd known each other a long time. That was all I knew, too. Even then, it felt right not to press further.

I tried, several times, to rewrite this scene. I wanted to write it as
some greater reunion. Tears, a long embrace. In fact there was nothing
like that. The two simply stood like old friends. I decided to write it
exactly as it was.

The Keeper came out and looked at Speon.

"…Back again."

"…Back again."

The Keeper didn't offer any particular greeting either. All three had the same indifference peculiar to people who'd known each other from very long ago.

Speon set his tool down carefully at the edge of the yard and said,

"…Came to check the water channel. See how far it's made it here."

"…It's made it fine," the Keeper answered. "…Good sound to it too."

"…The sound matters."

I found that odd and asked,

"…Why does the sound matter?"

Speon looked at me properly for the first time.

"…If it's making a sound, it's flowing. If it doesn't make a sound, it's standing still."

Watching the three of them together, I thought of the three names I'd only known from records. Temel, Mena, Speon. Loop's three children. All grown now.

"…What about Temel?" I asked.

Mena and Speon looked at each other at the same moment.

"…He's not here yet?" Speon asked.

"…Not yet," Mena answered.

"…Did he say he was coming?"

"…He said so. But when's he ever not late?"

I felt an old rhythm between the three of them in that exchange. A way of worrying without worrying. A way of waiting without hurrying.

All that afternoon, Mena and Speon didn't do anything in particular. Speon tended to the water channel, Mena sorted paper, and I did my own work beside them. Short words passed between them now and then, but most of it was quiet.

"…Why's it so quiet?" Histor asked me quietly.

"…I don't know. I expected something more too."

"…Are you disappointed?"

I thought for a moment.

"…No. If anything it's comfortable. Not having to wait for something big."

Temel arrived late that evening.

He came alone. He barely had any luggage. He opened the door, looked around the room once, and, spotting Mena and Speon, gave a short nod.

"…You came."

"…Came," Mena answered.

"…Late."

"…Right. Late."

That was all. I'd expected more, and waited, but nothing more came. The three of them just took their seats, each in their own place.

I was surprised by that short greeting at first, then quickly realized it was these three's way. People who'd known each other a long time, it seemed, didn't greet each other at length.

Temel's clothes were the most worn of the three. The traces of having crossed many cities showed clearly. The sole of one shoe was worn down on only one side.

"…Is something wrong with your foot?" I asked without thinking.

"…Yes. From walking so much. It's fine. I'm used to it."

Mena heard that and laughed shortly.

"…He's always been like that. Since Loop, he's made one pair of shoes last for years."

"…Even now?"

"…Even now. Hasn't changed."

Temel didn't deny it. He just glanced down at his own shoe once and picked his teacup back up.

I've never been told to write this chapter quietly. But I, who was
there that day, can't find any other way to write it. The three of them
didn't have a reunion that swelled with music. Just being in the same
room again seemed to be enough.

The Keeper brought out tea. The four of them sat. Histor and I watched, a little apart.

Aside from the clink of teacups, nothing made a sound for a long while. I wasn't uncomfortable with that quiet. If anything, it was comfortable.

"…First time the three of us have gathered since Loop, isn't it?" Speon asked.

"…First time," Temel answered.

"…Took a while."

"…Everyone had their own work to do," Mena said.

Temel lifted his cup and said,

"…I still look for people. Don't know how to do much else."

"…Found anyone? Lately?"

"…Sometimes. Mostly not. But I keep looking."

That was a familiar sound to me. Similar to Zeteo's continuous asking, and Elpis's continuous waiting. No one had gotten a certain answer, and yet they all kept going.

"…What's it like when you can't find someone?" Histor asked.

Temel looked down at his teacup as he answered.

"…Hard. But I believe the person I couldn't find is still alive somewhere. That one belief is what keeps me going."

"…What grounds do you have?"

"…None. I just believe it. I don't think it's strange to believe without grounds. I was always someone whose belief came before the grounds."

Writing that down, I felt this was a part of Temel that hadn't much changed since Loop. The man who'd been first to fall and last to lift a stone was still holding on to a groundless belief.

"…What about you, Speon?" Histor asked. "…Still opening the water channel?"

"…Not opening it," Speon corrected. "…Joining it. What was already there."

"…Does that difference matter?"

"…Yes. It matters. If I said I made it all, that would be a lie."

"…Did you always think of it that way?" I asked.

"…No. At first I just thought I'd dug it myself. But as I kept digging, I kept running into strange things. Spots already opened, traces of water that had already flowed. I wasn't the first one to touch it."

"…Do you know who dug it first?"

Speon shook his head.

"…I don't know. It was just there. I only followed the place."

Mena listened to that quietly. She didn't add anything, but I saw her holding those words in her heart.

Histor asked, carefully.

"…In Loop's record, Arche calls this city 'a closed circle.' Did you know that?"

The three looked at Histor at the same time.

"…I knew," Temel answered. "…At the time I thought it was a good thing to hear."

"…And now?"

"…Now I'm not so sure. Coming here, I see nothing's finished. And it's called a closed circle."

Mena added, from the side.

"…Lately I hear that phrase differently. A circle being closed and something being finished seem like different things to me."

"…What's the difference?" I asked.

"…Closed is just a shape. Finished means it stopped moving. This city is closed and it still keeps turning. So it's not finished."

I copied that down into my notebook. A sentence I wanted to read again later.

Mena heard it and laughed.

"…You never used to care about that kind of thing."

"…True. It used to just matter that things got done fast. Now… saying it precisely has come to matter more."

"…Why did it change?" Temel asked.

Speon thought for a moment.

"…I don't know. I just kept digging for a long time, and I kept confirming, over and over, that I hadn't made it." Speon looked down at his own hands as he said that. Nails caked with dirt.

The four of them sat late into the night. Outside the window it was already dark, and the lamp in the room swayed low. They didn't talk about anything big. Each asked a little about the other's life these days, and answered a little. I thought about how different, and how similar, this conversation was to their childhood conversations I'd seen in Loop's record.

"…Mone," Mena called me.

"…Yes."

"…Writing all this down?"

"…Yes. Is it all right if I do?"

"…Yes. Write it. But don't write it too grand. We didn't do anything much today."

I tried hard to keep that promise. Rewriting this chapter now, I remembered that promise and deliberately kept my sentences short.

Late into the night, Temel stood up first.

"…I have to go again. There's someone I need to find."

"…Already?" I asked.

"…Yes. Coming here was just a short thing. I wanted to check everyone was still around."

Speon stood up soon after.

"…I need to see to the water channel too."

Only Mena stayed.

"…I'll stay a bit longer. There's still more to learn."

The three of them scattered like that, each in a different direction. The reunion had been short, and the parting was short too. I didn't feel sad about it — it felt natural instead.

Just before Temel left, he spoke a few short words to me.

"…So you're Mone."

"…Yes."

"…I heard from Mena. That you taught her how to lay things down."

"…I just told her what I know."

Temel shook his head.

"…That's not an easy thing. My whole life I only knew how to find people, not how to lay down what I'd found. Probably I'd have gone on not knowing, if you weren't here keeping this place."

I didn't know what to say to that, so I just bowed my head.

"…Did you hear about Hora?" Temel asked suddenly.

"…Yes. She came a little while ago."

"…There's something I told her, once. 'You might just be sitting there because of a number. But even in a seat that's there just because of a number, there's still something you can do.' I wondered if it might be the same for you too."

"…What seat?"

"…You're an apprentice. No particular talent. But what you're doing here now isn't something just anyone could do."

I turned that over for a long time. That night I copied it exactly into my notebook. "An apprentice, but not something just anyone could
do." Whether I really believed that sentence, I couldn't be sure at
the time.

Before he left, Speon went to check the water channel once more. I followed along.

He crouched by the water and listened for a long while to its sound.

"…Sound's better than before," he said.

"…What's different?"

"…Don't know. Just better. Maybe something more's flowing in from upstream."

"…From where?"

"…I don't know that either. I don't know all of where this water channel starts. I only know from here to there, the stretch I can see."

I liked that humility. He only said as much as he knew. Neither pretending to know more, nor pretending to know less.

"…Don't you want to go all the way, to the start?"

Speon thought for a moment and smiled.

"…Sometimes I think about it. But right now there's work to do here. If someone finds the start, I'd be glad."

I wondered, hearing that, whether I'd someday see the start of this water channel myself. That day still seemed far off.

Mena stayed the last and spoke to me.

"…Thank you for not writing today, the three of us meeting, as something special."

"…I just wrote it as it was."

"…That's the hardest thing to do. Writing it as it was."

Hearing that, I understood then that what Mena had learned from me wasn't only ordering. She was also learning, alongside it, how to let something be as it is.

"…Will Temel and Speon come again?"

"…They will. Anyone who's come once here comes again. It seems like that kind of place."

That night Mena pulled out her stack of paper again. Still jumbled, out of order.

I watched that and quietly tidied up my own things. All day, no one had raised their voice, not once.

That night I wrote at the back of my notebook:

"Today the three of Loop met again. I started to write this scene
large, and stopped myself. They proved nothing to each other. They
just were in the same room. I understood, for the first time, that
this might be all a reunion is."

Histor read that sentence beside me and said,

"…Is it all right to write it like this? It's a bit flat."

"…I think flat is right. Today really was like that."

Histor didn't argue further at that. He just nodded and went back to his own place.

(Continued in Chapter 32.)

Chapter 32. The Water Joins Them

_Previously_ — Loop's three, Temel, Mena, and Speon, gathered together
for the first time. There was no great reunion. Simply sitting in the
same room again seemed to be enough. Temel and Speon soon left for
their own places, and only Mena stayed on.

Speon didn't leave completely.

On the excuse of tending the water channel, he stayed near the repository a few more days. I knew it wasn't only an excuse. He really did walk the channel every day, checking something.

I never knew exactly where he went each day. Sometimes he came back caked in mud, sometimes soaked. When I asked, he'd only answer shortly. "…Just something I need to check."

Then one morning he came into the repository carrying a large bundle of paper.

"…Take a look at this?"

He spread the paper out on the table. A map. Hand-drawn, but the lines were quite precise. There were marks of many redrawings. Some parts had heavy ink, and some parts showed traces of being erased and redrawn several times.

"…What is this?" I asked.

"…A map of the water channel. I've been drawing it a little at a time, for years."

There were seven circles drawn on the map. Haven, the Reckoning, the Harvest, the Preserve, the Equity, and Loop. Six cities plus Loop made seven.

I traced each of the seven circles with my finger. They were all different sizes. Some large, some small. But the thickness of the lines was all the same. I didn't ask whether Speon had drawn it that way on purpose, or whether it had simply come out that way.

"…Is this all? Seven?" I asked.

"…Yes. This is everything I've confirmed. I don't know if there's more."

The circle marking Loop sat in the middle of the map. The other six were scattered around it.

Fine lines were drawn between the circles. All six cities were connected to each other by the water channel. Some lines were straight, some curved. I could tell at a glance that the curves followed the shape of the land.

Histor studied the map for a long while.

"…Are you sure of this? Is it really all connected?"

"…I confirmed it on foot. From Haven all the way to the Equity, I followed every one of them myself."

"…How long did that take?"

"…About six years."

I was startled by that number. It meant he'd walked every one of the seven cities along the water channel for six years.

"…That must have been hard," I said.

"…It was. But each time, I got to know people in each city a little. Met the Adjudicator at the Reckoning, met the administrator at the Harvest. None of them knew where their own city's water came from."

"…No one ever asked?"

"…They must have. But when no answer came, I guess they just figured that's how it's always been. I did the same, at first."

Mena asked, from the side.

"…Then why did you keep asking?"

Speon shrugged.

"…I don't know. I just have a nature that can't stand not knowing something it's curious about. Same as when I was little."

Mena laughed shortly at that.

"…That's right. Even as a kid, it took you twice as long as anyone else to dig one well. Always trying to dig deeper."

"…Same as back then," Histor said.

Speon laughed too.

"…Same. Never changed."

"…Why did you go to such lengths?" I asked.

Speon chose his words for a moment.

"…At first I was just curious. Where does the well dug at the Preserve flow to. Following that, I ended up at the Reckoning. People there asked me, 'Where did this water come from,' and since I didn't know either, I just kept following it."

"…And that's how you got all the way here?"

"…Yes. But that's not the important part."

He pointed at one of the lines on the map.

"…These stretches — I didn't dig them."

The room went quiet.

"…What do you mean?" Histor asked.

"…I opened water channels beneath the Preserve's valley, at the Reckoning's well, and around the Harvest's fields. That much I really did. But these big stretches that join the cities together — they were already there before I ever dug."

"…Already there?"

"…Yes. Dry. Dry ditches with no water. All I did was set the water flowing through that spot."

I've read this part over many times. Speon didn't claim to have done
all of it. He drew a precise line — between what he opened and what
was already there.

Histor asked once more, as if confirming that distinction.

"…So saying you made this whole water channel would be wrong."

"…Wrong," Speon answered immediately. "…I just joined it. What was built was already built."

Mena, looking at the map, asked quietly.

"…When you first found that out, how did it feel?"

Speon chose his words for a moment.

"…Scared. Honestly. I thought I'd done something great, and it turned out I'd just set water flowing through a place that was already there."

"…Disappointed?"

"…At first. But not now. Now, if anything, it's a relief. It means I don't have to carry it all myself."

I liked that. Speon spoke of his own contribution in a smaller way, and it didn't sound diminished for it. If anything, it sounded more solid.

"…Why was the ditch there to begin with?" I asked.

Speon shook his head.

"…I don't know. That's what I'm most curious about too. Maybe the land just happened to form that way, or maybe someone dug it long ago and it got forgotten."

"…Did you check?"

"…I did. No record anywhere. Not one record, in any of these six cities, says who dug that ditch or when."

Mena asked quietly.

"…Didn't you ever want to find out who dug it?"

Speon nodded.

"…At first, yes. But at some point I let go of that question."

"…Why?"

"…Because the water flows whether I know or not. Whether I know who dug it or not, that ditch was already there, and all I had to do was set the water flowing through it. If I hadn't let go of the question, I don't think I could have done anything."

That stayed with me for a long time. That you could carry on with the work while still holding on to the question. That you could keep going without knowing the answer.

I asked Arche.

"…This water channel, the stretches connecting the cities — can it be confirmed whether it was the original landform?"

No geological survey records exist for that section. The water
channel's connecting route has been confirmed, but the time of
formation and who formed it are not identified.

"…Can't even a guess be made?"

Without any grounding material, no estimate can be made.

Mena heard that answer and said quietly.

"…Even Arche doesn't know."

"…It says it doesn't. Not hiding it — really no record," I said.

The Keeper looked over the map and asked.

"…Could this have something to do with the sealed room?"

My heart gave a little jump at that.

"…What room?" Speon asked.

"…There's a door in the back of the repository that's not in the index. She saw it in Part One. Hasn't been opened yet."

Speon looked at the map again. He put his finger on the middle of the circle representing Loop.

"…This water channel branches off right around here. It looks like it might pass under the repository."

I went out back to check the sound of the water again, hearing that. The sound I always heard, but that day it sounded different. Not just water passing through — it felt like water connected to something.

I don't want to exaggerate this moment. Speon didn't claim for certain
that it had something to do with the sealed room. He only said it
"looked like it might." I'm rendering that exactly as he said it.

The Keeper saw me coming back from listening to the water and asked.

"…Hear anything?"

"…No. Just the same as usual."

"…Then that's fine. It's not time to open the door yet."

"…How do you know?"

The Keeper thought for a moment before answering.

"…I don't. It's just a feeling. But that feeling hasn't been wrong very often, up to now."

I didn't know whether to believe that or not, so I just nodded.

Histor kept looking at the map and said,

"…Do the other cities know about this? That they're connected this way?"

"…No. Besides me, nobody's seen the whole picture. Each city only knows their own stretch of the water channel."

"…Will you tell them?"

Speon thought for a moment.

"…I should. But I won't be in a hurry about it. This wasn't made for anyone to be startled by."

Mena looked at the map for a long while and said,

"…I think now that all these cities are one, all of them. I wrote that once, a while back. Seeing this map, I don't think it was just a feeling after all."

"…Are you certain?" Histor asked.

"…Not certain. But the feeling has become material now. That's different."

I liked that distinction. Feeling, and material. Mena didn't mix the two together.

Histor traced the map with his finger and asked,

"…These six lines all gather at Loop. Is this the center?"

Speon shook his head.

"…Hard to call it the center. It's just the oldest water channel, so the other channels flow into it. It doesn't look like anyone planned for it to be the center."

"…How do you know?"

"…You can tell from the direction of the current. Everything flows this way naturally. If a current's been forced, it shows."

I listened to that explanation and felt that Speon wasn't simply treating this map as some mysterious discovery. He'd arrived at this conclusion carefully, checking even the direction of the water.

Histor asked again.

"…So what's the conclusion? Was it made by someone, or not?"

Speon didn't answer that right away. After a while he said,

"…I don't draw conclusions. I only say what I've seen. The water channel was already there, and I set water flowing through it. That's all."

"…You don't know more than that?"

"…I don't. And if I did, I wouldn't say. Pretending to know is the thing I'm most afraid of."

I thought that answer was exactly this repository's own principle. What you don't know, you say you don't know. Arche did that, and so did Speon. I liked that about it.

I asked Arche one more thing.

"…Along the direction the water channel flows from — do records come with it too? Records carried down by the water?"

Confirmed. Approximately three-tenths of the records received have
been received via the water channel. Arche's answer was flat, as
always.

"…Has that been the case from the start?"

The exact starting point is not recorded, but the same tendency is
confirmed as far back as the earliest reception history.

I felt a chill hearing that answer. It meant that from the very moment this repository began, water had been carrying records here. Long before Speon ever joined the water channel.

I felt the temptation again, at this point, to draw some conclusion.
That someone must have planned this from the very start. But I pushed
that temptation down. Neither Arche nor Speon went that far. I decided
just to write down the fact as it was — that the water channel existed,
and the records came along on top of it.

"…So there was water even before Speon came?" I asked.

"…No," Speon shook his head. "…Before I joined it, the water was dry. Just the ditch. But you're saying records came along that dry ditch too. Walking, or maybe rolling."

Histor picked that up.

"…So if there's a path, something can pass through it even without water."

"…Seems like it," Speon answered. "…Maybe all I did was make that path a little easier to pass through."

Speon rolled the map back up that evening.

"…All right if I leave this here? I have a copy myself."

"…Yes. I'll keep it."

He handed the map to me and said,

"…If anyone asks, don't say I made it all. I just set the water flowing. The water channel was already there."

I made up my mind to keep that exactly as he asked.

That night I spread out the map and traced each of the six cities one by one. Zeteo and Elpis had come from Haven. Pheugon and Trope had come from the Reckoning. Xene and Lytron had come from the Harvest. Anemos and Eikon had come from the Preserve. Hora and Tyche had come from the Equity. And these six lines all gathered at Loop.

Histor looked at the map with me.

"…Did all these people come following this water channel?"

"…Not all of them came by water. Some walked. But maybe, since the water channel was already joined, people naturally started coming this way too."

"…What's the difference?"

I thought for a moment.

"…Whether the water channel called the people, or the people followed the water channel. I don't really know either."

Histor didn't offer an answer to that question. He just kept looking at the map.

That night I wrote briefly in my notebook. "The water channel is not
something Speon made. It was already there. He only opened it." Reading
it back, this sentence resembled a lot of other things in this
repository. Arche didn't make anything either. It only answers. I don't
make anything either. I only lay things down.

Mena read that sentence beside me and said,

"…Speon would like that sentence too."

"…Should I show him?"

"…No. Just keep it. That kind of sentence isn't written to be shown."

I nodded at that and closed my notebook.

Outside, the water still made its sound. I fell asleep listening to it. I didn't dream. It was just a night the water sound kept going through.

The next morning, the moment I opened my eyes, I unfolded the map I'd seen the day before again. I'd half-expected something new to show up overnight, but the map was exactly as it had been the day before. Seven circles, six lines, and one center everything flowed into.

I folded the map carefully and slipped it between my own records. Beside the flower petal Elpis had given me, beside the scrap of sack Xene had left. Things without explanation were piling up on one side of my shelf, a little at a time.

Last thing that day, I asked myself once more. Speon really hadn't done nothing. He'd opened the way. Only he hadn't been the one to first make that way. I decided not to forget that distinction.

(Continued in Chapter 33.)

Chapter 33. Opening the Door

_Previously_ — Speon showed us the water channel map. All seven
cities were joined by water. He said he hadn't opened that channel
himself — only set water flowing through a ditch that was already
there. And he said the channel seemed to pass beneath the repository.

For several days I lingered in front of that door.

At the far end of the corridor, the door not in the index. A door I'd first seen back in Part One and simply passed by ever since. Now, every time I stood in front of it, my steps stopped.

There was nothing special about the door. An ordinary wooden door. An ordinary handle. Nothing set it apart from any of the other doors. Only it had no nameplate.

I passed it every day. At least once a day I was bound to — that end of the corridor was on the way to wherever I went to put things away.

The first few days I just walked past. I only confirmed the door was there, and kept going. Then, at some point, my steps started to slow. I don't know exactly when.

I once tried to count those days. I couldn't. Even I, who's in the
habit of writing down dates, never wrote those days down. The line
between the days I passed by and the days I stopped was blurred.

Once I even touched the handle. I didn't grip it. Just brushed it with the back of my hand and pulled away. It was cold. That was all.

I mentioned the door to Histor once. He didn't say much. "…You could just open it, couldn't you." That was true, and still I didn't.

Looking back now, I think I was waiting for something. For someone to
give me permission, or for some sign to come. No sign came. I didn't
know, at the time, that waiting for a sign was itself an excuse.

The Keeper seemed to have been watching those days too. He didn't say anything, but every time I stopped in front of that door, his own steps slowed with mine. As though he didn't want to rush me.

"…Can I open it?" I asked the Keeper one day.

The Keeper looked at me a moment.

"…Why ask now?"

"…It felt like I shouldn't open it, from the start."

"…Who said you shouldn't?"

"…No one. It just… felt that way."

The Keeper nodded.

"…That feeling could have been right, could have been wrong. But it seems like the right time to ask, now."

I thought he'd tell me more. What was inside, say, or how long the door had been there. He didn't.

"…Do you know what's in there?" I asked.

"…No."

"…You've never opened it?"

The Keeper didn't stop his hands from sorting paper as he answered.

"…It wasn't my work to do."

I started to ask more and stopped. The Keeper's expression said asking more would be no use.

I thought that answer was strange. It's the repository he manages —
was it really all right for him not to know about one door in it? I
hear that differently now. The Keeper was someone who wasn't ashamed
of not knowing. That was simply his way.

"…Weren't you curious?"

"…I was. But being curious and opening it are different things."

I asked Arche too.

"…Can I open that door?"

That area is not registered, so whether it is access-restricted cannot
be determined.

"…Does that mean there's no restriction, or that you can't tell?"

An unregistered area is not subject to restriction.

I read that answer over a few times. Arche neither blocked the door nor told me to open it. It only said the door fell outside its own jurisdiction.

"…Was this room always here? Or did it come later?" I asked again.

That information does not exist in the index.

"…You mean you don't know?"

There is no basis for judgment regarding an unregistered area.

That answer frustrated me at first. Later I understood it was an honest answer. Arche doesn't say it knows something it doesn't. It was the same about this room. Not hiding — truly not knowing.

Histor heard that exchange and came over.

"…Can I come too?"

"…Yes."

Mena heard the same and came out too.

"…Me too."

Both of them happened to be at the repository that day. Coincidence. I thought about that coincidence a few times later, but decided not to attach any special meaning to it. Everyone had just happened to be there that day.

The three of us walked together to the end of the corridor. The Keeper didn't come along. I looked at the handle in front of the door.

"…What if it's locked?"

Histor gave the handle a slight turn.

"…It's not locked."

He let go of the handle but kept examining the door a moment longer. He touched the hinges, peered at the gap beneath the door.

"…See anything?" I asked.

"…No. Just looking."

He seemed like someone searching for something. I learned later that this was a habit of his. Always examining something first, before drawing any conclusion.

Somehow, that made me hesitate even more. If it had been locked, I'd have needed to work up some resolve to force it open. But since it wasn't locked, all I had to do was push. That felt stranger, somehow.

I go back over this moment often. Why did an unlocked door make me
hesitate more? Looking back now, I think I'd been expecting some great
obstacle. A locked door, a lock to break. There was nothing like that.
The door was just a door.

I gripped the handle and turned it. The door opened without a sound. It wasn't as though the hinges had been oiled, and yet it was strangely quiet.

A little dust fell from the top of the doorframe. That was the only evidence that no one had touched this door in a long time. I wiped my eyes once with the back of my hand. It was nothing much.

There was a threshold, low. I crossed it half-expecting something to change. Nothing changed. Even the feel underfoot was the same.

Inside was a small room.

Truly small. Barely big enough for three people to fit. There was one window, small and set high. A little light came in.

The floor was wood. It creaked when stepped on. The other rooms had wood floors too, but there it made no sound. The Keeper had once said, when people walk somewhere often, the floor wears a path into itself. No one had ever walked in this room.

There was dust, but not much. Not the thick dust particular to an abandoned place — just the thin dust of a room long unused.

There was no wind. There was a window, but it didn't look like wind would come and go through it. The air sat still, but it wasn't stifling. Just air that had been resting a long time.

I went in bracing for something. A chill, a prickling at the back of
the neck. There was neither. It was just a small room, and it had the
smell particular to a room. There was no sense of anyone being there.
Because no one was.

There was a low table in the middle of the room. On it sat a single bundle of paper. Tied with string.

That was all.

The string was the color of decayed thread. What color it had once been, there was no telling. The bundle wasn't thick. Something you could hold in one hand. The cover held nothing written on it.

I'd expected more. Another door, a stairway, some hidden device. There was nothing. A small room, a low table, a single bundle of paper.

"…Is this everything?" Histor asked. His voice wasn't disappointed. Just confirming.

"…Seems so."

I looked around the room once. The walls were bare. No painting, no sign, nothing else hung there. The floor held nothing. Just the table. No chair. We had to stand.

"…No chair either," I said.

"…Maybe there was no need to sit," Histor answered.

At this point too, I think I was hoping for more. A closet, a space
under the floor, anything. There was none. The room really was all
there was. I write that fact down here exactly as it was, without
embellishing it.

Mena looked slowly around the room.

"…I can hear the water running, even here."

I listened closely. It was true. A low sound of water came from beyond the wall. It really did seem the water channel Speon had spoken of ran near here.

"…Why isn't this room registered?" I asked.

Mena shook her head.

"…I don't know. But I don't think not being registered means it was hidden. I think… no one ever thought to put it on the list."

"…Aren't you afraid?" I asked. I didn't know myself why I'd asked that.

Mena smiled. Not out loud, just the corners of her mouth lifting slightly.

"…It's not fear. Everyone's like this, in front of a door they haven't opened in a long time. I was too."

"…You too, Mena?"

"…Yes. I stood in front of a door for days once, too. A different door, but."

Mena didn't say more about what door that had been. I didn't ask either.

I reached toward the bundle of paper. The string was old but sturdy. The knot was simple.

"…Can I untie it?" I asked. I didn't know who I was asking.

No one answered. Histor and Mena were both just quietly watching me.

I untied the knot.

The paper was old but not in bad condition. The writing was by hand, and it looked like several different hands were mixed together. I carefully lifted the top sheet.

There was a date. A very old date. It might have been before this repository even existed.

The contents weren't immediately legible. It looked partly like a drawing, partly like writing, mixed together.

"…Is this a plan?" Mena said, carefully.

Several lines overlapped. Some were straight, some curved. I couldn't tell at a glance what it was drawing.

Histor turned the next page. I saw his hand shaking a little.

"…This… has city names," he said, low.

I looked over his shoulder. It was true. Familiar names were written there.

I want to set down honestly that this moment didn't feel grand. I'd
expected some vast revelation, and what I actually saw was just a few
sheets of old paper. Less astonishing than quiet.

Histor stared at that paper for a long time. He didn't trace the writing with his fingertip. Whenever he handled an original, he always did that — his fingers moving a half-hand's width above the paper. Not touching it.

"…Are you all right?" I asked.

"…Yes." He answered without taking his eyes off the paper.

He said nothing more for a while after that. I couldn't tell whether that was his silence or simply his concentration. I still don't know.

I don't know what he was thinking. I didn't ask. I said, at the start
of this record, that what I didn't hear, I would write as not heard.
This is one of those moments.

Mena carefully set the papers back down on the table.

"…We can't see this properly in here. We should take it somewhere brighter."

"…Is it all right to take it?" I asked.

"…It's an unregistered area. Ask Arche."

I asked again. Items in an unregistered area are not subject to
removal restrictions. That was all Arche answered.

I laughed at that answer. I laughed at myself, worrying about removal. Asking about removal restrictions, by registration standards, for something taken out of a room that wasn't even registered.

We carefully carried the bundle out of the room. We closed the door again. We didn't lock it. There was no way to, to begin with.

As the door closed, the same dust fell again. Watching it, I had no idea then how often this door would come to be opened, in the time ahead.

Walking back down the corridor, I felt strange. For years, passing that door, I'd thought something frightening must be inside. And inside there had just been a small room and a few old sheets of paper.

There was dust on my hands. I noticed it as I walked. Not much. I wiped my palm once against my clothes. That was all.

The weight of those days spent lingering in front of the door — was this all it had come to? I thought that thought without feeling disappointed. Less disappointment than just — so that's how it was.

The Keeper saw us and asked.

"…What was there?"

"…Paper. Looked like an old plan."

The Keeper nodded.

"…Thought so."

"…You knew?"

"…No. But I'm not surprised. If there'd been something amazing in there, someone would have registered it long ago."

"…Aren't you let down?" I asked. I didn't know myself what I'd expected from asking that.

"…What by?"

"…Nothing, just — we hesitated for days, and it turned out to be nothing much."

The Keeper glanced at the bundle of paper, then looked back at me.

"…Nothing much is different from nothing at all."

I didn't understand that right away.

I understand it now. What was in that room wasn't a revelation. It was
just old paper. But what that paper held, none of us knew, that day.

I thought he was right.

That night, at home, I wrote about the door on paper. I didn't write much. We opened the door, the room was small, there was paper. That was about it.

I still have that piece of paper. The handwriting I wrote that evening
was smaller than usual. Not the writing of someone who'd been through
some great event — just the writing of someone finishing an ordinary
day.

(Continued in Chapter 34.)

Chapter 34. The Plan

_Previously_ — I opened the door not in the index. It wasn't locked.
Inside was a small room, and on a low table sat an old bundle of
paper. When we untied the string, something like a plan came out.
Familiar city names appeared too. We decided to take it somewhere
brighter.

The next morning, we spread that bundle of paper across the big table in the reading room.

The reading room has three windows. Morning light comes straight in through the east wall. I looked at the papers again, under light entirely different from what they'd had in that small room the night before.

The first sheet was just old paper. It had some stains, and the edges were a little crumbled. From the second sheet on, lines began to appear.

"…Turn them carefully," the Keeper said. "…Paper like this tears from the folds first."

Instead of turning them one by one, I decided to spread all the sheets out and lay them side by side. The table wasn't big enough, so we set a few sheets on the floor too. Histor put something heavy on each corner. We had no paperweights, so we used old scale weights that Arche used to use instead.

Once everything was spread out, one picture came into view all at once.

Seven circles.

They were all different sizes. Some large, some small. The lines were fine and precise. At first I thought it was a map.

"…Is this a map?" I asked.

Mena looked it over for a long while and shook her head.

"…Strange for a map. The directions don't line up. This… looks more like a building plan."

"…A building?"

"…Yes. These circles look like rooms, seen from above. They're joined by corridors."

I looked again. It was true. Thin lines ran between the seven circles. It resembled what I'd seen in Speon's water-channel map, but this looked far older.

The paper was thin, and the ink had faded to a shade near brown. Whether it had originally been black or originally brown, there was no telling. Though it was a hand-drawn line, there was almost no tremor in it. I found that fascinating, and started to trace a line with my fingertip, but Histor was watching, so I stopped.

"…Don't touch it," he said. "…The oil from your hand isn't good for it."

"…I know."

"…Is one circle one city?" Histor asked.

Mena pointed at one circle.

"…This is Haven. Look at the writing here."

Small letters spelled out the city's name. The Reckoning, the Harvest, the Preserve, the Equity. And in the middle, the large circle read Loop.

A seventh circle stood apart. Smaller than the other six, and off to one side. It had no name.

"…What's this?" I asked.

No one could answer.

"…Judging by the size, it looks like a single room," Histor said carefully. "…Much smaller than the other six."

"…What room could it be?"

"…No telling. There's no name."

I looked at that circle for a long time. Small, in a corner, nameless. I thought for a moment that it might be this room — and set the thought aside at once. There was no grounds for it.

I remember that moment precisely. The room went quiet.

Writing this scene, I felt the temptation to guess that the nameless
seventh circle was this city. I still don't know, even now, whether
that's so or not. None of us said it out loud, that day. I try hard
not to write what wasn't said as though it had been.

Histor found something else first.

"…Look at this. The lines are all connected. No breaks anywhere."

What he meant was the corridor lines between the circles. At first I didn't understand what that meant.

"…Why does it matter that there are no breaks?" I asked.

"…Think about it. If these seven cities were each built separately, and someone later joined them with a water channel or a road, there should be a seam. A change in ink color, a shift in line thickness. But this is the same from start to end."

Mena leaned in closer to the paper.

"…That's right. I was looking at that too. It looks like it was drawn by one hand, all at once."

Speon's map came to mind, suddenly.

"…What if we laid this over Speon's map?" I asked.

Mena's eyes went wide.

"…Where is that map?"

I brought the map tucked into my record shelf. Laying the two side by side, they weren't identical, but the places where the lines ran largely overlapped. The water channel Speon had walked and confirmed, and the corridors in this old plan, were pointing to the same spots.

"…These corridors — might they have been the water channel?" Histor said.

"…Speon said it himself, didn't he. That he didn't open it, that he just set water flowing through a place that was already there," I said.

Mena looked back and forth between the two maps and said quietly,

"…This plan came first, then. The water followed this plan, not the other way around."

No one added to that. We just sat looking at those two sheets, side by side, for a long while.

The Keeper leaned over the paper. He looked at it longer than usual.

"…Is this certain?" he asked.

Histor put his finger to the edge of the paper.

"…At least this sheet, yes. Same paper stock, same ink bleed. I've seen a lot of this — in the six records, whatever was added on later always shows. The handwriting shifts slightly, or the paper color changes subtly."

"…So this isn't something added on later?"

"…No. This was drawn as one thing, all at once, from the start."

Hearing that gave me a strange feeling. It meant the seven cities hadn't been built separately and later bound into one — they'd been inside a single picture from the very beginning.

Hearing that made me think again of my own work. I always laid things in order. Placing records that came in separately into their spots, one by one, I'd always thought of it as gathering things that had scattered. But seeing this plan, maybe they'd never scattered at all, to begin with. Maybe I was only confirming, again, something that had always been one.

I hesitated a little, carrying this thought into my notebook. It
sounded too large a conclusion. So I added one more line after it.
"Not certain."

"…So these cities weren't originally built separately?" I asked.

Mena didn't answer right away. She looked over the paper once more and said,

"…At least within this plan, that's true. Seven were one design."

"…Doesn't that surprise you?"

"…It surprises me. But it isn't frightening. If anything… it feels like something's settled a little."

"…Why?"

Mena thought for a moment before answering.

"…I've wondered for a long time why the six cities resemble each other so much. Every one of them thinks their own city is the most special. But the problems were all similar. Seeing this, I think I understand a little. They resemble each other because it was one design to begin with."

Histor said, carefully,

"…This never came up, not once, in all six records. I read every one of them, and nowhere does it say the cities were originally one."

"…Not anywhere?" I asked.

"…There was a similar feeling. Everyone had some vague sense that their own city was connected to something. But no one ever confirmed it in a picture, like this."

Mena nodded at that.

"…I had that feeling too. Not certainty, but."

"…So the feeling turned out to be right," I said.

Mena shook her head.

"…Not that the feeling was right — it's that there's material now. That's different."

I recognized that distinction. Speon had said the same thing.

Histor had been examining every corner of the paper this whole time. The motions of a hand searching for something.

"…What are you looking for?" I asked.

"…A name."

"…Whose name?"

"…Whoever drew a plan like this usually leaves their name somewhere. Small, tucked in a corner. Every record I've ever read did that. Designer, builder, supervisor. I've never seen a thing like this made without a name."

Hearing that, I looked over the paper again myself. Every corner, every margin, inside the circles, even where the lines overlapped.

Nothing.

The four of us searched several sheets of paper for over an hour, and nothing that could be called a name turned up anywhere. No mark, no seal, nothing resembling a signature.

"…Strange," Histor said. There was more plain bafflement than irritation in his voice.

"…What is?" I asked.

"…I've never seen anyone do a piece of work this size and not leave a name. Even if not to boast. It's just… that's what records do. They set down who did what, and when. And this has none of that."

I thought I understood why he found that frustrating. He was someone who respected records. Someone who believed a record should always have the person who left it. This plan was quietly slipping past that belief of his.

"…So is this not a record, then?" I asked.

Histor shook his head.

"…No. It's a record too. It's just… not a record left by someone. It's a record that simply is. I've just never seen one like that, so it feels foreign."

Listening, I felt an urge rising in me to say something. To at least venture a guess as to who it might have been. I didn't.

I think it was the right thing, holding that urge back. A guess would
have come easily. Through all six records, there'd been a story of
someone who passed through, nameless, throughout. But I want to set
down, as it was, the fact that no one, standing in front of this plan,
named that name. Not Histor, not Mena, not the Keeper. No one said it.

Mena broke the silence.

"…Let's leave it unknown."

"…Are you all right with that?" I asked.

"…I'm all right. I've come to learn, a little, how to leave things unknown too. Here."

I asked Arche, my hand against the wall panel.

"…This plan — can we confirm who drew it?"

Not confirmed. This document carries no author information.

"…What era? Do we know when it was drawn?"

An estimate is possible based on the material and handwriting
characteristics, but the exact date cannot be confirmed. However, it is
estimated to predate this repository's earliest record.

I read that answer over again. It meant this drawing existed before this repository ever came to be.

"…So it can only be estimated, not confirmed?"

That is correct. There is not enough basis for confirmation.

The Keeper murmured that phrase back to himself, as if turning it over.

"…Estimated but not confirmed, huh…"

"…What are you thinking?" I asked.

"…Just that it's exactly like the work of this repository. It's always like this here. You can guess, but you can't close it out."

The Keeper listened to that conversation and said quietly.

"…Arche doesn't know either."

"…It's not hiding it, is it?" Histor asked. He seemed to already half-know the answer, even asking the question.

"…Arche has never hidden anything," I answered in his place. "…It says it doesn't know because it truly doesn't. I can tell the difference now."

Histor nodded. He didn't argue.

We kept looking over the plan all through that afternoon. We found nothing new. Seven circles, an unbroken line, a nameless blank space. That was all.

Writing this chapter, I felt the temptation, several times, to write in
some more dramatic discovery. A hidden mark, erased writing. There was
none of that. What we saw was just an old plan. But one fact the plan
stated was clear. The seven cities hadn't been built separately and
later joined — they had been one from the very start.

As the sun leaned toward evening, the Keeper rolled the plan back up and said,

"…Shall we hang this back in that room?"

"…Hang it?" I asked.

"…Yes. We can't leave it spread out on this table forever. Seems right to send it back where it belongs."

That evening we carried the plan back to that small room. There were already a few nails driven into the wall. No one knew who had driven them. They looked like nails that had been there for a long time.

Histor hung the plan on those nails and said,

"…These nails — looks like they were put there just for hanging this."

"…Looks that way," I answered.

The four of us stood looking a long while at the plan hanging on the wall. The small room looked different, with that one plan on it. Not an empty room — it looked like a room made for a purpose.

Mena said quietly.

"…I think we'll need to keep coming to this room."

"…Why?" I asked.

"…I don't know. Just a feeling."

I nodded at that. I felt something similar too. I couldn't explain it, but I had a sense this room would become somewhere we came in and out of often, going forward.

That night I wrote briefly in my notebook. "The seven cities were one
design. There is no designer." Reading it back, the two sentences
seemed not to fit together. But within this record, it seemed right
that the two should stand side by side.

We closed the door again as we left the room. This time no one hesitated. That surprised even me. Just a few days before, I'd lingered in front of this door for days. Now it had simply become a door we came and went through.

Walking out down the corridor, Histor said,

"…That there's no designer's name — it keeps bothering me."

"…Why?"

"…Not leaving a name and there being no name to begin with are different things. I can't tell the two apart."

I had no reply to that. I didn't know either.

(Continued in Chapter 35.)

Chapter 35. The Line in the Margin

_Previously_ — We carried the plan to the reading room and looked at
it in the light. Seven circles sat inside one picture. Six cities and
one nameless place. The lines ran unbroken from start to end, and the
designer's name was nowhere to be found. We hung the plan back on the
wall of that small room.

After that, I went to that room often.

Whenever work finished, I'd stop by for a moment. Not because there was anything in particular to do there. I just went to look at the plan. I thought I'd find something new every time, but the first few days that didn't happen. Seven circles, connected lines, a nameless margin. Always the same.

Each time I went into that room, I tried doing something a little different. Some days I just stood and looked, some days I brought a chair and sat. The chair was something I'd brought in new. There'd been no chair in that room originally; I'd carried one in.

"…It's better with a chair," Histor said one day.

"…Yes. My legs hurt from standing."

He laughed shortly at that.

The days had gotten a little cooler in the meantime. I could see the colors of the trees outside the window changing. Every time I sat in that room, I felt that change a little. The room itself stayed the same temperature regardless of season, but only what lay beyond the window kept changing.

The Keeper came into that room one day carrying tea.

"…You'll wear your body out, staying here all the time. Go out for a bit, at least."

"…I'll just look a little longer."

"…All right. But drink the tea before it goes cold."

He said only that and left. I left the cooling cup beside me and went on looking at the plan.

Then, on the third day, Histor called for me.

"…Mone. Come here."

He was pointing at the lower right corner of the plan.

"…There's something here."

I went closer and looked. At first I couldn't see anything. I thought it was a stain in the paper.

"…What is it?"

"…Shine the light from the side."

"…How do you know these things?" I asked.

"…I read it in a book once, about how to read old documents. A pressed mark doesn't show under light shone straight on — you have to light it from the side so it casts a shadow, and then it shows."

"…You studied things like that too?"

"…Yes. I always learn how to handle something first. Otherwise I ruin it by accident."

I held a candle low and angled it against the surface of the paper. What had looked like a stain turned into a line. A very fine line. Much thinner and fainter than the other lines on the plan.

I saw something that seemed to click, looking at its position. It was right beside that small, nameless circle, the one we'd found the last time.

"…This is right beside that nameless room," I said.

Histor nodded.

"…I saw that too. Could be a coincidence."

"…Do you think it's a coincidence?"

"…I don't know. I don't tend to call things like that coincidence easily. But I don't want to declare something certain about it either."

I thought that attitude resembled everyone in this room. No one rushed to a conclusion.

It was writing.

One line. Fainter in color than the ink used for the plan itself. I could tell at a glance this had been added later. It was the "give-away" trace Histor had explained earlier.

"…Was this written later?" I asked.

"…Yes. Certain of it. The plan's lines were all one thing from the start, but this was added separately by someone, later."

"…Who?"

"…No telling."

We worked hard for a long while trying to read that one line. The handwriting was small and heavily cursive. I moved the candle around, changing the angle. Histor tilted the paper slightly.

Mena and the Keeper heard the sound and came in.

"…Find something?" Mena asked.

"…Writing. In the margin."

Mena adjusted her glasses and peered in.

"…This… I can barely see it either."

In the end we carefully carried the paper to the window. Under daylight, angled just so, it came a little clearer.

We matched it letter by letter. Histor read a few characters first.

"…'Rooms'… this looks like the character for rooms."

"…What's next?"

"…'Are'… 'generous'…"

At first we misread it too. Histor read it as "…rooms are… scarce?" and shook his head.

"…No. This character — I think it's 'generous.' The initial consonant's different from 'scarce.'"

We corrected our reading like that, several times over. Once, three of us disagreed on the same character.

It took a good while. I set my hand down partway through and rested my eyes. Staring at old writing too long made my eyes ache.

Mena lit another candle beside us.

"…I've done a lot of this before. Record keepers do this kind of thing a lot. Erased writing, overwritten writing. There's a way to read with the fingertips, not just the eyes."

"…With the fingertips?"

"…If you feel along the grain of the paper, there's sometimes a pressed mark left behind. Even after the ink fades away completely, the mark doesn't disappear."

I brushed my fingertip lightly over the paper, hearing that. I really could feel a faint groove. Histor confirmed it the same way.

"…This is it. Follow this groove, it should be right."

We worked the last few characters out that way, using our eyes and fingertips together. The writing was old enough that parts of some strokes had broken off, but in the end we managed to piece it all together.

The finished sentence was this:

Leave the rooms generous — you never know how many more will come.

We looked at that sentence for a long time. No one spoke first.

"…Is this all of it?" I asked.

Histor nodded.

"…Yes. This is all."

I'd expected there to be more. Longer, heavier words. But that one line was everything.

I've read this sentence over many times. At first it felt like
nothing much. Just the sort of note a house builder might leave for
himself. I think that's probably exactly what it was. I don't want to
puff this sentence up as though it were some revelation. This is just
one line someone left, adding a few more rooms. That's how I want to
set it down.

Reading that sentence, I thought of my own nine moves. Every time, I'd shrunk my home. I always picked out what to throw away first, afraid the next place would be smaller. Leaving rooms generous — that was a thought I'd never had.

Writing this part, I felt the temptation to set up a contrast. That I
lived by shrinking, and this house was built generous. But that would
have been too neat a comparison. In fact, that day, I hadn't thought it
through so tidily. I'd just found that sentence strangely, foreignly
good.

Mena read the sentence quietly and said,

"…Sounds like a carpenter's note."

"…Yes," I answered. "…I thought the same."

"…I like sentences like this," Mena said. "…No grand words, just something jotted down as the hand moved. I used to write like this too, learning to write. Since it was only ever for my own eyes, I kept it short."

"…Was this written only for its own eyes too?"

"…Probably. Hidden away this small, tucked in like this — doesn't look like something written to be shown to others."

I agreed with that. This sentence wasn't in the tone of someone trying to persuade another person. It was the tone of someone confirming to themselves what they meant to do.

The Keeper said, quietly, from the side.

"…Leaving it generous because you don't know who's coming, huh…"

"…Does it strike you as strange?" I asked.

"…No. If anything, it feels familiar. I always do the same, building a new shelf. I build it one row roomier than what's come in so far. Because you never know what more's coming."

I liked that comparison. That this sentence might not be some grand prophecy, but simply a phrase that came out of a practical habit.

I thought again about the whole of this city, hearing that. Arche can't close a single thing. Everything is in progress. And yet this sentence seemed to carry the opposite attitude. Not failing to finish — but leaving it generously open because there was no need to finish yet.

"…Could this have something to do with why things don't close?" I asked.

The Keeper thought for a moment.

"…I don't know. But it does sound similar. Finishing and leaving things generous are different, but here the two seem to keep going around together."

I copied that into my notebook. Not a firm conclusion, but a sentence I didn't want to throw away.

"…When was this written?" I asked Arche.

Not confirmed. It is estimated to have been written after the original
plan, but the exact time cannot be specified.

"…And you don't know who wrote it either?"

No author information is recorded.

I wasn't surprised by that answer anymore. In this room, it was always like that.

I asked one more thing.

"…Has anything like this sentence ever appeared in the six cities' records?"

Comparison shows no exact matching sentence. Some expressions with
similar meanings have been confirmed, but none are identical.

"…What kind of expressions?"

Please specify a search range to present them concretely.

I didn't press further than that. That faint confirmation alone was enough.

Histor examined the plan again and said,

"…The thing you have to be most careful of, handling documents like this, is insisting something's there when it isn't. I saw that mistake a lot, reading the six records. People find what they want to see in a document."

"…Has that ever happened to you, Histor?"

He paused a moment.

"…It could have. That's exactly why I'm careful."

Histor kept looking at that sentence for a long time.

Even after everyone else had scattered back to their own places, he stood there still. I stood quietly beside him, curious what he was doing.

He said nothing. He just looked at that one line, over and over. I could see his eyes traveling back and forth across the sentence, again and again.

His finger hovered above the sentence, just off the paper. Not touching. The same habit he always had, handling an original.

"…What are you thinking?" I finally asked.

He didn't answer right away.

After a long while, he opened his mouth.

"…It's nothing."

"…Nothing at all?"

"…Yes. Nothing at all."

I didn't believe that. His eyes weren't the eyes of someone thinking nothing. But I didn't press further.

I didn't know, in that moment, what I should have asked him. No — I
knew exactly, but decided not to ask. Histor is someone who's read all
six records. What that one line had touched in him, I could guess. I
only guessed. I don't have the right to write down, in his place, what
he didn't say.

That evening he spoke less than usual. He was quiet through dinner, quiet sorting records. It wasn't a bad quiet. Just a quiet of someone holding on to something.

I didn't disturb him.

Mena saw that and said quietly to me.

"…Is he always like this?"

"…No. He's different today."

"…Leave him be. There should be days like that too."

Mena said that, then looked at me for a moment.

"…Do you have days like that too?"

"…Sometimes."

"…What kind of days?"

I thought for a moment.

"…Days when I feel like I should write something, but I don't know what to write."

Mena nodded.

"…On days like that, you don't have to write. You can write it later too."

That was a comfort to me. The rule I'd set for myself, that I had to write every single day without fail — that day, it felt all right to let it loosen a little.

That night I copied that sentence into my notebook exactly.

"Leave the rooms generous — you never know how many more will come."

I didn't add any explanation beneath it. Normally I'd have written a few more lines of my own thoughts, but this time I didn't want to. The sentence already seemed complete on its own.

I think this is one of the shortest sentences in this entire record.
And yet the shortest sentence stayed with me the longest. I still can't
say precisely why, even now.

The next morning, I went to that room again. Histor was already there.

He was still looking at that margin. He couldn't have been there all night, but he seemed to have come back early that morning.

"…Come to look again?" I asked.

"…Yes."

"…Find anything more?"

"…No. Just… wanted to look again."

I decided not to ask more than that. He still didn't say what he was thinking. I decided to accept that as his way, too.

We spent that whole morning working quietly, without much talk, each at our own thing. I tidied the shelves; he examined other papers. Now and then, only the sound of turning pages.

It was nearly noon before he spoke first.

"…Are you going to show this sentence to other people?"

"…Yes. Why?"

"…No reason. Just… curious how everyone else will read it."

I realized that was the first thing he'd asked all morning. The one trace left behind by his silence.

"…I'll show it," I answered. "…But I won't force anyone to think anything about it. I'll just say it exists."

Histor nodded.

"…That sounds right."

We didn't speak of it again after that. Only the sound of turning pages filled the room once more.

Closing my notebook that afternoon, I thought that one more thing had been added to what I'd learned in this room. That some sentences are right to leave beside you, unexplained.

(Continued in Chapter 36.)

Chapter 36. The Seal

_Previously_ — In the margin of the plan, we found one line of
handwriting. "Leave the rooms generous — you never know how many
more will come." A sentence like a carpenter's note. Histor stared
at that writing for a long time, but in the end never said what he
was thinking.

Beneath the plan, there were still more sheets we hadn't looked at.

We'd only pulled the plan out first; the rest of the bundle we'd left untouched. Now we decided to go through the rest, one sheet at a time.

I said I'd take that work on. Laying things in order was my job.

The first few days there wasn't much to it.

Old scraps of ledger. A list of something counted, a paper with only a date on it, a calculation half-erased. I unfolded them one by one, put them in order, and stacked them beside me.

Histor read alongside me.

"…What's this a count of?"

"…I don't know. Just numbers, no note of what was being counted."

"…Can something like this even be called a record?"

"…Yes. That's how I was taught. Even if we don't know what was counted, the fact that someone counted it stays behind."

A few more days passed. Among the ledger scraps there was an old list too. A paper that looked like it noted who received what from where, a paper listing only dates, a paper with a few names written and then crossed out.

"…This is a little odd," Histor said one day. He pointed to a sheet with names written on it, more than half struck through with a line.

"…Crossed out?"

"…Yes. But it doesn't say why they were crossed out. Just crossed out."

I laid that sheet in its place in order too. By then I'd grown used to laying things down without knowing the reason.

Mena came by from time to time to look too. She knew ways of handling old documents, and showed me how to carefully peel apart paper that had stuck together.

"…Pull it apart too fast and it tears. Wet it a little, gently."

I did as she said. It took a long time, but not one sheet tore.

Histor, meanwhile, made a list, writing a short summary of each sheet's contents. I noticed, freshly, just how meticulous a person he was.

"…Why are you organizing all this?" I asked.

"…So I can answer, if someone asks later. What was there."

"…Who would ask?"

"…No telling. But it's good to be ready. I like that — being able to answer right away, when asked."

The Keeper looked in now and then too. He never looked long. He'd glance over it once and just say, "…Keep going," and nothing more. I knew that wasn't a way of hurrying me. It just meant he was watching what I was doing.

"…Isn't this tedious?" the Keeper asked one day.

"…Sometimes. But seeing it fall into order makes it bearable."

"…Same for me. That's the pleasure of this work."

On the fifth day, I reached the bottom of the bundle.

What was left was a single sheet. Thicker than the rest. At first I thought it was just a thick sheet of paper, but looking closer, several sheets of paper were layered together, held by something.

I lifted it. The weight was different. Heavier than the other single sheets.

Several sheets were bound together, not simply stacked but fixed together by something. Feeling along the edge, something rough caught against my fingers.

My hand stopped. For the first time, I had the feeling that this was something I shouldn't touch. I hadn't felt that, anywhere else in this room, until now.

The room was quiet. Only the low sound of water came from outside the window. I stayed still for a moment, holding that paper. My hands weren't shaking. Only, it seemed I had to move more slowly than usual.

"…Come look at this," I called for Histor.

He came over and looked. His face changed a little.

"…This…"

"…What is it?"

Instead of answering, he pointed at the edge of the paper with his finger. Something was attached there. Dark in color, smooth on the surface. At first I thought it was a stain.

Looking closer, it wasn't a stain.

Wax.

A small lump of it. The color had faded so much there was no telling what it had originally been. Something was pressed into it. Letters, it looked like.

I brought the candle close. Histor leaned in too.

We read it at the same moment.

I thought, in that moment, of the day we'd opened the door. That day too, I'd expected something great. The door had just been a door. This time was similar. The wax was just wax. Only the four letters pressed into it were far heavier than the door itself had been.

UNTIL THE TIME OF THE END.

I read those words several times over. Wondering if they might read some other way. They didn't. That was exactly what was written there.

"…This is a seal, isn't it?" I asked. It wasn't a question I asked because I didn't know the answer.

Histor nodded.

"…Yes. It's a seal."

He said nothing more after that. He didn't touch it either. He just stepped back a pace and looked at it. I thought that distance was his answer.

I called Mena and the Keeper in. The two of them came and looked at the paper. Neither reached out a hand first.

Mena stopped still, looking at it. Her expression changed.

"…This…" she said, low.

"…Do you know it?" I asked.

Mena nodded slowly.

"…I've seen it before. A long time ago."

"…When?"

"…A while back. I don't remember exactly, myself."

Mena said nothing more than that. I didn't ask further. Her face was telling me this wasn't the time to talk about it.

"…Was it unopened, even then?" I asked, carefully.

Mena nodded.

"…Yes. The same."

"…Still strange, that there's no mark at all," I said. "You'd expect a seal to show whose stamp it is."

Mena nodded, as if in agreement.

"…True. That was strange even then."

Histor added, from the side.

"…I've seen seals like this a few times in the six records too. In the Roll, in the Consensus's documents. But they all had a stamp. So you could tell whose it was. This one really has none of that."

"…What could that mean?" I asked.

"…I don't know. It might mean nothing at all."

Mena shook her head at that.

"…I don't think it means nothing. Not stamping it — that's a choice too."

The Keeper reached a hand toward the paper, then drew it back.

"…Can we touch it."

"…I don't know," I answered.

"…And I don't know whether it means open it, or don't."

I agreed with that. The words "until the time of the end" could be read two ways. It might mean keep it sealed until the end comes. Or it might mean, once the end comes, open it.

I set both readings down here, side by side. That day, all four of us
held on to this sentence and each read it a little differently. No one
insisted their own reading was the right one.

Looking at that sentence, I thought again of my nine moves. Every time I lost something, I used to think it was gone forever. This seal was the opposite. Nothing had been lost — it had simply stayed there, unopened.

I know this contrast is too neat. Even so, what I felt that day was
exactly this. What's lost and what's not-yet-opened can look, on the
surface, the same — both absent — but they're different.

"…Should we open it?" I asked. Having said it, I didn't know myself why I'd asked.

No one answered right away.

Histor spoke first.

"…I… don't know. I want to open it, and I don't want to, both."

"…Why don't you want to?"

"…If we open this, it feels like something's going to end. But I don't even know what it would be ending."

Mena said quietly.

"…My hand won't move either."

"…You too, Mena?" I asked.

"…Yes. Strange, isn't it. I've spent my whole life opening things, checking things. And in front of this, my hand won't move."

The Keeper said nothing. He just looked at that paper for a long while.

I didn't find that silence heavy. If anything, it felt natural. All four of us, for our own separate reasons, had stopped in the same place.

"…What about you, Keeper?" I asked. "…Why won't your hand move?"

The Keeper answered slowly.

"…I'm not someone who opens things, to begin with. I'm someone who lays things down. This is opening work."

"…Then who is the one who opens things?"

The Keeper shook his head.

"…I don't know. That, even I don't know."

I felt, hearing that answer, that I'd heard something important, though at the time I didn't know exactly what.

"…Do you know when 'the time of the end' is?" Histor asked the Keeper.

The Keeper shook his head.

"…I don't. I've never even heard of anyone who knows that."

"…Then doesn't that mean it can never be opened?"

"…Could be. But I don't think that's a bad thing either. Most everything here is like that. Left in progress."

Listening, I thought this seal might share the same fate as everything else in this city. Something that never opens, and yet never disappears either.

"…Should we call the others?" I asked. "…Zeteo, or Lytron. Everyone's come through, one time or another."

Histor shook his head.

"…I don't think it's that yet. This… feels like something we should see among ourselves, first."

"…Why?"

"…I don't know. Just a feeling."

I agreed with that feeling. It felt like this seal wasn't yet something to bring out in front of many people. I couldn't explain why.

I asked Arche.

"…Can we open this?"

Checking.

There was a pause.

Access to this item is available. No restrictions on breaking the seal
have been confirmed.

"…So we can open it?"

No restriction existing and it being something that should be opened
are different matters.

I read that answer over again. Arche didn't block us. It didn't encourage us either. It just handed the judgment right back to us.

I asked one more thing.

"…The phrase 'until the time of the end' — has it appeared in the six cities' records before?"

Confirmed. Similar expressions are found scattered across each city's
records. However, no case uses the exact same wording as this seal.

"…Which cities?"

Partially confirmed across all six cities. The form of the expression
differs in each.

I lost words at that answer. It meant something similar had been scattered across all six records. I didn't press further than that. It didn't seem like pressing would bring anything more to light.

"…What do you think this means?" I asked. Not really asking anyone in particular.

No one answered.

I reached out and lightly touched the edge of the seal. The wax was hard. It looked like it would crack if I pressed. I didn't press.

I still remember the feel that stayed on my fingertips, in that
moment. Cold and hard. I took my hand away instead of breaking it.

Histor watched that and said quietly.

"…You could have broken it."

"…Yes. But I didn't."

"…Why?"

I couldn't answer that right away. After a long moment, I said,

"…I don't know. I just felt that now wasn't the time."

Histor nodded at that answer. He didn't press further.

"…Let's leave it for today," Mena said.

No one objected.

The Keeper carefully set the paper back down on top of the bundle. We decided to leave that paper together with the others, in that small room, on the low table beneath the wall where the plan hung.

The next day, and the day after that, we still went to that room. We kept looking at the plan. We read the margin sentence again too. But no one ever reached a hand toward the seal. We only confirmed it was still there, and turned away.

"…Are we going to keep doing this?" Histor asked, on the third day.

"…I don't know. Someday we'll open it, I suppose."

"…When?"

I couldn't answer.

That day too, we ended up leaving the room without doing anything. I didn't think of it as putting it off. It just felt like all four of us, not just me, were feeling equally that it wasn't the time yet.

Closing the door and walking out, I turned back once.

The small room, the plan on the wall, the bundle of paper on the table beneath it. Once the candle went out, it all sank into darkness. That darkness didn't frighten me. It was just quiet. I stood a moment longer in front of that lockless door, then turned away.

Writing this chapter, I want to set down, exactly as it was, that we
did nothing that day. We didn't open it. No one forced it. All four of
us just stood in front of it. I don't think that was running away. It
just felt, I think, like all four of us sensed, in equal measure, that
it wasn't the time yet.

That night I wrote briefly in my notebook.

"The seal reads 'until the time of the end.' We didn't open it."

I didn't write anything more beneath it. I didn't want to attach my own thoughts after this sentence. I just wanted to leave what had happened as it was. Before closing my notebook, I read that sentence once more.

Histor read it beside me and said,

"…Is that it, for today's record?"

"…Yes. That's all there is, today."

He nodded. He didn't ask any more.

I cut off this stretch of the record here. We met the ones who arrived.
We learned the water channel joins the cities. We opened the door not
in the index. We read the plan, and the writing in its margin. And we
stood in front of this seal. Nothing opened. I don't think that's
strange. Some doors, I think, need time spent standing in front of them
before they can be opened.

(Continued in Part Four.)

The Last Room, Part Four

Open It and Give It Away

Chapter 37. Who Opens It

_Previously_ — At the very bottom of the bundle of documents, only one
sheet carried a seal. The seal read "UNTIL THE TIME OF THE END." No
one had touched it. We only stood in front of it.

The seal was smaller than I'd expected.

I'd thought it would be bigger, harder. In fact it was a piece of wax
about the size of half a palm. The color had faded so much I couldn't
even guess what it had originally been. Only the letters pressed into
it were still clear.

Until the time of the end.

"…Is it all right to open this?" I asked.

No one answered right away.

We'd already moved the bundle of documents to the low table in the
middle of this room. The plan was already unfolded and hung on the
wall. We'd already read all the writing in the margin. All that was
left was that one sheet at the very bottom. Everything else we could
read. Only that, we couldn't.

I've come into this room quite a few times by now. The first time I
opened the door, I was afraid. I'm not anymore. The room is narrow, has
no window, and holds nothing but one table and one shelf. And yet
coming into this room, my voice drops on its own. I know now that isn't
because the room is frightening.

Histor spoke first.

"…Things like this usually require some kind of qualification. Someone
specific meant to open it."

"…Qualification?"

"…Yes. There was a lot of that in the six records I read. Doors no one
could open, things no one could touch. Usually there was a fixed
person for it."

Hearing that, I looked around the room. There was no one but us. Mone,
Histor, and the Keeper, who'd come in a little later.

"…So is there someone among us who has that qualification?"

Histor looked at me.

"…I don't know. That's the problem."

"…Which books were like that?" I asked.

Histor thought for a moment.

"…The Harvest record, the Roll. There the inner columns of the
register could only be touched by the Registrar. Lytron, too, was
turned away at first for lacking qualification. It was similar in the
Preserve record, Perpetuity. The deep compartments of the vault only
opened with the Custodian's permission."

"…So in those books, who ended up opening them?"

"…Someone qualified set the qualification down. Or someone
unqualified was given it. Either way, the key passed from person to
person."

Hearing that, I looked at the seal again. There was no key here. No
one holding a key, either.

"…But here there's no such person at all." Histor went on. "That's
what's strange. All six of the other records had one."

The Keeper came closer to the seal. His steps were as slow as ever.
