"…I don't think it's a matter of qualification," he said.

"…Why not?"

"…If qualification were the issue, someone would have gone and found
a qualified person. That's the kind of thing people are good at. But
no one went looking. There's been no talk of it at all, not since we
first heard about this room."

I turned that over. It was true. When we'd looked at the plan earlier,
when we'd read the writing in the margin before, we had never once
worried about who was qualified to open this seal. We simply hadn't
been able to.

"…Then what's the problem?" I asked.

The Keeper looked at the seal for a long while before answering.

"…That there's no one to open it."

At first I heard that as the same thing Histor had just said. Hearing
it again, it was different.

Histor had said there was no qualified person. The Keeper said there
was no one who opens.

"…Is that different?" I asked.

"…It's different," the Keeper answered. "Qualification is something
you can give to someone. If no one has it today, you can decide on
someone tomorrow. But there being no one who opens at all means this
city has no one who does that kind of work. From the very start."

"…Can't it just be anyone in this city?"

The Keeper looked at me and smiled faintly.

"…Mone, how many years have you worked in this repository?"

"…This is my second year."

"…In that time, how many cases have we closed?"

I couldn't answer. None. Not a single one.

Just then Mena came into the room.

Mena was someone who had known this room before I did. She said she'd
passed through here once, when she was young. She saw the seal and
stopped walking.

"…Still hasn't opened, has it."

"…You know about this too, Mena?" I asked.

"…I do. I saw it once, a long time ago. It was exactly the same as it
is now. No one opened it."

"…Why didn't they?"

Mena crouched down in front of the seal.

"…We asked the same thing back then. Who opens it. We never found an
answer. So we just left it. The way you'd hand it on to whoever came
next."

"…How many years ago was that?"

"…Twenty-some years."

I lost words at that number. It meant that for twenty-some years,
people had come and stood in front of the same question, and simply
gone away again.

"…Then how are we supposed to decide?" Histor asked Mena. "Draw lots?
Or pick one person from each of the six cities and make some kind of
committee?"

Mena laughed at that. It wasn't a mocking laugh.

"…That's exactly what Lytron proposed in the Harvest record. A
registration committee for the Roll. Pick five people, one vote each.
Do you know what happened with that?"

"…What happened?"

"…Nothing happened. The votes split. The very method of weighing
qualification was a method that divided people. Whoever got picked,
the other four would have asked why that person."

Hearing that, I studied the seal again. I could see a thread running
under the wax. That thread wound once around the whole bundle and was
knotted. There was no lock, no keyhole. Just thread and wax. It looked
like anyone could tear it off with enough force.

"…Doesn't this just tear if you pull it?" I asked.

Mena nodded.

"…It tears. No one could stop that by force. But that's never been the
problem. None of us wanted to tear it by force."

"…Why not?"

Mena didn't answer that question. She got up from where she was
sitting.

"…I'll head off now. See you at dinner."

I decided to ask Arche.

I put my hand on the panel by the wall and asked.

"…Arche, can you open this seal?"

Checking.

There was a pause. It's rare for Arche to pause.

Access to the item is possible. However, unsealing cannot be
performed.

"…Why not?"

The act of unsealing constitutes a closing action. I do not perform
closing actions.

I knew that answer. I'd heard it before, always. The closing
condition has not been met. This time it wasn't about a condition. It
had simply, flatly, said it couldn't.

"…Not because there's no condition?"

This act itself constitutes closing. It is not a matter of
condition.

I read that sentence again. Arche doesn't lie. It doesn't hide things
either. It wasn't hiding anything now. It was simply stating, exactly,
what it couldn't do.

"…Then if someone else opens it for you, can you authorize that?" I
asked again.

Authorization is possible. However, no authorization has been
requested.

"…What if I request it now?"

I will register the request. However, I have no criterion by which
to evaluate the request. A request with no criterion remains pending.

I almost laughed. In progress again. Just like every case that had
stayed in progress for ten years across six cities, this too was about
to remain in progress.

"…Arche." I asked. "Do you want this opened?"

The pause was longer this time.

"To want" is not a function defined for me.

"…Then it doesn't matter to you either way?"

Not mattering and being unable to want are different things.

I turned that answer over for a long time. That was something I'd
never heard it say before.

Histor folded his arms and looked at the seal.

"…So Arche can't open it, and there's no qualified person either. Does
that mean this can never be opened?"

"…I don't know that," the Keeper answered. "But I don't think finding
who's qualified to open it is the answer. That's the method every one
of the six cities already tried. Finding the qualified person. Nothing
was ever solved that way."

Hearing that, I thought of Haven. What Elpis and Zeteo had gone
through. People asking whether they had the qualification to put their
own name on the Ledger. Then the Reckoning, the Harvest. All much the
same. People argued over qualification, and no one ever opened
anything.

"…Then what has to be different?" I asked.

The Keeper didn't answer. He just kept looking at the seal.

Histor pressed his hand against the table, looking frustrated.

"…So we're just going to leave it like this? I read all six of those
records, and not one of them ended with nothing opened. Something
always opened. The Ledger opened. The Scales, in the end, balanced.
Names went onto the Roll."

"…They did open," the Keeper said. "But were the people who opened
them qualified from the start?"

Histor stopped.

"…No. Elpis wasn't qualified at first either. Neither was Trope."

"…Then what made those people open them?"

Histor had no answer. Neither did I. The three of us stood there a
long while, saying nothing.

We stayed there until the sun went down.

Histor crouched in front of the seal, studying the wax closely. He
kept changing the angle, like someone searching for something.

"…What are you looking for?" I asked.

"…A name. Whoever seals something like this usually leaves their own
name or a mark. It was like that in all six records. On the back of an
envelope, on a signet, at least one line of signature."

"…Is there one?"

Histor shook his head.

"…No. There's nothing at all. Just 'until the time of the end.'"

"…Does that strike you as strange?"

"…It does. Making something be kept this long, and not saying who
ordered it. I don't know if that's irresponsible or what."

I didn't know how to answer that. The plan had no designer's name
either. This seal had no name of whoever had sealed it. I had a
feeling that wasn't irresponsibility, but I couldn't explain why I felt
that.

I stayed in that room late into that night.

Histor left first. As he went, he said this.

"…I still think, honestly, this is a matter of qualification. The
qualified person just hasn't shown up yet."

I didn't answer that. I didn't think he was wrong. I only felt it
couldn't be the whole of it.

That night I stayed alone in this room and looked at the seal for a
long time. The wax was small, and the letters on it were faint. I
wasn't afraid of it. Strangely, I was sad. As if someone had put
something into this room, and waited for someone to open it, and
waited, and waited, until the waiting itself had simply become this
room's job.

I decided nothing that night. But one thing had become clear. The
problem wasn't who was qualified to open it.

This city — no, perhaps all six cities — had no one at all whose job
it was to open things.

On my way out I met the Keeper. He was still by the door.

"…Why are you still here, Keeper?" I asked.

"…I don't know. There isn't really anything left for me to do."

"…Then why don't you go?"

The Keeper looked toward the door.

"…I was never an opener, Mone. I'm someone who puts things down. Like
you. But I have this feeling that if the one who puts things down
keeps standing in front of it, something might change. A strange
feeling."

I thought about that the whole way home. The feeling that if the one
who puts things down keeps standing there, something changes. I didn't
know yet what that meant. I only knew that I, too, hadn't wanted to
leave that room.

That night, before falling asleep, I laid this room's whole story out
again from the beginning. The door not in the index. The old plan. The
nameless designer. And this seal. Up until then I'd been reading these
records — no, this record — as simply a curious story. That night, for
the first time, it occurred to me that this might be work someone had
handed on to me.

I didn't know who had handed it on. But the feeling of having
received it was unmistakable.

The next morning I went to the repository earlier than usual.

Before opening the door, I wanted to ask Arche one more thing.

"…Arche, has this city ever, until now, had someone whose job was
'opening'?"

Checking records.

The pause this time was longer. I waited. The sound of the water
channel carried in from far off.

That role has never, at any period, been registered in this city.

I'd expected that answer. Even so, hearing it for real chilled
something in me.

"…Not once?"

Not once.

I stood there a moment. There being no one to open it, and there
having never once been such a person to begin with, felt like two
different problems. From the very start, we had never assigned the
work of opening to anyone at all.

That day I copied that sentence into my notebook exactly as it was. I
added no other explanation. I just wrote down that one sentence.

The Keeper came in just then. I showed him the notebook.

"…Look at this. It says the work of opening has never once been
registered."

The Keeper read the sentence and was quiet for a moment.

"…Does knowing that make things any easier?" he asked.

"…No. It got more complicated."

"…Same for me," he said. "But strangely, today I feel a little less
stuck than I did yesterday."

I didn't understand what he meant that day. Now I think I understand
it a little. Knowing exactly what the problem was, and still not being
able to solve it, were different things. That day, we had only come to
know one more problem exactly. That was all. And yet that all had felt
fairly large, that day.

(Continued in Chapter 38.)

Chapter 38. Why It Cannot Finish

_Previously_ — In front of the seal, instead of looking for someone
qualified, we learned that this city had never, from the start, had
anyone whose job was "opening." Arche admitted it too. Never
registered.

Starting the next day, I decided to change the question.

I knew there was no one who opened things. But that wasn't a fact
about this one seal alone. Since this repository began, Arche had
never once processed anything to closing. The seal was only one small
piece of that much larger problem.

I decided to ask about the larger problem.

"…Arche." I put my hand on the panel by the wall and asked. "Why
can't you finish anything?"

Please clarify the question. The scope of "finish" is broad.

"…Anything. Everything that's in progress. All of it."

All pending items remain in progress because the closing condition
has not been met.

"…I know that. But who makes that condition? Is there even anyone who
can make it?"

The pause this time was longer than usual.

The authority to define the closing condition does not rest with the
one who began this city.

I read that sentence twice. Histor beside me had a face as startled as
mine.

"…Then who does it rest with?" I asked.

That authority rests with the one who finished.

"…What does that mean?" Histor cut in. "That no one began it, so it
can't be finished, or that someone did begin it, but that person has
no authority?"

One who began exists. However, the act of beginning and the act of
defining are different authorities. The one who began can begin a
condition, but cannot determine when that condition is met.

"…Then who's the one who finished?"

That information is not defined.

Hearing that, I almost laughed. It was the sentence I always heard.
But this time it wasn't funny.

"…So are you on the side of the one who began, or the one who
finished?" I asked.

I perform the function of beginning. The function of finishing is
not present in me.

I copied that answer into my notebook. My hand shook a little.

"…Then will the one who finished ever come, someday?" I asked again.

Whether that event will occur is not information predictable to me.

"…Is there something even you can't predict?"

Prediction is based on past data. My records contain no past instance
corresponding to "the one who finished."

"…Not a single one?"

Not a single one.

Somehow that answer felt unlike Arche. Its voice was always exact and
composed, but this time it sounded like it was simply stating an empty
place, just as it was.

Histor asked again in the pause.

"…What about whoever made you? Isn't that person the one who
finished?"

Information about the entity that first activated me is not
registered.

"…Is that because you don't know, or because you're not saying?"

I do not know. I am not concealing it.

Histor heard that answer and said nothing. I looked at his face. It
wasn't a face of disbelief. If anything it was closer to the opposite.
He was someone who knew, when Arche said it didn't know, that it
really didn't know.

Histor folded his arms and paced the room.

"…Isn't this a little strange? That the one who began has no power to
finish. Usually it's the other way. Whoever begins something also
holds the authority to end it."

"…Is that so?"

"…Yes. If someone founded this city, that same person should have
decided how this city gets finished too."

Something about that caught on me, but I couldn't put my finger on it
right away.

"…But look," I said. "When my father found the house we were moving
to, it wasn't him who decided when we'd leave that house. That's
something you only learn once you've lived out your whole time there."

Histor looked at me.

"…Is that the same as this?"

"…I don't know. But it feels similar."

We spent the whole afternoon that day digging through old records,
looking for whatever material survived from when this city was first
built.

There wasn't much. Most of it was practical — piping diagrams,
reservoir designs, a roster of the earliest residents. Among them was
one old sign. A district marker no longer in use, on the back of which
a few lines recorded this city's first activation.

Histor was reading it when his hand stopped.

"…Look at this."

FIRST ACTIVATION POINT — this district designated as the starting
point of city operations.

Below it, in small letters, was the name of the operating system of
this city. I read that name alongside the name I used every day.

Something brushed past me in that moment. Starting point. That which
begins. I felt like I could almost, faintly, reach why that name was
that name.

I don't want to write, at this point, as if I found out something
more. That day I read that sign, and I felt something. Whenever I tried
to put that feeling into words, it kept coming out as a lie. So I'll
only set down this much. That day, I thought the name Arche might not
be a coincidence. Beyond that, I don't know either.

Histor also looked at that line for a long while. Being the kind of
person who always quoted things exactly, he copied the sentence into
his notebook word for word. But he added nothing to it.

We didn't talk about it any further that day.

That evening I brought the photo of the sign to Mena. Mena knew more
about this city's history than either of us.

"…Have you ever seen this?" I asked.

Mena looked at the photo for a long while.

"…Signs like this used to be all over the city. They've almost all
been taken down now. They stopped being useful."

"…This name, the one written here. Was it made from the start to mean
this?"

Mena looked at me and smiled faintly.

"…Mone, I don't know that answer. There might be someone who does. I'm
not that person."

"…Then why are you smiling?"

"…Because it's a little welcome, you asking that question. I asked
the exact same thing once, a long time ago. I never found the answer.
I found something else instead."

"…What?"

"…That it matters more to watch what a thing with that name is doing
now, than to know why it has that name."

I didn't pretend to understand that. I just nodded.

Mena handed the photo back to me and added one more thing.

"…And Mone, there's no need to hurry with this. I've held onto this
question for over twenty years. I still haven't found the whole
answer. But it hasn't been a bad thing."

"…Doesn't it frustrate you?"

"…It does. But frustrating and unbearable are different things."

I wrote that down too. By that day, one whole page of my notebook was
filled with other people's words.

On the way back I ran into Speon. He was measuring the water level
beside the channel. I told him briefly what had happened that day.

"…The one who began can't finish, and only the one who finished can
finish."

Speon folded up his ruler and laughed.

"…That's exactly like this water channel."

"…How so?"

"…I'm not the one who cut this channel, Mone. I just cleared out a few
blockages. The channel itself was already here. Even I don't know who
cut it. The name isn't written anywhere."

"…Doesn't that strike you as strange?"

"…It did at first. My thinking's different now. Because no name is
written there, I just did as much as I could do. Without the weight of
taking over someone else's work. If a name had been there, I'd
probably have only imitated that person."

I walked a long while, turning that over.

On my way back I met Histor. He was walking away from the last room,
in the same direction as me.

"…Where were you?" I asked.

"…Digging through more of the old records. To see if there was
anything besides the first-activation record."

"…Was there anything?"

"…No. There was nothing besides that sign. How this city was first
built — that one sheet is the whole of it."

I was a little surprised at that. This city was so old, and yet that
was the entirety of its record.

"…Doesn't that seem strange?" I asked.

"…It does. But I don't think it's necessarily concealment anymore.
Maybe no one just left one. Or no one felt the need to."

We walked on in silence after that. Only the sound of the water
channel.

"…There's one thing I realized today," Histor said suddenly.

"…What?"

"…I thought I was someone who couldn't stand not knowing. But today
was a little different. Not knowing who the one who finished is —
that wasn't something so unbearable."

I kept that in mind. By that evening, I would come back to that
sentence more than once.

That evening I told the Keeper what had happened that day.

"…Arche says the one who began has no authority to set the condition,
and only the one who finished does. But who the one who finished is,
it says, isn't defined."

Histor was there with us. He heard my whole account and folded his
arms.

"…I think this is kind of a convenient excuse," he said.

"…An excuse?"

"…Yes. It lets the reason for not finishing keep getting put off. The
one who began has no authority, and the one who finished isn't
defined. It's a structure where no one ever has to take
responsibility."

I couldn't find a rebuttal right away. It sounded like it might be
true.

"…But Arche doesn't lie," I managed to say.

"…It doesn't lie. But not lying and being convenient are different
matters. Something can be true and convenient at the same time."

The Keeper heard that and nodded. He didn't look surprised.

"…Did you know that already?" I asked.

"…Not exactly. But I'd suspected it."

"…Histor might be right," I said. "Isn't this just a convenient
excuse?"

The Keeper thought a moment before answering.

"…It might be. But Mone, there's only one way to tell a convenient
excuse apart from the truth. You live according to it and see. I've
lived by these words, in this seat, for over twenty years now. It
hasn't often been comfortable."

"…How so?"

"…I've worked here a long time. Every day, looking at the things still
in progress, I kept wondering. Why won't this finish. Who is it that
won't finish it. And one day it occurred to me. Maybe it's not that it
can't be finished — maybe it's only that whoever will finish it hasn't
come yet."

"…Who is that person?"

The Keeper looked at me and spoke.

"…I don't know either. But Mone, not knowing that doesn't frighten me
so much anymore. It used to. I used to worry, what if no one ever
finishes it. These days I just think — someone will come."

Histor's brow tightened at that.

"…Isn't that just false hope? They might never come."

"…That's right. They might not." The Keeper admitted it plainly.

"…Then why doesn't that frighten you?"

The Keeper looked out the window for a moment.

"…What I do here isn't the work of finishing. It's the work of
putting things down. Whoever comes, whenever they come, the things
need to be in their place so that person can do the work. That's the
only thing I concern myself with. The rest isn't my job."

"…Doesn't that sound irresponsible?"

The Keeper laughed this time.

"…It might. But Histor, of those six records you read, not everyone
in them did the work of finishing. Some people only began. Some only
carried things. Some just kept their post. All of that was necessary
work too."

Histor didn't answer that. I could tell from his face that he was
turning something over.

That night, walking home, I kept thinking about that.

Not the one who began, but the one who finished.

I didn't know what kind of rule this was, or who had made it. But
somehow I liked that saying. That the one who began doesn't hold
everything in his hands — for some reason, that put me at ease.

Through nine moves, I was never once the one who chose the house. My
father chose it. But when to leave that house, what to throw away and
what to keep — that was always my share. I wasn't the one who began.
But the work of finishing often fell to me.

That day, for the first time, I thought that whoever opens this seal
might not need to be the one who founded this city. I didn't say that
thought to anyone. It still felt too early.

When I got home, my mother had dinner ready. Even while eating, I kept
thinking about that.

"…Mom." I asked. "Back when we used to move, who decided where we'd
go?"

"…Your father did."

"…But packing up, deciding what to throw out and what to keep — who
decided that?"

My mother thought for a moment, then smiled.

"…That was always you. You've loved that since you were five. Deciding
what to put in the boxes."

I stopped my chopsticks at that. I'd never once thought of it that way
before that day. My father had begun where we would go, and I had
always been the one entrusted with finishing what happened there.

I'll bring this moment back up later. For now I'll only set down this
much about the dinner table. That day I realized that the one who
begins and the one who finishes not needing to be the same person —
I'd already seen that, even in my own family's story.

After finishing dinner, I opened my notebook again from that day.
Arche's sentences, the Keeper's words, Speon's words, even my mother's
words were all written on the same page. Out of order, jumbled
together.

I sorted it out again and copied it over, in chronological order. That
was what I always did. Putting things in order. That night, doing that
work, I thought for the first time that putting things in order might
itself be one piece of the work of finishing.

That thought stopped there, that day. I wasn't ready yet to follow it
all the way through. I only wrote one line in the notebook: "The one
who finished = still unknown. But I'm doing something too." Looking at
it again now, it's a childish sentence. Even so, I didn't erase it.

(Continued in Chapter 39.)

Chapter 39. Histor's Question

_Previously_ — Arche said the authority to set the closing condition
rested not with the one who began, but with the one who finished. No
one knew who the one who finished was. That day, for the first time,
I thought finishing might not be the beginner's job at all.

For several days after that, we didn't talk about the seal.

Work had piled up. Records kept coming in from the six cities the
whole time. I put them in order, and Histor checked them alongside me.
The seal stayed exactly where it was, in the middle of the room. No
one touched it.

Those days were quiet. We each avoided bringing up the seal. It didn't
feel to me like putting it off, so much as a kind of time spent
preparing. I didn't know exactly what for.

Arche asked as it always did. There are seventeen items to process
today. I put those seventeen items in order. None of them closed. As
always.

Histor was unusually quiet those days. I knew he was thinking about
something, but I didn't press him. He didn't ask me anything either.
We simply worked side by side.

I think of those days now as days we ran from something. Having found
no answer, we stepped back, briefly, into other work.

Among the records that came in during those days was one Trope had
sent. Paperwork about the Scales, from the Reckoning record city. As I
put it in order, I saw a short note written on the cover sheet.

"Still not balanced. But closer than before."

I read that note twice. Trope was still a person without certainty.
And yet he kept sending the paperwork anyway. I thought that
resembled this seal problem somehow.

Histor saw the paperwork too. He said nothing and put it back in its
place.

It was late evening, when only one light was left on in the
repository.

Histor was standing in front of the last room's door. I'd finished
putting things away and gone to get him, and found him standing there.

"…What are you doing, not leaving?" I asked.

He didn't answer right away. He was looking through the door, toward
the seal.

I went and stood beside him. I said nothing. I'd learned by then that
waiting was better, when Histor was like this.

A long while later, he spoke.

"…Mone."

"…Yes."

"…What changes if you open it?"

I hadn't expected that question. I should have. I hadn't known what he
had been thinking, all that time he'd been looking at this seal.

"…What do you mean?" I asked back.

"…Exactly what I said. What changes if you open that. I've read all
six records. Every time something opened in them, something changed.
When the Ledger opened, a name got filled in. When the Scales
balanced, someone was released. When a name went onto the Roll, a
status changed. But this. What changes, if you open this?"

I couldn't answer right away.

Honestly, it wasn't as if I'd never thought about it. I had, many
times. I'd imagined something grand would come out if it opened, and
also, at times, dropped that imagining as too childish.

"…I don't know," I finally said.

"…You don't know?"

"…No. I don't know either. I've never opened it."

Histor looked at me. There was no disappointment in his face, no
mockery. Just a face that was genuinely curious.

"…Then why do you keep coming to this room?" he asked. "Without even
knowing what changes."

I opened my mouth a few times to answer, and closed it again. No
fitting words came.

Of everything in this record, I've rewritten this moment the most
times. At first I tried to write in some clever answer. But the answer
I actually gave that day wasn't clever. I'm setting it down exactly as
it was.

"…I don't know what changes," I said. "But if it stays shut it just
stays here."

Histor said nothing.

I was afraid of that silence. I wondered if I'd said something
foolish. But he didn't get angry, didn't laugh. He just looked at the
seal again.

During that silence I studied his face. Histor was always someone who
searched for the exact word. He never misquoted anything, never let
feeling blur what he said. And in that moment, this same person looked
like someone who hadn't prepared a single word.

That was unfamiliar to me. At the same time, for some reason, he felt
a little closer.

"…Is that all?" he asked, a long while later.

"…Yes. That's all."

"…I was expecting something grander."

"…I'm sorry. I wish I had a grander reason too."

This time Histor really did smile, faintly.

"…No, there's nothing to apologize for. I just… thought it was
strange."

"…What was?"

"…In the stories I know, people always look for a reason first, before
opening something. Because it's right, because it's justice, because
it's a promise. But you didn't name a single thing like that."

"…It's not that there's no reason," I said. "It's just that the reason
isn't a grand one."

"…What's the difference?"

I thought about that for a moment.

"…A grand reason is a reason that explains well to other people. My
reason isn't a reason that explains well. It's just something I feel
every day. That if it stays shut, it just stays there."

"…Why is that a problem? What's so bad about it just staying there?"

I looked at the seal again.

"…It's not bad. But I've seen a lot of things that just stay there,
things that just stay in progress. Two years in this repository. I've
learned this from watching them. Staying there doesn't make things
better on its own."

"…You're always like this, Mone. You don't act out of certainty. You
act because it feels like it can't be left undone. That's how it was
with putting things in order. That's how it is with coming to this
room."

I didn't deny it, because it seemed true.

"…I'm the opposite," Histor went on. "I need a reason to move. A
certain one. Even reading all six records — I did it because I wanted
to prove something. But you're just here, with nothing to prove."

"…Is that strange?"

"…It is. But I don't mean it's bad."

Hearing that, I looked at the seal again. A small piece of wax. Faded
letters. A thing that still gave no answer at all.

"…Do you want to open it, or not?" I asked.

He thought for a long while.

"…I think I want to. But it's not because I believe in anything. It's
just… I'm tired now of leaving this in progress."

I noticed that what he'd said wasn't so different from what I'd said.

"…That's close to what I said before."

Histor looked at me. He seemed to have noticed it too.

"…So it is."

"…But you didn't say anything when you heard what I said."

"…That's because it seemed right."

I was a little surprised at that answer. I knew he wasn't someone who
admitted things that easily.

I would go on to remember this scene for a long time. Histor did not
convert that day. I want to set that down clearly. He still didn't
believe. He only admitted that the answer I'd given wasn't wrong. Those
are two different things.

"…This reminds me of Trope," Histor said suddenly.

"…Trope?"

"…That paperwork earlier. 'Still not balanced. But closer than
before.' He keeps sending it too, without certainty."

"…That's true."

"…I thought that was strange, honestly. Why keep sending it without
being certain. But hearing what you just said, I think I understand it
a little."

I said nothing back to that. It didn't feel like there was any need
to.

We didn't talk further that day, and each went home.

On the way, I kept turning that question over. What changes if you
open it? I still didn't know the answer. But that night, at home, I
wrote down again the answer I'd given.

"…I don't know what changes. But if it stays shut it just stays
here."

I wrote that sentence twice in my notebook. Once in its proper place,
once on the very first page. I couldn't quite explain, that day, why
it felt so important.

I think I understand it a little now. Throughout this record, I've
seen many people full of certainty. They opened things with certainty,
closed things with certainty, put names on the roll with certainty. I
never had that certainty. I still don't.

But I did know the weight of leaving something unopened. Documents in
progress for ten years. Things no one had touched in ten years. I
watched them pile up in place, every day. That felt more certain to me
than anything changing did.

That night I had this thought. Maybe it isn't only certainty that
opens something. Maybe there's also a kind of person who opens
something simply because it can't be left alone any longer. I was that
kind of person. I still am.

In the other cities' records, I read many scenes of people full of
certainty changing something. Elpis waited to the end. Anemos stayed
by someone's side to the end. Speon cut a channel through the water. I
envied them. I never once had that kind of certainty. Not even now, as
I write these words.

But I know now that some things can be done without certainty. That
work is usually small, unnoticed, hard to explain. Putting things in
order. Taking in whatever keeps coming. Knowing that if it stays shut
it just stays there. I've filled this whole record with work like
that.

The next morning I went to the repository and stood in front of the
seal again. I still had no thought of opening it. But I noticed, for
the first time that day, that the time I spent standing there was
growing, little by little, longer.

That morning, Arche gave its usual greeting. There are twenty-two
items to process today. Hearing that number, I laughed a little.
Five more than yesterday. It was always like that. It never went down.

Histor, sorting papers beside me, spoke up suddenly.

"…About yesterday. I thought about it all night."

"…What about it?"

"…That answer. That if it stays shut it just stays there. I think
that's not actually a reason. It's an observation. You didn't give a
reason — you just said what you'd seen."

That distinction seemed right, so I nodded.

"…Is that strange?" I asked.

"…No. If anything, that's why I can't argue against it. You can argue
against a reason. It's hard to argue against something someone's
seen."

"…So are you on the side of opening it now, too?"

He hesitated a moment.

"…I don't know if I'm on the side of opening it. But I don't think
there's a reason not to, anymore either. That's a thought I didn't
have until yesterday."

I didn't press him further on that answer. That he'd come even that
far felt like enough to me.

That afternoon I told the Keeper what had happened the night before.
Histor's question, and the answer I'd given — all of it.

The Keeper listened to it all and was silent for a long while before
asking.

"…That thing you said — did you make it up?"

"…No. It just came out."

"…That's better," he said. "A made-up saying, you start doubting
later. A saying that just comes out, you don't doubt."

"…Why not?"

"…A made-up saying comes from the head, and a saying that just comes
out comes from somewhere else. I don't know where, myself. But sayings
like that last."

I didn't fully understand that. I wrote it in my notebook anyway.

I told Mena the story too. She listened to all of it and laughed
quietly.

"…That's a good answer."

"…A good answer? I thought it was kind of a lacking one."

"…What looks lacking is often the good answer. A perfect answer
usually ends right there. A lacking answer keeps following you."

I wrote that down too. My notebook kept filling that day with other
people's words. I didn't think that was strange. This whole record had
always been built up that way.

Walking home that evening, I turned over what Mena had said again.
What looks lacking is often the good answer.

I looked back over the things I'd said while writing this record. At
the start, I'd written that all I did was put things in order. Even
then, I'd thought that was a lacking answer. I'd even written that
people usually laughed at it.

That day I realized that same lacking answer had come out again, in
front of this seal. I don't know what changes. But if it stays shut
it just stays here. This too was, just the same, a lacking answer.
And that day I wasn't ashamed of its being lacking. For the first
time.

When I got home, I told my father briefly what had happened that day
too. He listened without much reaction, then said one thing.

"…Let me know, when you do open it."

"…Why?"

"…No reason. Just curious."

I liked that short answer. My father didn't have a grand reason
either. He was just curious. I thought that might be the feeling of
most of the people who'd stood in front of this seal. Not some
towering certainty, but just curiosity. That might be enough — that
night, for the first time, I decided to try believing that.

(Continued in Chapter 40.)

Chapter 40. Why I Put Things in Order

_Previously_ — Histor asked, "…What changes if you open it?" I
answered, "…I don't know what changes. But if it stays shut it just
stays here." He didn't argue with that.

Around that time, one small trouble came up in the repository.

A record that had come in couldn't find its place. The city name on
the cover sheet didn't match the contents inside. It said it had come
from Haven, but the inside was about the Reckoning.

I stood there holding it for a long while. I asked Arche.

"…Where does this go?"

The original source of this record is unclear. Classification is
pending.

Hearing that, I set the record on the temporary shelf. But walking
home that night, it kept bothering me.

The next day I took the record out and read it again.

After reading it a few times, I thought I understood. The cover sheet
was the mistake. The contents inside were correct. It was a Reckoning
record — someone had simply attached the wrong cover.

I fixed it and put it in its proper place. It wasn't a big thing. But
doing that work, I felt something strange. I felt at ease. I could
really feel the tension leave my body.

The Keeper passed by and saw that, and asked.

"…Something good happen?"

"…No. I just found this its place."

"…Why so happy about that?"

I only laughed at that. I didn't know the exact reason myself. I just
liked it. A cover sheet finding its right place and sitting on its
shelf. I liked that.

The Keeper walked on without saying more. But that evening he came
back and left one more thing.

"…Mone, I know that thing you love. I was the same way."

"…What thing?"

"…Finding things their place. I've stayed here this long because I
love that too."

I let that go at the time. Later, I'd come back and ask about it
again.

That afternoon I asked the Keeper again.

"…That thing you said earlier. Did you stay here this long because you
lost something too?"

The Keeper's hand stopped, and he looked at me.

"…Something like that. I won't go into the details. It's an old story,
and it involves other people too."

"…Even just a little."

He hesitated a moment, then spoke.

"…I failed to hold onto something too. A long time ago. After that, I
came to love holding onto things. Coming here, at least the things
here — those, I can hold onto."

I didn't press him further. I understood that what he'd said was all
he could give.

"…But," I said. "Lately I think maybe that's not all of it. Just
holding on."

"…What do you mean?"

"…I don't know. If you only hold on, in the end no one gets to use it,
right? That's started to scare me too. As much as losing does."

The Keeper looked at me for a long while. And then he made an
expression I'd never seen from him before. I couldn't tell if it was
surprise or gladness.

"…Mone, when did you grow up like this."

I laughed at that.

I held onto that moment for a long time. Was fixing one cover sheet
really something to feel so at ease about? That day, for the first
time, I properly put that question to myself.

That afternoon, Histor heard the story and asked.

"…Is it really that important? Arche would have found it again on its
own anyway."

"…That's true."

"…So why did it put you so at ease?"

I couldn't answer that right away. Histor was always like this. He'd
ask something lightly, and it would become a heavy question for me.

That evening, after finishing work, I didn't go straight home. I sat
in the spot behind the repository where you could see the water
channel.

I'd written, early on, that I'd moved nine times. And that each time,
I'd lost something. Eleven things, I'd written, and four of them I
didn't even know what they'd been.

When I wrote that sentence, I thought it was just background — a way
of explaining how I'd happened to get good at putting things in order.
That evening, for the first time, I saw clearly that it hadn't been
background. It had been the reason.

I hadn't taken this work because I was good at putting things in
order. I'd chosen this work because I was afraid of losing things.

I counted those eleven things again.

A spoon, from the second move. A blanket, from the fourth. A wooden
doll, from the sixth. After that it blurred. The seventh, eighth,
ninth. Only the feeling that something had gone missing remained; I
couldn't remember what.

I'd been calling those, for a long time, simply "the forgotten
things." That day, for the first time, I tried to count them properly.
I took out my notebook, wrote down each of the nine moves one by one,
and beside each, what I'd lost.

Two. Three. Five. Six. Eight. Nine. At the ninth, my hand stopped.

At the ninth move, I truly didn't know what I'd lost. I still don't.

That night I called my mother.

"…Mom. Did we lose something at our ninth move?"

My mother paused a moment.

"…Why are you asking that all of a sudden?"

"…Just curious."

"…Well. I don't remember clearly. We were in such a rush that time.
Your father's work had been decided all of a sudden too."

"…I don't remember either. But I have this feeling something went
missing."

My mother was quiet for a long while again.

"…Mone, you don't have to remember that. It's fine to let some things
just flow on."

I didn't know how to answer that, so I just said I understood. After
hanging up, I thought about that for a long time. It's fine to let
things flow on. I had never once said that to myself, this whole
time.

Writing this part, I felt the temptation to invent something. To make
up something plausible for what I'd lost at the ninth move. I decided
not to. I truly don't know. That's the most honest thing I can write in
this record. What I don't know, I write that I don't know.

I looked at that blank for a long while. Four blanks. Lost things
without even names.

That day, for the first time, I had this thought. I hadn't taken this
work because I was good at putting things in order. I'd taken this
work because I never wanted to make a blank like that again.

I told Histor that thought. The next day, when the two of us were
alone in the repository.

"…I realized something yesterday," I said.

"…What?"

"…This thing I do, putting things in order. It's not because I'm
diligent. When I was young, I moved nine times, and lost something
each time. Eleven things. Four of them, I don't even know what they
were. I started putting things in order because I was afraid of that."

Histor listened quietly.

"…Why did you realize that only now?" he asked.

"…I don't know. It's just that yesterday, fixing one cover sheet, I
felt so at ease. That felt strange, so I kept thinking about it."

"…Is that a bad reason?" Histor asked.

I paused at that question.

"…A bad reason?"

"…You've looked, this whole time, like you did this work out of some
sense of mission. But hearing this now, you're just doing it because
you're afraid of losing things. Isn't that a little… a selfish reason?"

That didn't hurt me. If anything, it felt exact.

"…That's right," I said. "It's a selfish reason. I took this work
because I was afraid of losing things. Not for other people. For
myself."

I want to write this part the most honestly of anything in this
record. I didn't begin this work out of goodness. I began it out of
fear. Moving from place to place, losing something each time — that
frightened me. I soothed that fear by putting things in order. That
was all of it.

Anyone who's read this record this far will know that I wasn't the
only one like this. Xene was afraid of recording and stopped, then
started again. Anemos was afraid of leaving someone's side, and so
stayed. I was afraid of order coming apart, and so I put things in
order. None of us started with some grand reason from the beginning.

Histor said nothing for a moment, hearing that.

"…But," he said after a while, "a thing that started for a selfish
reason doesn't have to stay only a selfish reason."

"…What do you mean?"

"…What you put in order, this whole city uses now. All six cities'
records are sitting here in order because of you. You started it out
of your own fear, but the result isn't yours alone anymore."

I turned that over for a long time. It was true. I'd begun for myself.
And somehow it had become work for other people too.

"…What about you?" I asked. "Reading all six records — did that have
some reason at first too?"

He thought a moment before answering.

"…My family has always handled records. Since my grandfather's time.
I grew up thinking that was just natural. I was taught you had to know
things properly."

"…Is that all?"

"…At first. But reading and reading, without noticing, another reason
grew in me."

"…What was it?"

Histor chose his words for a moment.

"…I hated the thought of anyone being remembered wrong. When what a
person actually said was different from what people remembered them
saying. I felt like that was doing that person wrong."

Hearing that, I understood a little better why he was someone who
quoted things so exactly.

"…Is that a selfish reason too?" I asked.

He laughed.

"…Probably. Maybe I just hated being wrong."

We laughed together at that. I felt, that day, that we both stood in a
similar place. We'd begun for different reasons, but now we'd each
arrived somewhere neither reason alone could explain.

That night, I wrote this in my notebook.

I began in order to hold onto things. So as not to lose them. But now
I know that isn't only the work of holding on. What I've put in order
has, in the end, been for the moment someone else comes looking for
it. I was someone who held on, and at the same time, someone preparing
to hand it over.

I read that sentence twice. Someone who holds on and someone
preparing to hand it over. This whole time, I'd only thought I was
the first.

That evening I stood in front of the seal again.

Standing there, I thought again about why I kept coming to this room.
In the last chapter, I said I couldn't open it because I didn't know
what would change. Now it felt a little different.

It wasn't that I hadn't opened it because I was afraid to. I'd been
hesitating because I didn't know what I'd have to do after opening it.
That day, for the first time, I recognized that difference.

Through nine moves, I lost eleven things. I became someone who put
things in order to stop that. But in front of this room, I was
learning something else. That nothing finishes if you only hold on.
That someday, what you've held onto has to be given to someone.

I didn't push that thought all the way through that day. I felt I
wasn't ready yet. But that night, for the first time, I thought of this
room not as "the room I hold onto" but as "the room I will someday
hand over." That thought stayed with me a long time.

I went home and opened my drawer. In it were the few things I'd kept
through all those moves. An old key. A few old photographs. A
handkerchief I'd used as a child.

I took them out one by one. All of them were things I'd held onto.
Things I'd never once thought of giving to anyone.

That night, looking at those things, I asked myself again what I was
really afraid of. Was I afraid of losing things, or was I afraid of
being left with nothing useful to anyone. I didn't find that answer
that day.

But putting those things back in the drawer, I had this thought for
the first time. Would a day come when I'd give even these to someone.
That thought was unfamiliar to me. At the same time, not entirely
unfamiliar.

The next morning, I arrived at the repository a little earlier than
usual. No one else was there yet. I stood in front of the last room's
door and thought back over the night before.

From someone who holds on, to someone who hands over. I said that
sentence out loud. No one was there to hear it. But saying it aloud, I
felt like I'd taken a step forward.

I still hadn't opened the seal. That would be the next chapter's work.
But that morning, walking into this room for the first time, I didn't
feel afraid. Instead I felt like I was getting ready. That difference
was a large one, to me.

I sat in front of the seal for a while, then asked Arche.

"…Arche, am I allowed to open this?"

Permission is not required. There is no function preventing access
to this item.

"…Then why has no one opened it?"

My records hold no answer to that question.

Hearing that answer, I laughed quietly. That morning, for the first
time, I understood clearly that I had to find that answer not from
Arche, but from myself.

(Continued in Chapter 41.)

Chapter 41. Opening It

_Previously_ — I understood why I put things in order. I was afraid
of losing things. But that day, for the first time, I thought that
holding on might not be all there was.

That day wasn't a special day.

I want to set that down first. I would come back to this scene many
times later, and each time I felt the temptation to dress it up as
some special day. In fact it was just an ordinary Tuesday. The sky was
overcast, and nine records had come in that day.

I put those nine in order as usual. Then I walked to the last room.

On the way I asked Arche nothing. There was nothing to ask. Everything
I needed, I'd already asked days before, and had the answers. That
morning I needed no new information.

Up until then, whenever I made a decision, I always tried to find the
reason first. This time I didn't. I just walked. My feet moved first,
and the reason followed after. Even writing this now, I still can't
fully explain why it had to be that particular morning. Only this much
is certain: that day, I felt I could no longer put it off.

Walking down the corridor, I thought back over the past few days.
Histor's question. The answer I'd given. The record whose cover I'd
fixed. The phone call with my mother. The Keeper's words. Walking, I
felt all of it had pushed me, little by little, to this door.

Histor happened to be in that room. He was looking again at the
writing in the plan's margin. Seeing me, he made a face that asked
what was going on.

"…What are you doing today?" he asked.

"…Opening it."

He didn't react right away. Only after a few seconds did he set down
the paper in his hand.

"…Now?"

"…Yes. Now."

"…Why today, of all days?"

I had no exact answer to that question.

"…I don't know. It just felt like today."

The Keeper happened to be passing the door just then and saw us. He
saw what was happening and came in.

"…Going to open it?" he asked.

"…Yes."

"…Want help?"

"…No. I'll do it."

He nodded and stepped back. He didn't stop me. He didn't ask about
qualification either. I was grateful for that.

Mena came in soon after too. No one had called for her, but that day
people gathered, one by one, into that room. I couldn't tell if that
was coincidence or not. I didn't press to find out.

I sat down in front of the seal.

The wax was still small. About half a palm's width. The letters on it
were still faded. Until the time of the end.

I ran my fingertip over the letters once. It was cold. That particular
texture of old wax. I closed my eyes for a moment and opened them
again. I wasn't trying to work up some great resolve. I just wanted to
steady my breath once.

If anyone is expecting something dramatic in this moment, I want to
say beforehand: I didn't pray, and I didn't take a vow. I just put
strength into my hand.

The wax came off more easily than I'd expected.

There was a small sound of thread breaking. That sound startled me a
little. That was all. There was no other sound in the room. No one
held their breath, and no light leaked out.

I unfolded the bundle carefully.

Histor came up beside me. He was holding his breath. I thought that
wasn't like him. He was always composed. But not in that moment.

The Keeper also stepped closer. Mena watched from a little farther
off. I asked her once, later, why she hadn't come closer. She answered
this way: "…Because that was your hands' work to do."

I remember, even now, that my hands didn't shake in that moment. That
was strange. I was ordinarily someone whose hands shook easily in
situations like that. But not that day.

Inside was paper.

Not one sheet. Several. I turned them one by one.

The first few sheets were blank. I thought maybe a preface or cover
sheet was missing, and kept turning. The next sheet was blank too.
And the next. I turned all the way to the end of the bundle.

All of it was blank.

I sat there for a long while.

"…Is that all?" Histor asked.

"…Yes. That's all."

"…There's nothing at all?"

"…Nothing."

He took the sheaf of papers from my hands and turned through them
himself, one by one. He held them up to the light too. Checked the
back of each. There was nothing. Truly blank paper.

"…No hidden writing or anything?" he asked. "The kind that shows up
under heat or light?"

"…Have you tried?"

"…Not yet. Can I try?"

I nodded. He brought a lamp and held each sheet up to it. Nothing
appeared. Only after checking once more did he set the papers down.

"…Nothing," he said quietly.

"…Disappointed?"

"…Yes. A little."

That honest answer put me more at ease, oddly. I told him I was
disappointed too. We didn't hide that disappointment from each other.
That was the one certain thing we did that day.

On the very first sheet, there was exactly one box.

I only noticed it then. A box drawn in a thin line near the top of the
sheet. Beside it, small letters read something.

Recipient.

That box was empty.

I looked at that box for a long while. It was a space meant for a
name. The way you'd write a recipient's name on the front of an
envelope. But no one had written any name there.

I put my hand on the panel by the wall and asked Arche.

"…Arche, what is this? I just opened it."

Checking.

There was a pause.

A new item has been identified. Classification is beginning.

"…Do you know what's in it?"

This item is blank. There is no recorded content.

"…Do you know what it is?"

In format, it is identical to the six cities' record documents.
However, author information, sending city, and recipient information
are all blank.

"…Is this classified as in progress?"

This item is registered as in progress. The closing condition is not
defined.

That answer made me laugh. It was the sentence I always heard. This
one time, it didn't sound hateful to me.

I want to write this moment as quietly as I can, in this record.
There's a temptation to write that I wasn't disappointed then.
Honestly, I was a little disappointed. I'd thought something grand
would come out. It was a thing that had been sealed for so long. I
think I'd expected something to match that long waiting.

But what came out was blank paper. The recipient box was empty too.

The Keeper took the papers and turned through them slowly.

"…This is the seventh city's record format," he said.

"…The seventh?" I asked.

"…Yes. We have all six cities' records. This format is the same as
theirs. The cover, the inner pages, even the recipient box. But this
is the seventh."

"…Where's the seventh city?"

The Keeper looked at me.

"…Here, Mone. This is the record with no city name. You saw it
yourself, at the start. That our city, too, should have a record —
but it had no name."

Hearing that, my breath caught. This was our city's own record. And
nothing had been written in it yet.

I thought back to the beginning. That first day, when I'd received the
nameless bundle, what the Keeper had said. You don't write your own
name yourself. The way you don't write your own address on your own
envelope. At the time I'd let that pass. Only now did I realize that
saying had led all the way to this moment.

"…Then how long has this been here?" Histor asked.

"…I don't know either," the Keeper answered. "I don't know exactly
how long this room itself has existed. I've only ever kept watch over
what's here."

"…Then why did it open now, of all times?"

No one could answer that question. I didn't know either. I'd only
felt, that morning, that I could no longer put it off.

The Keeper broke that silence.

"…Maybe that's the answer. No one can explain why it had to be today.
Mone just did it, today. Maybe that's enough."

Histor neither agreed nor argued with that. He just looked back down
at the blank paper.

"…So this…" Histor said slowly. "This is our city's record, and
nothing at all has been written yet?"

"…Seems that way," I answered.

"…Then what was this seal protecting? Why seal and protect paper with
nothing on it at all?"

I didn't know the answer to that. I didn't have it yet.

Mena spoke quietly then.

"…Protecting something not yet written might still be a kind of
protecting."

None of us answered that right away.

The Keeper set the papers back down and said.

"…I don't quite know how to take this. But there's one thing I do
know. This wasn't made to give us an answer."

"…Then what is it?" Histor asked.

"…I don't know. But it's clear it isn't an answer."

Mena touched one of the sheets lightly with her fingertip.

"…I keep thinking about what it means, set beside the other six
records. The five cities' records, and Loop's own. But only Loop's is
blank. Six are written, and one isn't."

"…Why does that matter?" I asked.

"…I don't know. But strangely, it puts me at ease. If it had all
already been written, I think I'd have been frightened instead. It
would have meant our city's share was already decided."

Hearing that, I looked at the blank paper again. The emptiness I'd
first seen as disappointment looked a little different, now that I'd
heard Mena's words.

I carefully put the papers back in order. I set the first sheet, with
the recipient box, on top, and put the rest of the blank sheets in
order beneath it.

In that moment, I realized I was doing what I always did. Putting
things in order. This time the object was only blank paper. Strangely,
that comforted me. Even without some grand revelation, I was still
doing the work I knew how to do.

I set the sheaf of papers on the table, and the rest of that day
passed with everyone standing around it.

Histor said, at last.

"…Honestly I thought I'd be angry. After waiting this long, for there
to be nothing."

"…But?" I asked.

"…But I'm not angry. Strangely."

I would remember that for a long time. Histor had every right to be
angry. But he wasn't. He just looked at that blank paper for a long,
long while.

That evening, I stayed behind alone in that room and put the papers
in order again. The first sheet with the recipient box, the blank
inner pages beneath it, and the cover last. I bound them in order
again and set them on the table.

I want to set this moment down this way. I'd thought I would discover
something grand. What I discovered was blank paper. At first that felt
empty to me. But putting those papers in order, I slowly came to know
it wasn't emptiness at all.

The six cities' records were already all written. What had happened
was recorded in them. Only our city's was still unwritten. I didn't
fully understand, that night, what that meant. But I decided not to
feel afraid of that blank space.

I didn't put that sheaf of papers back where the seal had been.
Instead I moved it to the table, where we could see it every day.

I can't exactly explain why I did that. I just no longer wanted to
keep it in a hidden place. There was a feeling that what the seal had
guarded was finished now, and that it was no longer our turn to watch
over it, but our turn to fill it.

On the way home, passing beside the water channel, I thought back over
what had happened that day. I still hadn't fully shaken the
disappointment. At the same time, there was a feeling of having seen a
place where something new could begin. Those two feelings sat side by
side, all through that night.

When I got home, I wrote down what had happened that day in my
notebook. Briefly, as always. Opened the seal today. Inside was blank
paper. The recipient box was empty too.

Beneath that sentence I wrote one more line. I set down exactly the
thought that had come to me that day, for the first time. I still
don't fully know that being blank isn't the end. But I've decided to
try believing it.

I closed the notebook after writing that line. I still didn't know
that night what to do the next day. But for the first time, I felt
that not knowing wasn't as frightening as it used to be.

Closing this chapter, I want to make one thing clear. That day I
proved no qualification. No one ever asked me whether I had it. I
simply walked over and put strength into my hand. That's the whole of
what happened in this chapter. Everything else was what we said to
each other afterward, standing around that blank paper.

I want to leave both things down honestly — that the blank paper
disappointed me, and that the disappointment didn't last long. Leaving
only one of those would be a lie. I decided, from the start, not to
leave any lie in this record.

(Continued in Chapter 42.)

Chapter 42. What Is Not Yet Written

_Previously_ — Inside the seal there was only blank paper. The
seventh city's record — Loop's own. The recipient box was empty too.
I was disappointed. That disappointment didn't last long.

The next day, even at the repository, all I could think about was
that paper.

Work had piled up as usual. Twelve records from the six cities were
waiting for me. I put them in order. My hands moved out of habit, but
my mind kept going back to that room from yesterday.

Sleeping on it, I felt the same way the next day as I had the day
before. Disappointment, and the strange relief that the disappointment
didn't last. That day too, I still couldn't fully explain how those
two feelings could sit together.

That morning, Arche greeted me as usual. There are nineteen items to
process today. I knew that among that number was the blank sheaf
registered the day before. It was being treated exactly like the
other eighteen. That felt strange to me. At the same time, it felt
right too.

I scanned through that list of nineteen. An application from Haven, an
adjustment proposal from the Reckoning, a distribution record from the
Harvest. And at the very bottom, one entry that read only: Sending
city unknown. Six-city standard format. Recipient unknown.

I stood beside that entry and thought for a long while. All eighteen
of the others recorded something that had happened. Only this one did
not.

Among that day's records was one Xene had sent, from the Harvest
record city. A short note was attached to the cover sheet.

"This entry's record came late. I'm sorry. But I wrote it, even
late."

I read that note and smiled. Xene, still afraid of the work of
recording, was still writing anyway. Putting it in order, I thought
that note, set beside our city's blank sheaf, made a strange kind of
pair. One was a record written far too late. One was a record not
written at all, yet.

I placed the two side by side on the shelf. The late and the
not-yet-written. That day, for the first time, I thought there might
not be much difference between the two. Both, in the end, were places
that could only be filled once someone decided to raise their hand and
write.

At lunchtime, Histor came looking for me. He spoke less than usual.

"…What are you doing?" I asked.

"…Nothing. Just thinking."

"…About what?"

He didn't answer right away. We sat side by side in silence for a
moment.

"…I thought," he said after a while, "I thought I'd be angry. Really."

"…Why?"

"…I've read all six records. I saw how long people waited in front of
seals, locked doors, things like that. I thought if it opened, at
least something worthy of that waiting should come out."

"…But?"

"…But what came out was blank paper. Nothing written on it at all. I
thought that made a mockery of that long seal, that long waiting. So
by rights I should be angry."

I waited for him to go on.

"…But I'm not. I don't know why, myself."

I didn't add anything to that. I knew that was a problem he had to
solve for himself.

That afternoon we went back to the last room. The sheaf of papers was
still on the table. I reached to put it away, but Histor stopped me.

"…Wait a moment. I want to look at it a little more."

He sat down in front of it. And for a long time he did nothing, said
nothing, only looked at those papers.

I want to set this scene down exactly. I expected Histor's face to
show some kind of realization. There was none. He just sat there for a
long time. He blinked a few times, turned the paper once, and closed
it again. That was all.

That day, for the first time, I saw that not being angry and
accepting are two different things. Histor didn't accept that paper.
His face still looked like he didn't understand. He was only enduring
that not-understanding, for a long time.

"…Why blank, of all things?" he asked after a long while. Less to me
than to himself.

"…I don't know," I answered honestly.

"…I had this thought," he went on. "If something had been written in
here. A prophecy, or some rule. Then we'd just have to follow it. But
if it's blank, no one tells us what we're supposed to write."

I nodded at that. It was exact. Up until then I'd only felt
disappointment in front of the blank page. Hearing him, I noticed
another feeling beneath that disappointment. It was weight. The weight
of having to fill it — of someone having to fill it.

"…Isn't that scarier?" I asked.

"…It might be scary," he answered. "But strangely, it doesn't feel
stifling."

"…It does to me, a little," I said.

"…You too?"

"…Yes. There being nothing fixed for me to write — that feels heavier
to me, if anything. If there were a rule, at least I could just follow
it."

Histor thought about that for a moment.

"…But do you know something? Reading all six records, the people I
envied most —"

"…Who?"

"…People who, without any rule, just did what they could. When Speon
cut the channel, no one had ordered him. He just cleared what was
blocked. I envied that. I always went looking for the fixed answer
first."

"…And now?"

"…Now I think I'm standing in that same spot. And it doesn't feel
stifling — strangely, it feels a little freeing."

"…Why?"

"…Because this time, no one has the answer before me either. When I
read the six records, the answer was already inside them. All I had
to do was find it. This isn't that. Instead…"

He stopped.

"…Instead?" I asked.

"…Instead there's a space. A space to write something into."

I turned that over for a long time. Since the day before, I'd only
been able to see that blank paper as disappointment. Only after
hearing Histor's words could I see it differently.

Standing there, I called to mind each of the six cities' records, one
by one. The Ledger. The Scales. The Roll. Perpetuity. The Consensus.
And Loop's own. Five had each begun by one person's hand and been
brought to a close by one person's hand. Only the last one had no one
bringing it to a close. I'd thought that was strange, and that day,
for the first time, it looked different. The five were books recording
something already lived through. Only the sixth was a space for
something not yet lived.

I still don't fully know why the sealed thing was blank. I don't know
even now, writing these words. But that day, I knew at least this
much — that blank wasn't nothing. Blank was a space.

The Keeper came into the room just then. Seeing us sitting there, he
stood quietly beside us.

"…Figured something out?" he asked.

"…No," Histor answered. "Just kept looking at it."

"…What do you see?"

Histor thought for a moment.

"…Nothing. But that nothing doesn't feel like nothing at all, anymore."

The Keeper smiled faintly at that.

"…I felt exactly that. A long time ago, in this same spot."

"…You felt that too, in this room, Keeper?" I asked.

"…I did. When I was young, I agonized for a long time over whether I
had the right to be here at all. But in the end no one ever gave me
that right. I just came here, every day. And at some point, weighing
that right stopped mattering."

"…Why?"

"…Because this room never once demanded that I prove anything. There
was only someone who kept coming."

I wrote that down in my notebook.

That afternoon, I passed on Histor's words to Mena too. Instead
there's a space. A space to write something into.

Mena heard that and smiled quietly.

"…That boy is right."

"…You think so too, Mena?"

"…Yes. I once had this thought. What if something had already been
fully written on that paper. What we should do, when things would
happen — all of it already decided. Then I probably would have just
trusted the paper and done nothing at all."

"…Is that a bad thing?"

"…It's not bad. It's just different. In front of blank paper, I had to
find what I should do, myself. That was hard, but it's also, I think,
what brought me this far."

Hearing that, I thought of the road Mena had walked, from the first
city ever founded, all the way to now. She had never once received an
answer. And yet she'd kept walking.

"…But Mone," Mena added. "Don't think you have to fill that whole
space yourself."

"…What do you mean?"

"…That paper being blank probably doesn't mean one person has to fill
it all. The six records weren't written by one person either. Five,
six people shared the writing. This will be the same."

That eased something in me. I think I'd been unconsciously feeling,
this whole time, that I alone had to carry that whole box.

That evening, leaving the room with Histor, I asked one last thing.

"…Histor, how do you feel right now?"

He thought a moment before answering.

"…Strange. I couldn't find the answer, and I'm not angry. I thought
that wasn't like me. But thinking about it now, maybe this is like me
too."

"…What do you mean?"

"…I was always someone who looked for the exact thing. But today I
looked at something inexact for a long time. And that wasn't as bad as
I expected."

Closing this chapter, I want to make one thing clear. Histor did not
come to believe, that day. He still said he didn't know why that paper
was blank. Neither do I. But that day, the two of us both learned, a
little, how not to be angry in front of that not-knowing.

Before stepping out the door, the Keeper asked one last thing.

"…So what will you do now?"

I couldn't answer that right away.

"…I don't know. But I'm thinking about copying out the six records."

"…Copying them?"

"…Yes. I want to lay the six cities' records side by side with this
blank paper. I don't know what it'll turn into. I just feel like I
have to do it."

The Keeper heard that and nodded.

"…That sounds like a good idea."

"…Why?"

"…With only blank paper beside it, people can't get any sense of what
they're supposed to write. But with the six already-written records
laid alongside it, at least you can see the shape a thing takes when
it gets filled."

Hearing that, I felt that the plan I hadn't yet spoken aloud had
already half taken shape.

"…But that's hard work," the Keeper added. "Copying out all six
records. Ask Arche to do it, and it wouldn't take a day."

"…I know."

"…And you still want to do it by hand?"

"…Yes."

"…Why?"

I couldn't answer that right away either.

"…I still don't quite know. I just feel like I should."

The Keeper didn't press further. He just patted my shoulder once and
left. Before stepping out the door, without turning around, he left
one more thing.

"…Sometimes you find out the reason later."

I wrote that down small, at the bottom of my notebook page. And I
folded that page to mark it. It meant a page I'd come back and open
again later.

I don't want to call this a conversion. That wouldn't be the exact
word. I'll only write this much. He looked at it for a long time. And
he wasn't angry. Only those two things are true.

On the way home, I thought again of that blank paper's recipient box.
No one had filled that box yet. That day, for the first time, I
thought that filling it might rest in my own hands. That thought was
frightening, and at the same time, strangely light.

Earlier I wrote that I seemed to be changing from someone who holds
on into someone who hands over. That night, I took that sentence out
and read it again. I hadn't fully understood, then, what it meant. Now
I think I understand it a little better. Someone who holds on protects
something that already exists. Someone who hands over gets ready to
give away even what doesn't exist yet. That day, standing in front of
blank paper, I was becoming the latter.

I couldn't fall asleep late into that night. Not out of fear. It was
the feeling that some work had come into being for me to do. That
feeling was unfamiliar. At the same time, it wasn't a bad one.

Closing this chapter, I want to copy down once more what Histor said
that day. "…Instead there's a space. A space to write something
into." I've held onto that sentence longer than any other in this
record. I still do.

That night, for the first time, I accepted that this space wasn't a
space of fear, but a space of margin. A margin isn't nothing. A margin
is only a place where nothing has been set down yet. I'd spent over two
years putting things in order, and it was only in front of this seal
that I properly learned the difference between the two.

(Continued in Chapter 43.)

Chapter 43. Copying

_Previously_ — Histor wasn't angry in front of the blank paper.
"…Instead there's a space. A space to write something into." I held
onto that for a long time. That night, I thought about copying out
the six records.

The next morning, I brought out all six records.

The Ledger, the Scales, the Roll, Perpetuity, the Consensus, and
Loop's own record. I laid them side by side on the table in the last
room. I set the blank sheaf beside them too. I readied a fresh sheaf
of paper.

"…What are you going to do?" Histor asked.

"…Copy them. All six."

His eyes went wide.

"…All of them? That's hundreds of pages."

"…I know."

I opened the first record and began to copy.

By hand. Letter by letter. Even copying the first sentence took time.
I wasn't someone with beautiful handwriting. I crossed things out and
rewrote them several times.

Arche, watching, spoke up.

Mone, I can perform this task in your place. Copying the whole set
would take under three minutes.

"…I know. But I'll do it."

May I ask why?

I stopped my hand at that question. Why. I had no exact answer to it.

"…No reason. I just want to."

Understood.

Arche didn't ask further.

A few days later, Arche asked again.

Mone, I have checked your progress rate. At the current pace,
completion will take roughly one month. If I perform it instead, it
will be completed instantly, with no error. Would you like to
reconsider?

I laughed a little, hearing that.

"…Are you worried about me?"

I do not perform the function of worry. However, presenting an
alternative upon identifying a low-efficiency method is my default
behavior.

"…I know. But this time I'll go with the low-efficiency method."

Understood. I will continue.

I liked that answer. Arche didn't try to stop me. It just accepted it,
exactly, as it was.

Histor watched all this from beside me.

"…Can I ask why too?" he said.

"…You can ask, but I don't really know the answer."

"…Arche is much faster."

"…I know."

"…Then why?"

I sat holding the pen, thinking for a moment. A few reasons came to
mind. That copying by hand would stay in memory longer. That if Arche
did it, it would be so easy it might end up feeling weightless. But
none of those reasons felt completely right.

"…I don't know," I finally said. "I just feel like it has to be by
hand."

Histor didn't press further. He just nodded and sat down beside me,
and began watching me copy.

The first day I managed only a few pages.

My wrist ached. My finger joints were stiff. That night I wrapped a
cloth around my wrist and slept.

My mother saw that and asked.

"…What did you do to make your hand like that?"

"…I'm copying something."

"…Can't Arche do that?"

"…It could."

My mother didn't press further. She just brought me something to warm
my wrist with. That night I wondered why she hadn't pressed me, but I
didn't ask. Maybe she too, like me, had once done something she
couldn't explain.

I want to write these days as the most tedious part of this record.
Because they were tedious. Every morning I went to the repository and
wrote until evening. Nothing dramatic happened at all. My hand just
hurt, my shoulders knotted, my eyes grew blurry.

The second and third days were similar. I copied out the first part of
the Ledger. Elpis and Zeteo's story. Copying those sentences by hand,
I felt something strange. Different from how it had felt reading it
the first time.

"…What's different?" Histor asked. He seemed to have noticed me
stopping my pen and staring off for a moment.

"…Reading and writing are different."

"…How?"

"…Sentences that pass by quickly when you read don't, when you write.
You have to make every letter with your hand. Elpis waiting by the
well — when I read that, it was just one paragraph. Copying it took a
long while. The whole time, I felt like I was there at that well."

Histor heard that and brought over a sheet of paper himself, and began
copying alongside me. I looked at him, surprised.

"…Can I do it too?" he asked.

"…Sure. But why?"

"…I want to see for myself whether what you said is true."

From that day we copied together.

We didn't talk much. We each just moved the page in front of us. Now
and then one of us would stop and look out the window, or shake out a
wrist.

I remember those days clearly, even now. There was no great
conversation. We just sat side by side and wrote. That felt strangely
comfortable to me. Some silences are awkward, and some aren't. Those
days' silence was the latter kind.

The Keeper came sometimes too and helped with a few pages. Mena came
one day as well. People took turns sharing that work a little at a
time. Even so, copying all six records took many days.

Zeteo and Elpis stopped by around that time too. Zeteo looked for a
long while at the part where his own name appeared, then silently took
up a pen. Watching him copy his own story, I felt he was still
searching for something. His face was still the face of someone who
didn't believe. But his hand pressed down hard as he wrote.

Elpis, beside him, simply put the papers in order. As always, he
stayed in the place of waiting.

Pheugon came for a day too and helped with a few pages. Copying the
verdict portion of the Scales record, he said one thing.

"…This is the sentence I wrote back then, but writing it again now,
with my own hand, it feels different somehow."

"…How so?" I asked.

"…Back then I wrote it with certainty. Now I'm just copying, but that
certainty feels a little heavy."

Trope didn't come. Instead he sent a short letter. "Thank you for
copying my part too. I'm still not confident enough to sit in that
seat." I set that letter side by side with the six records.

Xene and Lytron came for a day too. Xene's hand shook as she copied
the part where she'd stopped recording. Lytron, beside her, quietly
took on other pages. He no longer said, as a kind of boast, that he'd
never lost anything. He just wrote quietly.

Anemos and Eikon stopped by briefly too. Eikon still said he lacked
nothing. And yet he copied out to the end the few pages placed in
