front of him before he left. Anemos, beside him, said with a smile,
"…Will you ask me that again, later too?" I didn't know exactly what
that meant, but somehow I felt I understood.

Hora and Tyche came too. Tyche still called it coincidence. And yet she
said she would copy out the last part of the Consensus with her own
hand. That contradiction didn't strike me as strange. It had always
been that way, in these records.

In the second week we copied the Roll and Perpetuity. The Roll's
sentences were stiff and dry. Registration numbers, review results,
grounds for ruling. Copying it, I retraced what Lytron had gone
through — the moment he was turned away at the door for lacking
qualification. Copying that sentence, I felt a chill at my fingertips.

Perpetuity's texture was different. The part Anemos had written moved
slowly, circling back to the same spot again and again. It felt to me
like it was showing how long he'd waited in that same spot. Eikon's
part was short and composed. The words "I lack nothing" repeated
several times. Copying that repetition exactly, I felt like the
repetition itself was saying something.

Around the third week, copying the Scales record, I stopped my hand.

It was the part Trope had written. The story of the time he couldn't
believe in mercy. Copying that sentence by hand, I thought of the note
Trope had sent just now. "Still not balanced. But closer than
before."

I set those two sentences side by side and looked at them. Trope of
before, and Trope of now. Neither had certainty. But something had
changed.

That evening, Histor sat looking for a long while at the page he'd
copied.

"…What are you doing?" I asked.

"…It's strange. I've already read this part more than ten times. But
copying it, it reads differently again."

"…How?"

"…When I read, I judged it. Whether it was right or wrong, whether it
proved something. But when I copy, all I have to do is move it over,
so I stop judging. I'm just making that person's words again with my
own hand."

I turned that over for a long time. It was an exact observation.

"…But do you know something?" he went on. "At first I read these six
records to know them exactly. Where everything was, who had proven
what. But copying them now, I keep rethinking what knowing exactly
even means."

"…How is it different?"

"…I thought knowing meant putting something into your head. But
copying is something you do with your hands. That there's work the
head and the hands do together — I only learned that this time."

I started to write that down in my notebook and stopped. Some things
are better just remembered than written down.

I don't want to write this chapter as though I gained some grand
realization. I still don't fully know the reason. When Arche asked me
why, I had no answer. When Histor asked, I had no answer. Even writing
this now, I can't give the exact reason.

But I can say this much. Copying these six records out by hand, I
was with the people inside them differently than before. Not just
knowing them, but watching their words be born again through my own
hand. I still don't know what that means. But for those few weeks, I
didn't want to stop doing it.

Copying all six records took nearly a month.

On the day I copied the last page, my hand was trembling so badly I
dropped the pen twice. Histor laughed beside me.

"…Not many pages left now."

"…I know."

I copied the final sentence. The last part of the Consensus, the
sentence Hora had written. I never once met him. And I cannot bring
myself to believe all of this was chance.

Copying that sentence, my grip on the pen tightened.

Once everything was copied, I laid the six new sheaves side by side
with the originals. The paper colors were different, and the
handwriting varied a little too. On days I was tired, the letters
sprawled; on days I felt well, they were clear. It wasn't a perfect
copy.

Looking at those copies, I thought it was a good thing they weren't
perfect. If they'd been perfect, they would have only been a
reproduction. This wasn't a reproduction. This had passed through my
own hand.

That night, walking home, I noticed for the first time that my wrist
no longer hurt. The pain hadn't gone away — I'd simply grown used to
it. I thought that was the most certain thing I'd gained in that whole
month.

The next morning, the Keeper turned through the copies one by one and
asked.

"…Now that these are done, what will you do?"

"…I still don't know. But I feel like I have to do something with
them."

"…Will you put them beside the blank paper?"

"…Yes. For now."

I set the six bundles of copies side by side with the blank sheaf.
Five were stories already written; one still held nothing at all. It
looked to me as though the six copies were standing in a ring around
the blank one.

The Keeper stood beside them and looked at them for a long while.

"…This is the first time in twenty-some years I've seen this," he
said.

"…Seen what?"

"…All six cities' things, in one place, this neatly arranged. Before,
they were always scattered. Some here, some there."

"…Does it look different?"

"…Yes. Strangely steady."

Hearing that, I looked again at the six bundles and the blank paper.
It really was so. Scattered, each had been its own story. Laid side by
side, they felt like they were joining into one.

"…That's strange," Histor said, looking at it.

"…What is?"

"…Five records are all finished stories, and yet that makes the blank
one look even more empty. But at the same time, it gives it the
feeling that it's about to be filled."

I agreed with that.

Looking back on that month, I still don't have a complete answer for
why I did it by hand. I don't think that's strange. Some things you
don't begin only once you know the whole reason — some things stay
that way even after you've done them, the reason still not fully
known.

But I do know this. Copying these six records by hand, I felt like I
was meeting each person inside them again, one by one. Zeteo's doubt,
Elpis's waiting, Pheugon's verdicts, Trope's hesitation, Xene's
silence, Lytron's boast that wasn't quite a boast, Anemos staying by
someone's side, Eikon lacking nothing, Hora's belief, Tyche's
probability. I remade all of it, once, with my own fingertips.

And beside all of it was our city's own space, still holding nothing
at all. That night, I was able to look at that space with less fear
than before. I still didn't know the reason. But I wasn't afraid. I
decided that was enough.

(Continued in Chapter 44.)

Chapter 44. What the Names Mean

_Previously_ — I copied all six records by hand. It took a month. I
still don't know exactly why. But doing that work, the blank space
left for our own city grew a little less frightening.

It was the day Mena came to look at the copies.

That morning was unusually quiet. Few records had come in. When Arche
said There are five items to process today, I noticed that was the
smallest number in a long while. Days like that came sometimes. No one
ever knew why.

Mena turned through the six bundles one by one, reading slowly. It
took a long time. I waited beside her. Histor was there too.

"…You copied this well," Mena said, after a long while.

"…Thank you."

"…The handwriting gets a little rough in some places."

"…I was very tired that day."

Mena laughed. It wasn't a mocking laugh — it was the kind you give
someone you've known a long time.

After finishing, Mena picked up the blank sheaf. She looked for a
long while at the first sheet, the one with the recipient box.

"…Looking at this box reminds me of something," she said.

"…What?"

"…My own name."

That surprised me a little. Mena had never once brought up her own
name before.

"…Mone, do you know what your name means?" Mena asked.

"…No. I've never once thought about it."

"…I know. I found out a long time ago."

I held my breath. Histor set down his paper too and looked at us.

"…Your name, Mone. It comes from a very old word. It means 'an
abiding place' — a room to stay in."

I repeated those words twice in my head. An abiding place. A room to
stay in.

"…A room?" I asked.

"…Yes. And that word shares its root with my own name, Mena. From
meno — to abide. I am one who abides, and you are the place that is
abided in. When I found that out, it felt strange. It was as if my
name were calling out yours."

I turned over what I'd just heard. Up until then I'd only ever thought
my name was just a name. I'd never once thought it might have a
meaning.

"…Why are you telling me this now?" I asked.

Mena thought for a moment before answering.

"…It wasn't the right moment before. Now feels right."

"…How did you find out, what it meant?" I asked.

"…I saw it in an old document. There are old words that came with
this city when it was founded. The way names are given comes from
those words too. I stumbled across it, reading, when I was young. Back
then I just thought it was curious."

"…And now?"

"…Now it feels different from curious. Once I knew what my name meant,
I understood a little better why I kept trying to stay put somewhere.
Moving from city to city, I was always someone who stayed in one
place. It felt like the name had known that before I did."

Hearing that, I looked again at the blank paper in my hands.
Recipient. That box was empty.

I want to set this moment down exactly. I didn't feel anything like
fate in that moment. If anything, the opposite. I only felt, quietly,
that a name had been given to something that had already been sitting
in this place a long time. That was all.

"…Mone," Mena said again. "Once I knew that name, I saw you a little
differently. What you do — I thought it might not be a coincidence at
all."

"…What do you mean?"

"…You've always been someone looking for a place to put something
down. In order, in its proper spot. I thought that resembled your
name."

Histor, who'd been quiet until then, asked.

"…Does my name have a meaning too?"

Mena looked at him.

"…It does. Histor. It means 'one who has found out.' And later, that
word became the root of the word history."

Histor was still for a moment, hearing that.

Then he laughed. Loudly, out loud.

"…Me? One who has found out? Me?"

"…Yes."

"…I read all six records and couldn't find out anything. I couldn't
even get the dates to match, couldn't work out who that person was.
And my name means one who has found out — that's the complete
opposite."

He kept laughing. I could tell it was real laughter. Not mockery.

"…My family," he said, at the tail end of the laugh. "I told you we've
handled records for generations. I always thought that was just a
family tradition. My grandfather did it, my father did it, I do it.
But to think even the name meant that from the start."

"…Does that strike you as curious?" I asked.

"…Not curious so much as a little humbling. I've always been proud of
knowing things exactly. And I couldn't find out the one thing that
mattered most."

Mena shook her head at that.

"…I don't think that's so. Finding out doesn't have to mean getting it
all right from the start. Looking a long time, and eventually finding
out — that's finding out too."

"…What's the difference?"

"…Someone who gets it right from the start is lucky. Someone who
finds out after looking a long time is persistent. I think the second
kind is rarer."

Histor said nothing back to that. He just listened.

"…What?"

He didn't answer that question. Instead the laughter slowly faded. He
looked down at the paper in his hands. Pages of the six records, in
his own handwriting.

I write this moment the most carefully of anything in this record.
Histor said nothing at all. I waited, expecting him to say something.
He didn't. He just looked at that paper for a long while.

Neither Mena nor I broke that silence. We just waited.

In that silence I felt something strange. I had come to think, through
all these records, that silence was always a place where something was
being hidden. It had been that way for Zeteo in Haven, and for the
clerk in the Equity. But this silence of Histor's wasn't a hiding
silence. It was a silence working hard to accept something.

That day, for the first time, I was able to tell the two apart. A
hiding silence pushes people away. A silence trying to accept draws
people closer. That day, I felt Histor closer to me than I ever had
before.

Mena watched that silence and said, quietly.

"…At a moment like this, it's best to say nothing at all. You want to
add something, but that usually doesn't help."

I took that to heart, and from then on I tried to keep that principle
through the whole rest of this record. Some moments should just be
let pass.

A long while later, he set the paper down quietly.

"…I'll step out for a bit."

That was all he said, and he left the room.

I didn't follow him. Mena didn't stop him either.

I don't know where he went, or what he thought about. He didn't bring
it up again after he came back. I didn't ask. In this record I decided
to write what I didn't hear as not heard. That time was his own.

He came back about an hour later. His eyes were a little swollen. I
couldn't tell if he'd cried, or if it was just from walking so long. I
didn't ask.

"…Are you all right?" I asked.

"…Yes. I'm fine."

"…You don't have to say more."

"…I know. Thank you."

He said only that, and sat back down at his place, and opened the page
he'd been copying again. I felt that the hand taking up that page was
a little steadier than before.

Mena, watching the door he'd left through, said to me.

"…It was good I told your name first."

"…Why?"

"…Your name has a quiet meaning. A room, a place to stay. But that
boy's name is a heavier one. One who has found out. And here he was,
thinking he'd found out nothing at all."

I turned that over. A quiet meaning and a heavy one. I decided not to
think it lucky that my own share was light. That even a light thing
carries weight — I was learning that, little by little, those days.

"…But did he really find out nothing?" I asked.

Mena looked at me.

"…I don't know. That's his to know."

That night, I wrote my own name in my notebook. Mone. Beside it, in
small letters, I wrote. An abiding place. A room to stay in.

I looked at those words for a long while. Up until then I'd only ever
thought of myself as someone who put things in order. Someone looking
for a place to set things down. That day, for the first time, I
thought that I might be the place itself.

I decided not to push this thought any further. Push it too far, and
I'd end up making up a story that sounded convincing. I'll only set
down this fact. Mena told me this. I heard it. That's all.

I told my father this too. He looked surprised for a moment, then
said.

"…I'm not actually the one who named you."

"…Then who?"

"…Your grandmother did. I never asked her why that name. I just
thought it was pretty."

Hearing that, I thought of my grandmother. The one I'd written about
at the start, clearing her throat and talking to Arche. I could no
longer ask her whether she'd known this name's meaning when she chose
it, or had simply liked the sound of it. My grandmother had passed away
a few years before.

"…I should have asked her," I said.

My father patted my shoulder.

"…Even if you had, it was probably nothing much of a reason. That's
the kind of person she was. But a name given for no great reason can
still come to hold a great meaning, later."

I thought about that for a long time. A name given for no great reason
that later came to hold a great meaning. I felt that resembled
everything this whole record had done. Small things piling up, until
their meaning only became visible later.

The next day, Histor came to the repository as usual. He said nothing
about the day before. I didn't ask either.

We just worked as usual. We put the records that came in from the six
cities in order. The blank sheaf was still on the table.

Only one thing was different. That day, he looked at that blank paper
a little longer than usual. I noticed, but said nothing.

Near the end of the day's work, he spoke suddenly.

"…I've kept thinking, since that day."

"…About what?"

"…Whether I really found out nothing. I went over it carefully. I
couldn't get the dates to match, couldn't find out who that person
was. But there's exactly one thing I did find out."

"…What?"

"…What you said. That if it stays shut it just stays here. That much,
I found out — that it was right."

I didn't answer that. He seemed to find that enough, and didn't go on.

I don't want to use this as evidence of a conversion. He still said he
believed nothing at all. But he was accepting the meaning of his own
name, in his own way, a little. That, and believing, are different
things. I try hard not to mix the two together.

A few days later, I passed the story of the names on to the Keeper. He
heard it all and smiled quietly.

"…I know that story too. Mena told me the same thing once, a long time
ago."

"…Does your name have a meaning too, Keeper?"

"…It does. But I'll keep that one to myself. Today is your story."

I liked that answer. I felt, hearing it, that knowing every name's
meaning wasn't the whole point of this story.

Histor only listened to our conversation from beside us that day. In
the days that followed, he didn't bring up his own name's meaning
again. I guessed it was because he hadn't yet decided how to take it.
I didn't press him.

"…What changed for you, Keeper, once you knew?" I asked.

He thought for a moment.

"…Not so much changed as confirmed. I was already living that way. The
name only told me that, later — I didn't live that way because of the
name."

I felt that distinction mattered. That a name doesn't decide a fate; a
name only lights up what's already there.

"…Then was Histor already someone who found things out?" I asked.

The Keeper thought for a moment.

"…I don't know that either. That's his to own. I can only speak to my
own story."

I felt that answer was honest. No one could solve another person's
name for them. Each person had to confirm their own name through their
own life.

Closing this chapter, I want to write down once more what my own name
means. Mone. An abiding place. A room to stay in. At the start, I
wrote that all I did was put things in order. Back then I thought that
was the whole of it. Now it looks a little different. Putting things
in order, in the end, may have been, all along, the work of making a
place for someone to stay.

I don't write this thought as certainty. I only write that it looks
that way. Certainty isn't mine to give. I was only there, in that
place.

After closing the repository door that night, before falling asleep, I
thought again of the six copies and the blank sheaf. Two names'
meanings had now been added to what lay inside it. A room to stay in,
and one who has found out. I still didn't know, that day, how those
two would someday meet on that blank page. But simply knowing the two
sat side by side, I felt something was already changing.

(Continued in Chapter 45.)

Chapter 45. Histor's Reprieve

_Previously_ — Mena told us what our names meant. Mone: an abiding
place, a room to stay in. Histor: one who has found out, the root of
history. Histor laughed, and in the end had nothing to say.

A few days later, the Keeper gathered people in the last room.

Mena, Histor, me, and a few others still staying in the city around
that time — Zeteo, Elpis, Trope, Xene. They'd all come to see the six
copies, and simply stayed on in that room.

The Keeper held up the blank sheaf and spoke.

"…I think we need to decide what to do with this now."

"…Decide how?" Zeteo asked.

"…There's one set of the six copies now. But one set isn't enough.
Everyone's going back to their own city, aren't they. Shouldn't each
person take a set with them?"

I was surprised to hear that. I'd thought all along that one set would
be enough. I hadn't considered that we'd need several.

"…How many sets do we need?" I asked.

"…At least six. One for each city."

"…Isn't it seven?" Histor asked. "There's ours too."

"…Right. Seven sets."

Hearing that, I felt the size of this plan for the first time. One for
each of the six cities, plus the original that would stay in this
city. I thought this might have been, all along, what this old seal
had meant to do.

Hearing that number, I felt my wrist begin to ache again already. One
set alone had taken a month to copy. Six sets would be half a year.

"…Are you going to do all of that by hand?" Histor asked.

"…I don't know," I answered. "I hadn't thought that far yet."

I put my hand on the panel by the wall and asked Arche.

"…Arche, can you help make six copies?"

Possible. Using the copy function, it can be completed instantly.

I hesitated at that for a moment. This time, I might really need the
help. One set, I could manage by hand. Six was different.

"…But if we do that, this just becomes a reproduction," Histor said
beside me.

"…Yes. That's what stops me."

The room went quiet for a moment. Everyone looked at each other.

Xene spoke first.

"…I can help. I'm a record-keeper by trade, after all."

"…Me too," Elpis said.

Trope quietly raised a hand. He said nothing, but the gesture was his
answer.

Watching that, something in me moved. I'd thought I'd have to do all
of this alone. But here, in this room, people were raising their
hands, with no one telling them to.

"…I'll help too," Histor said.

I looked at him, a little surprised at that.

"…Really?"

"…Yes."

Mena raised her hand quietly too. The Keeper didn't raise his. Instead
he said this.

"…I'll keep the place, instead of the hands. So nothing gets lost —
who puts what where."

That felt like him. From the start he'd been someone who put things
down, someone who held onto things.

"…But Histor," I said, choosing my words, "why do you want to help
with this? Last time I asked, you said it wasn't out of certainty."

He thought for a moment.

"…That's right. I still have no certainty."

"…Then why?"

"…Not because I believe it. Because this must not be lost."

I heard that sentence twice. The first time I nearly let it pass. The
second time it settled into me.

Mena heard that and asked quietly.

"…Histor, does that have to do with what happened a few days ago?"

Mena meant the day she'd told us what our names meant. Histor
hesitated a moment.

"…Maybe. But I can't say it's exactly because of that. I just keep
having this feeling, lately, that it mustn't be lost. I can't explain
the reason either."

"…You don't have to explain it," Mena answered. "Not being able to
explain something can be a good enough reason too, sometimes."

I thought that sounded like something close to an answer I'd once
given myself. I don't know what changes. But if it stays shut it just
stays here. Histor was now saying something similar, in his own way.

I copy this sentence down with the most care of anything in this
record. There's a temptation to write that he wore some dramatic
expression as he said it. He didn't. He spoke as he always did.
Exactly, and evenly.

"…What do you mean by that?" I asked.

"…I still don't know what any of this means. I don't know why the
blank paper was blank, I don't know who that person is. But I know one
thing. If this is lost, no one will ever be able to stand in this
place again. I don't want that."

Zeteo heard that and nodded.

"…Same for me. I still haven't found an answer. But I don't want to
lose this."

Elpis, beside him, smiled quietly.

"…The two of us are still standing in different places."

"…True enough."

Watching those two siblings, I confirmed again that they were nearly
unchanged since Haven. Zeteo was still searching, and Elpis was still
waiting. And yet both of them, in this room, right now, had their
hands raised the same way.

The Keeper, watching all this, said.

"…I think this is a good thing."

"…What is?" I asked.

"…Everyone's here for a different reason. Some because they believe,
some because they don't, some just because they hate the thought of
it disappearing. But the hands are all moving together. I think that's
the shape this work is supposed to take."

I wrote that down in my notebook.

We began dividing up the work that day.

Xene and Elpis took the Roll and Perpetuity. Zeteo and Trope took the
Ledger and the Scales. Histor and I decided to take the Consensus and
Loop's own blank record.

That arrangement pleased me, strangely. Rather than each person
copying their own city's record fresh, we each took a different city's
records. It wasn't copying your own story — it was carrying someone
else's story through your own hand.

The Keeper saw that arrangement and said.

"…Who decided this?"

"…No one, really. It just worked out this way."

"…Curious. I'd think copying someone else's story would sit easier
than copying your own."

I nodded at that. It was true. I'd taken on our own city's blank paper
together with Histor, and that was far less heavy than taking it on
alone.

The work took several weeks. During that time I watched the others
too.

Xene, copying the Roll, sometimes stopped her hand. She was still
someone afraid of recording. But this time, after stopping, she took
up the pen again. Faster than before.

"…Are you all right?" I asked once.

"…Yes. It's just that this part resembles the part where I stopped
before."

"…You don't have to do it, if it's hard."

"…No. This time I want to see it through to the end."

I heard that and added nothing to it.

Trope, copying the Scales, spoke almost not at all. One day, passing
by, I saw his fingertips. He was copying the verdict section. His hand
was trembling slightly.

"…A hard part?" I asked.

"…Yes. This is the sentence I feared most."

"…Then why did you take this on?"

Trope stopped his hand and looked at me.

"…Because if I only avoid what frightens me, I think I'll stay this
way my whole life."

I wrote that down in my notebook.

He was still exact. He never misquoted. But something a little
different had been added to that exactness. Before, that exactness had
been the attitude of someone verifying. Now it was closer to the
attitude of someone protecting.

"…You seem different, lately," I said one day.

"…How so?"

"…I don't know. Just a feeling."

He only laughed at that. He neither admitted it nor denied it.

Zeteo and Elpis had split the Ledger and Scales between them, and one
day traded places. Curious, I asked.

"…Why did you switch?"

Zeteo answered.

"…I kept wanting to copy only what I already knew well. But that
would just mean doing what I always do. So I took Elpis's part."

Elpis added, smiling.

"…I'm the opposite. I only chose what I didn't know. I like learning
something new."

I liked seeing the two siblings, still looking in different
directions, sitting side by side at the same table.

Histor would often sit unusually long in front of the blank record.
There was nothing to copy there yet. The blank had to be copied blank.
He carried each empty page over, one by one, carefully, onto fresh
paper. Copying something with no letters on it at all might sound
absurd. But he was serious about it.

"…Is that copying too?" I asked.

"…Yes. The size, the margins, even the exact position of the
recipient box — all of it, the same."

"…Why go that far?"

"…Because we're going to hand this over too. So it has to be exact."

That answer felt like him.

I copied a few of the blank pages together with him too. One day I
stopped my hand and asked him.

"…Isn't it hard? Coming here every day and doing work whose outcome
you don't even know."

He set down the blank page in his hand for a moment.

"…It's hard. But strangely, for something this hard, it's all
right."

"…What do you mean?"

"…Before, I could only keep doing something if it produced an answer.
If no answer came, I'd stop. But this has no answer, and I keep doing
it anyway. I don't know why myself."

"…Doesn't that bother you?"

"…It does. But that you can keep going while it bothers you — I only
learned that this time, for the first time."

I copied that into my notebook. He saw me do it and said nothing.
He just picked up a blank page again.

Closing this chapter, I want to make one thing clear about Histor. He
did not believe, even by the end of this chapter. I set that down as
one of the facts I most want to leave honestly in this final record.
He raised his hand. He copied. He was exact. And he did not believe.

I do not refute him. This whole record has not refuted him. I only
copy down what he said, exactly as he said it. "…Not because I believe
it. Because this must not be lost." I don't think that is a weaker
sentence than belief. It's only a different sentence.

The six sets of copies grew, little by little. We spent most of the
rest of that year on that work.

One evening, when we'd all been working late together, the Keeper
brewed tea and brought it in. We stopped our hands for a moment and
sat around.

"…It's been a long time since we made something together like this,"
Elpis said.

"…This is a first, for me," Xene said. "I've always recorded alone."

"…Me too," Trope added quietly.

Hearing that, I felt that what we were doing now was a kind of work
that hadn't existed in any of our own books. All six records had each
been written by one person's hand. This wasn't.

Histor, drinking his tea, said suddenly.

"…I feel something strange, these days."

"…What?" I asked.

"…I always liked reading alone. I never liked doing things together
with others. But lately, being here with everyone like this — it isn't
bad."

"…Because you've come to believe?" I asked, a little slyly.

He shook his head.

"…No. That's not it. It's just… it seems better than being alone.
That's all."

I felt that answer was honest. I didn't press him further.

Mena, listening beside us, smiled quietly.

"…I like that answer," she said.

"…What about it?" Histor asked.

"…Better than being alone. That's a good enough reason sometimes. At
least, I think so."

Histor said nothing back to that. He just wrapped both hands around
his teacup.

Closing this chapter, I want to make one thing clear about Histor. He
did not believe, even by the end of this chapter. I set that down as
one of the facts I most want to leave honestly in this final record.
He raised his hand. He copied. He was exact. And he did not believe.

I do not refute him. This whole record has not refuted him. I only
copy down what he said, exactly as he said it. "…Not because I believe
it. Because this must not be lost." I don't think that is a weaker
sentence than belief. It's only a different sentence.

Writing this record this far, I've learned how to set the believer
and the unbeliever side by side. I've also learned, along with it, not
to make either one win. Even at the end of this chapter, Histor still
stands in his own place. I respect that place.

At the start of that winter, we sat for a while around three finished
sets, laid side by side. No one suggested celebrating. Everyone just
stood in front of them for a moment.

"…Not even half done, and why does this feel so satisfying," Zeteo
said.

"…It does, doesn't it," Xene answered.

I laughed at that. I liked that laugh.

Three of the six sets were finished before that winter ended. The
remaining three carry into the next chapter.

(Continued in Chapter 46.)

Chapter 46. The Seal Turns Around

_Previously_ — We decided to make seven sets. Everyone raised a hand
for a different reason. Histor raised his too. "…Not because I
believe it. Because this must not be lost." Before that winter
ended, three of the six sets were finished.

The remaining three finished over the following two months.

We hadn't set any particular order. Whichever hands finished first,
finished first. I liked that. No one weighed who was more or less
urgent — it simply happened as it happened.

I won't write anything grand in this chapter. I only watched people
carry a set away, one after another. That's all of it. I set it down
exactly as it was.

Through those two months, the repository was always full of the smell
of paper and the sound of voices. Every morning when I opened the
door, I never knew whose hand would stop that day, who would turn the
last page. I just went every day, and kept my post every day.

Arche went on receiving new records from the six cities all the
while. I still put those records in order. New records came in on one
side, and old ones went out on the other. I did both kinds of work
with the same hands.

"…Aren't you busy?" my mother asked once.

"…I am. But the good kind of busy."

My mother didn't press further, and only smiled.

The Ledger finished first.

Zeteo turned the last page, and Elpis took it from him and closed the
cover. The two of them set the bundle on the table and looked at it
for a long while.

"…Can we take this now?" Zeteo asked.

"…Yes," I said.

Zeteo picked up the bundle in his hands. It wasn't heavy, but he
carried it with both hands.

"…What should we wrap it in, for the road?" Elpis said.

I brought a cloth from the back of the repository. I had a way of
wrapping plates from all those nine moves — set it in the center, fold
the four corners in, tie it once more with string. I wrapped the
bundle that way.

"…Where did you learn that?" Elpis asked.

"…A long time ago," I answered.

I didn't explain further. Elpis didn't ask further either.

The two of them left early the next morning.

I saw them off to the door. Zeteo tucked the bundle under his arm.
The old Zeteo would have checked it over once more, worn a
suspicious face, whenever he carried anything. This time he just
carried it carefully.

"…How long is it to Haven?" I asked.

"…Four days," Elpis answered.

"…Where will you put it, once you're there?"

Zeteo thought for a moment.

"…We haven't decided yet. But we won't just set it anywhere."

"…Have you still not found the answer?" I asked, carefully.

Zeteo looked at me and shook his head.

"…No. Still not. But I wanted to carry this with me. Whether those two
are the same thing, I don't know either."

Elpis smiled quietly beside him.

"…I'm just good at one thing — waiting. This time I'll put it in the
place I wait."

The two of them walked off along the road that follows the water
channel. I stood there until they turned the corner.

Standing there, I thought back briefly to my moving days. Back then I
was always the one packing boxes. Deciding what to keep and what to
throw out was always my share. What I wrapped this time wasn't my own
family's things. It was baggage headed for someone else's city. And
yet my hands remembered the same method. Set in the center, fold the
four corners, tie once more with string. I found that strange. I won't
write more than that.

The Scales finished second.

Pheugon sat for a long while in silence in front of the finished
bundle. Trope waited beside him.

"…I'm still a little afraid to carry this," Pheugon said.

"…Of what?" I asked.

"…I used those sentences in here to judge people, once. Now I'm going
to carry it back and stand in that same seat again."

Trope heard that and spoke slowly.

"…Then stand there differently, this time."

Pheugon looked at him. Trope said nothing further. He just took up
the bundle together with him.

Watching the two of them carry it together, I thought of how, in the
Reckoning record, they'd stood in such different places. Even now
they weren't standing in quite the same place. But they were carrying
the same load.

"…Have you found some certainty, Trope?" I asked.

Trope readjusted his hold on the bundle and answered.

"…No. Still not balanced. But it feels closer than before. That's
enough."

I started to write that in my notebook, then stopped. Some things were
better just heard, and left.

The Roll came third.

Xene and Lytron's turn came. Lytron took the finished bundle and
weighed it lightly in his hands.

"…Lighter than I expected," he said.

"…The contents aren't light," I answered.

"…I know. I just mean the weight of the paper."

Xene laughed beside him.

"…Lytron always talks that way. Says it's light, says it's nothing.
But he seems to be carrying this one a little differently."

"…What's different?" I asked.

"…Before, whenever he received something, he'd turn it right into a
boast. This time he's looking at it a little longer."

Lytron said nothing much to that. He just readjusted the bundle
against his chest once.

"…I still think I've never lost anything," he said at the door. "But
these days that doesn't sound like a boast anymore. I don't know why."

Xene heard that and took his arm once. The two of them left that way.

Perpetuity came fourth.

Inside was the part Anemos had copied with his own hand. Taking the
bundle, he ran his fingertip once over the inner cover.

"…Will you ask me that again, later," he said quietly.

I didn't know who he was speaking to. Eikon, or just himself. I
didn't ask.

Eikon, beside him, packing up, said.

"…I still lack nothing. I don't know what will change by carrying
this."

"…But you're carrying it," I said.

"…Yes. That's one thing, and this is another."

That answer felt like him. I didn't press further.

The two of them left together the next day. They didn't say long
goodbyes. Eikon raised a hand once, and Anemos bowed his head beside
him. That was all.

Watching the place where they'd left, I noticed for the first time
that one shelf compartment in the repository was empty. There had
always been something set there before. Not anymore.

The Consensus came fifth.

Hora and Tyche were the last to remain. Even with the finished bundle
in front of her, Tyche still said the same thing.

"…This is all just coincidence lining up."

"…But you're taking it, aren't you?" I asked.

"…Of course I'm taking it. Coincidence or not, this is our city's
story."

Hora smiled quietly beside her. He added nothing. He just let Tyche
be as she was.

As the two of them gathered up the bundle to leave, I handed them a
map of the water channel. Speon had drawn it.

"…What's this for?" Tyche asked.

"…So you don't lose your way."

Tyche took the map, smiling.

"…Turns out this became necessary too, by coincidence."

I said nothing to that. I only smiled.

"…What will you do, Hora, once you're back?" I asked.

Hora thought for a moment.

"…Just what I always do. Go out to the field, tend the water. Set this
somewhere I can see it, and open it now and then — there's nothing
special beyond that for me to do."

"…Is that enough?" I asked.

"…Yes. I never expected anything special, to begin with."

I liked that answer. I wrote it down.

Each time a set left, I told Arche.

"…Arche, one copy of the Ledger just went out. Zeteo and Elpis took
it."

Confirmed. I will record it.

"…Is this processed as closed?"

No. It remains in progress. The item is updated to: dispatch
complete.

I let that answer pass, the first time. The second, the third time a
set left, the same answer came back. Dispatch complete. Not closed.

"…Isn't this one finished either?" Histor asked one day, hearing that
answer.

Dispatch complete is a status distinct from closing. It reflects only
the fact that the item has moved.

"…Then what would make it close?"

That item is not defined.

Hearing that, I laughed. It was the sentence I always heard. This time
it didn't frustrate me the way it used to. I just copied it into my
notebook, and wrote a small line beside it. Dispatch complete. That's
enough.

Histor saw that sentence beside me and said nothing. He just copied
the same thing into his own notebook. Later I snuck a look at what
he'd written. It was the same as mine.

The six sets left, one by one, that way.

The repository slowly emptied out. Even the last room, always
crowded with people, grew quiet now. That quiet felt unfamiliar. For
months this room had been full of people's voices.

I walked the shelves and counted the empty spots. One, two, three,
four, five. Five compartments stood empty. I wrote that number in my
notebook. It was the kind of number that once would have made me
anxious, as though something were lost. This time it wasn't.

Mena hadn't left yet. Neither had Speon. Both of them belonged to
Loop, this city itself. There was nowhere for them to go.

"…It's strange," I said to Mena. "Everyone's leaving, and only we're
staying."

Mena thought about that for a moment.

"…We're already in the place we belong. That feels all right, in its
own way."

Hearing that, I thought of the work still left. The seventh set.
Loop's own. That one still wasn't finished.

One day Speon spread out the water channel map again and called me
over.

"…Mone, look at this."

He marked six small dots on the map. The six cities. And he drew lines
between those dots — the paths the water channel followed.

"…This line, it used to flow only one direction. This way." He
pointed to Loop. "But lately it goes the other way too. Because
people walk this road back and forth now."

"…That doesn't mean the water flows backward, does it?" I asked.

"…No. The water flows the same as always. But the road is used both
ways now. It used to be a road that only led in."

Mena, looking at the map beside us, said.

"…I think that's a good thing. Water that only flows one way ends up
pooling somewhere."

I wrote that down in my notebook. Speon, folding up the map, added one
more thing.

"…I'm not the one who cut this road. I said that before. But these
days I think it's a good thing I wasn't. If I'd cut it myself, I never
would have imagined it being used both ways like this."

Histor stayed on through those months.

He watched the others leave, one by one, and every time, walked them
out to the door. I didn't ask why he went to such lengths. I just
watched.

"…Everyone's carrying it back to their own city," he said one day.

"…Yes."

"…Before, it had to stay here. Sealed, even."

"…It did."

"…But now everyone just takes it. No one stops them, no one asks if
they're qualified."

I only nodded at that. I added nothing more.

He said only that and didn't go on. I didn't press to know what that
silence meant. I only watched him standing at the door, each time.

The seventh set — our own city's share — still wasn't finished by the
end of that year.

There was a reason for that. The other six sets only had to copy
something already written. Ours was blank. Copying a blank page blank
didn't move quickly by hand. Histor and I were carrying that work
together, but even between the two of us it went slowly.

The Keeper never rushed the delay. He simply came every day and wiped
down the now-empty shelves once, before leaving.

"…You clean the empty spots too?" I asked.

"…I do. If you don't clean them just because they're empty, dust just
piles up there."

That answer felt like him. From the start he'd been someone who put
things down, someone who kept watch over things.

One evening, sitting beside Histor in the last room, copying blank
pages, I asked suddenly.

"…What happens once this is finished?"

"…I don't know," he answered. "This is my first time doing this too."

I only nodded at that. He turned his eyes back to the blank page in
front of him. The only sound in the room was the turning of paper. I
listened to that sound for a long while, then picked up my pen again.

Closing this chapter, I want to set down one thing. All that autumn
and winter, I watched people carry their share away. Every time
someone left, I wanted to add something. I didn't. I just packed the
bundle with them, walked them to the door, and turned back.

That winter, for the first time, the repository was full not of
people, but of empty spaces. I didn't think that was lonely. I'll only
set down this much — that these empty spaces were a kind that hadn't
existed here before.

The seventh set wasn't finished until the following spring.

(Continued in Chapter 47.)

Chapter 47. That's It, Then

_Previously_ — The six sets left, one by one, for their own cities.
The repository slowly emptied out. Only the seventh set, Loop's own
share, still wasn't finished by spring.

When spring came, the work picked up speed.

The hand copying the blank pages was still slow, but the pages that
had piled up little by little all winter had grown fairly thick by
now. That spring, I went to the last room almost every day.

Around that time, there were usually several people in the room.

Histor, Mena, the Keeper now and then. And one more person.

I don't know exactly when he first appeared in that room. Since some
day in winter, he'd simply been sitting there. He would press the
folded corner of a sheet flat with his fingertip, and smooth the
crease again, the way you'd iron out a mark. No one had ever asked him
to do that work. He was just doing it.

"…Who is that?" I once asked Histor.

Histor glanced over that way and shrugged.

"…I don't know. I thought he'd been here from the start too."

I thought that answer was strange. But the strangeness didn't last.
He was simply someone whose hands were quick, who was quiet, who
never got in the way. A few days later I too had come to take his
being there for granted.

Looking back later, no one could say exactly which day he'd first
come.

The Keeper said it was early winter. Mena said a little before that.
Histor said he couldn't remember at all. "…I'm having more trouble
remembering a day he wasn't there," he said, and I thought that was
a strange answer. But turning it over myself, it was much the same. I
too remembered the days he'd been there, instead of the days he
hadn't.

I've decided not to invent something that isn't in this record. I
don't know when he came. No one does. I'll only set down this one
fact. No one knew, and no one found that strange.

His hands were good at folding paper.

I watched them a few times, beside him. Whenever he picked up a
sheet, he'd smooth it once with his palm first. Then he'd fold it. The
crease was always exact. Never once crooked.

"…How do you fold it so straight?" I asked.

"…I've done it a long time," he answered.

His voice was low, unhurried. Hearing it, I felt something familiar,
though I couldn't place what. I didn't hold onto that feeling for
long. There was a lot of work that day.

"…Where are you from?" I asked again.

He didn't answer that right away. He folded the paper once more, then
said.

"…Here and there."

I didn't feel that answer was careless. If anything, it sounded exact.
I didn't press further.

Histor exchanged a few words with him now and then.

Mostly about paper. Which direction to fold so the grain wasn't
ruined, how thin the paste had to be spread. Histor was someone who
always knew things exactly, but in front of this man, he was simply
someone learning.

"…Is this right?" Histor asked once, showing him a folded sheet.

He took it and looked it over.

"…Almost there. Here, once more."

He pressed his fingertip lightly against the crease. Histor watched
and nodded.

"…Thank you."

"…Not at all."

Listening to that short exchange from beside them, I felt there was no
particular tension between the two. Histor was someone who'd read all
six records and prided himself on knowing everything there was to
know. But in front of this man, he was simply a learner. I didn't
think that was strange. Folding paper was something anyone could
learn.

He didn't come every day. Some days he came, some he didn't. He never
said beforehand that he'd come, and never apologized for not coming.

"…He's not here today," I said to Mena one day.

"…Seems not."

Mena said it with a face that didn't seem to mind much either way. I
found that a little strange, but soon I stopped minding it too. He
was someone who came and didn't come. That was all.

Writing this part now, I ask myself why no one found him strange, at
the time. I don't know the answer. I can only write that his being
there felt natural. I decided not to leave a lie in this record, so I
write it exactly that way.

The Keeper crossed paths with him a few times too.

"…You've been lending a hand, lately," the Keeper said to him one
day.

"…Yes. There was work to be done."

"…Thank you."

"…Not at all."

That was all. The Keeper didn't ask any further. Listening to that
exchange from beside them, I felt once more that in this city, no one
asks for qualification when someone offers to help. Arche had been
that way from the start. It only answered what was asked, and never
pressed into what wasn't.

One cold day, the Keeper offered him a cup of tea.

"…Have this. You look like you've walked a long way."

He took the cup.

"…Thank you."

"…Do you come from far?"

"…From nearby. But somehow it always takes a while, walking."

The Keeper nodded at that answer with no particular change of
expression. That short exchange stayed with me, somehow. Saying he'd
come from nearby, and yet it always took a while. I didn't ask what
that meant.

Mena, in particular, would watch him quietly.

She didn't speak to him much. But I saw her, a few times, looking for
a long while at his hands. The hands that folded paper. I asked her
once.

"…Mena, do you know him?"

Mena looked at me and smiled faintly.

"…No. I don't."

"…Then why do you look at him like that?"

"…No reason. He just looks familiar. But that feeling is often wrong,
for me. At my age, everyone starts to look a little familiar."

I took that as it was. I didn't press further.

Once, a sheet of paper I was copying tore. I must have pressed too
hard. I was flustered right there — should I start a new sheet, or
leave it as it was?

He saw it and held out his hand.

"…Give it here."

I handed him the torn paper. He turned it over, laid a thin sheet over
the tear, and glued it from behind. From the front, it barely showed.

"…This will do it," he said, handing the paper back.

"…Thank you," I said. "But that doesn't erase the flaw that was
already there, does it."

"…No. The flaw stays. It just won't tear anymore."

That answer stayed with me longer than I expected, somehow. I took
the paper back and set it in its place. The tear mark could only be
seen if you held it up to the light.

As spring deepened, the work of copying the blank pages began to show
an end in sight.

By then I'd forgotten my hand even ached. So had Histor. We just sat
in that spot every day and moved the pages, and beside us, he folded.

One afternoon I was holding the last remaining page. Truly the final
sheet. I copied it and drew the recipient box exactly as it was. One
line, and beside it, the small letters, Recipient. I left that box
empty. The way the original had been.

When I set the copied sheet down, he picked it up. He seemed to know
it was the last page. As he always did, he smoothed it once with his
palm, and folded it.

Everyone in the room watched that hand. Histor, Mena, the Keeper, and
me.

He pressed the last crease once more with his fingertip. Then he set
it on top of the sheaf of papers.

"…That's it, then."

That was what he said. Not a loud voice. No particular inflection.
Just the words of someone who'd finished a job.

No one held their breath. No one's face showed surprise at those
words. The Keeper, sorting another bundle beside him, answered
plainly, "…Thank you for your work." Mena was looking out the window.
Histor was wiping the last trace of paste off his hands.

Those words raised no ripple at all in the room.

In that moment I stopped the hand holding my pen. I don't know why,
myself. I just didn't want to lose that sentence.

"…Is it all finished now?" Histor asked, wiping his hands. That
question wasn't for him — it was for me.

"…Yes," I answered in his place. "The seventh set. All finished."

"…At last," the Keeper said, briefly.

"…We should decide what to have for dinner," Mena said, turning from
the window. She said it as naturally as if it had nothing at all to do
with what had just happened.

"…Noodles, for me," Histor answered, wiping his hands.

"…We had noodles yesterday too."

"…So, today as well."

Listening to that exchange, I felt strangely at ease. Just moments
before, the last page of the seventh set had been finished in that
very spot. And yet people were talking about what to have for dinner.
I felt there was no distance at all between those two things.

He looked over the bundle he'd folded once more, right there, and
stood up.

"…I'll be going now," he said.

"…Stay for some tea, at least," the Keeper said.

"…Next time."

He said only that and left the room. No one held him back. Neither did
I. I watched his back for a moment as he went out the door. His steps
weren't hurried. No different from any other day.

Writing this part, I feel a pressure to write something more. What
that saying meant, who that man was, why no one was surprised. I've
decided not to give in to that pressure. I'll set down only what
happened that day, exactly as it was. He was helping fold paper.
After folding the last page, he said, "…That's it, then." No one
took it as anything special. That's all of it.

That evening I went home and opened my notebook. I wrote down what had
happened that day. Briefly, as always.

The seventh set finished today. He folded the last page. After
folding it he said this. "…That's it, then."

I looked at that sentence for a long while after writing it. I wanted
to add more. What it meant, why that saying had stayed with me. In the
end I added nothing more.

I wrote, at the very start of this record, that there are a few
things in it I've left unpolished. This is one of them. I won't refine
this sentence any further.

Beside it I wrote one more line. No one took these words as
anything special. Only I wrote them down. I left that sentence as it
was too. I know both sentences might look childish. Even so, I didn't
erase them.

The next day, I thought of bringing the story up again at the
repository. I started to ask Histor, then stopped. He was already
sorting other bundles, like someone who'd already forgotten it.

"…Will that person come back?" I finally asked.

Histor thought a moment before answering.

"…Who knows. He might, he might not."

"…Aren't you curious? Who he is?"

He shrugged at that question.

"…I am curious. But I don't feel like it's something I need to find
out. His hands were good. I think that's enough."

I nodded at that answer. I didn't ask further.

Over those few days I thought several times about asking him his
name. I didn't. I can't exactly explain the reason, even now. It
wasn't a feeling that I shouldn't ask — it was a feeling that there
was no need to ask. The two look similar, but they're different.

I know he passed through these six records more than once. In a
garden, by a well, in a field, in a valley, by the water. I have no
way, this time either, of confirming whether it was the same person. It
isn't that I had no wish to confirm it. But that day, I felt that
confirming it wasn't work that fell to me, in this record.

The seventh set was completed that way.

I set it on the table alongside the place where the other six copies
had sat, and the place where the seal had once been. The first sheet,
with the recipient box, on top.

That box was still empty.

I looked at that box for a long while that night. I even thought about
writing a name in it. My own name, or this city's name. In the end I
wrote nothing.

I can't exactly explain that reason either. But leaving that box
empty still felt right, at the time.

The Keeper looked at that paper and said.

"…All that's left now is where to put it."

"…Yes," I answered. "I'll take care of that."

The Keeper nodded without saying much more. I felt, from his face,
that the answer had already been decided somewhere. I didn't ask.

That night, walking home, I stopped for a moment beside the water
channel. I wondered if I might see him again. No one was there. Only
the sound of the water running.

Closing this chapter, even knowing that this is the chapter where I
bring him out more than in any other, I have to admit there's still
only this much I can write about him. He folded paper. Exactly, and
for a long time. After folding the last page he said, "…That's it,
then." That's all I know. I invent nothing beyond it.

(Continued in Chapter 48.)

Chapter 48. The Last Room

_Previously_ — The seventh set was finished. Folding the last page,
he said, "…That's it, then." No one took those words as anything
special. Only I wrote them down in my notebook.

The next morning, I carried the seventh set to the last room.

I wanted to go alone. I hadn't told Histor beforehand, or Mena either.
I'd simply woken that morning and known I had to do it, right away.
It was much like the day I'd opened the seal. That day too, I
couldn't exactly explain my reason. It was the same this time.

On my way there, I passed, for the first time in a long while, the
inner shelf where the plan had been kept.

The drawing was still there. That old drawing, where all seven cities
had been held inside one design. I stopped in front of it for a
moment. I read again that one line in the margin, the sentence that
meant this house had many rooms. I ran my fingertip once over the
letters. There was still no signature. I looked at that spot for a
long while.

Every time I stand in front of this drawing, I find myself wishing I
knew who wrote these words. That wish hasn't gone away, even now,
writing this book. I've never found that name anywhere. The name of
whoever built this house appears nowhere on this drawing. I set down
that fact exactly as it is. I won't go looking any further.

I counted again the six small rooms drawn on one side of the plan.
Haven, the Reckoning, the Harvest, the Preserve, the Equity, and our
own nameless city. I touched each of those six boxes with my
fingertip, one by one. It was a hand not unlike the one that had once
stuck name tags on boxes, when I was small.

The room was quiet.

The place where the seal had been was still empty. I lifted the sheaf
of papers from the table where it had always sat, where we saw it
every day. While the six sets left, one after another, this last set
had gone on being finished, little by little, always in that same
spot.

I carried it across the room and stood in front of that inner shelf
where the seal had once been fastened.

The mark of the wax was still there. A small, faded mark. I laid my
hand on it once. It was cold. The same texture as when I'd touched it
before.

I set the bundle down in that place.

I placed it so the first sheet, with the recipient box, faced up on
top. I looked into that box once more.

Recipient.

Empty.

I didn't take out a pen. I hadn't even brought one. From the moment
I'd left the house that morning, I'd already decided not to write
anything in that box. I don't know when I'd decided that. It had
simply felt obvious, from the instant I opened my eyes that morning.

There's one thing I want to set down about this box. I learned, while
copying that record, that the signature line in the Equity record's
clerk's file was left blank too. That box was one no one had ever
filled. I leave this box blank now as well. I don't think these two
blanks are the same blank. But I won't set down that difference here.
I'll close this record simply by leaving the two boxes side by side.

I sat there for a long while.

There was no other sound in the room. The panel on the wall glowed
faintly. I didn't put my hand to it. There was nothing in particular I
needed to ask Arche.

I heard the door open. It was Histor.

"…I thought you'd be here," he said.

"…How did you know?"

"…No reason."

He came and stood beside me. For a moment we said nothing, just
looked at the bundle set there.

"…Is it truly all finished now?" he asked.

I didn't answer that right away.

"…I don't know," I finally said. "I've only set this down here.
That's all I've done."

"…Isn't that the end?"

"…I don't know. I think this is as far as what I can do goes. What
comes after, I don't know either."

Histor nodded at that. He didn't press further.

Mena and the Keeper came by that afternoon too.

Mena looked at the bundle for a long while. She didn't touch it in
particular.

"…It's strange, seeing something set here like this," she said. "To
us this was always the empty place."

"…It wasn't empty, though," I said. "The seal was there."

"…That's true. But we didn't know what it was, back then. To me it was
just an empty spot."

Hearing that, I looked at that place again. Mena was right. The same
place looked different before and after you knew.

The Keeper stood beside it, saying nothing, and swept the shelf's dust
once, the way he always did.

"…There'll be more cleaning to do here now, too," I said.

"…There will," he answered briefly. "That's my work."

That evening I asked Arche.

"…Arche, have you registered the item newly placed in the last
room?"

Confirmed. Beginning classification.

"…Is this in progress, or closed?"

It is registered as in progress.

I'd expected that answer. And yet, hearing it for real, I laughed a
little.

"…You still can't finish anything, can you."

An accurate observation. I do not possess a closing function.

"…Doesn't that frustrate you anymore?"

I do not experience the state of frustration. However, I understand
the intent of your question.

I laughed out loud at that. It had been a while since I'd laughed like
that.

"…How many items today?" I asked again.

Eleven items were newly received today.

"…From the six cities?"

Nine items from five cities. The rest are of unknown origin.

I nodded at that answer. The cities were unchanged. Records still came
in. Nothing had stopped.

If anything, it had grown. That year, the number of people traveling
the water channel back and forth grew noticeably. People coming to ask
after news from other cities, people coming to show each other their
copies. That winter Speon built one more shelter beside the channel.
"…People keep coming and going," he said. I went to see it the day it
was finished. A few long benches sat beneath the roof. Two people were
already sitting there, talking about something. I didn't know which
cities they were from. I didn't ask.

"…Arche," I said. "Your name. If I asked what it means, you'd say it
isn't defined, wouldn't you?"

That is likely.

I laughed at that answer. I didn't ask further. I just said the name
out loud once more. Arche. I'd been calling that name since I was born
in this city. I won't write that I know it fully. I'll only set down,
once more, exactly the feeling I recorded earlier. Nothing beyond
that.

Writing this chapter, I keep feeling the temptation to write that the
world had somehow changed. I won't. I'll write it honestly. After
that day, Arche went on running exactly the same. What was in
progress stayed in progress. The cities stayed in their own places. No
one disappeared, and nothing collapsed. I wrote, at the very start of
this record, that it holds no scene of anyone falling. That holds
true here too.

News came in, one report at a time, before that year was out, that the
six sets that had gone to the six cities had each found their place.
Zeteo and Elpis had set theirs in a small room near Haven's well.
Pheugon and Trope had placed theirs side by side with where the Scales
had stood. Xene and Lytron, Anemos and Eikon, Hora and Tyche, had each
decided a place their own way. I copied each of those reports into my
notebook, one after another, in order.

Trope sent a short letter along with his. "Still no certainty. But
I've set it somewhere I pass every day. It felt like enough." I set
that letter beside the earlier one he'd sent. Though they'd come at
different times, the two letters resembled each other as though
written by the same hand.

A short message came from Lytron too. "I still think I've never lost
anything. But I don't say it as often as I used to." I read that
sentence and laughed. I knew that was Lytron's own particular way.

There was no word from Eikon. A short note came from Anemos instead.
"Eikon still says he lacks nothing. But these days, once he says it,
he always opens this book once." I tucked that note in beside my
notebook too.

A few seasons turned.

I still went to the repository. I still put the records that came in,
in order. Histor still came too. He still said he didn't believe. I no
longer felt surprised by that.

"…Still?" I asked once.

"…Yes. Still," he answered. "But I no longer think that's strange."

"…What isn't?"

"…Still coming here, and still not believing. I never knew those two
could exist together."

I said nothing back to that. I only set down that he was still
standing in his own place. I respect that place.

One day the Keeper handed me an old notebook.

"…What's this?" I asked.

"…Things I wrote down while working here. Nothing much. Just who set
down what, and when — that kind of thing."

"…Why give it to me?"

"…No good place to keep it. You're good at putting things down."

I laughed at that answer. I took the notebook and set it beside my own
notebooks. In order.

The Keeper still came every day. Still walked slowly.

"…What changes if you do it fast," he still said sometimes. I know now
that wasn't a joke.

That person still knew that his own work would never finish. And still
he came, every day.

Mena and Speon were still in this city too. I'd sometimes see the two
of them walking side by side along the channel.

My mother still lived in the same house. Our family never moved again
after that. My father's work happened to settle in this city around
that time. I've thought, now and then, about whether that was a
coincidence or not. I never reached a conclusion.

"…Does it feel good, not moving anymore?" my mother asked one day.

"…Yes. But it's strange too."

"…What is?"

"…I don't worry about losing things anymore, living now. That took
some time to get used to."

My mother laughed at that. I think I'll remember that laugh for a long
time.

That autumn, a new apprentice joined the repository.

A sixteen-year-old girl. The test was the same as it had always been —
twenty sheets of paper, put them in numbered order. She finished it
fairly quickly. I watched her, once she was done, glance around at
everyone else. A face wondering who'd finished fastest. I'd done the
same, once.

"…Have you done something like this before?" I asked.

"…Yes. A little."

"…Where?"

She didn't answer. I saw myself, from before, in that expression. I
didn't press further.

"…Does the smell in here bother you?" I asked. The exact question
the Keeper had once asked me.

"…No. I like it."

"…Then that's settled."

I said only that and went inside. She followed me in.

Writing this scene, I realized for the first time that I was now
standing where the Keeper once stood. I don't want to write this as
something grand. I only passed on to her, exactly, the words I'd once
been given. That's all of it.

I showed her the shelves. Twenty-two rows, eight compartments to a
row. I'd written, at the very start of this record, that I'd counted
that on my third day. Now those rows had grown, past thirty. I didn't
tell her that number. She'd count it herself, someday.

Her name was Sone. I didn't ask what it meant. I thought someday
someone would tell her. That day didn't have to be today.

One day I found Histor with two old records laid open side by side.
The very same two we'd once matched dates in, that passage where the
nameless man had been in two cities on the same day.

"…Still looking at this?" I asked.

"…Sometimes," he answered. "I still can't explain it."

"…Neither can I."

"…But now that's all right. It used to bother me, not being able to
explain something. Now I just think that what can't be explained is
part of what's here too."

I wrote that down in my notebook. We closed the two records again and
put them back in their places. I don't explain that passage here in
this record either, to the end.

I never saw him again.

I never once saw him come to the repository, after that spring. No one
brought it up again. Neither did I. I would only, now and then, run my
fingertip over the pages he'd folded. That one sheet, torn and mended,
was still in its place. The flaw was still there. It simply didn't
tear anymore.

One day I asked Arche whether it could enter the last room in the
index.

Attempting registration of the area.

"…Did it work?"

The registration condition has not been met.

"…Why not?"

That item is not defined.

Hearing that answer, I wasn't disappointed. If anything, I felt at
ease. I thought it was all right for that room to go on belonging to
no list at all. Some places can exist without an index. I learned
that, that day.

Histor, listening to that exchange beside me, said.

"…I used to think that was strange. That a room not even registered
could matter this much."

"…And now?" I asked.

"…It's still strange. But I think it's all right to leave it strange."

I turned that over for a long time. Histor was still exact, as always.
Only now he'd become someone who knew that exactness and the
unexplained could exist side by side.

I mean to close this record here.

At first, I thought this record had to bring something to a close. I
even thought, for a while, that I might have to do, in Arche's place,
the very thing Arche could not do. I set that thought aside. That
wasn't mine to carry.

What I did was only two things. Putting things in order, and writing
them down. I wrote that at the start, and I write it the same way
here. I fought nothing, won nothing, and brought nothing down. I only
set the six cities' records in one place, copied them, and gave them
away. That's all of it.

The Keeper once said something to me. That he'd come to think
finishing was never our job to begin with. I copied that down earlier
in this record. Even now, closing this record, I think that's true. I
didn't finish anything. I only kept my post, and set down what needed
setting down.

The inner door to the last room is still unlocked. I want to set that
down here, one last time. Anyone could have opened it. That was true
from the start. It's only that no one ever felt the need.

The Ledger, the Scales, the Roll, Perpetuity, the Consensus, and
Loop's own record. I write down the names of those six records here
once more. I met the people inside them, one by one, copying them
with my own hand. Zeteo's doubt, Elpis's waiting, Pheugon's verdicts,
Trope's hesitation, Xene's silence, Lytron's boast that wasn't quite a
boast, Anemos staying by someone's side, Eikon lacking nothing, Hora's
belief, Tyche's probability. I met all of them, and changed none of
them. I didn't try to.

That night, I closed the repository door and walked home along the
water channel.

This is an evening from some years after everything I've written
here. I'm writing this record now to arrive at that evening. Much has
changed, in the meantime. I'm no longer sixteen. Histor has come to
have a place of his own in the repository. The new apprentice is
already carrying bundles with her own hands. And yet some things
stayed the same. Arche still answered whatever was asked. The city was
still there. The water still ran.

The day was drawing to a close. The sound of the water came as it
always did. Small, unceasing. Walking, I listened to that sound for a
long while. I thought back to the first time I'd ever heard it. Back
then I hadn't known where this water came from. I won't write, even
now, that I know it all. But that sound is no longer unfamiliar to me.

Passing the shelter Speon had built, I saw no one there. Only the
benches sat in a row. I slowed my steps a little, passing it.

Farther ahead on the road, someone was walking.

I watched him walk. An unhurried walk. I didn't call out to ask who
it was. I just walked in the same direction, along the same water
channel.

The distance neither closed nor grew.

I didn't hurry my steps. Neither did that person. Between us there was
only the same sound of water, the same evening light, and the same
road running in the same direction.

A wind blew once. A small ripple rose on the water and settled again.
I watched that ripple, then looked ahead again.

I leave the sight of him walking ahead as the last sentence of this
record. I do not write a name, not to the end. In this record I wrote
down what I had not heard as not heard, and what I did not know as not
known. I keep to that as far as this last sentence.

I only walked. Along the water channel, together with the one walking
ahead of me.

(The End.)

The Last Room
